# The Influence of Shoujo Manga Consumption on Gendered Pronoun and Sentence-Final Particle Use Among Queer Japanese Young Adults
# 1 Introduction and Research Rationale

The relationship between mass media and everyday language has long animated sociolinguistic inquiry, but it has rarely been settled. Whereas earlier variationist work treated television, film, and print fiction as marginal to processes of language change—sites of representation rather than transmission—a growing body of scholarship now treats mediatized speech as a resource that speakers actively draw upon to construct identity, signal stance, and access registers unavailable in their immediate face-to-face networks. This shift in sociolinguistic theorizing demands cases in which the imprint of media on language can be isolated with some clarity. The present study identifies one such case at the intersection of three highly specific phenomena: the structurally gendered grammar of contemporary Japanese; shoujo manga as a stylized, ideologically saturated mediatized register; and the linguistic practices of queer Japanese young adults, whose social identities sit uneasily with the binary indexical economy that joseigo (women's language) and danseigo (men's language) presuppose. By examining how queer young adults in Japan use first-person pronouns and sentence-final particles in relation to their shoujo manga consumption, this report aims to test, refine, and where necessary complicate the claim that mass media functions as a linguistic resource through which minority speakers fashion non-normative selves.

## 1.1 Research Problem and Central Inquiry

The central problem motivating this study can be stated simply: under what conditions, and through what mechanisms, does sustained engagement with a stylized media register reshape the linguistic repertoire of speakers who already stand in an oblique relationship to that register's prescriptive social meanings? The question is not new. Sociolinguistics has long entertained competing positions on whether media exposure can drive linguistic variation and change, ranging from skepticism about any direct effect on core grammar to more nuanced "mediated innovation" frameworks that allow for media to act as a catalyst, amplifier, or stylistic resource even when it is not the primary engine of structural change. Jannis Androutsopoulos has argued that conceptualizing this relationship requires moving beyond the catch-all notion of "the media" toward more analytically precise concepts of mediation—the technological facilitation of linguistic signs—and mediatization—the staged representation, uptake, and recontextualization of linguistic forms across communicative circuits.[^1] On this view, "the media" is "a theoretical cul-de-sac," and progress depends on tracking how specific genres stage particular linguistic varieties and how audiences subsequently take them up, recirculate them, or resist them.[^1]

Within this broader debate, Japanese offers a uniquely tractable empirical setting because two of its most socially loaded grammatical subsystems—first-person pronouns and sentence-final particles (SFPs)—are marked for gender in ways that English and many other extensively studied languages are not. Japanese speakers must, in nearly every utterance involving self-reference or modalized predication, choose among forms that carry probabilistic or, in some cases, near-deterministic gender associations. The pronouns *ore*, *boku*, *watashi*, *atashi*, *uchi*, and *jibun* index distinct configurations of gender, formality, age, and stance; the sentence-final particles *wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*, *ze*, *zo*, *na*, and *sa* are similarly stratified, with some forms (e.g., *kashira*, *wayo*) ideologically anchored to feminine speech and others (e.g., *ze*, *zo*) to masculine speech. Importantly, prior sociolinguistic work has shown that the relationship between these forms and biological sex is not deterministic in actual usage, and that there are well-documented gaps among prescriptive ideology, naturalistic production, and listener perception. This already-loose mapping creates a system in which speakers can manipulate the variables strategically—and in which the imprint of stylized models, including those drawn from mediatized fiction, becomes a plausible source of variation worth investigating empirically.

Shoujo manga, the focus of this study, is a Japanese comics genre marketed primarily to young female readers but consumed by audiences far beyond that demographic. Crucially, shoujo manga deploys a highly stylized register of gendered speech: hyper-feminine heroines, sensitive *bishōnen* love interests, gender-crossing protagonists, and characters whose pronoun and SFP choices systematically diverge from the more muted patterns documented in corpus studies of everyday Japanese conversation. The genre has, moreover, generated subgeneric conventions—Boys' Love (BL), yuri, and the broader vocabulary of *otokoyaku*-style cross-gender performance inherited from Takarazuka and shoujo theatrical traditions—in which homoromantic narratives, gender ambiguity, and emotionally intensified speech are foregrounded. The central inquiry of this study is therefore whether queer Japanese young adults, who routinely encounter shoujo manga as part of their media diet, draw upon its distinctive linguistic patterns when they themselves use Japanese—particularly in moments of identity-relevant self-reference and stance-taking. Do their pronoun and SFP choices mirror the conventions of the manga register? Do they exceed those conventions, innovating new combinations? Or do they remain anchored to the unmarked patterns documented for the general population, with manga functioning as an interpretive resource rather than a productive one?

These questions are not merely descriptive. They engage directly with the theoretical architecture of third-wave variationist sociolinguistics, in which linguistic variables are understood not as passive correlates of social categories but as resources within indexical fields that speakers mobilize to construct stylistic personae. They also engage with debates about the role of fictional and mediatized registers—what Japanese sociolinguists term *yakuwarigo* or "role language"—in supplying speakers with exemplars they may consciously or unconsciously incorporate into their own production. The present study treats the queer Japanese young adult as an especially informative speaker-type for testing these claims, precisely because the alignment between their social identity and the binary prescriptive grammar is, by definition, non-default.

## 1.2 Rationale for the Queer-Shoujo-Japanese Intersection

The decision to focus on the convergence of queer identity, shoujo manga consumption, and Japanese gendered grammar is methodologically deliberate rather than thematically opportunistic. The argument for this case rests on a simple analytical premise: media-derived linguistic resources are most clearly visible when speakers cannot fully rely on default, prescriptive patterns to express who they are. For majority cisgender, heterosexual Japanese speakers, the binary system of joseigo and danseigo provides—at least ideologically—a ready-made repertoire that aligns with their socially expected identities. Even when their actual production diverges from prescription (as the literature consistently demonstrates), the deviations are easily reabsorbed into normative readings. For queer speakers, by contrast, the prescriptive system is inadequate, ambivalent, or actively hostile to the identities they wish to construct. This mismatch creates analytic leverage: any systematic patterning in their linguistic choices, especially patterning that aligns with stylized media registers, becomes more visible and more traceable than would be the case in a population whose default linguistic options already match their social location.

Several considerations reinforce this rationale. First, the binary architecture of Japanese gendered language is not merely a matter of statistical tendency but of explicit metapragmatic ideology, taught in schools, modeled in etiquette manuals, and continuously reproduced in popular discourse. Speakers who do not identify with one of the two ideologically sanctioned gender categories—including lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, X-gender, and nonbinary individuals, among others—face the practical problem of self-reference and stance-marking in a grammar that offers no neutral ground. They must choose, and each choice indexes them in ways the prescriptive system has already pre-interpreted. This pressure makes alternative indexical resources, including those drawn from media, especially valuable.

Second, shoujo manga is uniquely positioned to supply such resources. Unlike news media or sports broadcasts, shoujo manga is a register in which gendered speech is consciously stylized, where deviations from "normal" gendered patterns are routinely deployed for characterization, and where queer-coded characters—from the gender-ambiguous *bishōnen* hero to the romantic protagonists of yuri and BL—speak in ways that prescriptive Japanese would not predict. The genre thus does what naturalistic conversation rarely does: it makes the gendered indexicality of pronouns and SFPs both salient and manipulable. Readers encounter not just a model of how women or men "should" speak, but a curated catalogue of how characters across a wider gender spectrum can speak, with each combination of pronoun and SFP typifying a particular emotional register, social stance, or romantic positioning.

Third, the intersection is empirically rich. Japan has a substantial and demographically diverse queer population, and shoujo manga is one of the country's most widely circulated cultural exports. The conjunction of a structurally gendered grammar, a richly stylized media register, and a minority population for whom the prescriptive grammar is necessarily problematic creates conditions in which the question "does media exposure affect language use?" can be operationalized and tested with greater precision than in many other sociolinguistic settings. The case thus offers a sharper test of media-driven linguistic influence than equivalent studies of majority populations could provide, in line with the principle that minority and non-default speakers often illuminate the structure of a sociolinguistic system more clearly than statistically dominant ones.

It is essential, however, to note from the outset what this rationale does not assume. It does not assume that queer Japanese young adults form a homogeneous community, that they all consume shoujo manga, or that those who do necessarily incorporate manga-derived forms into their speech. It does not assume that media exposure is the sole or even primary driver of any patterns observed; peer networks, other media genres including BL and yuri, broader social media discourse, and individual stylistic agency are all live alternative explanations that subsequent chapters will weigh carefully. Nor does it assume that Japanese queer identity maps onto Anglo-American LGBTQ+ categories; Japanese-specific terms such as *seiteki shōsūsha* (sexual minorities), *onee*, and *X-jendā* (X-gender) carry their own histories, communities, and linguistic associations that any responsible analysis must respect. The rationale for this intersection is therefore not that it guarantees a finding of media influence, but that it constitutes an unusually clear arena in which such influence, if present, can be detected, characterized, and theoretically situated.

## 1.3 Research Questions, Hypotheses, and Scope

Building on the problem statement and rationale, this study is organized around three interlocking research questions, each accompanied by a working hypothesis. These hypotheses are explicitly framed as falsifiable expectations rather than predictions, in keeping with the empirical orientation of the project.

| # | Research Question | Working Hypothesis |
|---|-------------------|--------------------|
| RQ1 | To what extent does the frequency and intensity of shoujo manga consumption correlate with queer Japanese young adults' choice and distribution of first-person pronouns (*ore*, *boku*, *watashi*, *atashi*, *uchi*, *jibun*) across communicative contexts? | Higher consumption is associated with more diverse and contextually flexible pronoun repertoires, including increased use of forms that diverge from sex-assigned-at-birth prescriptions. |
| RQ2 | To what extent does shoujo manga consumption correlate with the use of gendered sentence-final particles, including stereotypically feminine forms (*wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*) and masculine forms (*ze*, *zo*, *na*)? | Consumption correlates with selective uptake of marked SFPs that pattern with manga conventions, particularly in identity-salient or affectively heightened utterances, rather than with a uniform shift across all contexts. |
| RQ3 | How do contextual factors—interlocutor identity, situational formality, communicative modality, and metalinguistic awareness—mediate the relationship between manga exposure and observed linguistic patterns? | Manga-derived forms appear most prominently in peer, in-group, written, and identity-performative contexts, with strategic and partly conscious deployment, rather than as undifferentiated background features of all speech. |

Several boundary conditions should be made explicit. The study's population is queer Japanese young adults, operationally understood as adults aged approximately 18–35 who self-identify with a non-heterosexual and/or non-cisgender identity within a Japanese cultural and linguistic context; the operationalization of "queer" will be elaborated in Chapter 6 with attention to community-internal heterogeneity and the politics of self-identification in Japan. The linguistic features under analysis are restricted to first-person pronouns and a curated set of sentence-final particles; second- and third-person pronouns, address terms, honorific morphology, and lexical gendered vocabulary are acknowledged as relevant but lie outside the present analytical scope. The media corpus is delimited to shoujo manga as conventionally defined by publication venue and target demographic, including its overlap with subgenres (yuri, certain BL works) where stylistic conventions are continuous; adjacent genres such as shōnen manga, seinen manga, anime adaptations, and live-action drama are treated as comparator or control categories rather than primary objects of analysis.

Equally important is what the study does not claim. It does not seek to establish that shoujo manga causes language change in any general or population-level sense. Following critiques of overly strong "media influence" claims, the project frames its analytical target as the role of mediatized registers in individual stylistic repertoires, not as a mechanism of systemic change.[^1] It does not treat queer young adults as informants representative of all queer Japanese speakers, nor of all shoujo readers; the population is bounded and the findings are correspondingly bounded. It does not adjudicate normative questions about whether queer speakers "should" use particular forms, or whether shoujo manga's representations of gendered speech are progressive or regressive. Its remit is descriptive and analytical: to characterize the patterns and to theorize the mechanisms that produce them.

## 1.4 Significance and Contribution of the Study

The significance of this inquiry can be articulated along three converging axes—theoretical, empirical, and practical—each of which connects to ongoing scholarly conversations.

Theoretically, the study contributes to third-wave variationist sociolinguistics and to the sociolinguistics of mediatization. Third-wave approaches conceptualize linguistic variables as carrying social meaning within indexical fields, with speakers strategically deploying variables to construct personae and stances. By examining how queer Japanese young adults navigate a highly indexical grammar with reference to a stylized media register, the study provides an empirically grounded test of how indexical resources circulate from staged media performances into individual repertoires—a process Androutsopoulos describes as the uptake and recontextualization of linguistic fragments across the "circuit" of mediatized representation.[^1] The case promises to refine our understanding of when and how mediated language influences immediate language, contributing to long-running debates among scholars such as Trudgill, Stuart-Smith, Tagliamonte, and Sayers about the proper place of media in sociolinguistic theory. Beyond Japan-specific concerns, the study also engages broader questions about identity performance and language increasingly raised in studies of digital and platform-mediated communication, where users are observed to "construct and curate their identities through stylized language choices" and to draw on shared cultural texts as resources for "ambient affiliation" and group-aligned self-presentation.[^2]

Empirically, the study fills a substantive gap at the intersection of Japanese sociolinguistics, language and sexuality studies, and media linguistics. While Japanese gendered language has been extensively documented, and while shoujo manga has been analyzed by literary and cultural studies scholars, there has been comparatively little systematic investigation of how the manga register interacts with the actual linguistic practices of minority readers, particularly queer ones. By collecting both production and perception data from queer young adults, and by triangulating self-reported manga consumption with elicited and naturalistic language use, the study aims to contribute fine-grained, methodologically transparent evidence to a domain currently dominated by either ideological discourse or anecdotal observation.

Practically, the study has implications for how we understand the agency of marginalized speakers within prescriptive language ideologies. Japanese gendered language has often been described as a coercive system that constrains how speakers can present themselves; the present inquiry asks whether and how queer speakers, drawing on a popular media register, find room for stylistic maneuver within—and at times against—that system. To the extent that the answer is affirmative, the study contributes to a broader picture of how minority communities use cultural texts as raw material for self-fashioning, a picture that resonates with comparable findings in contemporary digital sociolinguistics, where marginalized users have been shown to mobilize platform-specific linguistic forms to assert visibility and challenge dominant narratives.[^2]

The remainder of the report is organized to develop these contributions systematically. Chapter 2 establishes the theoretical framework, integrating third-wave variationism, indexicality, exemplar-based models, and theories of mediation and mediatization. Chapter 3 surveys the structure and ideology of Japanese gendered language, with detailed attention to the pronouns and sentence-final particles at the heart of the study. Chapter 4 characterizes shoujo manga as a mediatized register, including its hyper-feminine, hyper-masculine, gender-crossing, and queer-coded conventions. Chapter 5 situates queer young adults within the contemporary Japanese sociolinguistic landscape. Chapter 6 details the research design and methodology, including operational definitions, instruments, and ethical considerations. Chapters 7 and 8 present the analysis of pronoun and SFP usage, and examine the role of context, awareness, and indexical salience. Chapter 9 synthesizes findings within the broader discussion of media, minority identity, and linguistic agency, and Chapter 10 offers implications, limitations, and conclusions, including avenues for future research. Throughout, the study seeks to remain attentive to the specificity of the Japanese case while contributing to the broader theoretical project of understanding how mass media participates—not deterministically, but consequentially—in the linguistic lives of minority speakers.

# 2 Theoretical Framework: Social Meaning, Indexicality, and Media-Mediated Language

The empirical questions posed in Chapter 1—whether and how queer Japanese young adults' consumption of shoujo manga shapes their use of gendered pronouns and sentence-final particles—cannot be answered by assembling correlations alone. They require a theoretical apparatus capable of explaining why a linguistic variable carries social meaning at all, how that meaning can shift across contexts and speaker types, by what cognitive route stylized fictional speech might be incorporated into the repertoire of a flesh-and-blood speaker, and under what conditions media exposure becomes sociolinguistically consequential rather than merely incidental. This chapter assembles such an apparatus by integrating four bodies of work that have, until recently, often been pursued in relative isolation: the three-wave history of variationist sociolinguistics, Silverstein's theory of indexical order as developed by Eckert into the notion of an indexical field, exemplar-based usage models drawn from cognitive and laboratory phonology, and the sociolinguistic theorization of media as mediation and mediatization. To these the chapter adds a Japan-specific layer, Satoshi Kinsui's concept of *yakuwarigo* (role language), which describes precisely the kind of stylized, enregistered fictional speech that shoujo manga deploys. The synthesis offered at the end of the chapter is not a freestanding theory but a working framework: a vocabulary of variables, indexical orders, indexical fields, exemplars, mediatization circuits, and role language, organized in a way that allows the empirical analyses of Chapters 7–9 to interpret pronoun and SFP patterns as the joint outcome of structure, ideology, cognition, and media circulation.

## 2.1 From Correlation to Construction: The Three Waves of Variationist Sociolinguistics

To understand why a third-wave framework is essential to the present study, it is useful to retrace briefly the analytic path that led to it, since each wave both bequeathed and constrained what its successors could ask. Penelope Eckert's influential periodization identifies three successive "waves of analytic practice" in the treatment of social meaning in sociolinguistic variation, each marked by a different conception of what variables are and what they do[^3][^4].

The first wave, inaugurated by William Labov's New York City studies in the 1960s and elaborated through decades of large-scale survey research on phenomena such as English /-t, d/ deletion, treated linguistic variables primarily as correlates of macro-social categories—socioeconomic class, age, gender, ethnicity—and concentrated on identifying the systematic patterning of "orderly heterogeneity" within speech communities[^5]. The methodological achievements of this wave were substantial: through carefully sampled face-to-face interviews and the elicitation of vernacular speech via topic modules designed to override the formality of the interview situation, Labovian variationism demonstrated that variation is constrained simultaneously by internal linguistic factors and external social ones, and that synchronic variation is the primary site at which diachronic change becomes visible[^5]. Junko Hibiya's Tokyo study of voiced velar nasalization, in which the alternation between word-internal [g] and [ŋ] was shown to correlate with age and with contact with the prestigious *yamanote* district, exemplifies the productive application of this framework to Japanese[^5]. Yet, as Eckert later argued, the cost of this analytic rigor was a tendency to treat the social as "a fixed and external structure that is only reflected in linguistic variability," with meaning becoming "the stuff of casual speculation, but not part of the enterprise"[^6]. The famous correlations between variables and demographic categories were taken as self-explanatory; the actual content of "being working class" or "being female" that the variables supposedly indexed remained largely unexamined.

The second wave responded to this limitation by introducing ethnographic depth, with researchers embedding themselves in local communities and replacing imposed macro-categories with locally salient ones such as Detroit's "jocks" and "burnouts" or particular peer groups within a school[^6]. While still correlational in spirit, the second wave allowed variables to point to socially meaningful local types rather than abstract demographic strata, preparing the ground for the recognition that the same variable might mean different things in different communities of practice.

The third wave, which Eckert articulates most fully in her 2008 paper "Variation and the indexical field," reconceptualizes variables as resources for stylistic practice rather than as reflections of pre-given social positions[^6]. On this view, "variation constitutes an indexical system that embeds ideology in language and that is in turn part and parcel of the construction of ideology"[^6]. Speakers do not merely "have" a variable distribution because of who they are demographically; they deploy variables as part of stylistic moves through which they construct, claim, modify, or contest particular personae and stances. Eckert is explicit that this approach does not abandon the macro-social categories that earlier waves established; rather, it insists that variables "index demographic categories not directly but indirectly, through their association with qualities and stances that enter into the construction of categories"[^6]. The example of Trudgill's Norwich study illustrates the shift: where the first-wave account assigned "toughness" as an explanatory motive for working-class men's covert prestige, the third wave asks how toughness itself enters the indexical field of a variable, why it becomes more salient than, say, egalitarianism, and how speakers across genders and classes can selectively activate it through stylistic practice[^6].

Two features of the third-wave framework are decisive for the present study. The first is its conception of style as **bricolage**—a process drawn from Hebdige's subcultural theory in which "individual resources (in this case, variables) can be interpreted and combined with other resources to construct a more complex meaningful entity"[^6]. Eckert's analysis of Palo Alto preppy girls who pegged their jeans to claim a measure of independence without surrendering their preppy identity exemplifies the principle: stylistic agents segment a noticed style into meaningful elements, attribute particular indexical values to particular elements, and selectively recombine them with elements from their own existing repertoire[^6]. Crucially, "linguistic style is rarely constructed in as intentional a fashion as clothing style," but it is "similarly a process of bricolage"[^6]. This formulation is exactly what is needed to theorize a queer reader who encounters a shoujo manga heroine using *atashi* with *wayo*, a *bishōnen* hero softening his speech with *no*, or a gender-crossing protagonist alternating *boku* and *watashi*, and who may—consciously or not—incorporate fragments of these stylized configurations into a personal repertoire while leaving others aside.

The second decisive feature is the third wave's reconfiguration of speaker agency. Where first-wave variationism implicitly cast speakers as bearers of demographic indices whose linguistic behavior could be predicted from their location in social space, the third wave treats them as agents who "use variables not simply to reflect or reassert their particular pre-ordained place on the social map but to make ideological moves"[^6]. The use of a variable becomes "an indexical claim which may either invoke a pre-existing value or stake a claim to a new value"[^6]. This shift is indispensable for analyzing queer Japanese speakers, whose social positions are neither fully accommodated by the binary categories *joseigo* and *danseigo* presuppose nor cleanly indexed by the variables those ideologies enregister. If queer speakers were treated as passive correlates of demographic categories, the analyst would be forced into the absurd position of asking which side of the binary they "really" belong on. The third wave instead allows the analyst to ask what ideological moves are being made when, for example, a lesbian-identified young adult uses *boku* in writing to a close friend, when a gay man modulates between *watashi* in formal contexts and *atashi* in *onee*-coded peer settings, or when an X-gender speaker deploys *jibun* as a strategically ambiguous self-reference. None of these moves is reducible to a demographic correlation; each is an indexical claim within a fluid and contested field.

A subtle but important consequence follows. Because third-wave variationism treats variables as components of styles rather than as expressions of fixed identities, it dissolves the distinction between "real" speech and "performed" speech that older sociolinguistic theory often relied upon. In the third wave, all speech is performance in the sense that all speech involves stylistic positioning; there is no neutral, unstylized vernacular that mediated speech merely contaminates. This dissolution is what permits the present study's central move: treating shoujo manga not as an external influence that distorts an otherwise authentic queer linguistic practice, but as one stylistic resource among many that queer speakers may, with varying degrees of awareness and intention, recruit into their ongoing self-construction.

## 2.2 Indexical Orders and the Indexical Field

The third-wave reconceptualization of variation requires a more granular theoretical vocabulary for describing how a single variable can carry multiple, shifting meanings. The vocabulary that Eckert adopts—and that the present study will adopt in turn—is Michael Silverstein's notion of indexical order, elaborated into Eckert's own concept of the indexical field[^6].

Silverstein's framework engages directly with the older Labovian distinction among indicators, markers, and stereotypes. Indicators are dialectal variables that distinguish populations but have not attracted enough metapragmatic attention to figure in stylistic variation; markers are variables that have entered stylistic awareness without becoming explicit objects of metapragmatic discussion; stereotypes are variables that have become subject to overt commentary[^6]. In Silverstein's terms, an indicator is a **first-order index**: it merely identifies membership in a population—Vineyarders, Beijingers, New Yorkers—without further evaluative content. But the social evaluation of any such population is "always available to become associated with the index and to be internalized in speakers' own dialectal variability to index specific elements of character"[^6]. When this happens, the variable acquires a second-order indexical value, becoming a marker that can be deployed to position the speaker with respect to the qualities now associated with the population. Crucially, the process does not stop at second order: any nth-order index "is always available for reinterpretation—for the acquisition of an n + 1st value"[^6]. Eckert emphasizes that the apparent linearity of these orders is misleading; rather, the reconstruals are "always already immanent" because they take place within "a fluid and ever-changing ideological field"[^6].

From this iterative process, Eckert develops her central concept of the **indexical field**. An indexical field is not a list of fixed meanings but "a constellation of meanings that are ideologically linked," any one of which can be activated in the situated use of a variable[^6]. The field is "fluid, and each new activation has the potential to change the field by building on ideological connections"[^6]. The indexical field is therefore at once a representation of accumulated meaning potentials and a site of ongoing ideological reproduction.

The illustrative cases Eckert assembles clarify what an indexical field looks like in practice. Kathryn Campbell-Kibler's experimental work on (ING) demonstrates that listeners associate the velar variant with education, intelligence, articulateness, and—in some hearings—pretentiousness or condescension, while the apical variant indexes casualness, lack of effort, unpretentiousness, easygoingness, and at the negative pole laziness or rebelliousness; which meaning is activated depends on the speech style in which the variant occurs and the listener's pre-existing assessment of the speaker[^6]. /t/ release in American English furnishes an even more striking case, since Eckert shows that the same variable participates in three apparently disparate styles—nerd-girl speech, Orthodox Jewish boys' Talmudic argumentation, and gay men's "prissy" or "bitchy diva" personae—because all three styles draw on overlapping but distinct portions of an indexical field that includes clarity, education, refinement, emphasis, exasperation, and stance accretion[^6]. Qing Zhang's study of Beijing managers shows that the rhotacized "Smooth Operator" variable and the interdental "Alley Saunterer" variable index local Beijing personae that emergent yuppies strategically distance themselves from while still relying on the indexical field to position themselves as cosmopolitan rather than provincial[^6].

These examples carry several implications that bear directly on the analysis of Japanese gendered language. First, an indexical field is heterogeneous and includes both "permanent qualities" and "stances," with the latter capable of accreting into the former through repeated use—what Mirka Rauniomaa calls stance accretion[^6]. Second, the meanings within a field are not random but ideologically linked; Eckert insists that "no trivial meanings come to be associated with variables" and that indexical values are "part and parcel of the ideological work of society"[^6]. Third, different participants in a speech community may construe the same variable differently, with class-stratified ideological orientations producing class-stratified evaluations of the same form[^6].

Mapping this framework onto Japanese gendered pronouns and SFPs yields an analytical picture quite different from the prescriptive table in which *ore* equals "masculine," *atashi* equals "feminine," *wa* equals "feminine," and *ze* equals "masculine." Each form is better modeled as an anchor for an indexical field encompassing not only gender but also age, formality, affect, regional and class associations, and stance. *Boku*, for instance, anchors a field that includes (probabilistically) masculine gender, but also boyhood, modesty, intellectual or sensitive masculinity, and—in particular subcultural and shoujo-manga contexts—a stance of androgynous self-presentation available to female and nonbinary speakers. *Atashi* anchors a field that includes femininity, casualness, intimacy, and—in *onee* registers—a flamboyant, knowing stance available to gay men. *Wa* anchors a field that includes feminine softness, but also emphasis, self-confirmation, and, in regional dialects, masculine assertion. *Ze* anchors masculine roughness, but also playful camaraderie, performative bravado, and shoujo-manga-style swaggering hero speech. The ideological linkages among these meanings explain why a single form can be used to make ideological moves that are not reducible to any one of them in isolation.

The third-wave model thus makes the central analytic claim of the present study tractable. When a queer Japanese young adult uses, say, *boku* in a context where the prescriptive system would predict *watashi* or *atashi*, the move is not best described as "talking like a man" or as deviance from a feminine norm; rather, it is an activation of one or more meanings within the indexical field of *boku*—perhaps modesty, perhaps androgyny, perhaps a stance of intellectual seriousness, perhaps an alignment with the *bishōnen* heroes of shoujo manga, or some bricolage among them. Whether the move builds on a pre-existing indexical value or stakes a claim to a new one is itself an empirical question, and one that the indexical-field framework enables us to pose precisely.

## 2.3 Exemplar-Based Models and the Cognitive Storage of Stylized Speech

Indexicality theory provides a robust account of social meaning, but it leaves underspecified the cognitive question of how speakers come to associate particular forms with particular fields and how those associations enter into production. The third-wave framework presupposes that speakers somehow store rich, socially indexed traces of the speech they encounter and deploy fragments of these traces in subsequent production. Exemplar-based models, drawn from cognitive and usage-based phonology, provide the mechanistic account that the third wave requires.

The foundational claim of exemplar theory, articulated by Joan Bybee and Janet Pierrehumbert and elaborated by subsequent scholars, is that speaker-hearers store "specific tokens of use" categorized phonetically with reference to variables in the context, rather than (or in addition to) abstract phonemic representations[^7][^8][^9]. Bybee's *Phonology and Language Use* develops this into a usage-based model in which "a speaker/hearer's experience with language affects the representation of phonology," accounting for phenomena such as phonetically gradual sound change, lexical variation, and the disproportionate effect of token frequency on reductive change[^7][^8]. The model is explicitly anti-abstractionist in its starting point, though more recent work has converged on hybrid frameworks in which abstract categories and detailed exemplars coexist, with abstraction operating over experienced tokens[^10].

Two features of exemplar models are particularly relevant for the present study. The first is that the stored representations are not phonetically neutral but **socially indexed**. As the recent overview of bilingual exemplar theory by Babel and colleagues puts it, "phonetic detail beyond the abstract phonological category, often accompanied by social and speaker-specific information, is part of linguistic knowledge"[^10]. Listeners are "keenly aware of phonetic information and its associated linguistic and social meaning on implicit and, occasionally, explicit levels," and phonetic knowledge is "associated with social and contextual information, connecting 'core' linguistic knowledge with indexical and sociopragmatic cues and categories"[^10]. This proposition is critical for the present study because it provides the cognitive mechanism by which the indexical fields described in §2.2 are populated, maintained, and activated. A reader who repeatedly encounters *atashi* in shoujo manga heroines' interior monologues, *boku* in *bishōnen* protagonists' assertions, or *wayo* in older sophisticated female characters' dialogue is not merely receiving abstract grammatical information; she is accumulating socially weighted exemplars that link specific forms to specific configurations of character, affect, and stance.

The second relevant feature is that **not all exemplars are weighted equally**. As Babel and colleagues emphasize, drawing on Sumner and colleagues' work, "variation is socially weighted such that not all experienced items may contribute equivalently to the category"[^10]. The model articulated by Todd, Pierrehumbert, and Hay specifies multiple stages at which weighting can occur: activation, identification, discriminability evaluation, typicality evaluation, and—on the production side—type selection, target selection, bias, and imprecision[^10]. At each stage, the social and contextual relevance of an exemplar can affect whether it is encoded, retained, and selected as a target in production. The implication for the study of identity-relevant linguistic resources is significant: identity-salient exemplars—forms used by characters with whom a reader identifies, in scenes that resonate with the reader's own experiences, in genres that the reader values—may be disproportionately retained and disproportionately available for activation in subsequent production, even if the absolute frequency of exposure is modest.

Hybrid exemplar models are also explicit that abstraction and category formation proceed *over* experienced tokens, with thresholds for discriminability and typicality determining whether new tokens update the system[^10]. This architecture allows the model to accommodate two phenomena that any account of media-influenced language must explain. First, it explains how stylized fictional speech can leave a measurable trace on a speaker's repertoire even without direct face-to-face exposure: written and audiovisual exemplars feed the same exemplar-storage mechanisms as spoken-interactional exemplars, albeit with potentially different weightings reflecting their different social and pragmatic profiles. Second, it explains why such traces are typically partial and selective rather than wholesale: a reader does not become a *bishōnen* simply by reading *bishōnen* dialogue, because the typicality thresholds operating in her own production environments will exclude many of the manga register's exemplars from being deployed in everyday interaction, while leaving others—those that align with her stylistic projects—available for selective use.

A further methodological implication, drawn from Pierrehumbert's foundational work on exemplar dynamics with respect to word frequency, lenition, and contrast, is that high-frequency and identity-salient items are more likely to undergo exemplar-driven shift than peripheral ones[^11]. For the analysis of pronoun use, this suggests that the high-frequency, identity-saturated act of self-reference will be especially sensitive to exemplar accumulation, since each speech act of self-reference both draws on and updates the stored distribution of pronominal exemplars indexed to particular personae. SFPs, while less obligatory than first-person pronouns, are likewise high-frequency and stance-laden, making them a second prime locus for exemplar-driven variation.

It is worth noting that exemplar models were developed primarily to account for phonetic and phonological variation, and their extension to morphosyntactic items such as pronouns and discourse particles requires care. The principle that experienced tokens are stored with social indexation, however, is not specific to phonetics; usage-based grammar more broadly assumes that constructions are stored with their pragmatic and social profiles. The present study therefore adopts exemplar-theoretic vocabulary as an analogical and mechanistic resource rather than as a strict phonological theory, treating stored pronoun and SFP tokens as indexed exemplars that can be activated in production according to context, identity-salience, and the bias of co-activated material.

Together, third-wave indexicality and exemplar-based usage models supply the two halves of a single account: the former specifies what variables mean and how those meanings are organized; the latter specifies how meanings come to be carried by particular forms in particular speakers' minds and how those speakers retrieve and deploy the forms in production. What remains to be specified is the third leg of the framework: the mechanisms by which mediatized speech enters this circuit in the first place.

## 2.4 Mediation, Mediatization, and the Sociolinguistics of Media Influence

The question of whether and how mass media affect language has been one of the most contested in modern sociolinguistics. As noted in Chapter 1, Jannis Androutsopoulos has argued that conceptualizing this relationship requires moving beyond the catch-all notion of "the media" toward more analytically precise concepts. His distinction between **mediation**—the technological facilitation of linguistic signs across distance and time—and **mediatization**—the staged representation, uptake, and recontextualization of linguistic forms across genres and audiences—provides a productive starting point. On Androutsopoulos's view, "the media" is a "theoretical cul-de-sac," and progress depends on identifying specific genres and tracing how their linguistic stylizations are taken up by particular audiences in particular ways.

This formulation reframes the older debate, in which scholars such as Trudgill argued that broadcast media play at most a peripheral role in core grammatical change, while others such as Stuart-Smith documented apparent effects of soap opera viewership on phonological variation in Glaswegian English, and Sayers and Tagliamonte continued to debate the proper place of media in sociolinguistic theory. The mediation/mediatization distinction does not adjudicate the debate but redirects it: rather than asking whether "media" cause "change," the analyst asks how a particular mediatized register stages a particular configuration of linguistic and social meaning, how audiences encounter it, and how fragments of it may—or may not—be incorporated into individual or collective repertoires.

Several conditions emerge from this body of work as plausibly necessary or facilitating for media influence on language. First, the mediatized forms must be **salient**, both in the sense of being perceptually noticeable and in the sense of being pragmatically distinctive against the background of the audience's other linguistic experience. Second, the audience must in some way **identify** with, attend closely to, or actively engage the mediatized speakers, since unattended exposure does not appear to produce the kind of socially weighted exemplar storage that the cognitive model in §2.3 requires. Third, **repeated exposure** is necessary for the mediatized forms to accumulate sufficient exemplar density to compete with face-to-face input. Fourth, and most consequentially for the present study, mediatized forms appear most likely to enter individual repertoires when **face-to-face models are inadequate** to the speaker's communicative or identity needs—when, for example, the prescriptive linguistic options available in the immediate community do not provide the indexical resources the speaker requires for self-construction.

This last condition is precisely the situation of queer Japanese young adults in relation to the binary architecture of *joseigo* and *danseigo*. The prescriptive system, as Chapter 3 will document in detail, does not merely under-supply queer speakers with neutral options; it actively over-determines the available options with binary indexical content that may be incompatible with the personae such speakers wish to construct. Under such conditions, the framework predicts, mediatized registers that stage non-default gender configurations should function as especially valuable stylistic resources—catalysts and amplifiers, in Sayers's terminology, rather than primary drivers of population-level change. The present study does not claim that shoujo manga is changing the gendered grammar of Japanese; it claims something more modest and, in the framework articulated here, more theoretically defensible: that shoujo manga supplies socially weighted exemplars of indexically rich gendered forms that queer readers, lacking adequate face-to-face models, may selectively incorporate into their own stylistic practice.

It is worth noting two further qualifications that the mediatization framework imposes on the analysis. First, mediatized influence is rarely unidirectional: media draw on, exaggerate, and stylize linguistic forms that are already in circulation in the speech community, and audiences who recirculate mediatized forms feed back into the broader ecology in ways that media producers may subsequently re-mediatize. The "circuit" of mediatization is genuinely circular, and any apparent flow from media to speaker is also, in part, a flow from speaker (via earlier rounds of stylization) back to media. Second, the same mediatized register may be taken up differently by different audiences, with the resources of indexical fields being activated selectively along lines of pre-existing identification and need. The present study's focus on queer young adults specifically is therefore not an arbitrary narrowing but a theoretically motivated choice, since the framework predicts that this audience segment is among the most likely to engage shoujo manga's stylized gendered forms as stylistic resources rather than merely as entertainment.

## 2.5 Role Language (Yakuwarigo) and Stylized Registers in Japanese

The mediatization framework developed in §2.4 takes on additional specificity when applied to Japanese, where Satoshi Kinsui's concept of *yakuwarigo* or "role language" provides a Japan-specific theorization of exactly the phenomenon at issue. Although the search results returned for this report do not include Kinsui's primary publications, *yakuwarigo* has become a foundational concept in Japanese sociolinguistics and one that the indexical-field and exemplar frameworks set out above are particularly well suited to interpret.

*Yakuwarigo* refers to stylized speech patterns that are systematically associated, in the imagination of Japanese speakers, with particular character types: old-man speech (*rōjingo*) characterized by particular pronouns (*washi*), copula forms (*ja*), and SFPs; *ojō-sama* or aristocratic young lady speech with hyper-feminine pronoun *watakushi*, particles *wa*, *wayo*, *kashira*, and elaborate honorific morphology; hero speech with assertive *ore* and *ze*; and many others. The defining feature of *yakuwarigo*, on Kinsui's account, is that these registers are immediately recognizable to Japanese readers and viewers as belonging to particular character types despite—and indeed *because of*—the fact that they are rarely if ever produced in this concentrated form by actual speakers in naturalistic conversation. *Yakuwarigo* is, in this sense, a paradigmatic case of mediatized language: it is enregistered through repeated stylization in fiction, manga, anime, dubbed translations, and popular culture, and it functions as a culturally shared inventory of indexical resources that authors can deploy to evoke a character without lengthy description.

Three features of *yakuwarigo* are critical for the present framework. First, *yakuwarigo* is **enregistered**: in Asif Agha's terms, which Eckert invokes in her treatment of style and indexical field, an enregistered voice is one that has been "located in a continual process of production and reproduction," such that a particular configuration of features comes to be conventionally associated with a particular social type or persona[^6]. Japanese *yakuwarigo* is enregistered in precisely this sense, with each register's characteristic configuration of pronoun, copula, and SFP forms operating as a tightly bundled stylistic unit that audiences recognize as a whole.

Second, *yakuwarigo* is **iconic and indexical at once**. The forms that constitute a register such as *ojō-sama* speech do not merely correlate with a character type; they iconically perform the type, in much the same way that, in Zhang's Beijing study, rhotacization is heard as "oily" and that oiliness is in turn iconically linked to the Smooth Operator persona[^6]. Eckert's characterization of iconization, drawing on Irvine and Gal, applies directly: the linguistic form is heard as embodying a quality of the social type it indexes[^6]. In *yakuwarigo*, the elaborated honorifics, the breathy *kashira*, and the soft-feminine *wayo* are heard as embodying the gentility, distance, and refinement of the *ojō-sama*; the sharp *ze*, *zo*, and *ore* are heard as embodying the assertiveness of the hero. This iconicity is what makes *yakuwarigo* such a powerful stylistic resource and, simultaneously, what makes its indexical fields susceptible to recursive elaboration when characters and contexts are recombined in non-default ways.

Third, *yakuwarigo* is **stylistically modular**. Because each register is composed of identifiable, partially separable features, stylistic agents can—through the bricolage process Eckert describes—appropriate fragments of a register without adopting it wholesale[^6]. A shoujo manga heroine who uses *atashi* with *no* but eschews *kashira* and *wayo* is engaging in precisely such modular bricolage: she selects elements from a fully feminine register and combines them with elements drawn from a more contemporary, casual configuration, producing a composite register specific to the shoujo genre. A queer reader who, in turn, adopts *atashi* with *no* in particular contexts while deploying *boku* in others is performing a further round of bricolage on the manga register itself, segmenting its modules and recombining them with elements drawn from other indexical sources.

Shoujo manga, on this account, does not constitute a single *yakuwarigo* but rather a curated and internally diversified set of registers. The hyper-feminine heroine, the gentle and androgynous *bishōnen*, the gender-crossing protagonist, the *otokoyaku*-inflected princely figure, and the queer-coded characters of yuri and BL each instantiate distinct configurations of pronoun and SFP usage, and each carries a distinct portion of the larger indexical field of Japanese gendered language. Chapter 4 will develop the empirical specifics of these conventions; the theoretical point to register here is that shoujo manga's distinctiveness as a media register lies precisely in its diversification of *yakuwarigo* along lines that the prescriptive binary system of *joseigo* and *danseigo* does not accommodate. Where the prescriptive system offers two ideologically sanctioned configurations, shoujo manga stages many, and stages them with sufficient repetition and emotional intensification to make them available as socially weighted exemplars in the storage systems of attentive readers.

## 2.6 Synthesis: A Framework for Media-Influenced Repertoires of Minority Speakers

The four strands of theory assembled above converge on a layered analytical model that the empirical chapters of this report will operationalize. The model can be stated as a sequence of four claims, each licensed by the preceding sections.

**Claim 1: Gendered Japanese variables anchor indexical fields rather than carry fixed gender meanings.** Following Silverstein's account of indexical order and Eckert's elaboration of the indexical field, each of the pronouns and SFPs at the center of this study—*ore*, *boku*, *watashi*, *atashi*, *uchi*, *jibun*, *wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*, *ze*, *zo*, *na*, *sa*—is best modeled not as a sign with a fixed value but as the anchor of a constellation of ideologically related potential meanings spanning gender, age, formality, affect, stance, and persona[^6]. The relative salience of any one meaning within the field is determined in context, by the surrounding stylistic configuration and the listener's pre-existing assessments[^6].

**Claim 2: Shoujo manga, as a stylized media register, stages and circulates particular configurations of these indexical fields through *yakuwarigo*.** Drawing on the mediatization framework set out in §2.4 and the theorization of role language sketched in §2.5, shoujo manga is understood as a site at which gendered linguistic forms are staged in iconic, enregistered, and emotionally intensified configurations that often diverge sharply from naturalistic majority usage. The genre's hyper-feminine, hyper-masculine, gender-crossing, and queer-coded character speech selectively activates and elaborates regions of the indexical fields anchored by particular pronouns and SFPs, making available to readers a set of stylized possibilities that the prescriptive binary system does not authorize.

**Claim 3: Readers store these staged configurations as socially weighted exemplars.** Following the exemplar-based usage models reviewed in §2.3, the speech of shoujo manga characters does not merely pass before readers' eyes; it accumulates in long-term memory as indexed exemplars of gendered forms tied to particular character types, emotional contexts, and stylistic configurations[^7][^8][^9][^10]. These exemplars are not weighted uniformly: identity-salient exemplars—those tied to characters with whom the reader identifies, in scenes that carry particular affective weight, in genres the reader values—are likely to receive greater storage weight and to be more readily activated in subsequent production[^10].

**Claim 4: Queer speakers, whose default indexical options are inadequate to their identities, draw selectively on these exemplars in stylistic practice.** Building on the third-wave conception of style as bricolage and on the conditions for media influence specified in §2.4, queer Japanese young adults are theorized as stylistic agents whose construction of non-normative personae requires indexical resources that the prescriptive binary grammar does not supply[^6]. Shoujo manga's stylized configurations, having been stored as socially weighted exemplars, become available for selective bricolage—activated in some contexts and not others, recombined with elements from other registers, and deployed to make ideological moves whose specific content depends on the speaker's local stylistic project.

This layered model generates a set of falsifiable predictions that subsequent chapters will operationalize and test. First, the model predicts that **higher consumption** of shoujo manga, conceived as denser exemplar input from the staged register, should be associated with **broader and more flexible repertoires** of pronoun and SFP use among queer speakers, since denser input supplies a richer stock of stylistic resources for bricolage. Second, the model predicts that consumption-related differences should be most pronounced in **identity-salient and affectively heightened contexts**—self-reference at moments of self-disclosure, stance-marking in peer in-group interaction, written and creative production—because these are the contexts in which the exemplars stored from shoujo manga are most likely to align with the activation requirements of speech production. Third, the model predicts **selective rather than wholesale uptake**: queer readers should not produce shoujo manga registers as such, but should incorporate fragments—particular pronoun choices in particular contexts, particular SFPs at particular pragmatic moments—while leaving other register elements aside, in keeping with the bricolage logic of stylistic practice and the typicality thresholds of exemplar-based production[^6][^10]. Fourth, the model predicts that **metalinguistic awareness should vary across variables and across speakers**, with some forms appropriated consciously as deliberate stylistic moves and others incorporated below the level of explicit attention, in keeping with the indicator–marker–stereotype salience hierarchy as reframed in indexical-order terms[^6]. Fifth, the model predicts that **the same form may carry different indexical activations for different speakers and listeners**, since indexical fields are construed differently across positions in the social and ideological landscape[^6]; queer speakers' uses of *boku*, for instance, may activate meanings (androgyny, intellectual seriousness, alignment with shoujo *bishōnen*) that majority observers do not perceive or perceive differently.

To consolidate the framework, the following table summarizes the four theoretical strands, the contribution each makes to the integrated model, and the corresponding analytical commitment for the empirical chapters.

| Theoretical Strand | Core Concept | Contribution to the Framework | Analytical Commitment |
|---|---|---|---|
| Three-wave variationism | Style as bricolage; speaker agency in stylistic practice[^3][^4][^6] | Reframes variables as resources rather than reflections; legitimates analysis of queer speakers as agentive stylizers | Treat pronoun and SFP choices as ideological moves, not demographic correlates |
| Indexical order and indexical field | Iterative reconstrual of nth-order indexes; constellation of ideologically linked potential meanings[^6] | Models gendered variables as anchors of multi-meaning fields rather than fixed gender markers | Map each variable's indexical field across gender, age, formality, affect, stance |
| Exemplar-based usage models | Storage of socially indexed tokens; weighted retention; production via activation, target selection, and bias[^7][^8][^9][^10][^11] | Supplies cognitive mechanism by which mediatized speech enters individual repertoires | Treat reading exposure as exemplar input; expect identity-salient exemplars to be disproportionately retained |
| Mediation/mediatization and role language | Distinction between technological mediation and staged mediatization; *yakuwarigo* as enregistered fictional speech | Specifies how shoujo manga functions as a circulator of stylized indexical configurations | Treat shoujo manga as a curated, modular set of *yakuwarigo* registers available for selective bricolage |

The framework also imposes important constraints on what the empirical analysis can and cannot claim. It does not license claims that shoujo manga *causes* particular linguistic outcomes in a population-level sense; the cognitive mechanism is probabilistic, the uptake is selective, and competing influences—peer networks, other media genres, individual stylistic agency—remain operative throughout. It does not license claims that observed correlations between manga consumption and linguistic patterns straightforwardly reflect media effects, since selection effects (queer young adults already drawn to non-default gender configurations may consume more shoujo manga *because* those configurations resonate with pre-existing identity-driven preferences) and reverse causation (incipient stylistic projects may motivate increased manga consumption rather than vice versa) must be entertained alongside exposure-based exemplar accumulation. The framework does, however, license a distinctive analytical move: rather than asking whether media "cause" linguistic change, it asks how mediatized exemplars, indexical fields, and stylistic agency interact in the repertoires of speakers whose identities are not adequately served by prescriptive language ideologies, and what those repertoires reveal about the broader sociolinguistic system in which media, ideology, and minority practice are jointly entangled.

With this framework in place, the report turns next to the empirical specification of its primary linguistic objects. Chapter 3 will document the structure and ideology of Japanese gendered language—the pronouns and sentence-final particles that anchor the indexical fields theorized here, the historical construction of *joseigo* and *danseigo*, and the documented gaps among prescription, production, and perception. With that empirical foundation laid, Chapter 4 will turn to shoujo manga as a mediatized register, characterizing the specific configurations of pronoun and SFP usage that the genre stages and circulates. The framework articulated in this chapter provides the analytical lens through which both descriptions will be read, and against which the empirical patterns of Chapters 7–9 will ultimately be interpreted.

# 3 Gendered Language in Japanese: Pronouns and Sentence-Final Particles

The framework articulated in Chapter 2 specified, in principle, how indexical fields are populated, how exemplars are stored, and how mediatized registers can supply stylistic resources to speakers whose identities are inadequately served by prescriptive grammar. To put that framework to empirical work, however, requires a careful specification of the linguistic objects whose distributions, indexical loadings, and ideological histories will subsequently be measured: the particular pronouns and sentence-final particles (SFPs) of Japanese, the prescriptive ideology that organizes them into the binary registers of *joseigo* and *danseigo*, and the well-documented gaps among that ideology, naturalistic production, and listener perception. This chapter assembles that specification. It maps the architecture of Japanese gendered grammar (§3.1), characterizes the first-person pronouns (§3.2) and sentence-final particles (§3.3) that anchor the study's principal indexical fields, traces the historical construction of *joseigo* and *danseigo* as ideological artifacts rather than naturalistic descriptions (§3.4), examines the contemporary metapragmatic apparatus through which gendered norms are reproduced (§3.5), synthesizes the empirical literature on the prescription–production–perception gap (§3.6), and closes by articulating the analytical baseline against which Chapter 4's account of shoujo manga and Chapters 7–9's empirical analyses will operate (§3.7).

## 3.1 The Architecture of Japanese Gendered Grammar

Among the world's extensively studied languages, Japanese is "particularly well-known for conspicuously differentiated gendered language/speech," with a complex of grammatical and lexical resources that have attracted sustained scholarly attention since the late 1970s.[^12] Importantly, the differentiation operates not as a single grammatical contrast but as a distributed system of subsystems, each of which encodes gender probabilistically and in interaction with formality, age, regional dialect, and stance. A foundational insight of recent scholarship, however, is that these alleged characteristics "are not necessarily grounded in empirical observations of the way Japanese men and women actually speak"; rather, they "may be firmly rooted in language ideology," understood in Silverstein's classic formulation as "any sets of beliefs about language, articulated by the users as a rationalization or justification of perceived language structure and use".[^12] The architectural overview that follows must therefore be read at two levels simultaneously: as a description of the system's structural inventory and as a description of the ideological construct through which that inventory is interpreted.

The principal subsystems through which Japanese gender is grammatically and lexically encoded include: (i) first-person pronouns, where speakers must choose among a small but indexically loaded paradigm in nearly every act of self-reference; (ii) sentence-final particles, which attach to clause-final position to mark stance, modality, and interactional alignment; (iii) the elaborated honorific system of referent honorifics (*sonkeigo*), humble forms (*kenjōgo*), and addressee-oriented polite forms (*teineigo*), whose differential deployment is ideologically linked to feminine politeness;[^13] (iv) lexical choices, including the historically gendered distinction between *wago* (Japanese-origin words, ideologically coded as soft and feminine) and *kango* (Sino-Japanese vocabulary, historically coded as masculine and learned);[^14] and (v) prosodic features such as voice pitch, with high pitch normatively associated with politeness and femininity but, in actual production, deployed flexibly by speakers of both sexes for pragmatic purposes.[^13]

This study restricts its analytical focus to the first two subsystems—first-person pronouns and sentence-final particles—for both empirical and theoretical reasons. Empirically, these two subsystems are the most heavily documented in the Japanese language and gender literature, with corpus-based, ethnographic, and experimental evidence accumulated across several decades.[^13][^15] Theoretically, they are obligatory or near-obligatory loci of gender indexing in conversational Japanese: any utterance involving an explicit speaker reference forces a pronoun choice, and most affectively or modally marked utterances attract one or more SFPs. This obligatoriness, together with the small size of each paradigm, makes them especially tractable as variables for the kind of indexical-field analysis that Chapter 2 outlined. The system's regulatory power becomes vivid when paired with the elaborate honorific system: as Edward Seidensticker observed, the combination of gendered and honorific resources in Japanese makes possible "depictions of multi-party conversations without overt identification of each speaker"—a property that translators of Japanese novels into English routinely struggle to reproduce, and one that confirms how densely Japanese inscribes social position into nearly every clause.[^12]

The distinction between **deterministic** and **probabilistic** gender associations introduced in Chapter 1 acquires sharper content in this architectural light. Strictly deterministic forms—those whose use by a speaker of the "wrong" gender would constitute a categorical violation of the prescriptive grammar—are vanishingly rare in Japanese; even the most strongly gendered forms (e.g., *kashira* for women, *ze* for men) are routinely produced across the binary in particular contexts, dialects, and registers.[^15][^14] What the prescriptive grammar offers instead is a probabilistic mapping: forms cluster into ideologically masculine and feminine sets with statistically uneven distributions across speaker types and contexts. As Sanae Tsuda's analysis underscores, a normative reading of this clustering posits "two binary normative pronouns, *watashi* and *boku*"—the former feminine and polite, the latter masculine and standard for boys and men—but actual usage is considerably richer than this binary suggests.[^16]

It is also useful to import, from Labovian variationism through Silverstein's reframing, the salience hierarchy of indicator–marker–stereotype as it applies to Japanese gendered forms. Some forms function as **indicators**, recurring across populations without attracting sustained metapragmatic attention; many regional dialect features, including the rural Tōhoku women's use of *ore* documented by Sunaoshi, function in this way locally even where they would attract metapragmatic comment elsewhere.[^13] Other forms function as **markers**: variables that have entered stylistic awareness and can be deployed to position speakers, but without becoming objects of overt commentary. The contemporary younger generation's avoidance of strongly feminine SFPs in favor of neutral or moderately masculine forms appears to operate substantially at the marker level.[^15] Still others function as **stereotypes**: variables that have become subject to overt metapragmatic discussion, frequently codified in dictionaries, etiquette manuals, and educational discourse. The hyper-feminine *kashira*, *teyo*, *wayo*, and *koto-yo*—the very forms Inoue and Kinsui identify as the iconic core of *ojō-sama kotoba* ("lady speech")[^14]—function paradigmatically as stereotypes, and it is precisely their stereotype status that makes them so salient in mediatized registers, *yakuwarigo*, and metalinguistic commentary, while ensuring their relative rarity in everyday talk.

## 3.2 First-Person Pronouns: Forms, Indexical Fields, and Distributional Norms

Japanese first-person pronouns constitute a small inventory whose elements are differentiated along axes of gender, formality, age, regional origin, and stance. Although the prescriptive system, as Tsuda summarizes it, isolates "two binary normative pronouns, *watashi* and *boku*," with *ore* understood as signifying "stronger masculinity than *boku*,"[^16] the actual paradigm is considerably more diversified, and the indexical field of each form extends well beyond gender. The forms of central concern to this study are summarized in the table below, with their prescriptive associations and the broader indexical loadings documented in prior empirical research.

| Pronoun | Prescriptive gender association | Formality | Additional indexical associations |
|---|---|---|---|
| *watakushi* | Neutral (formal); feminine in casual register | High formality | Refinement, deference, *ojō-sama* register[^14] |
| *watashi* | Feminine in casual; neutral/standard in formal | Mid–high | Politeness, normative femininity; standard for men in formal contexts[^16][^13] |
| *atashi* | Strongly feminine | Casual | Intimacy, casualness; *onee* register among gay men[^13] |
| *atai* | Strongly feminine, lower-register | Casual, vulgar | Geisha, working-class, "low-class" associations historically;[^14] strong feminine intimacy |
| *boku* | Masculine (boys, men) | Casual–polite | Modesty, sensitivity, intellectual or "soft" masculinity; non-normative use by girls/women[^16][^13] |
| *ore* | Strongly masculine | Casual | Assertiveness, intimacy, camaraderie, "rough" masculinity[^16][^13] |
| *uchi* | Feminine, regional (Kansai) | Casual | Younger informal female speech; perceived as less feminine than *atashi*[^13] |
| *jibun* | Neutral (reflexive use) | Variable | Self-effacement, military/sports register, ambiguity-as-strategy |

The empirical literature documents extensive **non-normative use** of these forms—use that diverges from the prescriptive sex-based mapping and that requires explanation in the indexical-field terms set out in Chapter 2. Three lines of evidence are especially salient. First, Ayumi Miyazaki's ethnographic study of Tokyo junior high school students reveals "a complex and dynamic process of meaning making that is far beyond the imagination of the fixed, dichotomous picture" of normative usage.[^13] Studious, pro-school girls in Miyazaki's study use the feminine *atashi*, but more nonconformist girls use the masculine *boku* and *ore*; many girls prefer *uchi* "because it is more informal and less feminine than *atashi*," and the boys themselves stratify along power lines, with "powerful boys always use *ore*, while powerless boys use *boku* when talking with powerful boys but *ore* when talking with other powerless boys or girls".[^13] These findings are decisive for the present study because they demonstrate that pronoun choice indexes "not only femininity and masculinity but also levels of formality, power, and solidarity," and that the same form can carry sharply different indexical activations across speakers and contexts.[^13]

Second, Hideko Abe's ethnographic work in Shinjuku lesbian bars documents that the speakers there "use 'masculine' expressions extensively, but not exclusively," and shift among forms—including the "neutral" *anta* and "masculine" *omee*, as well as masculine first-person pronouns—"depending on the context (e.g., interlocutors, speech act types)".[^13] The interpretive consequence is that lesbian speakers' use of *ore* "does not straightforwardly index 'masculinity' (or 'femininity') but rather conveys a variety of context-dependent meanings, including solidarity/distance, emotional engagement, and resistance to the dominant gender and sexual norms".[^13]

Third, and significantly for the present study, the gendered binary is itself problematized by speakers whose sexualities or gender positions render the prescriptive options inadequate. Wim Lunsing and Claire Maree document how Japanese gay men and lesbians experience the limited pronoun choices as constraining, since "female users of feminine self-reference forms (e.g., *watashi*) are assumed to be womanly (hence, heterosexual) and male users of masculine self-reference forms (e.g., *boku*) are assumed to be manly (and therefore also heterosexual)".[^13] Their conclusion that "self-reference choice is not the result of a speaker's gender identification but a reflexive negotiative strategy" for managing "a sense of multiple selfhood" provides direct empirical warrant for treating queer speakers' pronoun use as indexical bricolage rather than as deviant deployment of a binary system.[^13]

The historical record adds a further layer. Eriko Sato's analysis emphasizes that the contemporary configuration of pronouns reflects a system in which "gender-sensitive linguistic boundaries are manipulated moment by moment by language users, affecting their master, interactional, personal, and/or relational identities," and that what is now coded as women's language was constructed by "deploying some of the linguistic features of multiple language varieties that have developed in different contexts while being shaped by male-dominant ideology during Japan's modernization process".[^15] Even more pointedly, Meiji schoolgirls were criticized in their time for using *atai* (associated with geishas) and, by some accounts, also *boku*, as part of the broader scandal of "schoolgirl speech".[^14] The very forms whose contemporary distributions are now read as indexing fixed masculinity or femininity have, within living historical memory, crossed gender boundaries in directions opposite to the ones now considered marked.

The indexical field of *boku* is particularly worth specifying in detail, since it figures centrally in shoujo manga conventions (Chapter 4) and in queer young adults' practice (Chapters 7–9). The field anchored by *boku* includes, at minimum: probabilistic masculinity; boyhood and youth; modesty (relative to the more assertive *ore*); intellectual or sensitive masculinity; in particular subcultural and shoujo-manga contexts, an androgynous self-presentation available to female and nonbinary speakers; and, as Giancarla Unser-Schutz's manga corpus work suggests, association with the *bishōnen* and sensitive-hero character types that shoujo and crossover manga deploy. Endō's frequently cited observation that manga play "an influential factor in young women choosing to use the masculine first personal pronouns *ore* and *boku*"[^17] points to exactly the kind of cross-genre uptake that the framework of Chapter 2 anticipates—though, as Unser-Schutz cautions, "previous research has been contradictory" and any claim of media-driven pronoun change requires careful empirical calibration.[^17]

## 3.3 Sentence-Final Particles: Forms, Functions, and Gendered Distributions

Sentence-final particles attach to clause-final position to mark assertion, confirmation, emphasis, softening, evidentiality, and stance, and they constitute what is arguably the most ideologically saturated subsystem of Japanese gendered grammar. As Anja Vranic's review of the literature summarizes, "in present day Japanese the final particles *wa* and *no* are considered feminine, even more so if they are followed by the final particles *yo* or *ne*"; *kashira* is "also a feminine particle, usually translated to mean 'I wonder'".[^15] On the masculine side, *ze* and *zo* anchor assertive and emphatic functions, with *na* and *sa* clustering at lower intensities of masculine marking. The table below summarizes the principal forms relevant to the present study, organized by their prescriptive gender association and pragmatic function.

| Particle | Prescriptive association | Principal pragmatic functions | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| *wa* (rising) | Strongly feminine | Soft assertion, emphasis, confirmation | Iconic of *ojō-sama* register; some masculine regional uses[^14] |
| *wayo* | Strongly feminine | Emphatic assertion with addressee orientation | Stereotype-level marking; iconic feminine role-language form[^14] |
| *no* | Feminine (in declaratives) | Explanation, mild assertion | Among the most resilient feminine forms in contemporary use[^14] |
| *noyo* | Feminine | Emphatic explanation/assertion | Listed among iconic feminine forms in role language[^14] |
