| *kashira* | Strongly feminine | Wondering, uncertainty ("I wonder") | Stereotype-level marking; rare in naturalistic younger speech[^15] |
| *teyo* | Feminine (Meiji origin) | Notification, mild assertion | Caricatured component of "lady speech" / *teyo dawa kotoba*[^14] |
| *koto* (clause-final) | Feminine (older register) | Exclamation, mild assertion | Largely confined to *ojō-sama kotoba* in contemporary perception[^14] |
| *ze* | Strongly masculine | Strong assertion, emphasis | Iconic of "rough" masculine register |
| *zo* | Masculine | Self-directed or emphatic assertion | Co-distributes with *ze* |
| *na* | Masculine (in particular uses) | Confirmation, mild emphasis, prohibition | Lower intensity than *ze*/*zo* |
| *sa* | Masculine-leaning, contemporary | Casual emphasis, narrative marker | Heavily used in contemporary youth speech across genders |
| *yo*, *ne* | Gender-neutral | Information assertion (*yo*); confirmation/agreement (*ne*) | Frequently combine with gendered particles[^15] |

Anja Vranic's overview of this paradigm articulates the prescriptive assumption that "a girl was a schoolgirl was revealed by how she ended her sentences," with Meiji-period intellectuals focusing on "the sentence-final forms, such as *teyo*, *dawa*, *koto-yo*, *wa*, *chatta*, *noyo*".[^14] Yet the intervening century has decisively undermined the prescriptive picture as a description of contemporary practice. Drawing on Okamoto's work, Vranic notes that contemporary younger women's speech is characterized by "68.8% neutral forms" rather than by the stereotypically feminine SFPs the prescriptive system anticipates, and that university-aged women's actual speech is "not feminine as is prescribed by language ideology, but neutral to moderately masculine".[^15] This finding aligns with a now-substantial corpus literature, including McGloin's, Hiramoto's, and Philips's studies of feminine SFP usage, all of which document significant retreat from the prescriptive feminine inventory.[^15]

The contemporary distributional picture is further complicated by the rise of *kogal* speech and other youth registers that selectively incorporate masculine and neutral SFPs into nominally female speech. As Elin Nilsson's comparative study summarizes, "Kogals, more than other girls, are known for using masculine words (like *boku* instead of *watashi*, when saying 'I') and avoiding honorifics. This is seen as an attempt to redefine what it means to be a woman in Japan, and should not be seen as the kogals trying to be masculine".[^14] The convergence of survey and corpus evidence suggests that, by the early twenty-first century, Japanese society has begun to lament "the 'loss' of its feminine speech"—a perception that takes the prescriptive ideology as the historical baseline and reads any deviation as decline,[^14] when the more accurate description is that the prescriptive feminine register was always more an ideological construct than a description of speech (see §3.4).

A subtler but theoretically critical point concerns the **multi-meaning indexicality** of these particles. As the synthesis in *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology* makes plain, "speakers observed in these studies all use stereotypically gendered expressions to convey not only femininity or masculinity but also a variety of other pragmatic meanings (e.g., solidarity, relative power), suggesting that indexicality is multiple and indirect and that the interpretation of gendered expressions as feminine or masculine is not given but normative and ideological".[^13] Cindi Sturtz Sreetharan's analysis of men's conversations in Kansai and Kantō exemplifies the point: she finds that "all speakers used 'masculine' forms relatively infrequently" and that, when used, masculine SFPs index "a variety of pragmatic meanings; for example, 'masculine,' or forceful, expressions are used to signal camaraderie, authority, anger, and so on".[^13] On the feminine side, Yoshiko Matsumoto's study of middle-aged housewives shows extensive inter- and intraspeaker variation, with forceful and delicate expressions conveying "various pragmatic and social meanings, such as friendship, emotion, and deference," and women using "these linguistic resources variably to construct complex and flexible personae and relationships".[^13]

Yumiko Ohara's work on prosody adds a further dimension: although high pitch is normatively associated with politeness and femininity, "the women in her study did not always use it and that men also used it at times," suggesting that even prosodic gender markers function as flexible resources rather than as direct gender correlates.[^13] Importantly, however, only the women in Ohara's study "varied their pitch levels substantially across interlocutors," indicating that "depending on the context, women but not men may use a high pitch level according to the cultural constraint that links this feature to politeness and thence to femininity".[^13] This asymmetry—women, but not men, treating a feminine-coded resource as a context-sensitive obligation—is a recurring pattern in Japanese sociolinguistic research and one that any account of the prescription–production–perception gap must grapple with.

## 3.4 The Historical Construction of Joseigo and Danseigo

The forms surveyed in §3.2 and §3.3 are not historical givens. Both *joseigo* and *danseigo*, the contemporary registers that organize them, are **ideological constructs** whose contemporary appearance of naturalness conceals a history of deliberate construction, contestation, and prescription. As Vranic's thesis frames the issue, "the development of women's language has not occurred naturally through actual speech, but rather, it has evolved as a product of prescribed language use through instructions in disciplinary books as well as media, and has been used as a tool to distinguish women from men".[^15] A robust scholarly literature, including the foundational work of Inoue, Nakamura, and Washi, has reconstructed the principal stages of this construction in considerable detail.

The most consequential moment in the modern history of *joseigo* is the Meiji period (1868–1912), and specifically the emergence of **teyo dawa kotoba** ("teyo dawa speech") among the new class of secondary-school-educated women. Nilsson's careful summary, drawing on Inoue's primary research, lays out the sequence: "When girls in Japan acquired the right to higher education in the wake of the Meiji reformation in the 19th century, these girls began to talk in a way that was considered vulgar by the public. It was called *teyo dawa kotoba* (teyo dawa speech) – and over time it became what we call female speech today".[^14] Crucially, "during the Meiji and Taisho periods proper ladies did not talk that way, and it was considered to be vulgar slang. Today that is the way proper ladies are expected to talk. *Teyo dawa kotoba* was not considered to be female speech until the 1930s".[^14]

The features of *teyo dawa kotoba* that drew Meiji intellectuals' criticism are, with little modification, the iconic "feminine" features of contemporary *yakuwarigo*. Vranic notes that "Meiji intellectuals focused on the sentence-final forms, such as *teyo*, *dawa*, *koto-yo*, *wa*, *chatta*, *noyo*," and Inoue classifies "both *teyo* and *dawa* as 'non-referential and context-dependent, with neither any established semantic nor etymological origin'".[^15][^14] The forms were criticized on multiple grounds: as ungrammatical (Meiji intellectuals interpreted *teyo* as a past-tense form that schoolgirls were misusing for present and future, when in fact the suffix *-te* could span both temporalities);[^14] as morally suspect (because they were said to have originated among "low-class" Tokyo neighborhoods, geishas, and pleasure quarters);[^14] as evidence of haste and inattention (because the contractions and rising intonations were heard as "lazy and sloppy");[^14] and as "gender-transgressing" when combined with *kango* and English vocabulary that had previously been male preserves.[^14] The schoolgirls were also criticized for inadequate honorific usage—using *soo desu* rather than *soo de gozaimasu*, *itteyo* rather than *yukimashitayo*—a charge that, as Vranic notes, "linked moral corruption to vulgar speech" and used "the lack of proper honorifics" as "proof of this proposed lack of morals".[^14]

The mechanisms by which *teyo dawa kotoba* was transformed from "vulgar slang" into the iconic *joseigo* of the contemporary imagination are well documented. Domestic novels (*katei shōsetsu*) played a critical role: as Nakamura argues, the authors of these novels "may have chosen to use *teyo dawa* speech because it was useful in emphasizing the young woman's identity," and "by not using male language they differentiated between male and female language, and by not using polite language they excluded their characters from normative female students".[^14] Schoolgirl magazines (*jogaku zasshi*) further amplified circulation,[^14] and by the end of World War I "various shop-keepers and/or salesmen started to address girls and young women with *teyo dawa* speech," advertisements deployed it for products targeted at young women, and "columnists in women's magazines started to use *teyo dawa* speech in their writings". By the 1930s the register had completed its transformation "from 'vulgar' to the 'feminine language'".[^14] Inoue's classic formulation captures the irony: by the postwar period, Japan was beginning "to lament the 'loss' of its feminine speech," with the historical specificity of the construction obscured by the very success of the ideological project.[^14] Kinsui's observation that, while *no* and *wa* "are commonly heard nowadays, *teyo* and *koto* have become more of a concept and a caricature of what he calls *ojō-sama kotoba* ('lady speech')" is therefore not a marginal aside but a precise description of how a once-naturalistic register has been progressively sedimented into stereotype-level *yakuwarigo*.[^14]

Inoue's scholarship situates this transformation within the broader project of Japanese linguistic modernity. As outlined in the editors' introduction to *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology*, Inoue's account locates the construction of *joseigo* in the dialogues and reported speech of the new modern Japanese novel, "where women's voices were 'objectified, reified, and re-presented'".[^13] The process was selective: writers "selected, systematized, and disseminated particular 'feminine' forms, forms neither inherently feminine nor inherently refined, which, through the popular consumption of *katei shōsetsu* 'domestic novels' by a female readership, served to ascribe these forms to that new icon of appropriate or socially desirable femininity, the middle-to-upper-class Tokyo woman".[^13] This is, in Inoue's phrase, "the origin of today's 'Japanese women's language'"—an origin that highlights, rather than obscures, "the importance of historicizing women's speech, of incorporating an understanding of the sociopolitical nature of the indexical process".[^13]

Rumi Washi's investigation of language policy from 1920 to 1945 documents the consolidation phase. Drawing on Washi's analysis, the introduction to *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology* notes that the National Language Association, founded in 1930 to "rationalize, improve, and promote the national language," established a women's wing in 1939 with the explicit purpose of obtaining "the cooperation of women in preserving and honoring the national language".[^13] The "ideal form of female speech" promoted by the association was retrofitted to "much earlier historical forms of speech associated with women, specifically *nyooboo kotoba* 'court women's language,'" providing a manufactured genealogy that linked the new *teyo dawa*-derived *joseigo* to a putative ancient tradition.[^13] The wartime period therefore both intensified the prescriptive discourse around female speech and provided the ideological scaffolding—"sexual complementarity," "separate equality"—through which female educators were enlisted in disseminating it. As Washi shows, "it was only later that Japanese women were to 'uncover the trick' whereby they were led to espouse speaking patterns that—far from affording them 'separate but equal' status—relegated them to subordinate positions in the larger social order".[^13]

A parallel and equally significant ideological development concerns *danseigo* and its relation to the "national language." As the Brill abstract on masculinity and national language frames it, "the notion of a unified Japanese national language was an androcentric language ideology created during the period of Japanese nation-state" formation.[^18] The implication is that *danseigo* operates not as a marked counterpart to a marked *joseigo* but as the unmarked, default register against which *joseigo* is measured—the linguistic embodiment of what feminist scholarship has long identified as the male-as-norm structure of androcentric ideology. Nakamura's work, summarized in *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology*, treats this asymmetry as central: "the central but unacknowledged importance of men and masculinity in the study of language and gender" is one of the field's most consequential blind spots, and the construction of *joseigo* must be understood as paired with the silent construction of *danseigo* as the default.[^13]

The decisive analytical implication is that **both registers are historically constructed ideological artifacts**. As the editors of *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology* summarize their volume's stance, "'women's language' and 'men's language' characterized as such are cultural constructs informed by the hegemonic ideology of language and gender—constructs that have been widely disseminated as linguistic norms in Japanese society not only through popular media but also by language policy makers and linguists".[^13] Treating either register as a description of how Japanese women or men actually speak is therefore not merely empirically inaccurate but theoretically misleading, since it obscures the indexical work that the registers perform and the agency of speakers who navigate, contest, or repurpose them.

## 3.5 Metapragmatic Ideology and Prescriptive Discourse

The historical construction of *joseigo* and *danseigo* did not end with their wartime consolidation; the registers continue to be actively reproduced through a contemporary metapragmatic apparatus that includes school instruction, etiquette manuals, dictionaries, media commentary, language pedagogy, and the implicit pragmatics of mass-circulation texts. This apparatus is the ideological infrastructure that gives the prescriptive system its ongoing force, and it is the structural counterpart to the indexical fields that Chapter 2 theorized: the apparatus does not so much describe how Japanese is spoken as enregister the meanings that particular forms are heard to carry.

Shigeko Okamoto's analysis, summarized in the editors' introduction to *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology*, identifies "the remarkable consistency of messages to women that, in order to be *onnarashii* 'feminine', they should speak in ways that are gentle, polite, and refined".[^13] These messages are conveyed through education ("language policy makers and linguists"), media, and three "ideologically nonneutral assumptions" embedded in much linguistic theorizing itself: (1) "that most or all women share the same attributes and therefore (should) use language in the same way"; (2) "that certain linguistic expressions, such as honorifics, are inherently polite"; and (3) "that speaking politely, that is, showing deference or refinement through the use of honorifics and other expressions, indexes femininity or the female gender".[^13] Okamoto's critique demonstrates that all three assumptions are ideological rather than descriptive—simultaneously constituting and rationalizing the prescriptive system rather than reflecting an antecedent linguistic reality.

Orie Endō's work on dictionaries provides perhaps the most concrete documentation of the metapragmatic apparatus's institutional embedding. As summarized in the editors' introduction, Endō's "fine-grained analysis of the treatment of three representative derogatory expressions related to women, *onnadatera (ni)* 'despite being a woman', *rooba* 'old woman/old crone', and *oorudo misu* 'spinster' (lit., 'old miss')" across a 66-volume dictionary corpus reveals "the substantial lag of dictionaries compared with popular or even other 'official' usage".[^13] Dictionaries—"the ultimate 'authoritative source' for words and their uses"—have, in Endō's account, "not played a more active role in re-presenting women and their roles in Japanese life," and have instead helped to perpetuate gendered ideologies that even mass-circulation media have begun to revise.[^13]

The metapragmatic apparatus extends well beyond dictionaries. Laura Miller's analysis of *burikko*—a derogatory label used to describe women whose speaking and other behavioral patterns are seen as exaggerated and false exhibitions of naïveté, innocence, and cuteness—demonstrates that women are subject to "close scrutiny of their behavior" through evaluative terms that police the boundaries of acceptable feminine performance.[^13] At the same time that "a Japanese woman is being taught that being feminine is a desirable social trait, she also is learning that there are relatively strict limits on the performance of femininity".[^13] The "behavioral paradox" Miller identifies—that "many of the prescriptions for proper female behavior—that is, the linguistic reflexes of modesty and innocence—overlap with traits commonly associated with *burikko*"—captures with particular sharpness how the metapragmatic apparatus works as a double bind: the same linguistic features that are enregistered as "feminine" can be reread as "phony" depending on the speaker, the context, and the evaluator's stance.[^13]

Magazine discourse contributes another layer. Momoko Nakamura's analysis of women's and men's lifestyle magazines shows how publications "construct a specific identity for a specific set of targeted readers" using "speech styles that are, again, imagined to appeal to targeted readers".[^13] Several stereotypically female features—"exclamations, inclusive stance marked by hortatives, interactively oriented sentence-final particles"—occur in young women's magazines, while stereotypically masculine patterns—"absence of affect indicators, imperatives, use of assertive *da* forms"—occur in young men's magazines.[^13] Nakamura also identifies contrastive metaphors—"FASHION AS TASK versus FASHION AS BATTLE"—that align differentially with gender ideologies, illustrating that magazines do not merely reflect gendered language norms but actively reproduce them through their entire textual organization.[^13] As the editors note, "covert prescriptions—or 'implicit pragmatics'… take the legitimacy of their models for granted" and "may be equally or even more effective in serving to regiment women and men into normative femininities or masculinities".[^13]

For the present study, the most consequential feature of this apparatus is its **over-determination of available options for speakers whose identities do not align with the binary**. Lunsing and Maree's analysis, already cited in §3.2, provides the decisive observation: "self-referencing practices require that women and men use different sets of forms," and "these gender norms are equally strongly associated with sexual orientation norms"; "female users of feminine self-reference forms (e.g., *watashi*) are assumed to be womanly (hence, heterosexual)" and "male users of masculine self-reference forms (e.g., *boku*) are assumed to be manly (and therefore also heterosexual)".[^13] The "lingering distaste" expressed by Lunsing's and Maree's queer consultants for the available pronoun choices makes visible "the heteronormative underpinnings of Japanese language and gender ideology"—and demonstrates that "prescription is never fully reflected in actual language use" precisely because the prescription itself is structurally inadequate to the lived realities of non-normative speakers.[^13]

This over-determination provides the structural justification for the present study's focus on queer speakers. If the prescriptive apparatus offered neutral options, queer speakers might simply select them and the question of media-derived stylistic resources would be less pressing. Because the apparatus instead saturates every available pronoun and SFP with binary indexical content—and, moreover, with binary indexical content tied not only to gender but to presumed heterosexuality—queer speakers face a system in which the act of self-reference is structurally already an act of stylistic positioning. Under such conditions, the framework of Chapter 2 predicts, mediatized registers that stage non-default configurations should be especially valuable as resources, and metapragmatic awareness of the prescriptive system should be unusually acute among the speakers most disadvantaged by it.

## 3.6 The Gap Between Prescription, Production, and Perception

The triangle of **prescription**, **production**, and **perception** structures one of the most robust empirical findings in Japanese language and gender research: that all three are in significant disjuncture with one another, and that the interpretation of any given utterance depends on which corner of the triangle the analyst privileges. The chapters in *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology* converge on this finding, and the convergence carries direct analytical implications for the present study.

On the **production** side, the empirical record now contains decades of corpus and ethnographic evidence demonstrating extensive deviation from the prescriptive model across speaker types, regions, and ages. Vranic's study, drawing on Okamoto's earlier work, finds that university-aged women's speech is "neutral to moderately masculine, which aligns with previous research".[^15] Sturtz Sreetharan's analyses of Kansai and Kantō men's conversations exhibit "not only individual and regional differences but also considerable 'deviation' from the normative usage, which undermines the idea that there is a single 'Japanese men's language'".[^13] Matsumoto's middle-aged housewives—precisely the demographic that prescriptive ideology imagines as the canonical *joseigo* speaker—exhibit "wide inter- and intraspeaker variation," with forceful and delicate expressions conveying multiple pragmatic and social meanings.[^13] Sunaoshi's farm women in Ibaraki use a regional dialect "that lacks the morphological, lexical, and phonological features of the normative construct 'Japanese women's language,'" raising the pointed question of "the extent to which the dominant gender and linguistic norms are relevant to real women, especially women who are in some sense linguistic 'others'".[^13] Miyazaki's junior high school girls and Abe's lesbian bar patrons have already been cited in §3.2.

On the **perception** side, the evidence is equally striking but operates in the opposite direction. Even as production retreats from prescriptive norms, listeners continue to evaluate speech through prescriptive lenses. Inoue's work on what she terms "**indexical inversion**" captures the central mechanism: by virtue of the historical construction of *joseigo* documented in §3.4, contemporary listeners hear feminine SFPs and pronouns as evidence of natural, even ancient, feminine speech, rather than as contingent products of Meiji-era stylization.[^13][^19] The naturalization of the construct means that listeners' evaluative grids remain calibrated to the prescriptive system even when speakers' actual production has shifted. This asymmetry produces, in turn, the perception that contemporary younger women are "losing" the feminine speech that they "should" possess[^14]—a perception that takes the prescriptive baseline as the historical norm and reads any divergence as decline.

The **prescription–production gap** is therefore matched by a **perception–production gap** of comparable magnitude. As Vranic's questionnaire study illustrates, even college-aged speakers and their elders frequently misidentify the origins of contemporary slang and gendered forms. When asked about *teyo dawa kotoba* and feminine speech more broadly, "only three young people had an inkling of what *teyo dawa kotoba* was, and none could give a specific and correct description"; many associated it with *ojō-sama kotoba* without recognizing the historical genealogy.[^14] When the same speakers were asked about contemporary *kogal* slang, they likewise often did not recognize that words they themselves used were of *kogal* origin.[^14] These findings indicate that the metapragmatic system through which speakers reason about gendered language is itself substantially detached from both the historical reality of the system's construction and the contemporary reality of its use.

The **third-wave reframing** of these gaps, central to the editors' synthesis in *Japanese Language, Gender, and Ideology*, treats them not as anomalies but as structural features of how indexical systems operate. As the editors put it, "indexicality, or the relationship between linguistic expressions and social meanings, is understood, not as direct and fixed, but rather as multiple, flexible, context-dependent, and mediated by speakers' beliefs and attitudes concerning language use, which are variously influenced by hegemonic ideologies".[^13] On this view, "a particular stereotypically gendered form in Japanese (e.g., a sentence-final particle) may not necessarily be used to index the speaker's gender, although it may be interpreted as doing so by hearers".[^13] The disjunction between speaker intention and listener interpretation is precisely what the indexical-field framework predicts: different participants in a speech community construe the same variable differently, and ideologically dominant construals can persist as evaluative resources even when production has moved on.

For the present study, the analytical implications are clear and consequential. **First**, any account of queer young adults' linguistic production must be calibrated against the documented production patterns of their peers, not against the prescriptive system alone; deviations must be measured from a baseline that already incorporates younger speakers' general retreat from prescriptive feminine forms. **Second**, perception data—including listeners' evaluations of recorded queer speech, perception experiments, and metapragmatic interview commentary—must be treated as a partially independent stream of evidence, since listeners' judgments may activate prescriptive indexical fields even when speakers do not intend them. **Third**, the contemporary metapragmatic discourse about "lost" feminine speech provides an ideological backdrop against which queer speakers' choices acquire additional layers of meaning: a queer speaker who uses *boku* is making an indexical move not only against prescriptive *joseigo* but also within a broader landscape in which feminine *joseigo* is already in retreat among non-queer peers. **Fourth**, the gap raises a methodological caution: speakers' self-reports about their own usage are likely to reflect prescriptive ideologies more than actual production, and any study relying on self-report alone will systematically under-estimate the extent of deviation from prescription.[^13]

## 3.7 Implications for the Study of Queer Speakers and Mediatized Registers

The chapter's findings can now be consolidated into a set of analytical commitments that will structure the remainder of the report. The principal claim is that the Japanese gendered grammar examined in this study is best understood as a system in which a small set of high-frequency, indexically saturated forms anchors a set of ideologically organized indexical fields, and that the prescriptive system regulating these fields is in significant disjuncture with both contemporary production and contemporary perception. Several specific commitments follow.

**On the densest indexical loadings**, the empirical record points to first-person pronouns and a curated subset of sentence-final particles as the loci where gendered indexicality is most concentrated. Within the pronoun paradigm, *boku*, *ore*, *atashi*, and *uchi* carry the densest loadings for the present study because they participate most actively in the kinds of cross-prescriptive bricolage that queer speakers and shoujo manga conventions are likely to deploy. *Watashi* and *jibun* function as relatively unmarked forms whose deployment is significant primarily by contrast with the more strongly indexed alternatives. Within the SFP paradigm, the stereotype-level forms *wa*, *wayo*, *kashira*, *teyo*, and *koto* on the feminine side, and *ze* and *zo* on the masculine side, are most likely to function as salient stylistic resources, while *no*, *na*, *sa*, and the gender-neutral *yo* and *ne* function at the marker level and are more likely to be deployed below explicit metapragmatic awareness.

**On the most likely sites of stylistic bricolage**, the synthesis of §3.2 and §3.3 suggests that the highest analytic yield will come from configurations rather than single forms. The combination of pronoun and SFP within a single utterance—*boku* with *ze*, *atashi* with *no*, *uchi* with *yo*, *watashi* with *kashira*—makes a more specific indexical move than either form in isolation. Shoujo manga's *yakuwarigo*, as Chapter 4 will document in detail, operates substantially at the configuration level: hyper-feminine heroines do not merely use *atashi*, they use *atashi* with *wayo*, *no*, or *kashira* depending on the precise feminine sub-persona being performed. Queer speakers' bricolage is therefore likely to operate at the configuration level as well, recombining pronoun and SFP modules drawn from different prescribed registers.

**On where the prescriptive system creates the conditions for mediatized exemplars to become especially valuable**, the structural over-determination identified in §3.5 specifies the locus precisely. The prescriptive system's saturation of every available form with binary, heteronormative indexical content means that queer speakers cannot use the system's resources without already taking a position with respect to it. Under such conditions, the framework of Chapter 2 predicts that mediatized registers staging non-default configurations should function as primary sources of stylistic alternatives. Shoujo manga, with its diversified *yakuwarigo* spanning hyper-feminine, hyper-masculine, gender-crossing, and queer-coded character speech, is uniquely positioned to supply such alternatives—a positioning that Chapter 4 will develop empirically.

**On the empirical questions that the remainder of the report will pursue**, the structural and historical analysis of this chapter, combined with the theoretical apparatus of Chapter 2, generates a refined version of the research questions sketched in §1.3. The pronoun analysis (Chapter 7) will need to determine whether queer young adults' pronoun repertoires align with shoujo manga conventions, with the broader younger-generation pattern of moderately masculine production documented for non-queer peers, or with neither—and whether higher manga consumption is associated with more diverse repertoires within whatever pattern emerges. The SFP analysis (Chapter 7) will need to distinguish stereotype-level forms (whose deployment is likely to be conscious and identity-performative) from marker-level forms (whose deployment is likely to operate below explicit awareness). The contextual analysis (Chapter 8) will need to attend to the production–perception gap directly, examining not only what queer speakers produce but how they expect their production to be interpreted and how interlocutor identity modulates their choices. And the discussion (Chapter 9) will need to weigh exposure-based exemplar accumulation against alternative explanations—peer networks, other media genres, pre-existing identity-driven preferences—within a framework that takes the prescriptive system, the production reality, and the perceptual ideology as three partially independent constraints rather than as a single unified force.

The chapter has, in this sense, completed its structural task. The forms have been catalogued, the indexical fields have been characterized, the historical construction has been traced, the metapragmatic apparatus has been described, and the prescription–production–perception gap has been documented. With these foundations in place, the report turns next to the specific configurations of pronoun and SFP usage that shoujo manga deploys—the staged register that, on the framework articulated here, is best understood not as a description of how anyone actually speaks but as a curated catalogue of indexical possibilities that readers, including the queer young adults at the center of this study, may selectively incorporate into their own stylistic practice.

# 4 Shoujo Manga as a Linguistic Register

The structural and ideological account of Japanese gendered grammar developed in Chapter 3 established that the prescriptive binary of *joseigo* and *danseigo* is a historical construct, that it is in significant disjuncture with both contemporary production and contemporary perception, and that it over-determines the available options for speakers whose identities do not align with its heteronormative architecture. With those foundations in place, the present chapter turns to the second of the three empirical pillars on which the report stands: the specification of shoujo manga as a mediatized linguistic register whose stylized speech conventions diverge systematically from naturalistic majority usage. The analytical task here is not to celebrate or critique the genre but to characterize, with the empirical and theoretical precision the framework of Chapter 2 demands, what shoujo manga actually stages linguistically and why those stagings matter for the indexical fields that queer young adults are theorized to navigate.

The argument proceeds in six steps. The chapter first delimits shoujo manga as an analytical object, situating it within the gendered publication ecology of Japanese manga and clarifying its overlap with adjacent subgenres (§4.1). It then synthesizes the corpus-based evidence on shoujo manga's distinctive distributions of first-person pronouns and sentence-final particles, drawing principally on Unser-Schutz's comparative analyses of shoujo and shōnen corpora (§4.2). Three subsequent sections analyze the genre's characterological registers in turn: the hyper-feminine and hyper-masculine poles of its gendered *yakuwarigo* (§4.3), the *bishōnen*, *otokoyaku*, and gender-crossing configurations that elaborate the indexical fields beyond binary prescription (§4.4), and the queer-coded conventions of yuri and Boys' Love subgenres in their relation to broader shoujo register and to the contrasting *onee*/*okama* stereotyping that circulates elsewhere in Japanese media (§4.5). The chapter closes by synthesizing these analyses into a characterization of shoujo manga as a curated, modular catalogue of indexical possibilities especially amenable to selective bricolage by minority readers (§4.6), and by registering the limitations and alternative readings that the empirical chapters must subsequently weigh.

A note of methodological transparency belongs at the outset. The directly available corpus-based linguistic scholarship on shoujo manga is dominated by a small number of researchers, most centrally Giancarla Unser-Schutz, whose dissertation, conference papers, and journal articles constitute the principal empirical record on which the linguistic claims below rest. The cultural and reception literature on adjacent subgenres (Boys' Love, yuri) is more extensive, particularly in the tradition extending from Mizoguchi, Welker, Fujimoto, Ishida, and others, but much of this scholarship is oriented toward narrative, ideology, and reception rather than toward the specific distributions of pronouns and SFPs at issue here. The chapter therefore moves between empirical specifics, where the corpus literature supports them, and more cautiously framed theoretical and cultural claims, where the existing record is thinner. Where evidence is anecdotal, where the available scholarship is small, or where claims must be inferred from the framework of Chapter 2 rather than directly demonstrated, this chapter says so explicitly.

## 4.1 Defining Shoujo Manga: Genre Boundaries, Demographics, and Publication Ecology

Shoujo manga is conventionally defined less by its narrative content than by its **publication venue and target demographic**. Japanese manga has historically evolved through a system of magazine serialization in which each title is identified primarily with the demographic of the magazine in which it appears: shōnen (boys), shoujo (girls), seinen (young adult men), and josei (young adult women). As one synthesis of the genre system puts it, this division "stems from the magazine serialization of manga," and "while in the same magazine any thematic genre may be present, gendered genres are distinguishable by their visuals as well as character settings and character relationships"; the magazine format "gave rise to manga's conventional genres, which have been less defined by the thematic content (such as science fiction, mystery, fantasy etc.) than age and gender, namely shōnen (boys), shōjo (girls) […], seinen (youth), and josei (women)"[^17]. The same source highlights an important asymmetry that bears directly on this study's analytical interests: "while female genres frequently position character's feelings as the kernel of the story, male genres tend to be action-oriented," with character design itself implying personality traits and serving as a key vehicle of stylization[^17].

This demographic-and-magazine-based classification is operationally useful but necessarily approximate. Shoujo titles routinely traverse thematic territories that overlap with shōnen and seinen works, and conversely, action- or fantasy-driven shōnen titles sometimes deploy interiority and emotional intensification associated with shoujo. What remains relatively stable across the boundary is the **stylistic register**: the visual conventions of large eyes and decorative panel layouts, the narrative emphasis on emotion and relationship, and—most consequentially for the present study—the linguistic conventions that Unser-Schutz's corpus work has documented in detail and that §4.2 will analyze.

The relationship between shoujo manga and its adjacent subgenres requires careful specification, because the empirical literature suggests a continuum rather than a clean partition. Boys' Love (BL), which depicts romantic or sexual relationships between male characters, is described in the principal scholarly synthesis as "a niche category of shoujo/josei manga that focuses on M/M relationships"[^20]. The genre is "still frowned upon, both for being gay content and for being mainly romance aimed at women"[^20], and its origins lie in the 1970s–80s shoujo manga reformation associated with the so-called "Year 24 Group"; "both BL and yuri had their origins more than a century later" than the Meiji-era homophobic backlash, "in the 1970's-80's"[^20]. As the broader survey of the BL genre summarizes, BL "originated in Japan as a form of *manga* portraying romantic or sexual relationships between male characters which later evolved into a variety of formats: fiction, *manga, anime,* games, live action series and films," and "its creators, amateurs and professionals, and consumers, are mainly heterosexual women"[^21]. Yuri, the female-female romance counterpart, similarly emerged from within shoujo's institutional and stylistic framework, and James Welker's broader study of "queer shōjo manga" treats both subgenres as part of a continuous tradition of shoujo-adjacent queer narrative that has interconnected with feminist and lesbian movements in late twentieth-century Japan[^22].

For analytical purposes, this study treats **shoujo manga in its narrow sense (girls' magazine titles) as the primary object**, while including yuri and BL as continuous-but-differentiable subgenres whose linguistic conventions partially elaborate and partially diverge from the broader shoujo register. Josei manga, marketed to young adult women, is treated as a closely related register that extends shoujo conventions into adult-themed narrative territory. Shōnen and seinen manga function as **comparator categories** against which shoujo's distinctive linguistic patterns become visible, in keeping with Unser-Schutz's own comparative methodology.

The actual readership of shoujo manga, including its queer-adjacent subgenres, extends well beyond the target demographic. The transnational BL literature documents "a growing number of gay audiences" engaging with BL texts despite the genre's heteronormative production conditions[^21], and Baudinette's work on Japanese BL among Chinese gay men in Japan treats the genre as a "resource of hope" that allows non-default readers to "explore their sexuality and cope with the heteronormative structure of their society"[^21]. Welker's study of "feminists, lesbians, and girls' comics artists and fans" in late-twentieth-century Japan documents the long-standing entanglement of shoujo and queer female readership[^22]. The empirical implication for the present study is that shoujo manga should not be conceived as a register encountered only by its target demographic of young heterosexual girls; it is, rather, a widely circulated cultural text whose readers include the queer young adults at the center of this report, and whose stylized linguistic conventions are correspondingly available as exemplars to readers across a wide range of identity positions.

A final boundary specification concerns the **corpus** the empirical chapters assume. Following Unser-Schutz's methodology, the analytic baseline of "shoujo manga" is constituted by titles published in shoujo magazines and identified as shoujo by their publishers and audiences[^17]. Adjacent subgenres (yuri, BL) are treated where their linguistic conventions are continuous with this baseline, and adjacent registers (anime adaptations, light novels, drama CDs) are noted as part of the broader media ecology that exposes readers to shoujo-derived stylized speech, but the principal linguistic claims rest on the manga corpus itself.

## 4.2 The Linguistic Distinctiveness of Shoujo Manga: Corpus Evidence on Pronouns and Sentence-Final Particles

The most direct empirical foundation for treating shoujo manga as a distinctive linguistic register comes from corpus-based comparative work, principally that of Giancarla Unser-Schutz. Her research program, conducted over several years and culminating in her 2013 dissertation at Hitotsubashi University, has systematically built a manga corpus and analyzed character speech across the gendered genre system, with particular attention to first-person pronouns, sentence-final particles, and the broader phenomenon of *yakuwarigo*.

Unser-Schutz's own framing of the research problem is telling. She observes that "manga—Japanese comics—are often noted popularly as one location of linguistic change amongst young Japanese," with attention paid "particularly to the role that manga play in changing gendered speech," and that "manga are often noted as an influential factor in young women choosing to use the masculine first personal pronouns *ore* and *boku* (e.g., Endo 2001)"[^17]. Yet, as she immediately notes, "research hitherto has, however, focused on conscious surveys and interviews amongst young people on their personal pronoun usage, and not on their actual use in manga. Before one can evaluate the role that manga may play, it is crucial to better grasp how personal pronouns are actually used"[^17]. The corpus approach she developed responds to precisely this need: rather than asking readers what they think manga characters say, the corpus quantifies what manga characters actually say.

The principal empirical findings of Unser-Schutz's comparative work, summarized across her conference presentations and journal articles, can be characterized as follows[^17]. First, **gendered speech patterns differ significantly between shoujo and shōnen genres**: "the findings show significant variations in speech styles used by male and female characters across these genres, contributing to understanding character representation in manga"[^17]. The genre boundary, in other words, is not merely thematic or visual but linguistic: characters in shoujo and shōnen manga use Japanese in measurably different ways, even where surface demographics of the characters (sex, age) are comparable. Second, the use of *yakuwarigo*—the stylized role-language registers theorized in Chapter 2—differs between genres: "shōnen manga, with larger character casts, may utilize yakuwarigo more extensively. In contrast, shōjo manga may employ different techniques, affecting reader engagement and narrative complexity"[^17]. Shoujo manga's use of stylized speech is therefore not simply less than shōnen's but qualitatively different in its strategic deployment.

Third, and most consequentially for the present study, Unser-Schutz's analysis of a corpus of ten popular manga series identifies a complex picture in which **all genders use a variety of gendered SFPs**, with genre-based variations appearing in directions that complicate naïve "media drives change" narratives. As she summarizes: "I looked at two markers of Japanese gendered speech, personal pronouns and sentence final particles (SFPs), finding that while personal pronoun usage was generally normative, all genders used a variety of gendered SFPs"[^17]. The fuller methodological summary clarifies the comparative structure: "while genre-based variations such as more stereotypical speech in manga for boys may reflect narrative differences, I suggest that the data show that manga reflect previously established changes, as with SFPs, thus appearing consistent with reports that mass-media are not a clear source of linguistic change. However, their perception as low texts may be influential in their association with negative changes, even when such claims are not supported empirically, as with PPs"[^17]. The conclusion is calibrated rather than triumphalist: pronouns in manga remain "generally normative" in their distribution across characters' gender, while SFPs are deployed more flexibly, with shoujo and shōnen each developing distinctive conventions that extend rather than violate the system documented in Chapter 3.

Several specific patterns emerge from this corpus literature that are critical for the empirical analyses of subsequent chapters. The summary in Unser-Schutz's pedagogical paper draws explicit attention to "**sentence-final particles in manga for young girls and older women**" as evidence of the genre's distinctive use of stylized stereotypical forms, suggesting that shoujo and josei manga are denser sites of marked feminine SFPs than equivalent samples of naturalistic conversation would yield[^23][^17]. This is precisely the pattern that the framework of Chapters 2 and 3 predicts: shoujo manga, as a mediatized register operating substantially through stereotype-level forms (in Silverstein's sense), should be expected to deploy *wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*, *teyo*, and similar feminine markers more frequently and in more pragmatically loaded configurations than the actual speech of young Japanese women, whose retreat from these forms toward neutral and moderately masculine SFPs was documented in §3.6. The genre, in other words, sustains the stereotype-level inventory of feminine forms even as naturalistic younger speech recedes from it.

The shōnen comparator establishes the other side of this asymmetry. The same pedagogical summary notes that "shōnen manga, with larger character casts, may utilize yakuwarigo more extensively"[^17], suggesting that shōnen's deployment of stereotype-level masculine forms (*ze*, *zo*, *ore*, hyper-assertive constructions) is similarly elevated relative to naturalistic majority usage. The two genres thus jointly preserve the binary stereotype-level inventory of *yakuwarigo* even as actual speakers of both sexes have moved toward neutral and converged registers. Manga, on this picture, functions less as an engine of linguistic change than as a **reservoir of marked indexical configurations**—a curated archive of stylistic resources that real-world speakers no longer routinely produce but that readers can encounter, store as exemplars, and selectively draw upon.

Two qualifications must be registered. First, the empirical record specifically on shoujo manga, as opposed to manga more generally, remains comparatively thin. Unser-Schutz herself notes that "while still in the beginning stages, a first attempt at looking at their distribution in manga has been summarized in Unser-Schutz (2010a)"[^23], indicating that systematic shoujo-specific corpus work is still under development. Several of her subsequent papers extend the analysis, but the granular distributional data—precise frequencies of each SFP across character types, contexts, and decades—remain less fully developed for shoujo than the broader theoretical claims rely upon. The present study therefore treats Unser-Schutz's general findings as the most authoritative available empirical baseline while acknowledging that more fine-grained analysis is a methodological priority for future work.

Second, the relationship between corpus-based findings about manga character speech and the theoretical claim that manga functions as exemplar input for readers is itself an inference rather than a direct empirical demonstration. Unser-Schutz's corpus work tells us what manga characters say, not what readers internalize. The framework of Chapter 2, however, supports the inference that high-frequency, salient, identity-engaging exemplars are disproportionately likely to be stored and to become available for activation in production. Shoujo manga's documented patterns—dense feminine SFPs, character-typed *yakuwarigo* configurations, occasional cross-prescriptive pronoun use by female characters—provide exactly the kind of input that this exemplar-storage mechanism would weight favorably. The empirical analyses of Chapters 7–9 will need to test whether queer young adults' production patterns align with the manga register's specific configurations, with the broader younger-generation pattern of moderately masculine retreat from feminine SFPs documented for non-queer peers, or with neither.

The following table summarizes the principal contrasts among the three reference points—prescriptive *joseigo*/*danseigo*, contemporary naturalistic younger speech, and shoujo manga register—as they emerge from the corpus and ideological literature.

| Reference point | First-person pronouns (female speakers) | Sentence-final particles (female characters/speakers) | Status of stereotype-level forms |
|---|---|---|---|
| Prescriptive *joseigo* (Meiji-derived ideology) | *watashi* default; *atashi* in casual; *watakushi* formal | *wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*, *teyo*, *koto* expected | Prescribed and naturalized as ideologically baseline |
| Naturalistic contemporary younger speech | Significant use of *uchi*, occasional *boku*/*ore*; *watashi* in formal | Predominantly neutral forms; ~68.8% neutral in Okamoto's data; retreat from stereotype-level feminine SFPs | In substantial retreat |
| Shoujo manga register (corpus evidence) | "Generally normative" pronoun distribution by character sex, but with marked exceptions for *bishōnen*/gender-crossing/queer-coded characters | "All genders used a variety of gendered SFPs"; dense stereotype-level feminine SFPs preserved across character types | Sustained, stylized, and pragmatically intensified |

The analytic implication is that shoujo manga does not coincide with either prescriptive ideology or naturalistic contemporary practice; it occupies a third position, one in which the stereotype-level inventory of *yakuwarigo* is preserved and pragmatically intensified, while particular character types exploit cross-prescriptive moves (female characters using *boku*, sensitive male characters using softened SFPs) that the prescriptive system does not sanction but that the genre routinely stages. This positioning is what makes shoujo manga theoretically interesting for the present study: it is neither what queer readers would find in their etiquette manuals nor what they would hear from their non-queer peers, but a third register whose distinctive configurations are precisely the kind of stylistic resource that the framework of Chapter 2 predicts queer speakers would find useful.

## 4.3 Hyper-Feminine and Hyper-Masculine Character Registers

The most immediately visible feature of shoujo manga's *yakuwarigo* is its **iconic deployment of stereotype-level forms** at both poles of the prescriptive binary. Shoujo manga makes routine use of hyper-feminine and hyper-masculine configurations whose density and pragmatic intensification exceed what naturalistic conversation supplies, and these configurations function as the genre's most easily recognized—and most easily caricatured—linguistic resources.

The **hyper-feminine register** is the more elaborately developed of the two within shoujo manga, in keeping with the genre's principal female protagonists and emphasis on emotional intimacy. Drawing on the framework articulated in §3.4, this register is best understood as a contemporary stylization of the *teyo dawa kotoba* register that Meiji-era schoolgirls developed and that subsequent novelistic and pedagogical practice progressively sedimented into iconic *joseigo*. The forms that anchor it—*atashi*, *watashi*, and (in *ojō-sama*-inflected sub-registers) *watakushi* on the pronoun side; *wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *noyo*, *kashira*, and (in stylized historical or aristocratic settings) *teyo* and *koto* on the SFP side—are precisely the forms that Inoue and Kinsui identify as the iconic core of feminine *yakuwarigo*. Importantly, these forms appear in shoujo manga at densities that survey and corpus evidence on real-world young women's speech does not support. Where Okamoto's data indicate "68.8% neutral forms" in university-aged women's speech, shoujo manga's heroines deploy stereotype-level feminine SFPs as a pragmatically loaded baseline. Unser-Schutz's general finding that "all genders used a variety of gendered SFPs" in her manga corpus, with shoujo manga functioning as a particularly dense site of feminine stereotype-level forms, supports this characterization[^17].

The pragmatic functions of the hyper-feminine register in shoujo manga are several. At the level of characterization, the register iconically performs traits that the indexical fields of its anchor forms make available: gentleness, refinement, emotional sensitivity, social grace, and (in *ojō-sama* sub-registers) class privilege and aristocratic distance. The framework of Chapter 2 is decisive here: these are not pre-existing meanings that the register passively reflects but ideologically linked elements of the indexical field of *atashi*-with-*wa*, *watashi*-with-*kashira*, *watakushi*-with-*teyo*, that successive iterations of *yakuwarigo* deployment have sedimented. At the level of narrative pragmatics, the register's deployment is **affectively intensified**: the hyper-feminine SFPs cluster at moments of emotional disclosure, romantic confession, romantic anxiety, and interior monologue—precisely the genre's most narratively saturated moments. Readers, on the exemplar-storage account, are accordingly likely to associate these forms not merely with abstractly "feminine" speech but with the affective intensities of romantic interiority, an indexical loading that subsequent uptake by queer readers may find especially useful.

The **hyper-masculine register** in shoujo manga is the symmetrical counterpart, deployed primarily through male love interests, rivals, antagonists, and (less prominently) male side characters. Its anchor forms—*ore* on the pronoun side; *ze*, *zo*, *na*, and assertive *da*-copula constructions on the SFP side—mark assertiveness, camaraderie, anger, dominance, or playful bravado, depending on context. As in the feminine register, density and pragmatic intensification distinguish the manga deployment from naturalistic majority speech. Sturtz Sreetharan's documentation that real-world Kantō and Kansai men "used 'masculine' forms relatively infrequently," with masculine SFPs functioning as pragmatic signals of "camaraderie, authority, anger, and so on" rather than as constant background features, contrasts sharply with the manga register's tendency to maintain marked masculine forms as baseline character speech for particular character types. Shōnen manga, on Unser-Schutz's account, maintains the masculine *yakuwarigo* even more densely, but shoujo manga's male characters, particularly the more conventionally masculine love interests and rivals, also draw heavily on *ore* and emphatic *ze*/*zo* in moments of assertion[^17].

The **iconicity** of these registers, in the sense theorized in §2.5, is what makes them so powerful as stylistic resources. Eckert's analysis of how indexical fields work via iconization—a form is heard as embodying the qualities it indexes—applies directly: the breathy *kashira*, the soft *wayo*, the elaborated honorifics of the *ojō-sama* heroine are heard as embodying her gentility and refinement; the sharp *ze* and emphatic *zo* of the rival love interest are heard as embodying his masculine assertion. Readers do not merely learn that certain forms correlate with certain character types; they come to perceive the forms as iconically constitutive of those types. This iconic loading is what makes manga registers susceptible to **recursive elaboration** when characters and contexts are recombined in non-default ways—a possibility that §4.4 will examine.

A further feature of shoujo manga's hyper-feminine and hyper-masculine registers is their **modular composability**. The genre routinely combines pronoun and SFP modules in pragmatically specific configurations (e.g., *atashi* with *wa* for casual feminine assertion; *watashi* with *kashira* for refined feminine wondering; *ore* with *ze* for confident masculine declaration; *ore* with *zo* for self-directed determination). Each configuration carries a more specific indexical activation than either form in isolation, and the predictable recurrence of particular configurations across works produces what Agha terms enregisterment: the bundled configuration becomes recognizable as a stylistic unit. Readers who internalize these enregistered configurations have available not just isolated pronouns and SFPs but **pre-bundled pragmatic packages** that can be deployed wholesale or decomposed into their elements for selective recombination.

The implication for queer readers, anticipated in §4.6 and tested empirically in subsequent chapters, is that shoujo manga's hyper-feminine and hyper-masculine registers supply the indexical extremes of the binary in densely staged form. A queer reader who finds herself unable to occupy either pole comfortably nonetheless encounters, in shoujo manga, both poles in their most pragmatically articulated form—and acquires, via exemplar storage, a stock of stylistic resources at each pole that can be selectively activated, partially adopted, or used as raw material for bricolage. The hyper-feminine register, in particular, occupies a paradoxical position: although its density in manga sustains the very stereotype-level feminine forms that real-world young women have abandoned, it does so in a context where the forms are stylized, performed, and recognizable as performances—making them, ironically, more available to queer speakers as resources for self-conscious stylistic positioning than they would be if encountered in a genuinely naturalistic setting.

## 4.4 Bishōnen, Otokoyaku, and Gender-Crossing Speech

Shoujo manga's distinctiveness as a register, however, lies not principally in its hyper-feminine and hyper-masculine poles—both of which are documented in adjacent genres as well—but in its **diversified middle**, where character types whose linguistic configurations cannot be accommodated by the prescriptive binary occupy narratively central positions. The most consequential of these character types are the *bishōnen*, the *otokoyaku*-inflected princely or gender-ambiguous protagonist, and the explicitly gender-crossing or crossdressing character. Each instantiates a configuration of pronoun and SFP usage that elaborates the indexical fields of Chapter 3 in directions the prescriptive binary does not authorize.

The ***bishōnen*** ("beautiful young man") is shoujo manga's signature male character type and its most analytically significant. Visually, the *bishōnen* is "thin, androgynous, pretty and emotionally sensitive," and as one cultural commentator notes, this configuration "may coincidentally fit western gay stereotypes, but this type of character just represents an East Asian beauty standard for men. Guys who fit these *bishounen* and *ikemen* types are considered desirable by Japanese women and are generally assumed to be straight"[^20]. The same source emphasizes the cultural specificity of the type: "in the US, your idea of a stereotypical gay dude may be a metrosexual twink with a lisp and a limp wrist, but different countries have different stereotypes. In Japan, the appearance of *ikahomo*/*ikanimo* (a stereotypical gay man) is a heavy-set masculine guy with short haircut, strong face, and facial hair"[^20]. The *bishōnen* is therefore not a stereotype of male homosexuality but a stereotype of male romantic desirability for a female (and queer-female) audience—a distinction critical for understanding the specific indexical work the type performs.

Linguistically, the *bishōnen* characteristically blends masculine pronouns with **softened or feminine-leaning SFPs**, producing a configuration that the prescriptive binary does not anticipate. The defining choice is often *boku* rather than *ore*, indexing not simply masculinity but, as Chapter 3 catalogued, modesty, sensitivity, intellectual seriousness, and—within shoujo manga's specific indexical field—the androgynous self-presentation that the type's visual code already announces. SFP usage tends toward neutral or mildly feminine-leaning forms (*ne*, *yo*, occasionally *no* in interrogatives or tentative assertions), avoiding the assertive *ze*/*zo* of the rival love interest while maintaining the masculine pronoun. The configuration thus selectively activates the indexical field of *boku*—the softer, more sensitive, more intellectually inclined regions of that field—while leaving aside the more assertive masculine markers that would activate different regions of the field of *ore*.

The result is what the framework of Chapter 2 would describe as a **bricolage register at the genre level**: shoujo manga's *bishōnen* assembles selected modules from the indexical fields of multiple prescriptive registers (modest masculine *boku*, neutral/feminine-leaning SFPs, occasionally elevated honorifics inherited from *ojō-sama* register, sometimes a soft prosody that Ohara's work would associate with feminine high pitch) into a composite configuration whose distinctiveness is precisely its non-coincidence with any prescriptive register. Readers who store these configurations as exemplars accumulate, over time, a model of how a sensitive, romantically desirable masculine speaker can sound—a model that draws on but rearranges the components of the binary system. For queer readers, particularly those navigating non-default masculine or androgynous self-presentations, this configuration is precisely the kind of stylistic resource that the framework predicts would acquire heightened salience.

The ***otokoyaku*** tradition adds a second axis of gender-crossing convention to shoujo manga. Originating in the Takarazuka all-female revue, where the *otokoyaku* role denotes the female performer of male roles, the *otokoyaku* aesthetic has long influenced shoujo manga's depiction of princely female protagonists, gender-ambiguous heroes, and crossdressing characters, with classics such as *The Rose of Versailles* serving as paradigmatic examples within the broader tradition that Welker's "Transfiguring Women in Late Twentieth-Century Japan" situates within the long entanglement of feminist, lesbian, and shoujo manga histories[^22]. Linguistically, the *otokoyaku*-inflected character is typically a female-identified or female-bodied protagonist who deploys masculine pronouns (*boku* or *ore*) and assertive SFPs in the performance of a princely or knightly persona, often within narratives that thematize gender disguise, military or aristocratic role, or romantic rescue. The configuration is doubly elaborated: the character's speech indexes the prescriptive masculine register, but the indexing is itself foregrounded as performance, since both the character (in narrative) and the genre's audience (in reception) are aware that the speaker's social or biological position does not align with the indexical field's default.

The ***crossdressing or gender-fluid character*** generalizes this pattern. Shoujo manga has a long tradition of narratives in which characters of one gender adopt the social and linguistic conventions of another for plot reasons—infiltrating a single-sex school, performing on stage, hiding from pursuers—and such narratives routinely thematize the linguistic dimension of crossing. A character disguised as the opposite gender must adopt the corresponding pronouns and SFPs, and the manga frequently makes the linguistic adjustment narratively explicit, sometimes through metalinguistic commentary by other characters and sometimes through scenes in which the disguised character struggles with or playfully exaggerates the new register. These narratives function as **explicit metapragmatic instruction** for readers: they not only deploy non-default pronoun and SFP configurations but reflect on those deployments as performances, making the indexical machinery of the system unusually visible.

The cumulative effect of these character types is to make shoujo manga a register in which the indexical fields of *boku*, *watashi*, *atashi*, and the major SFPs are routinely activated in **non-default configurations** that the prescriptive system treats as marked or transgressive. Where Chapter 3 documented that real-world non-normative pronoun use is significant but largely operates below explicit metapragmatic awareness, shoujo manga's deployment of the same patterns is foregrounded, narratively elaborated, and pragmatically intensified. The genre therefore functions as a kind of **stylized laboratory** in which the cross-prescriptive moves that real speakers make tentatively are dramatized in narratively saturated form, with each move tied to a particular character, plot situation, and emotional register.

A note of caution about reach is warranted. The corpus literature reviewed in §4.2 documents that shoujo manga's pronoun usage is "generally normative" in distributional terms[^17]; cross-prescriptive moves, while structurally significant, occur in a minority of utterances. The argument here is not that shoujo manga is dominated by gender-crossing speech but that it preserves and elaborates such speech as a recognized convention, available to readers as exemplars, in a way that adjacent genres and naturalistic conversation do not. The shoujo register's distinctiveness lies in its **modular elaboration of the indexical fields beyond binary prescription**, not in a wholesale displacement of the prescriptive system, and the analytical leverage this elaboration provides for queer readers' stylistic projects is correspondingly partial rather than total.

## 4.5 Queer-Coded Speech in Yuri and Boys' Love Subgenres

The continuum extending from shoujo manga into yuri and Boys' Love (BL) constitutes the most explicitly queer-narrative segment of the broader register, and its linguistic conventions both elaborate the patterns surveyed in §§4.3–4.4 and introduce specific configurations of their own. Because the cultural and reception literature on these subgenres is more developed than the corpus-linguistic literature, this section's analytical center of gravity tilts toward narrative and characterological observations, with linguistic claims framed cautiously.

**Yuri** depicts romantic and erotic relationships between female characters and emerged from within the shoujo manga tradition in the 1970s–80s, in tandem with BL[^20]. Welker's work on "queer shōjo manga" situates the emergence of yuri within a broader transfiguration of Japanese women's, feminist, and lesbian cultural expression in late-twentieth-century Japan, with shoujo manga artists and fans serving as central actors in this transformation[^22]. Linguistically, yuri's protagonists draw on the broader shoujo register surveyed in §§4.2–4.3—stereotype-level feminine pronouns and SFPs in many cases, *boku*-using or *otokoyaku*-inflected protagonists in others—but configure these resources around female-female intimacy, with the linguistic markers of romantic confession, longing, and desire deployed across configurations that the prescriptive heteronormative ideology does not anticipate. The detail-level corpus evidence for yuri-specific linguistic patterns remains underdeveloped in the directly available literature; what can be said with confidence is that yuri's continuity with broader shoujo conventions and its narrative centering of female-female romance make it a particularly salient site for queer female readers' identification, with the indexical loadings of the genre's pronouns and SFPs accordingly likely to acquire affective and identitarian weight beyond their distributional frequency.

**Boys' Love (BL)**, the more extensively studied subgenre, depicts romantic and sexual relationships between male characters and is most systematically documented in the BL studies literature surveyed in §4.1[^21][^20]. The principal scholarly observation about BL that bears on the present linguistic study concerns the genre's **production conditions and audience composition**: BL "creators, amateurs and professionals, and consumers, are mainly heterosexual women," and the genre is often described as "a non-gay text" whose representations of male-male intimacy are oriented to female reader engagement rather than to gay male experience[^21]. Mizoguchi argues that BL provides "a discursive space for women to participate actively in issues of female sexuality and identity that contest a hierarchised, heterosexual paradigm," and Ishida argues that "even though representations of gay men in BL are fantasies, they are points of identification for female readers" with BL "mutually structured on a representational appropriation of gay men's imagery and self-projection of women's desire"[^21]. Fujimoto's well-known formulation describes BL as a genre in which fans "play with gender" by "freely adopting various sexual roles" and through which "the constraints of oppressive female gender roles can be removed"[^21].

The **seme/uke characterological dyad**, central to BL's narrative architecture, has direct linguistic correlates. The *seme* "playing a dominant role" and the *uke* "less dominant and/or receptive" are typed not only by narrative position but by speech: *seme* characters tend to deploy more assertive masculine configurations (*ore*, *ze*, *zo*, direct imperatives), while *uke* characters tend toward softer, sometimes *bishōnen*-inflected, sometimes feminine-leaning configurations (*boku*, neutral or mildly feminine SFPs, more frequent emotional disclosure)[^21]. The dyad thus instantiates within BL a stylized contrast that the broader shoujo register supplies in less narratively codified form. Fan reception research documents that "Chinese fans" prefer BL stories in which the seme and uke roles "mirror that of traditional heterosexual relationships, rather than a reversible relationship"[^21], suggesting that the linguistic and characterological dyad maps onto a heteronormatively structured romantic ideal even within the formally homoromantic narrative—a structural observation with implications for how the genre's linguistic configurations are received by different segments of its readership.

It is important to register that the BL literature documents a sustained debate over whether the genre's representations of male-male intimacy "subvert" or "reinforce" gender norms[^21], and that this debate has direct bearing on the linguistic analysis. Readings that treat BL as subversive, including Wood's view of BL as "the deployment of romantic fantasy to imagine worlds in which 'the heterosexual couple is no longer the referent or the privileged example of sexual culture,'" emphasize the genre's queering potential[^21]. Readings that treat BL as reinforcing heteronormativity, including Zhao and Madill's documentation of Chinese fans reading BL through a "heteronormative frame," emphasize the genre's reinscription of binary dynamics under formally homoromantic surface[^21]. Both readings are linguistically intelligible: the seme/uke dyad's binary linguistic configuration can be read either as expanding the available indexical positions (since both are male characters now occupying both poles) or as preserving the binary indexical economy under cosmetic surface variation.

A crucial distinction must be drawn between BL's *bishōnen*-inflected register and the **separately enregistered *onee*/*okama* speech** that circulates in broader Japanese media as the principal stereotype of male homosexuality. As one widely circulated cultural commentary explains: "Gay men in Japan are stereotyped by the general population as being camp, and using feminine clothes, language and pronouns. Those who present femininely are often referred to as オネエ ('onee') because they use オネエ言葉 ('onee kotoba', feminine speech), and may or may not identify as male. Many entertainers who are out use *onee* personas on TV to, well, entertain the audience. That may be the only exposure an average Japanese person has to a real-life openly gay or trans person"[^20]. The okama character archetype in Japanese media "reflects how society thinks a gay man looks and acts" and is "not the willowy, androgynous *bishounen* of shoujo manga"[^20]. The term *okama* itself is "a homophobic and transphobic slur, directed at people who fit the *onee* stereotype," and "if you're not a Japanese queer man or transfem individual, you shouldn't direct it at anyone, period"[^20].

The linguistic implications of this distinction are consequential. **BL's queer-coded register is *not* the *onee*/*okama* register**: BL deploys the *bishōnen*-inflected speech surveyed in §4.4, characterized by masculine pronouns (*boku* often dominant) and softened or neutral SFPs, while *onee kotoba* deploys explicitly feminine pronouns (*atashi*) and feminine SFPs (*wa*, *no*, *kashira*, *wayo*) in male-bodied speech as a stylized performance of feminine-leaning gay masculinity, often with affective intensification through prosody and lexical choice. Real-world gay Japanese speakers' linguistic practice—as documented in Lunsing's and Maree's work cited in Chapter 3—often draws selectively on *onee kotoba* in particular contexts and registers, with *atashi* serving as a community-internal form of self-reference within gay male peer settings[^20]. BL, however, generally does not stage this register; its male protagonists speak in *bishōnen* configurations rather than *onee* configurations, reflecting the genre's orientation toward female reader engagement with idealized male-male romance rather than toward representation of contemporary gay male linguistic practice.

This divergence has direct theoretical consequences for the present study. Queer Japanese young adults encountering shoujo-adjacent BL acquire exemplars of *bishōnen*-inflected speech, which is characterologically stylized and pragmatically intensified but which does not coincide with either *onee kotoba* or with the prescriptive masculine register. Queer readers encountering broader Japanese media (variety shows, comedies, drama) acquire exemplars of *onee*/*okama* speech, which represents male-bodied speakers using feminine forms but does so within a register that is often homophobically caricatured and that participates in the metapragmatic apparatus of stereotyping that §3.5 surveyed[^20]. The two registers are therefore differentially available as identity-relevant resources: BL's *bishōnen* register, being shoujo-adjacent and marked by reader identification rather than by entertainment-driven caricature, may carry positive identitarian weight, while *onee*/*okama* speech, being marked by both community-internal use and external homophobic deployment, carries more ambivalent loadings whose activation depends on speaker stance, interlocutor identity, and performative context.

Yuri occupies a structurally analogous but linguistically less codified position. Welker's work documents the entanglement of yuri's emergence with feminist and lesbian cultural movements in late-twentieth-century Japan[^22], and the genre's narrative centering of female-female romance places it within the broader queer-shoujo continuum. Linguistically, yuri's configurations remain largely continuous with the broader shoujo register—stereotype-level feminine forms, *bishōnen*-inflected female protagonists, *otokoyaku*-style princely characters in the older and more theatrical strand—but are deployed around female-female intimacy in ways that activate the genre's indexical resources for queer female reader identification. The corpus-linguistic specifics of yuri are less developed in the directly available literature than those of BL, and the present study correspondingly treats yuri-specific linguistic claims with the same caution as in §4.2.

## 4.6 Shoujo Manga as a Curated Catalogue of Indexical Possibilities for Minority Readers

The analyses of §§4.1–4.5 converge on a characterization of shoujo manga as a mediatized linguistic register whose distinctiveness lies in three properties: its **modular diversification** of *yakuwarigo* across hyper-feminine, hyper-masculine, *bishōnen*-inflected, *otokoyaku*-inflected, gender-crossing, and queer-coded configurations; its **sustained density** of stereotype-level marked forms, particularly on the feminine side, that real-world contemporary speech has substantially abandoned; and its **narrative intensification** of these forms at moments of romantic disclosure, identity disclosure, and emotional crisis—precisely the moments at which reader identification with characters is most likely to consolidate into the kind of socially weighted exemplar storage that the framework of Chapter 2 specified.

Read through that framework, shoujo manga functions as **a curated catalogue of indexical possibilities** that the prescriptive binary system of *joseigo* and *danseigo* does not authorize but that the genre routinely stages.
