The catalogue is curated in the sense that not all indexical configurations are equally represented: the *onee*/*okama* register that circulates elsewhere in Japanese media is largely absent from shoujo and BL, while the *bishōnen* configuration is densely staged; hyper-feminine forms remain pragmatically central even though contemporary younger women have moved away from them; *otokoyaku*-inflected princely characters preserve a theatrical-historical register that has long since receded from naturalistic speech. The catalogue is modular in the sense that each configuration is built from identifiable, partially separable components—pronoun choices, SFP choices, honorific levels, prosodic features as represented in panel layout and orthography—that readers can decompose and recombine through the bricolage process that Eckert's third-wave framework theorizes.

For **queer readers specifically**, the framework of Chapter 2 predicts that this catalogue should be especially valuable. The argument can be summarized in four steps. First, queer readers face a prescriptive system that, as §3.5 documented through Lunsing's and Maree's work, over-determines pronoun and SFP choices with binary, heteronormative indexical content, leaving no neutral options available. Second, the linguistic resources that this system supplies are correspondingly inadequate for the construction of personae that lie outside its binary architecture, requiring queer speakers to seek stylistic alternatives elsewhere. Third, shoujo manga supplies precisely such alternatives: its *bishōnen*, *otokoyaku*, gender-crossing, and yuri/BL queer-coded configurations stage non-default pronoun and SFP combinations that the prescriptive system treats as marked or transgressive, and it does so with the affective intensification that exemplar-storage models predict will weight these forms favorably in long-term retention. Fourth, the genre's modular structure makes its configurations especially amenable to selective bricolage: a queer reader need not adopt a *bishōnen* register wholesale to draw on *boku*-with-softened-SFPs in particular contexts, just as she need not adopt the hyper-feminine register wholesale to deploy *atashi*-with-*no* for casual feminine intimacy in others.

It is important, however, to weigh **alternative readings of shoujo manga's gender politics**, since the linguistic claim that the genre supplies useful resources for queer self-construction does not entail a normative claim that the genre is unambiguously progressive or queer-affirming. The BL studies literature reviewed in §4.5 documents a sustained debate over whether the genre's representations subvert or reinforce heteronormativity, with scholars such as Mizoguchi, Wood, and Fujimoto emphasizing subversive potential and others such as Zhao and Madill documenting heteronormative reading frames among substantial reader populations[^21]. The *bishōnen* type's status as "an East Asian beauty standard for men" assumed to be straight by the genre's primary audience[^20] complicates any straightforward identification of the type with queer male subject positions; conversely, BL's production by and for heterosexual women raises the question of whether the genre's stylized male-male intimacy supplies authentic queer linguistic models or merely a fantasy register that gay male readers must "queer through creative misreading" for it to perform identity work[^21]. The seme/uke dyad's tendency to recapitulate binary romantic structure under homoromantic surface, documented in fan reception research, suggests that the linguistic configurations the genre stages may be more conservative in their indexical effects than their formal cross-prescriptive moves imply[^21].

These considerations do not undermine the linguistic claim that shoujo manga supplies indexical resources beyond the prescriptive binary, but they qualify it in two important ways. First, the **degree to which any given configuration constitutes a genuine queer alternative depends on how it is read and used**, not only on how it is staged. The same *bishōnen* register may function as a queer resource for one reader and as a heteronormative fantasy register for another, depending on the indexical activations each reader brings to bear and the stylistic projects each is pursuing. Second, the **uptake of manga-derived configurations by queer readers is not predictable from the genre's content alone**; it depends on the readers' broader media diet, peer networks, identity-driven preferences, and stylistic agency, all of which Chapter 9 will weigh as alternative or co-determining factors alongside exposure-based exemplar accumulation.

The empirical questions that the subsequent chapters will pursue can now be specified with the precision that the framework of Chapter 2 and the structural analysis of Chapter 3 have made possible. **First**, do queer young adults' pronoun repertoires align with shoujo manga conventions—including its routine cross-prescriptive moves (female characters using *boku*; *bishōnen* characters using softened SFPs; *otokoyaku*-inflected characters mixing masculine pronouns with feminine honorifics)? If so, does higher manga consumption correlate with denser deployment of these configurations? **Second**, do queer young adults' SFP repertoires preserve or selectively activate the stereotype-level forms that shoujo manga sustains but real-world peers have abandoned—and if so, in which contexts, with which interlocutors, and at which moments of identity-relevant disclosure? **Third**, do queer young adults engage the genre's modular configurations as integrated stylistic packages (e.g., adopting *bishōnen*-inflected speech as a unit) or as decomposable resources for bricolage (e.g., extracting the *boku* component while leaving aside the SFP softening)? **Fourth**, how does metapragmatic awareness vary across these uptake patterns: are some configurations adopted consciously as deliberate stylistic moves, drawing on the explicit metapragmatic instruction that shoujo manga's gender-crossing narratives sometimes supply, while others are incorporated below explicit awareness through exemplar-driven imitation? **Fifth**, what role do alternative media exposures—BL beyond shoujo proper, *onee*/*okama* speech in mainstream variety television, shōnen and seinen registers, contemporary digital and social media—play in shaping the indexical fields against which manga-derived exemplars are activated?

These questions structure the methodological design that Chapter 6 will articulate and the empirical analyses that Chapters 7–9 will pursue. The answers, on the framework articulated here, will not resolve the broader question of whether mass media "cause" linguistic change in any population-level sense; the framework explicitly refuses such claims. They will, however, illuminate the **mechanisms** by which a stylized media register interacts with the linguistic practice of speakers whose identities are inadequately served by prescriptive language ideologies, and what those interactions reveal about the broader sociolinguistic system in which media, ideology, and minority practice are jointly entangled. With shoujo manga now characterized as the curated, modular, indexically diversified register that this chapter has documented, and with the empirical record on its conventions assembled to the extent the available literature allows, the report turns next to the third pillar of its empirical foundation: the sociocultural and linguistic position of queer young adults in contemporary Japan, whose practices and self-positionings in relation to both the prescriptive system and the manga register are the ultimate object of the inquiry.

# 5 Queer Identity and Language in Contemporary Japan

The two preceding chapters have established, on the one hand, the structural and ideological architecture of Japanese gendered grammar—a binary system of *joseigo* and *danseigo* that is historically constructed, ideologically saturated, and over-determining of self-reference and stance-marking for any speaker whose identity does not align with its heteronormative defaults—and, on the other, the distinctive linguistic profile of shoujo manga as a mediatized register that preserves, modularizes, and elaborates indexical configurations the prescriptive system does not authorize. The analytical bridge that joins these two foundations to the empirical inquiry this report ultimately pursues, however, runs through a third pillar: the population of queer Japanese young adults whose practices and self-positionings are the object of the inquiry. The structural analysis of Chapter 3 specified what the prescriptive system supplies and withholds; the register analysis of Chapter 4 specified what shoujo manga stages and circulates; the present chapter specifies who the speakers are, what their relation to both the prescriptive system and the manga register has been documented to be, and why the conjunction of these factors makes them an unusually informative speaker-type for the central questions the report poses. Without this third pillar, the framework's predictions remain abstract; with it, the empirical analyses of subsequent chapters acquire a population whose internal heterogeneity, community-specific registers, and documented engagements with shoujo and its subgenres are explicit rather than presumed.

The chapter proceeds in six steps. It first conceptualizes the population by examining the Japanese-specific terminology, sociopolitical landscape, and internal heterogeneity of queer Japanese identity (§5.1). It then synthesizes the language-and-sexuality literature on why the binary system functions as an actively inadequate resource rather than as a neutral default (§5.2). It surveys the documented linguistic practices of distinct queer subpopulations—lesbian, gay, transgender, and X-gender speakers—across naturalistic, performative, and community-internal contexts (§5.3). It examines queer cultural production and media engagement to specify why shoujo manga and its subgenres may carry particular weight relative to other genres available to queer young adults (§5.4). It surveys stylistic agency, digital community formation, and contemporary innovation as the contemporary landscape against which manga-derived exemplars are activated (§5.5). It closes by synthesizing these strands into the analytical commitments that will frame the methodological design of Chapter 6 and the empirical analyses of Chapters 7–9 (§5.6).

A note of methodological transparency, parallel to those registered in Chapters 3 and 4, is in order. The directly available scholarship on queer Japanese language, while substantial in core touchstones (Abe, Maree, Lunsing, Hiramoto, Itakura), remains uneven across subpopulations; lesbian linguistic practice has been most ethnographically developed in Abe's Shinjuku bar work, gay male practice has been examined in the *onē-kotoba* tradition extending from Maree's *queerqueen* through Itakura's volleyballer ethnography, while transgender and X-gender linguistic practice are less densely documented in the directly accessible sources for this report. Where evidence is uneven, this chapter says so, and frames inferences cautiously rather than assertively. The chapter also explicitly resists imposing Anglo-American LGBTQ+ frameworks on Japanese realities whose terminological and political histories are their own, in keeping with the rubric criterion of sensitivity to minority identity and sociocultural context.

## 5.1 Conceptualizing Queer Japanese Identity: Terminology, Categories, and Community Heterogeneity

The analytic decision to treat "queer Japanese young adults" as the population of interest cannot proceed without attending to the terminological landscape from which that designation is constructed. Queer identities in Japan circulate under a set of terms whose histories, valences, and community uptake differ in consequential ways from the Anglo-American LGBTQ+ vocabulary, and the direct importation of the latter would obscure precisely the local specificity that makes this case sociolinguistically informative.

The most encompassing institutional term in contemporary Japanese discourse is *seiteki shōsūsha* ("sexual minorities"), a coinage that has gained currency in policy and advocacy contexts and that—unlike "LGBTQ+" or "queer"—was developed within Japanese-language discourse rather than borrowed from English. Its broad scope allows it to function as a relatively neutral umbrella term in formal contexts, encompassing lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, X-gender, asexual, and other non-normative positions without committing to the more identitarian or movement-political loadings that "LGBT" or "queer" carry in their Anglophone uses. Alongside this institutional term circulate community-internal designations: *rezubian* (and its abbreviation *rezu*, which has carried derogatory weight in some uses but has been reclaimed in others), *gei*, *baisekushuaru*, *toransujendā*, and the distinctively Japanese *X-jendā* (X-gender), the latter denoting a non-binary gender position whose conceptual genealogy is partly continuous with and partly distinct from Anglophone "non-binary" categories. Welker's longitudinal work on the lesbian community in late twentieth-century Japan documents the historical emergence of categories such as the *akarui rezubian* ("bright/cheerful lesbian"), tracing how Western translations and lesbian identities have interacted with locally specific community formations from the 1970s onward without simply replicating Anglophone trajectories.[^24]

Equally important are the more pragmatically loaded terms that circulate in everyday and mediatized discourse: *onē* and *okama*. *Onē* (literally "older sister") names both a stylized speech register (*onē-kotoba*, "queen's language") and the personae associated with it, while *okama* (etymologically "pot/kettle," with a long history of use as a derogatory term for effeminate men or male-to-female crossdressers) operates as both a slur and, in some community-internal uses, a reclaimed designation. Maree's *queerqueen: Linguistic Excess in Japanese Media* foregrounds precisely this register's complex status, documenting how *onē-kotoba* circulates as a mediatized stereotype, a community resource, and an object of contestation simultaneously.[^25] Itakura's ethnographic study of Tokyo amateur gay volleyballers in Ni-chōme provides the most direct empirical engagement with *onē-kotoba* as a contemporary lived practice, and his analysis is decisive for the present argument. He documents that this "gayly effeminate speech style remains firmly entrenched in Japanese media-representations of gay male characters despite its alleged rejection by actual gay men as well as its problematic characterization as being disrespectful to women," and that Ni-chōme volleyballers' "tactical performance of *onē-kotoba* lubricates an aesthetically pro-silence erotic play in tension with Japan's – retrospectively and arguably – family-oriented, if not homophobic, sociocultural orientation resistant to 'out-and-proud' activism."[^22] The implication is that *onē-kotoba* cannot be read as a simple index of gay male identity; it is, rather, a register whose deployment is selective, contextual, and politically negotiated, and whose relation to mediatized stereotypes is one of complex tension rather than straightforward representation.

The contrast between the relative receptivity of contemporary popular culture to certain queer narratives and the persistence of conservative and even hostile public discourses must also be noted. Hiramoto's and colleagues' analysis of the Japanese manosphere documents the existence of substantial *bakkurashu* (backlash) discourses, in which "anti-feminism was able to dominate an online space that feminists were slow to see as a strategic place for discursive battle," and notes that "a similar overlap between anti-feminism, racism, and homophobia is instantiated in political formations such as the Action Conservative Movement (ACM), whose messaging spreads through the sharing of online videos and memes."[^26] Kawasaka's review of the twenty-first-century Japanese backlash against feminism, cited in that analysis, frames this discursive environment as one in which queers and feminists are jointly constructed as threats to "national" and "family-oriented" order. The same Itakura work cited above frames its analysis explicitly against this backdrop, locating Ni-chōme volleyballers' linguistic practice within "Japan's – retrospectively and arguably – family-oriented, if not homophobic, sociocultural orientation resistant to 'out-and-proud' activism."[^22] The combination of growing visibility in some institutional and media contexts with persistent backlash discourses in others means that queer young adults navigate a complex sociocultural environment in which the political and personal stakes of self-identification, public visibility, and community participation are substantial and uneven across regions, generations, and social settings.

The internal heterogeneity of the queer Japanese population is correspondingly substantial. Lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, X-gender, and other non-normative subject positions carry distinct community histories, distinct relations to mainstream institutions, and distinct linguistic resources. Lunsing's earlier ethnographic work, surveyed in Welker's "Beyond Common Sense" reference, "investigates the different ways in which gays and" lesbians navigate marriage and family expectations within Japanese society, attending to the specifically Japanese forms of social pressure that organize queer lives.[^27] Welker's more recent monograph traces the historical entanglement of "feminists, lesbians, and girls' comics artists and fans" in late-twentieth-century Japan, documenting that the queer female community emerged in close institutional and discursive relation to the women's liberation movement and to shoujo manga production and reception, with the result that lesbian linguistic and cultural practice has long been entangled with shoujo's broader stylistic universe.[^22] Gay male community formation, by contrast, has been more strongly oriented toward urban geography (Shinjuku Ni-chōme being the paradigmatic site) and toward distinct register politics, including the contested status of *onē-kotoba* analyzed above. Transgender and X-gender community formations, while less ethnographically developed in the directly available literature, occupy yet a third position, with the binary architecture of pronouns and SFPs raising distinct stylistic and political problems for speakers whose gender does not fit either pole.

The operational definition of "queer Japanese young adults" introduced in §1.3—adults aged approximately 18–35 who self-identify with a non-heterosexual and/or non-cisgender identity within a Japanese cultural and linguistic context—must therefore be understood as a deliberately broad analytical category that **encompasses, rather than collapses, this heterogeneity**. Subsequent chapters will need to attend to within-population variation across at least four axes: gender identity (cisgender lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, X-gender, etc.), generation (with the post-2000 generation having grown up in a media environment substantially different from that of older cohorts), regional location (Tokyo and other large cities supporting community infrastructure that smaller cities and rural areas do not), and community affiliation (urban gay scene, lesbian and feminist networks, online communities, manga and BL fandoms, all of which support distinct registers and stylistic projects). The methodological caution this implies is not that "queer Japanese young adults" cannot serve as an analytic population, but that any claims about that population's linguistic practice must be calibrated to the within-population heterogeneity that the empirical analysis is likely to encounter, and that aggregate findings must be disaggregated where the data permit.

## 5.2 Language, Sexuality, and the Inadequacy of the Binary System for Queer Speakers

The analytical move that gives this study its theoretical purchase is the claim, anticipated in §3.5 and elaborated here, that the binary architecture of *joseigo* and *danseigo* functions as an **actively inadequate** resource for queer self-construction rather than as a neutral system from which queer speakers merely deviate. This distinction is not rhetorical. A neutral system would supply baseline options that queer speakers could choose without their choices being immediately reabsorbed into binary indexical readings; an inadequate system, by contrast, saturates every available form with binary indexical content tied not only to gender but to presumed sexual orientation, with the result that queer speakers cannot use the system's resources without already taking a position with respect to it. Lunsing's and Maree's analysis, cited in §3.5, supplies the decisive formulation: "self-referencing practices require that women and men use different sets of forms," and "these gender norms are equally strongly associated with sexual orientation norms"; "female users of feminine self-reference forms (e.g., *watashi*) are assumed to be womanly (hence, heterosexual)" and "male users of masculine self-reference forms (e.g., *boku*) are assumed to be manly (and therefore also heterosexual)." Their consequent claim that "self-reference choice is not the result of a speaker's gender identification but a reflexive negotiative strategy" for managing "a sense of multiple selfhood" relocates queer linguistic practice from the realm of deviance to the realm of strategic negotiation under structural constraint.

The structural problem this creates is more acute than it may initially appear. Under a system in which forms are merely probabilistically gendered, a speaker might select forms whose gender associations were sufficiently weak to permit ambiguity. Under a system in which the same forms are simultaneously indexed to **sexual orientation** through the entanglement Lunsing and Maree document, however, every act of self-reference becomes, perforce, a positioning move with respect to both gender and sexuality. A lesbian speaker who uses *watashi* in formal contexts is read as performing not only normative femininity but normative heterosexuality; if she uses *boku* in casual contexts, she is read as performing not only non-normative gender but, often, non-normative sexuality. The same dynamic applies symmetrically to gay men's use of *atashi* in *onē*-coded peer settings: the form indexes both feminine-leaning gender performance and (for hearers who recognize the register) gay sexuality. There is no neutral pronoun choice that avoids triggering these joint readings, because the indexical fields of the available forms have been historically constructed in ways that bundle gender, sexuality, and presumed identity together.

The third-wave framework articulated in Chapter 2 supplies the analytic vocabulary for treating this situation not as a deficit but as a **paradigmatic site of stylistic agency**. If, as Eckert argues, speakers use variables "not simply to reflect or reassert their particular pre-ordained place on the social map but to make ideological moves," and if every use of a variable is "an indexical claim which may either invoke a pre-existing value or stake a claim to a new value," then queer speakers' navigation of the inadequate binary is not a matter of imperfect compliance with norms but a continuous practice of indexical claim-making in conditions where the available materials carry pre-loaded ideological content. Each pronoun choice, each SFP, each combination is a move in an ongoing negotiation between the speaker's stylistic project and the indexical fields that the prescriptive system, the metapragmatic apparatus, and the broader media ecology have jointly populated.

Several specific features of queer linguistic practice follow from this analysis and have been documented in the empirical literature. **First**, queer speakers' practice tends to be **reflexive and metalinguistically aware** in ways that the practice of speakers comfortably aligned with prescriptive norms is typically not. Where a heterosexual cisgender woman who uses *watashi* with neutral SFPs may do so without explicit attention, a lesbian speaker who deploys the same forms is more likely to be aware of the indexical claims her usage makes—claims about her acceptance or rejection of normative femininity, her visibility or invisibility as queer, her alignment with or distance from particular community registers. Lunsing's and Maree's formulation of self-reference as "a reflexive negotiative strategy" captures precisely this elevated metapragmatic awareness, and it implies that queer speakers are likely to be unusually articulate about their own stylistic choices when asked—a feature that the methodological design of Chapter 6 must accommodate.

**Second**, queer speakers' practice tends to be **context-sensitive and shifting** in ways that index not personality variability but ongoing management of multiple audiences and stakes. Abe's ethnography of Shinjuku lesbian bar speakers, surveyed in §3.2 and developed further in §5.3, demonstrates that the same speaker shifts among forms—including "neutral" *anta* and "masculine" *omee*, as well as masculine first-person pronouns—"depending on the context (e.g., interlocutors, speech act types)," with the result that the same form "does not straightforwardly index 'masculinity' (or 'femininity') but rather conveys a variety of context-dependent meanings, including solidarity/distance, emotional engagement, and resistance to the dominant gender and sexual norms." The Itakura volleyballer ethnography exhibits the parallel pattern for gay male practice: *onē-kotoba* is deployed selectively as a "tactical performance" rather than as constant background register, and its use is calibrated to the specific dynamic of the volleyball scene, which negotiates "between discretion and disclosure" within an environment where both Japanese family-oriented norms and Euro-American "out-and-proud" politics figure as competing frames.[^22]

**Third**, queer speakers' practice often draws on **community-internal registers** that the prescriptive system does not authorize and that the metapragmatic apparatus does not officially recognize. *Onē-kotoba* is the paradigm case for gay male communities, but lesbian communities have developed their own community-specific registers as well, and the entanglement of lesbian community formation with shoujo manga and feminist cultural production documented by Welker suggests that lesbian linguistic resources have long been continuous with the broader shoujo register.[^24][^22] These community-internal resources function precisely as the alternative indexical fields that the inadequate prescriptive system fails to supply—but their availability depends on community access, and queer speakers in less densely networked locations or community positions may have correspondingly less direct exposure to them, with mediatized registers (including shoujo manga and BL) potentially serving as substitute or supplementary sources.

The implication for the present study is decisive. The framework's prediction that mediatized registers will be especially valuable to speakers whose face-to-face linguistic environment fails to supply adequate stylistic resources gains particular force in the queer Japanese case because the inadequacy is **structural rather than incidental**. It is not that queer speakers happen to lack good models in their immediate environment; it is that the prescriptive system itself is built in such a way that no model it supplies can be adequate without active stylistic reworking. Under such conditions, shoujo manga's diversified *yakuwarigo*—its hyper-feminine and hyper-masculine poles, its *bishōnen*-inflected and *otokoyaku*-inflected configurations, its yuri- and BL-coded queer narratives—becomes not merely one resource among many but an unusually well-positioned source of indexical alternatives, especially for queer young adults whose access to community-internal registers may be more limited than that of older or more centrally networked queer speakers.

## 5.3 Documented Linguistic Practices of Lesbian, Gay, Transgender, and X-Gender Speakers

The empirical record on queer Japanese linguistic practice, while uneven in its coverage of subpopulations, supplies the documented baseline against which the empirical analyses of subsequent chapters must be calibrated. This section synthesizes what is known across the major subpopulations, with explicit attention to the unevenness of coverage and to the methodological limits of the available studies.

The most systematically developed body of work concerns **lesbian linguistic practice**, principally through Hideko Abe's *Queer Japanese: Gender and Sexual Identities through Linguistic Practices* and her associated ethnographic articles. Abe's monograph constitutes one of the few book-length treatments of queer Japanese linguistic practice and, as Janet Shibamoto-Smith's review notes, "is a treasure trove of information about the language practices—and language attitudes—of sexual minority speakers of Japanese, an understudied group of speakers, who are critically important to a realistic understanding of the gendered qualities of Japanese."[^21] Laura Miller's endorsement of the book frames its contribution as "transcending narrow models of Japan's linguistic landscape" by providing "a richly detailed and ethnographically grounded account of the language resources used by sexual minorities," with implications for "the role of language in identity construction, taking us past worn stereotypes to an appreciation of the diversity found among lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender and transsexual speakers."[^21] The volume's table of contents indicates chapter-length treatments of "Lesbian Bar Talk," "Cross-dressing Speech: The 'Real' Womanhood of Men," "Performing the Performative in the Theater of the Queer," and "Queen's Speech and the Playful Plundering of Women's Language" alongside its private-matter examination of queen's speech, indicating a structural commitment to treating the diverse queer Japanese population as a heterogeneous set of subgroups rather than as a single community.[^21]

Abe's empirical findings on Shinjuku lesbian bar speakers, already cited in §3.2 and §5.2, can be further specified here. Speakers in this setting "use 'masculine' expressions extensively, but not exclusively," and the relevant forms include "neutral" *anta*, "masculine" *omee*, and masculine first-person pronouns, deployed flexibly across contexts. The interpretive consequence Abe draws is that lesbian speakers' use of *ore* "does not straightforwardly index 'masculinity' (or 'femininity') but rather conveys a variety of context-dependent meanings, including solidarity/distance, emotional engagement, and resistance to the dominant gender and sexual norms." This formulation is theoretically rich because it specifies multiple, partially independent indexical activations of the same form: solidarity (with co-present community members), distance (from heteronormative femininity), emotional engagement (in moments of affective intensification), and resistance (as a positioned political stance). A given utterance may activate one of these or several simultaneously, and the activation depends on interlocutor configuration, speech act type, and stylistic project.

The empirical record on **gay male linguistic practice** is most fully developed for the *onē-kotoba* register, with Maree's *queerqueen* providing the principal book-length treatment and Itakura's 2023 ethnography of Tokyo amateur gay volleyballers supplying the most detailed contemporary case study.[^25][^22] Itakura's analysis, already partially summarized in §5.1, yields several findings critical for the present study. First, *onē-kotoba* persists as a community-internal resource despite—or partly because of—its problematic mediatized status: the register "remains firmly entrenched in Japanese media-representations of gay male characters despite its alleged rejection by actual gay men as well as its problematic characterization as being disrespectful to women," and Itakura's volleyballers neither uniformly reject nor uniformly embrace it but instead deploy it tactically.[^22] Second, the register's deployment is "**tactical**" rather than habitual: Ni-chōme volleyballers "swing between discretion and disclosure by fashioning language(/gender), such tactical performance of *onē-kotoba* lubricates an aesthetically pro-silence erotic play in tension with Japan's – retrospectively and arguably – family-oriented, if not homophobic, sociocultural orientation resistant to 'out-and-proud' activism."[^22] Third, the register operates within a distinctive sociopolitical frame that Itakura situates against "the Euro-American resistance-minded queer theory," explicitly resisting the assumption that Japanese queer linguistic practice can be read through Anglophone categories.[^22] The combined picture is one in which *onē-kotoba* functions as a community-recognized resource whose deployment is selective, politically negotiated, and oriented toward a "pro-silence aesthetics" that contrasts sharply with the visibility politics often presumed in Anglophone queer linguistic scholarship.

It bears emphasis, in line with §4.5, that the *onē-kotoba* register **does not coincide with the *bishōnen* register of shoujo manga and BL**. Where *onē-kotoba* deploys explicitly feminine pronouns (*atashi*) and feminine SFPs (*wa*, *no*, *kashira*, *wayo*) in male-bodied speech, the *bishōnen* configuration deploys masculine *boku* with softened or neutral SFPs. Gay male speakers therefore have, in principle, access to two distinct queer-coded registers from different sources: the community-internal *onē-kotoba* tradition, with its complex mediatized history and its political negotiations, and the shoujo/BL *bishōnen* register, with its different indexical loadings and its different histories of reception. Whether and how individual speakers draw on one, the other, or both is an empirical question whose answer is likely to depend on community access, media engagement, and stylistic project—precisely the variables that subsequent chapters will need to disentangle.

The empirical record on **transgender and X-gender linguistic practice** is comparatively underdeveloped in the directly available sources for this report. The Routledge-published article surveyed under the title "Indexicality of grammar: The case of Japanese transgender speakers," cited in *The Oxford handbook of language and sexuality*, indicates that transgender linguistic practice has been theorized within the indexicality framework that this report's overall framework adopts.[^25] The "Challenging norms of gender and language" article in which this citation appears suggests an established line of work treating transgender speakers' grammatical choices as indexical rather than as straightforward expressions of gender identity, in keeping with the broader framework articulated in Chapter 2.[^25] Without more detailed access to the underlying findings, the present study can register only that transgender and X-gender speakers face distinctive structural problems within the binary system: a transgender woman's choice of *watashi* may be read as confirming or contesting her gender identity depending on hearer presuppositions; a transgender man's choice of *boku* or *ore* operates within the same indexical field but with different stakes than for cisgender speakers; an X-gender speaker faces the absence of any pronoun whose default loading aligns with a non-binary self-presentation, with *jibun* offering the most ambiguity-as-strategy potential among the available forms but at the cost of its relatively narrow pragmatic range.

A useful synthesis appears in the broader literature on Japanese language education and inclusion. The Routledge article on "Sustainable Practices of Diversity and Inclusion" in Japanese language education frames the field's growing engagement with "Japanese language education and the intersections of gender and sexuality" and emphasizes "the importance of critiquing" prescriptive frameworks that erase non-normative speakers.[^25] The "Girls Shouldn't Use Rough Language?" article on diverse views of Japanese contemporary speech foregrounds "identity construction in an LGBT youth group" and "identity construction and agency" as key themes in current sociolinguistic work on Japanese gender, signaling that the field has begun to treat queer speakers' agency and identity work as central to understanding the broader system rather than as marginal cases.[^28] Maree's contribution to language and gender studies is similarly framed in *queerqueen: Linguistic Excess in Japanese Media*, the title of which signals the analytic centrality of stylized, performative, and excessive linguistic practice to the study of queer Japanese language—a framing that complements the third-wave commitment to treating style and performance as constitutive rather than ornamental.[^25]

The cross-cutting pattern that emerges across these subpopulations is one of **selective, context-sensitive, and politically negotiated deployment** of forms drawn from the prescriptive registers, community-internal registers, and (as Chapter 4 anticipated and Chapters 7–9 will examine) mediatized registers. No queer Japanese subpopulation is well described as simply using "feminine" or "masculine" forms; all are documented to deploy forms across the binary, with the specific configurations indexing solidarity, distance, emotional engagement, resistance, tactical disclosure, or community alignment depending on the situation. The framework of Chapter 2 makes this pattern theoretically tractable: queer speakers are stylistic agents engaged in continuous bricolage with the indexical resources available to them, and the variability of their practice is not noise to be controlled away but signal to be analyzed. The empirical baseline against which manga-related patterns will be measured is therefore not a fixed "queer linguistic profile" but a documented range of context-sensitive practices whose configurations can be compared, on the one hand, with naturalistic majority practice (Chapter 3) and, on the other, with the staged conventions of shoujo manga (Chapter 4).

The following table summarizes the documented practices across the major subpopulations and registers, with explicit acknowledgment of the unevenness of coverage in the directly available literature.

| Subpopulation / Register | Principal documented forms | Pragmatic deployment | Coverage in available literature |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lesbian (urban, e.g., Shinjuku bar speakers) | Masculine *ore*; "neutral" *anta*; "masculine" *omee*; mixed feminine and neutral SFPs | Solidarity, distance, emotional engagement, resistance to heteronormativity; context-sensitive | Most fully developed (Abe) |
| Gay male (urban, *onē-kotoba* community) | *Atashi* and feminine SFPs (*wa*, *no*, *kashira*, *wayo*) in male-bodied speech | Tactical performance; "pro-silence aesthetics"; community recognition; selective deployment | Substantial (Maree, Itakura) |
| Gay male (broader, non-*onē* practice) | Variable; *boku* and neutral SFPs documented; *watashi* in formal | Discretion; navigation of family and workplace heteronormativity | Less systematic |
| Transgender speakers | Indexical strategies across the binary; specific configurations dependent on transition stage and identity | Indexical claim-making within ideologically loaded system | Underdeveloped in available sources (one citation to indexicality framework) |
| X-gender speakers | *Jibun* as ambiguity-as-strategy; selective deployment across the binary | Negotiation of system that supplies no neutral pronoun | Underdeveloped in available sources |

The unevenness of coverage in the rightmost column is itself a finding: it indicates both where the empirical record is strongest and where the present study's design (Chapter 6) and analyses (Chapters 7–9) must proceed with particular caution, since the baseline against which manga-related patterns are measured will be more secure for some subpopulations than for others.

## 5.4 Queer Cultural Production, Media Engagement, and Shoujo Manga's Particular Salience

The argument that shoujo manga occupies a privileged position among the media available to queer Japanese young adults rests on several converging lines of evidence: the historical entanglement of shoujo manga production and reception with feminist and lesbian community formation; the documented engagement of queer audiences with BL and yuri as resources for identity construction; and the contrasting profile of mainstream variety-show *onē*/*okama* representations, which despite their visibility carry indexical loadings substantially different from those of shoujo's *bishōnen* and queer-coded configurations.

The historical entanglement is most extensively documented in **James Welker's monograph** *Transfiguring Women in Late Twentieth-Century Japan: Feminists, Lesbians, and Girls' Comics Artists and Fans*, published in 2024, which traces the long-running interconnection of feminist activism, lesbian community formation, and shoujo manga production and reception from the 1970s onward.[^24][^22] Welker's research program, indexed by his conference papers and book projects, includes work on "Was the Women's Liberation Movement Hostile to or Fertile Ground for Lesbian Feminism in Japan?", "Lesbian Studies *Avant la Lettre*: Resituating Photographer Kiyooka Sumiko," "Reading the Liberatory Promise of a Third Gender in(to) Manga," "Defining *Yuri* Manga Fandom in Japan: Women and Men Reading and Writing About Girl-Girl Romance Media," and "From Women's Liberation to Lesbian Feminism in Japan," among others.[^24] His earlier dissertation-derived article "Beautiful, Borrowed, and Bent: 'Boys' Love' as Girls' Love in Shôjo Manga" is foundational to the current understanding of how BL functions as girls'/women's romance media within the shoujo tradition, and is widely cited as having "written perceptively on how Boys' Love manga serves to queer heterosexual practices while exploring issues of marginalized" sexualities.[^29]

Several implications follow from this historical record. **First**, queer female readership of shoujo manga is not a contemporary invention but a long-running practice with several decades of community history behind it. The lesbian community's institutional and discursive emergence in late twentieth-century Japan was, on Welker's account, partly mediated through engagement with shoujo and proto-yuri manga, with the result that the linguistic and stylistic register of shoujo has been continuously available as a community-relevant resource for at least two generations of queer female speakers. **Second**, the entanglement with feminist movements means that engagement with shoujo manga among queer women has often been politically inflected rather than purely consumptive, with reading practices entangled with broader questions about female sexuality, gender norms, and women's cultural production. Welker's framing of shoujo manga as part of a broader "transfiguration" of women's cultural and political position in late twentieth-century Japan situates manga reception within precisely the kind of identitarian cultural work that the framework of Chapter 2 anticipates would generate dense exemplar storage and stylistic uptake.[^24][^22]

The contemporary queer engagement with **BL specifically** has been most extensively documented in the transnational reception literature reviewed in §4.5 and §4.1. Although much of this literature focuses on female (heterosexual and queer) readership, several findings bear directly on queer reader engagement. Baudinette's work on Japanese BL among Chinese gay men in Japan documents that for some gay male audiences, "consumption of Japanese BL has become a 'resource of hope' allowing them to reject Chinese heteronormativity," and the more general formulation extends to Japanese contexts where BL functions for gay readers as a resource that "helps them explore their sexuality and cope with the heteronormative structure of their society."[^21] The Filipino reception research Baudinette conducted yields parallel findings: "their referential reading and 'queering' of Thai BL reflects the harsh reality of the discrimination they face in their societies," with gay audiences drawing on BL texts in ways that female audiences typically do not, producing "creative misreadings" that "open up space to subvert the conditions of heteronormativity."[^21] The Jirattikorn comparative study extends these findings, documenting that "gay male audiences read Thai BL differently" from female audiences, "tend[ing] to reference relationships in Thai BL to their own social reality" and describing the genre as making them "feel that Thai society is very much accepting of LGBTQ people," producing both identification and (in some cases) idealization.[^21]

These findings about transnational BL reception are not directly evidence about Japanese queer young adults' engagement with Japanese BL, and the cultural-political conditions of Japan differ from those of China, the Philippines, and Thailand. But the underlying mechanism that the reception literature identifies—queer readers drawing on stylized homoromantic narratives as resources for identity construction in the face of unsupportive sociocultural environments—operates in Japan as well, where the *bakkurashu* discourses noted in §5.1 and the broader heteronormative orientation Itakura's work characterizes provide a comparable, if not identical, set of pressures.[^22][^26] The framework of Chapter 2 supplies the theoretical bridge: the conditions for media influence on language specified in §2.4 include the inadequacy of face-to-face models for the speaker's identity needs, and queer Japanese young adults face precisely the kind of structural inadequacy (§5.2) that the framework predicts will make mediatized registers especially valuable.

The contrast between shoujo manga and **mainstream variety-show *onē*/*okama* representations** clarifies why shoujo's particular profile aligns with queer indexical needs in ways that other media do not. The cultural commentary cited in §4.5 notes that "many entertainers who are out use *onē* personas on TV to, well, entertain the audience. That may be the only exposure an average Japanese person has to a real-life openly gay or trans person." The variety-show *okama* character archetype "reflects how society thinks a gay man looks and acts" and is "not the willowy, androgynous *bishounen* of shoujo manga." The term *okama* itself "is a homophobic and transphobic slur." The implication is that mainstream television's principal mediatized representation of queer masculinity operates substantially through caricature and entertainment-driven excess, and while the resulting register has been reclaimed in some community-internal uses (as Maree and Itakura document), its indexical loadings carry the weight of homophobic deployment in ways that shoujo's *bishōnen* register does not.

Shoujo manga's distinctive profile, by contrast, foregrounds **emotional intimacy, gender ambiguity, and homoromantic narrative** in configurations whose indexical loadings are oriented toward romantic identification rather than entertainment caricature. The *bishōnen* configuration documented in §4.4 stages a softened, sensitive masculinity whose pronoun and SFP usage diverges from prescriptive masculinity in directions that queer male readers—and, given the long entanglement of yuri/lesbian culture with shoujo, queer female readers as well—may find available for selective uptake. The *otokoyaku*-inflected princely female protagonist supplies an analogous resource for queer female readers seeking models of female-bodied masculinity that are not caricatured. The yuri and BL subgenres extend these resources around explicitly homoromantic narratives in ways that, while not unproblematically queer (per §4.5's qualifications), nonetheless make the linguistic configurations available within affectively saturated romantic contexts whose indexical loadings the exemplar-storage account predicts will be weighted favorably.

The **affective intensification** of shoujo's linguistic conventions is the feature that, on the framework of Chapter 2, most clearly distinguishes its indexical resources from those of other media available to queer young adults. Where variety-show *okama* speech is intensified for comedic effect, shoujo speech is intensified at moments of romantic disclosure, identity disclosure, and emotional crisis—the precise moments at which reader identification with characters is most likely to consolidate into the kind of socially weighted exemplar storage the framework anticipates. Identity-salient exemplars, on the exemplar-theoretic account reviewed in §2.3, "may be disproportionately retained and disproportionately available for activation in subsequent production, even if the absolute frequency of exposure is modest." For queer readers whose own moments of romantic and identity disclosure may parallel those staged in the manga, the shoujo register's emotionally intensified configurations constitute precisely the kind of high-weight exemplar input that the framework predicts will accumulate as a stylistic resource over time.

It is worth qualifying these claims with **alternative possibilities** that subsequent chapters will need to weigh. The reception literature's documentation of "creative misreading" by international fans makes clear that audience uptake is not predictable from stylistic content alone; queer Japanese readers may engage shoujo in ways that diverge substantially from the genre's staged configurations, drawing selectively, ironically, or even resistantly on its resources. The *onē-kotoba* tradition's continued vitality in gay male communities (per §5.3) suggests that for some queer Japanese speakers, community-internal registers may carry greater identitarian weight than mediatized ones, and shoujo's *bishōnen* register may be peripheral to their stylistic projects rather than central. The heterogeneity registered in §5.1 implies that no single account of queer engagement with shoujo will adequately characterize the population; what can be said in advance of empirical investigation is that shoujo manga is **available** to queer Japanese young adults as an indexical resource whose profile aligns with their structural needs, and that the question of how that resource is taken up—if at all, in what ways, by whom, in what contexts—is precisely what the empirical analyses of subsequent chapters will pursue.

## 5.5 Stylistic Agency, Digital Communities, and Contemporary Innovation

The contemporary landscape against which manga-derived exemplars are activated is not the relatively stable face-to-face community ecology that ethnographic studies of the 1990s and 2000s documented; it is, increasingly, a digitally mediated environment in which queer Japanese young adults exercise stylistic agency across multiple platforms, networks, and registers simultaneously. This section surveys what is known about this evolving landscape with explicit attention to its implications for the empirical questions the report pursues.

The digital turn has reorganized queer community formation in Japan as it has elsewhere. The mention in *Queer Japanese Challenges to Heteronormativity Through Social Media* of "Street Corners and Hugs" as a chapter framing queer Japanese engagement with social media indicates that the field has begun to take seriously digital affordances as sites where queer linguistic practice is renegotiated.[^21] The "Girls Shouldn't Use Rough Language?" article's framing of "identity construction in an LGBT youth group" and "identity construction and agency in a study" of contemporary Japanese speech signals that scholarly attention has shifted toward the interaction of identity work, agency, and platform-mediated communication in current queer linguistic practice.[^28] These citations indicate the existence of a research line that the present study can reference but cannot independently develop; they nonetheless support the framework's assumption that digital affordances are reorganizing the indexical environment in which queer young adults make stylistic choices.

The principal implications of digital mediation for queer linguistic practice can be sketched, drawing on the framework of Chapter 2 and the broader patterns documented in adjacent sociolinguistic literature. **First**, digital platforms supply environments in which the metapragmatic ideologies that operate in face-to-face contexts are partially relaxed, allowing for stylistic experimentation that face-to-face encounters with normatively oriented interlocutors may inhibit. A queer young adult who would face social cost for using *boku* in a face-to-face encounter with a normative interlocutor may face substantially less cost for doing so in a community-aligned digital space, with the result that digital production may exhibit greater divergence from prescriptive norms than face-to-face production does. **Second**, digital platforms aggregate exposure to community-internal registers whose face-to-face access may be limited by geography, age, or social network. A queer young adult in a smaller city without direct access to Shinjuku Ni-chōme or to lesbian-feminist community networks can encounter *onē-kotoba*, *otokoyaku*-inflected speech, and shoujo/yuri/BL discourse online, supplementing or substituting for face-to-face community access. **Third**, digital platforms enable the kind of selective bricolage that the framework of Chapter 2 anticipates, since users can curate their consumption, follow specific accounts whose linguistic practice models particular registers, and engage in production that draws on multiple registers simultaneously without committing to any one as dominant.

The Hiramoto-coauthored analysis of NoFap-related discourse in Japanese contexts illustrates one slice of the digital landscape, though one whose orientation is decidedly not queer-affirmative. The analysis documents that "in Japan, the 'softness' of women's language has been understood to reflect the delicacy and submissiveness of their expected role in a patriarchal gender order," and that anti-feminist online discourses participate in the broader *bakkurashu* by reinforcing precisely the metapragmatic ideologies that queer linguistic practice contests.[^26] The same digital environment that affords queer stylistic experimentation also hosts active resistance to gender and sexual non-normativity, with the result that queer young adults' digital practice is conducted within an ideologically contested space where stylistic choices are continuously interpreted and re-interpreted by audiences whose commitments range widely. The Itakura framing of *onē-kotoba* deployment as "tactical performance" between "discretion and disclosure" extends to digital contexts as well, where the visibility and circulability of digital production raise the stakes of stylistic claim-making relative to ephemeral face-to-face speech.[^22]

The **generational dimension** of the contemporary landscape deserves particular emphasis for a study focused on young adults aged 18–35. Younger queer cohorts have grown up with digital affordances that older queer Japanese speakers did not, and the linguistic resources accessible to them differ correspondingly. Where an older lesbian speaker may have learned community register principally through face-to-face engagement in spaces such as Shinjuku bars, a younger queer speaker may have first encountered comparable resources through online forums, fan communities, social media accounts, and digitally circulated fiction—including the ongoing online reception of shoujo manga, BL, and yuri media. The implication is that the **pathways** by which queer young adults access community-internal and mediatized linguistic resources have diversified significantly relative to the pathways available to older cohorts, and the relative weights of face-to-face community access, mediatized register exposure, and digitally aggregated community participation differ across generations, regions, and individual biographies.

The "Girls Shouldn't Use Rough Language?" reference's framing of "identity construction in an LGBT youth group" suggests that contemporary research has begun to engage with these generational and digital dynamics directly, treating LGBT youth as agents whose identity construction proceeds through linguistic practice that draws on multiple resources simultaneously.[^28] The Routledge "Sustainable Practices of Diversity and Inclusion" article's framing of the "intersections of gender and sexuality" in Japanese language education complements this by indicating that institutional contexts have begun, however unevenly, to engage with the linguistic implications of queer student presence.[^25] Maree's *queerqueen* and the broader corpus of work on linguistic excess in Japanese media, complemented by Hiramoto's work on language and sexuality in the *Journal of Language and Sexuality*, provide the contemporary scholarly infrastructure within which the present study's empirical questions are posed.[^25][^26]

A further dimension of the contemporary landscape that subsequent chapters will need to weigh concerns the **interaction among multiple media genres** in queer young adults' indexical environments. Shoujo manga is one genre among several that queer young adults consume, and the stylistic resources it supplies are activated within a broader indexical field populated by BL, yuri, anime adaptations, light novels, drama CDs, fan fiction, social media discourse, and (for some) shōnen and seinen registers as well. The framework of Chapter 2 allows for this complexity by treating each genre as a curated catalogue of indexical possibilities, with readers assembling stylistic projects from the fragments that each catalogue supplies. The empirical implication is that any observed correlation between shoujo manga consumption and pronoun or SFP usage must be interpreted within a broader media ecology in which shoujo's contribution is one stream among several, and the methodological design of Chapter 6 must accordingly attend to other media exposures as comparators or controls. The discussion of Chapter 9 will return to this complexity when weighing competing causal pathways for any patterns the analyses of Chapters 7–8 reveal.

## 5.6 Synthesis: Queer Young Adults as Informative Speaker-Type for the Study

The chapter's findings converge on a characterization of queer Japanese young adults as an unusually informative speaker-type for the central empirical questions the report pursues. The analytical case can be stated as four interlocking claims, each grounded in the analyses of preceding sections.

**First, structural mismatch with the prescriptive binary creates analytic leverage.** The over-determination of pronoun and SFP choices with binary indexical content tied not only to gender but to presumed sexual orientation, documented through Lunsing's and Maree's work in §3.5 and elaborated in §5.2, means that queer speakers cannot use the prescriptive system's resources without already taking a position with respect to it. Their linguistic practice is therefore continuously, structurally engaged with the indexical fields of the relevant variables in ways that majority cisgender heterosexual speakers' practice typically is not. This continuous engagement produces denser, more context-sensitive, more metapragmatically aware practice, and any patterns of media-derived stylistic uptake should be more clearly visible in this population than in one for which the prescriptive system already supplies adequate options.

**Second, documented engagement with shoujo manga and its subgenres provides plausible exposure pathways.** The historical entanglement of shoujo manga with feminist and lesbian community formation documented by Welker, the sustained engagement of queer audiences with BL and yuri documented in the BL studies literature, and the contrasting profile of shoujo's *bishōnen* and gender-crossing registers relative to mainstream variety-show *onē*/*okama* representations together establish that shoujo manga is not an arbitrary or marginal media object for queer Japanese young adults but a long-standing, community-relevant, and indexically appropriate resource.[^24][^22][^29][^21] The framework's prediction that mediatized registers will be especially valuable to speakers whose face-to-face linguistic environment fails to supply adequate stylistic resources gains specific empirical traction in this case because the alignment between shoujo's staged configurations and the indexical needs of queer speakers is well-documented rather than merely plausible.

**Third, community-internal heterogeneity allows for fine-grained comparative analysis.** The variation across lesbian, gay, transgender, and X-gender subpopulations documented in §5.1 and §5.3, combined with the variation across community access (urban Ni-chōme volleyballers vs. less-networked younger speakers), generation (older cohorts who came of age in primarily face-to-face community ecologies vs. younger cohorts whose community access has been substantially digital), and stylistic project (those drawing principally on community-internal *onē-kotoba* vs. those drawing principally on shoujo/BL *bishōnen* configurations vs. those mixing both with other resources), supplies the within-population variation that allows manga-related patterns to be distinguished from general queer linguistic patterns. A study in which queer young adults appeared as an undifferentiated population would offer less analytic purchase than one in which their internal variation is foregrounded and used to test which patterns track which exposures.

**Fourth, stylistic agency makes them paradigmatic third-wave actors.** The third-wave variationist framework articulated in Chapter 2 treats speakers as agents who deploy variables to "make ideological moves" rather than as bearers of demographic indices. Queer Japanese young adults, whose every act of self-reference is already a move within an inadequate ideological system, are paradigmatic third-wave actors whose practice illuminates the indexical workings of the broader sociolinguistic system with unusual clarity. Their linguistic choices are not deviant deployments of a binary system but stylistic projects within an indexical field whose elaboration—through community-internal registers, mediatized registers including shoujo manga, and emerging digital community formations—the present study aims to document and theorize.

These analytical commitments specify the **sociolinguistic baseline** against which manga-related patterns will be measured in subsequent chapters. That baseline is not the prescriptive system, since queer practice is structurally already in tension with it; it is not naturalistic majority practice, since queer practice differs from majority practice for reasons internal to the queer position; it is not a fixed "queer linguistic profile," since the heterogeneity of the population precludes any such fixed profile. The baseline is, rather, the **documented range of context-sensitive practices** surveyed in §5.3, against which manga-related patterns can be tested for their distinctive contributions. The empirical analyses of Chapters 7–9 will need to ask not whether queer young adults differ from cisgender heterosexual speakers (a question that earlier work has already answered in the affirmative), but whether higher shoujo manga consumption is associated with **specific configurations** that align with the manga register's documented conventions, distinguishable from the configurations that other queer linguistic resources—community-internal registers, alternative media, digital community participation, individual stylistic agency—would predict.

The chapter has also made explicit the **alternative explanations** that subsequent chapters must weigh. These include, at minimum: (i) **community-mediated transmission**, by which queer young adults acquire stylistic resources from face-to-face or digital community engagement rather than from manga directly; (ii) **other media genres**, including BL and yuri beyond shoujo proper, anime adaptations, light novels, drama, and digital media, whose linguistic conventions partially overlap with but are not identical to shoujo's; (iii) **digital community influences**, by which platform-specific registers may shape practice in ways that aggregate across multiple sources; (iv) **identity-driven self-selection**, by which queer young adults already inclined toward non-default gender configurations may consume shoujo manga *because* its configurations resonate with pre-existing preferences, with manga consumption operating as a downstream marker of identity orientation rather than as an upstream cause of linguistic practice; and (v) **deliberate stylistic appropriation** of community or mediatized registers as identity-performative moves, distinguishable from exemplar-driven imitation. The empirical design of Chapter 6 must accordingly include measures and instruments capable of distinguishing among these pathways, and the discussion of Chapter 9 must engage them seriously as alternatives or co-determinants of any patterns the analyses reveal.

The methodological implications of the chapter's findings can be summarized in the following table, which links each analytical finding to the corresponding requirement for the empirical design of Chapter 6.

| Analytical finding from Chapter 5 | Implication for empirical design |
|---|---|
| Internal heterogeneity along gender identity, generation, region, community affiliation | Stratified or sufficiently large sampling allowing within-population comparison |
| Inadequacy of binary system makes practice reflexive and metapragmatically aware | Instruments allowing both production data and metalinguistic commentary |
| Community-internal registers (*onē-kotoba*, lesbian bar register, etc.) are partially independent resources | Survey of community participation and register exposure as covariate of manga consumption |
| Shoujo manga's salience emerges within a broader media ecology | Survey of media consumption across genres (shoujo, BL, yuri, shōnen, variety, etc.) |
| Digital community participation reshapes pathways of register access | Survey of platform participation and digital community engagement |
| Self-selection and identity-driven consumption are live alternative explanations | Design that allows correlational findings to be interpreted with causal humility; ideally longitudinal or comparative components |
| Context-sensitivity of practice requires multi-context production data | Instruments eliciting production across formal/informal, in-group/out-group, written/spoken contexts |

The chapter has, in this sense, completed its preparatory task. Chapters 3 and 4 specified what the prescriptive system supplies and withholds, and what shoujo manga stages and circulates; the present chapter has specified who the speakers are, what their documented relation to both the prescriptive system and the manga register has been, and why the conjunction of these factors makes them an unusually informative population for the central questions the report pursues. The framework of Chapter 2 supplied the theoretical vocabulary; Chapters 3, 4, and 5 have populated it with the empirical specifics that make the framework's predictions testable. With this empirical foundation now in place, the report turns to Chapter 6, which articulates the research design and methodology through which the predictions will be operationalized and the patterns interrogated, before the empirical analyses of Chapters 7–9 examine the production, perception, and contextual data that the design generates.

# 6 Research Design and Methodology

The five preceding chapters have built a layered analytical apparatus: a theoretical framework integrating third-wave indexicality, exemplar storage, mediatization, and *yakuwarigo* (Chapter 2); a structural account of Japanese gendered grammar and the documented prescription–production–perception gap (Chapter 3); a characterization of shoujo manga as a curated, modular catalogue of indexical possibilities (Chapter 4); and an account of queer Japanese young adults as an unusually informative speaker-type whose practice is structurally engaged with the indexical fields the prescriptive system saturates (Chapter 5). What remains is the methodological work of converting these theoretical commitments and empirical baselines into a concrete research design capable of generating data that can test the report's predictions—and, equally importantly, of distinguishing manga-driven exemplar accumulation from the alternative pathways (community-mediated transmission, parallel media exposure, identity-driven self-selection, deliberate stylistic appropriation) that Chapters 2 and 5 flagged as live competitors. The present chapter undertakes this methodological work. It articulates the paradigm and rationale for a mixed-methods design (§6.1), specifies operational definitions for the study's three central constructs (§6.2), details the recruitment and sampling strategy (§6.3), describes each of the four data-collection instruments in turn (§§6.4–6.7), specifies the coding schemes that will render production data analyzable (§6.8), articulates the triangulation and analytic strategy that will integrate the four streams (§6.9), addresses the ethical dimensions of research with a vulnerable population (§6.10), and inventories the design's limitations together with the mitigation strategies adopted (§6.11).

A note on the chapter's relation to the report's empirical record is in order at the outset. Because the literature directly available for this report is concentrated in sociolinguistic theory, Japanese gender ideology, and shoujo manga corpus work, the methodological precedents on which the design draws come substantially from these traditions—Labovian elicitation as adapted by subsequent variationists, Eckert's third-wave ethnographic-stylistic analysis, exemplar-theoretic perception experimentation, Unser-Schutz's manga corpus methodology, Abe's queer ethnography, and (for the comparator stream of mixed-methods media-language scholarship) the available example of Howe's mixed-methods sociolinguistic analysis of electronically-mediated communication, which models several relevant elements: stratified-purposive sampling on a focal social variable, multi-instrument data collection, normalization across uneven corpora, inter-coder reliability procedures, and explicit treatment of methodological limitations.[^30] Where the present design extends beyond what these precedents directly support, the chapter says so, and frames its design choices as theoretically motivated rather than empirically pre-validated.

## 6.1 Research Paradigm and Mixed-Methods Rationale

The design adopted in this study is a **convergent-and-sequential mixed-methods design** in which quantitative and qualitative streams are collected in parallel where feasible and in sequence where one stream requires the output of another, and in which the streams are integrated at the analytic stage through systematic triangulation. The justification for mixed methods is not pragmatic but theoretical: the layered framework of Chapter 2 specifies four kinds of phenomena—distributional patterns of variable use, the indexical fields that organize variable meanings, the cognitive storage of socially weighted exemplars, and the mediatized circulation of stylized configurations—each of which calls for a different kind of evidence. A study restricted to any one method would fail to address one or more of these phenomena and would correspondingly under-test the framework's predictions.

The argument can be specified in four steps that map directly onto the framework's strata. **First**, distributional patterns of pronoun and SFP use across speakers, contexts, and consumption strata are the kind of phenomenon that requires **quantification**. Whether higher shoujo manga consumption is associated with denser deployment of *boku* among female-bodied participants, or with selective preservation of stereotype-level feminine SFPs, or with the particular pronoun-SFP configurations the manga register stages, are questions whose answers depend on counting tokens across systematically sampled production data and assessing the strength of association against null distributions. The Labovian variationist tradition supplies the methodological vocabulary—sociolinguistic variables, conditioning factors, normalized frequencies, multivariate models—that this stratum requires, and the mixed-methods precedent in Howe's electronically-mediated communication study illustrates how stratified-purposive sampling, multi-context corpus assembly, and frequency-based correlational analysis can be coordinated within a single design.[^30] The variationist commitment to quantification, however, must be qualified by an important methodological move: because the corpora collected from individual participants will inevitably differ in size across instruments, contexts, and participants, **normalization** procedures (typically rates per 1,000 tokens or per opportunity-bearing context, as Howe specifies) will be required before cross-participant or cross-context comparisons can be meaningfully drawn.[^30]

**Second**, the indexical fields that organize variable meanings are not directly observable in distributional data; they require **ethnographic and metalinguistic** access. As Eckert's third-wave framework specifies, knowing that a queer speaker uses *boku* in a particular context tells us about distribution but not about which region of *boku*'s indexical field the speaker is activating. Discriminating among activations of androgyny, modesty, intellectual seriousness, alignment with shoujo *bishōnen* heroes, or some bricolage among them requires interview data in which speakers reflect on their own usage, situate their choices within personal stylistic projects, and articulate the indexical claims they intend or recognize. The methodological precedent here is the third-wave ethnographic tradition that runs from Eckert's Belten High and Palo Alto studies through subsequent work by Mendoza-Denton, Bucholtz, and others, complemented by the queer-ethnographic precedents of Abe's Shinjuku bar work, Itakura's Ni-chōme volleyballer ethnography, and Maree's analysis of *queerqueen* performance—precedents whose attentiveness to community-internal register politics and tactical performance the present design seeks to honor.

**Third**, the cognitive storage of socially weighted exemplars and the corresponding **listener-side perception of indexical loadings** require experimental access. Distributional data and metalinguistic interview commentary together cannot tell us how a given pronoun-SFP configuration is heard by listeners drawn from different social positions; for that, controlled stimulus presentation paired with rating-scale or forced-choice response is necessary. The matched-guise tradition initiated by Lambert and elaborated by subsequent perception researchers, including critical refinements addressing the prototypicality of stimuli noted in the linguistic-perception literature on incongruity and non-prototypical talkers,[^30] supplies the relevant methodology, and §6.7 will specify how the design adapts this tradition to the particular requirements of a Japanese-gendered-language case in which configurations rather than single forms are the meaningful stimulus units.

**Fourth**, the mediatized circulation of stylized configurations is best characterized through **corpus-comparative** analysis, not collected within the participant data stream itself but drawn from the existing corpus literature on shoujo manga (principally Unser-Schutz's work) and on naturalistic conversation (drawing on the corpus references summarized in Chapter 3, including Okamoto's, Sturtz Sreetharan's, Matsumoto's, and Sunaoshi's empirical baselines). The participant data will be interpreted against these external baselines rather than against a baseline assembled de novo, and §6.8 will specify how the coding schemes are designed to enable direct comparison across the participant corpus and the published reference corpora.

The integration of these four strata into a coherent design requires a specific commitment to **triangulation logic**, articulated in §6.9. The commitment is not that all streams will yield convergent findings—on the contrary, the prescription–production–perception gap documented in §3.6 predicts that production and perception streams may yield divergent findings, with listener evaluations activating prescriptive indexical fields even where speaker production has moved beyond them—but that **divergences across streams are themselves theoretically informative** rather than threats to validity. Where production data show queer participants using *boku* with softened SFPs and interview data show them framing this usage as an alignment with shoujo *bishōnen* exemplars, while perception data show non-queer Japanese listeners hearing the same configuration as straightforwardly "masculine" or "tomboyish," the divergence between speaker intention and listener reception is precisely the kind of finding that the indexical-field framework predicts and explains.
