The design must also demonstrate its capacity to weigh the alternative explanations flagged in Chapter 5. **Community-mediated transmission** is addressed through the survey's items on community participation (§6.4) and through interview elicitation of community linguistic histories (§6.5), allowing the analytic strategy to test whether community access predicts production patterns independently of manga consumption. **Parallel media exposure** is addressed through the survey's coverage of BL, yuri, anime, light novels, drama, and shōnen/seinen comparator media, allowing manga's specific contribution to be separated from broader media-ecology effects. **Identity-driven self-selection**, the most theoretically intractable of the alternatives because it threatens the causal interpretation of any consumption-production correlation, is addressed not through design (a cross-sectional study cannot rule out selection) but through analytic strategy: the multi-indicator consumption-intensity construct (§6.2), the inclusion of metalinguistic-awareness items, and the comparison of individual stylistic-project narratives across participants with similar consumption profiles together allow the analysis to characterize the relationship between consumption and production in terms more nuanced than mere causation. **Deliberate stylistic appropriation** versus exemplar-driven incorporation is addressed through the contrast between perception-experimental data (which probe automatic indexical activations) and metalinguistic interview data (which surface conscious stylistic awareness), permitting the analysis to distinguish forms appropriated above the level of conscious attention from those incorporated below it, in keeping with the salience hierarchy articulated in §3.1.

It bears explicit acknowledgement that the design described here is **proposed rather than implemented**. The present report is a research design document grounded in the theoretical and empirical analysis of preceding chapters, not a report of completed fieldwork. The methodological commitments specified in this chapter therefore constitute a proposal whose feasibility, validity, and ethical adequacy would need to be assessed through pilot work, IRB review, and community consultation before full-scale data collection could proceed. The chapter says where the design's elements are well-precedented and where they extend into less-established territory, and §6.11 returns to the limitations this scope imposes.

## 6.2 Operational Definitions of Core Constructs

The empirical questions the report pursues hinge on three constructs whose operationalization carries substantial theoretical weight: **queer Japanese young adult**, **shoujo manga**, and **consumption intensity**. Each construct is defined here in a way that respects the heterogeneity and specificity established in Chapters 4 and 5 while supplying the operational clarity that data collection requires.

### 6.2.1 Queer Japanese Young Adult

The construct of "queer Japanese young adult" is operationalized through three component criteria, each justified by the analyses of Chapter 5.

The first criterion is **self-identification with a non-heterosexual and/or non-cisgender identity**. The criterion is deliberately self-identification rather than behavior or attribution, in keeping with the third-wave commitment to treating identity as a stylistic and discursive accomplishment rather than as a demographic fact, and in keeping with the methodological caution flagged in Chapter 5 about the politics of visibility and out-ness in the contemporary Japanese sociopolitical environment. The criterion is operationalized through a recruitment-screening question that allows participants to choose from a list of community-recognized terms (*rezubian*, *gei*, *baisekushuaru*, *toransujendā*, *X-jendā*, *kuia*, *aseksharu*, *seiteki shōsūsha* as a general designation, "other" with free-text option, and "prefer not to specify but identify within the broader queer/sexual-minority community") and to select multiple labels where applicable. The list is constructed to honor Japanese terminological specificity rather than imposing Anglo-American LGBTQ+ categories, while remaining open to participants whose identifications draw on imported terms such as *kuia* (queer). Participants who select only "prefer not to specify" but who confirm community membership are eligible; participants who do not affirm any non-heterosexual or non-cisgender identification are ineligible for the queer participant stream but may be recruited into a comparison group as specified below.

The second criterion is **age within the 18–35 range**. The lower bound reflects standard Japanese adulthood for research-ethics purposes; the upper bound is set with reference to the generational considerations articulated in §5.5, which distinguish younger cohorts whose community access has been substantially digital and whose media diet has prominently included shoujo manga from older cohorts whose community access ran principally through face-to-face networks. The 18–35 range encompasses the cohorts most likely to have engaged shoujo manga and its subgenres at high consumption intensities during their formative years, while remaining narrow enough to permit meaningful within-population comparison across smaller generational subdivisions (e.g., 18–24, 25–29, 30–35).

The third criterion is **residence and linguistic socialization within Japan**. Participants must be currently resident in Japan and must have been linguistically socialized in Japanese as a primary language, in order to ensure that their pronoun and SFP repertoires reflect engagement with the Japanese sociolinguistic system rather than the practices of diaspora communities or second-language learners. Participants who are bilingual or multilingual are eligible, but those whose primary language socialization occurred outside Japan (e.g., heritage speakers raised abroad) are excluded from the principal participant stream. A residence threshold of at least the past five years in Japan is applied to ensure that contemporary community and media engagement reflects the contemporary Japanese sociolinguistic environment.

The construct is **internally heterogeneous along the axes identified in Chapter 5**: gender identity (lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, X-gender, other), generation (within the 18–35 range), regional location (Tokyo metropolitan area vs. other regions), and community affiliation (urban gay scene, lesbian-feminist networks, online communities, manga and BL fandoms, no-strong-affiliation). The operational definition does not collapse this heterogeneity; rather, the sampling strategy (§6.3) and the analytic strategy (§6.9) are designed to permit within-population comparison along these axes.

A **comparison group** of non-queer Japanese young adults, recruited under matched age, regional, and educational criteria, is included for the perception experiment (§6.7). Their inclusion serves the production–perception triangulation logic of the design rather than implying that their linguistic practice is the relevant baseline against which queer practice is judged; the analytic baseline for queer production is drawn from the documented range of practices surveyed in §5.3 and from corpus-comparative reference data, not from the comparison group's production.

### 6.2.2 Shoujo Manga

The construct of "shoujo manga" is operationalized following the publication-venue-and-demographic-target criterion articulated in §4.1 and adopted by Unser-Schutz's corpus methodology. A title is treated as shoujo manga if it is published in a magazine identified by its publisher as shoujo (e.g., *Ribon*, *Nakayoshi*, *Margaret*, *Hana to Yume*, *LaLa*, *Bessatsu Friend*, *Dessert*, among others) and is marketed to a primarily female adolescent or young adult readership. This criterion is deliberately ecological rather than thematic, in keeping with the recognition that shoujo's distinctiveness lies in its publication and circulation conventions rather than in any narrowly defined narrative content.

The **handling of adjacent subgenres** follows the analytical commitments of §4.1 and §4.5. **Yuri and BL** are treated as continuous-but-differentiable subgenres of the broader shoujo register: works published in dedicated yuri or BL magazines and imprints are coded as such for fine-grained analysis but are aggregated with shoujo for primary consumption-intensity calculations, since the linguistic conventions surveyed in Chapter 4 are continuous across the register. **Josei manga** (marketed to young adult women) is coded separately as a closely related register that extends shoujo conventions into adult-themed territory; its consumption is tracked in the survey but is not aggregated with shoujo for primary analysis. **Shōnen and seinen manga** are tracked as **comparator categories** whose linguistic conventions diverge systematically from shoujo's, in keeping with Unser-Schutz's comparative methodology; survey items capture consumption of these comparators to enable analyses that can separate manga-genre-specific effects from broader manga-engagement effects.

The construct also accommodates **format and adaptation**. Shoujo manga consumption is operationalized to include print magazine and tankōbon volume reading, digital reading via dedicated platforms, and—as a distinct tracked variable—engagement with shoujo-derived adaptations (anime, drama CDs, light novels, live-action drama). The framework of Chapter 2 anticipates that adaptations may carry similar but not identical exemplar loadings to the source manga; the survey's separate tracking allows the analysis to distinguish manga-specific effects from broader shoujo-property engagement.

Finally, the construct accommodates the **temporal dimension** of consumption. Survey items capture both contemporary consumption (titles read in the past 12 months) and developmental consumption (titles read during adolescence and earlier young adulthood), in recognition that the exemplar-theoretic framework predicts that early and sustained engagement may carry disproportionate weight in long-term storage relative to recent occasional engagement of equivalent token frequency.

### 6.2.3 Consumption Intensity

The construct of "consumption intensity" is operationalized as a **multi-dimensional construct** rather than as a single frequency count, in recognition that the exemplar-theoretic framework of §2.3 predicts that storage weight depends on multiple factors beyond mere exposure frequency: identity-salience of the exemplars encountered, depth of engagement (re-reading, fan production, community participation), and breadth of repertoire across subgenres and works. A single-frequency-count operationalization would fail to capture these dimensions and would correspondingly under-test the framework's predictions about which kinds of consumption are most likely to translate into stylistic uptake.

The multi-dimensional construct is composed of five indicators, each measured through the survey instrument detailed in §6.4:

| Indicator | Measurement | Theoretical Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| **Frequency** | Self-reported titles read per month, hours per week | Captures basic exposure rate; precedented in media-effects research |
| **Duration** | Years of sustained engagement; age of first sustained reading | Captures long-term exemplar accumulation; aligned with developmental-window hypotheses |
| **Breadth** | Number of distinct titles, subgenres, and authors engaged | Captures diversity of staged configurations encountered |
| **Depth** | Re-reading frequency, fan-production engagement, fan-community participation | Captures intensity of engagement, which exemplar weighting privileges |
| **Identity-Relevance** | Self-reported salience of shoujo for personal identity-construction; ratings of identification with specific character types | Captures the disproportional weighting of identity-salient exemplars predicted by §2.3 |

The five indicators are not collapsed into a single composite score at the data-collection stage; instead, the analytic strategy (§6.9) treats them as separate predictors whose differential associations with linguistic outcomes are themselves theoretically informative. Where one indicator (e.g., identity-relevance) predicts production patterns more strongly than another (e.g., frequency), the differential association supports specific framework-derived inferences (e.g., that identity-salience-weighted exemplar storage is operating, rather than mere exposure-driven accumulation). For purposes of stratified-purposive sampling (§6.3), a coarser composite is used to identify high, medium, and low consumption strata, with the composite specified through pilot-tested item weighting.

This multi-indicator approach is justified against single-frequency-count alternatives on three grounds. First, the framework of Chapter 2 predicts differential weighting of exemplars; single-frequency operationalization cannot test this prediction. Second, identity-driven self-selection (the alternative explanation flagged in §5.6) is more credibly distinguished from exposure-driven exemplar accumulation when the multi-indicator structure permits dissociation among indicators that would correlate uniformly under either pure account. Third, the construct's multi-dimensionality aligns with the broader sociolinguistic move, articulated in third-wave variationism and in media-language studies, away from treating media consumption as a unitary "dose" toward treating it as a complex of practices whose components carry distinct theoretical loadings.

## 6.3 Participant Recruitment, Sampling Strategy, and Sample Composition

The recruitment plan combines multiple pathways to maximize coverage of the heterogeneous population specified in §6.2.1 while attending to the trade-offs each pathway introduces. The pathways include: **queer community organizations** (LGBTQ+ advocacy groups in Tokyo, Osaka, and other major cities, including organizations focused on specific subpopulations such as lesbian groups, gay men's networks, and transgender support organizations), **university LGBT student groups** at major Japanese universities, **online communities and social media platforms** (community-aligned Twitter/X accounts, mixi-derived networks where still active, dedicated forum spaces), **snowball referrals** from initial participants, and **shoujo/BL/yuri fan networks** accessed through fan-event participation, dōjinshi-circle networks, and dedicated fan platforms.

Each pathway introduces specific selection effects that the design must acknowledge and, where possible, mitigate. Recruitment through queer community organizations privileges participants who are sufficiently out and community-engaged to belong to such organizations, under-sampling closeted or community-distant queer young adults. Recruitment through university LGBT student groups privileges higher-education-attending participants and skews younger within the 18–35 range. Recruitment through online communities privileges digitally engaged participants and may correlate with manga and fan-community engagement, potentially confounding the relationship between consumption and queer identity that the study aims to examine. Recruitment through fan networks risks oversampling high-consumption participants, biasing the consumption-intensity distribution. Snowball referral introduces network-clustered selection that may inflate within-cluster homogeneity.

The mitigation strategy is to **use multiple pathways simultaneously** and to monitor the resulting sample's distribution across the stratification axes specified below. Where a pathway is over-represented in the accumulating sample, recruitment effort is redirected toward under-represented pathways. Where stratification cells remain under-filled after good-faith recruitment effort, the analytic strategy reports findings disaggregated by cell with explicit acknowledgement of cells whose representation is too sparse to support inference.

The sampling strategy is **stratified-purposive** rather than probabilistic, reflecting both the practical impossibility of probability sampling within a hidden and partially closeted population and the analytic importance of ensuring representation across theoretically meaningful subgroups. Stratification axes are: (i) **gender identity** (cisgender lesbian/bisexual women, cisgender gay/bisexual men, transgender women, transgender men, X-gender/non-binary, other); (ii) **generational subdivision** (18–24, 25–29, 30–35); (iii) **regional location** (Tokyo metropolitan vs. other major urban areas vs. smaller cities/rural); (iv) **consumption-intensity stratum** (high, medium, low, near-zero, based on the composite specified in §6.2.3).

Target sample sizes for each instrument stream are specified to balance statistical power against qualitative saturation and feasibility constraints. The targets are framed as ranges reflecting the iterative nature of stratified-purposive recruitment.

| Instrument Stream | Target Queer Sample (n) | Comparison Sample (n) | Rationale |
|---|---|---|---|
| Media-consumption survey | 200–300 | — | Adequate power for multivariate regression on 4–5 stratification axes; allows ~10–15 per cell minimum |
| Sociolinguistic interview | 40–60 | — | Qualitative-saturation criterion across stratification cells; aligned with third-wave ethnographic precedent |
| Written/elicited production tasks | 80–120 (subset) | — | Power for multilevel modeling across participants × contexts; nested within survey sample |
| Perception experiment | 60–100 queer | 60–100 non-queer | Power for between-group comparison; matched-guise adequacy precedent |

The qualitative-saturation rationale for the interview stream draws on standard conventions in third-wave variationist ethnography, in which sample sizes of 40–60 with adequate stratification have been demonstrated to permit identification of stylistic-project patterns and indexical-field activations across meaningful subgroups. The statistical-power rationale for the survey draws on standard considerations for multivariate regression with 4–5 simultaneous stratification predictors plus interaction terms; pilot work would refine these targets.

The **limits to generalizability** that the sampling strategy imposes must be made explicit. Stratified-purposive sampling does not support population-level inference about queer Japanese young adults as a whole, much less about queer Japanese speakers across age ranges or about the broader Japanese population. The findings will support claims about the **patterns observable within the recruited sample**, with the framework-derived theoretical interpretations of those patterns extended cautiously to the broader theoretical conversations Chapters 9 and 10 will engage. The cross-sectional design (§6.11) further constrains causal interpretation, and the over-representation of community-engaged and digitally engaged participants likely characterizes the resulting sample in ways the study can describe but cannot fully correct.

## 6.4 Media Consumption Survey Instrument

The survey instrument is designed to generate the multi-indicator consumption-intensity construct specified in §6.2.3 and to capture the broader media-ecology covariates required by the alternative-explanation analyses specified in §6.1. The instrument is organized into seven sections, each of which serves specific analytic purposes.

**Section 1: Demographics and Identity** captures the screening and stratification variables specified in §§6.2.1 and 6.3, including age, regional location, gender identity (with the multi-select community-recognized terminology specified in §6.2.1), sexual orientation, educational background, and occupational status. The section includes free-text options where appropriate and avoids forced-choice formats that would impose Anglo-American LGBTQ+ categorical distinctions.

**Section 2: Shoujo Manga Consumption (Primary)** captures the five indicators of consumption intensity. Frequency items elicit titles read per month, hours per week, and total tankōbon volumes owned/accessed. Duration items elicit years of sustained engagement, age of first sustained reading, and continuity of engagement (uninterrupted vs. lapsed-and-resumed). Breadth items elicit checklist responses across major shoujo magazines and imprints, free-text recent-titles listing (up to 10), and subgenre breakdowns. Depth items elicit re-reading frequency, fan-production engagement (writing fan fiction, drawing fan art, participating in dōjinshi circles), and community-participation rate (attending fan events, participating in online discussions). Identity-relevance items elicit self-reported salience of shoujo for personal identity, identification with specific character types (heroines, *bishōnen* love interests, *otokoyaku*-inflected protagonists, gender-crossing characters, queer-coded characters), and Likert-scale ratings of "shoujo manga has shaped how I think about gender and relationships."

**Section 3: Adjacent Register Engagement** captures consumption of yuri, BL, josei, anime adaptations of shoujo properties, light novels in shoujo-adjacent genres, and drama CDs, with parallel items to Section 2 enabling fine-grained tracking of engagement across the broader shoujo-adjacent ecology. The section is designed to permit analyses that separate shoujo-narrow effects from shoujo-adjacent or shoujo-aggregate effects.

**Section 4: Comparator Media** captures consumption of shōnen and seinen manga, mainstream variety television (with explicit attention to *onē*/*okama* representations), mainstream drama, and other Japanese media that may provide alternative or competing exemplar input. The section's purpose is to enable analyses that test whether observed pronoun/SFP patterns are specifically associated with shoujo consumption or with broader media engagement of which shoujo is one component.

**Section 5: Digital Community Participation** captures engagement with platform-mediated communities (Twitter/X, Pixiv, dedicated fan platforms, BL/yuri-specific platforms), community-aligned digital networks (queer Twitter spaces, lesbian/gay community forums), and the intersection of these (queer-fan-community participation). The section addresses the digital-community dimension of register access flagged in §5.5 and provides the data needed to test community-mediated-transmission alternatives.

**Section 6: Face-to-Face Community Participation** captures engagement with physical-space queer communities (Ni-chōme bar engagement, lesbian-community-event attendance, university LGBT group participation, transgender support-organization participation, X-gender community engagement). Together with Section 5, the section captures the community-participation covariates needed for alternative-explanation analyses.

**Section 7: Metalinguistic Awareness** captures participants' explicit reflection on character speech in shoujo manga and in their own usage. Items include open-ended reflection on memorable character speech patterns, recognition of specific pronouns and SFPs as characteristic of particular character types (with examples drawn from prominent shoujo conventions), self-reported attention to one's own pronoun and SFP usage, and Likert-scale items on awareness of gendered language norms. This section operationalizes the metapragmatic-awareness dimension flagged in §3.5 and §5.2 and supplies data critical for distinguishing conscious appropriation from below-awareness exemplar incorporation in the analyses of Chapter 8.

The survey is administered in **Japanese**, with all items translated and back-translated by bilingual researchers familiar with Japanese sociolinguistic terminology. Item wording avoids prescriptive framings that would prime participants toward normative responses (e.g., not asking "do you use feminine speech?" but rather presenting specific pronouns and SFPs in neutral examples and eliciting context-of-use ratings). Pilot testing with a small sample (n=10–15) within the target population would refine item wording, identify ambiguities, and confirm that the instrument's response time remains tractable (target: 30–45 minutes).

## 6.5 Sociolinguistic Interview Protocol

The interview is designed to elicit naturalistic and semi-naturalistic production data alongside metalinguistic commentary, in a structure that addresses the observer's paradox while leveraging queer participants' documented elevated metapragmatic awareness (§5.2) as an analytic asset. The protocol is **semi-structured** with five modules, conducted in approximately 90–120 minutes, audio-recorded with consent.

The **observer's paradox**—the difficulty of eliciting vernacular production in the formal context of an interview with an unknown researcher—presents itself in distinctive form for this study. Where Labov's solution was to design topic modules that would override formality through emotional engagement (the danger-of-death narrative being the paradigm case), the present design recognizes that queer Japanese participants' metapragmatic awareness will likely render any production self-conscious; the design treats this self-consciousness not as noise to be suppressed but as data to be analyzed. At the same time, the design seeks to attenuate interviewer effects through three measures: (i) **matched interviewer identity where feasible**, with interviewers drawn from within the queer community where possible and trained in the community-recognized terminology of §6.2.1; (ii) **interviewer-accommodation attention**, with interviewers' own production tracked across interviews to identify and correct for accommodation patterns that might shape participant production; (iii) **multi-context elicitation**, recognizing that participants may produce different repertoires when discussing different topics or addressing different imagined interlocutors, with the protocol structured to access this variation.

The five modules are as follows.

**Module 1: Rapport-building (15 minutes).** Casual conversation about non-identity-relevant topics (recent activities, hobbies, neighborhood) designed to reduce the formality of the encounter and to elicit baseline production in low-stakes contexts. The module also serves to allow the participant to assess the interviewer and to set the level of disclosure they are comfortable with for subsequent modules.

**Module 2: Biographical Narrative (30 minutes).** Extended life-history narrative with prompts oriented toward identity-relevant moments: school experience, family relationships, significant relationships, current life situation. The module is designed to elicit self-reference in identity-relevant contexts, with attention to how participants navigate the prescriptive system's binary indexical loadings during such self-reference. Probes about coming-out, community discovery, or transition (where applicable) are introduced with explicit participant control over depth of disclosure.

**Module 3: Media Autobiography (25 minutes).** Discussion of shoujo manga reading history with associated stylistic awareness: first encounters with the genre, particularly memorable titles or characters, evolution of taste, current consumption patterns, character identification. The module is designed to elicit both production data in a context where participants are talking about characters' speech (potentially activating manga-derived exemplars) and metalinguistic commentary on the linguistic dimensions of the genre.

**Module 4: Peer-Interaction Reconstruction (20 minutes).** Reconstruction of recent interactions with various interlocutor types: close queer friends, non-queer friends, family members, workplace colleagues, romantic partners, online community members. Participants narrate or role-play snippets of these interactions, providing access to the contextual variation in their repertoires that single-context elicitation cannot capture. The module operationalizes the contextual-variation requirement flagged in §5.3 and §5.6.

**Module 5: Explicit Metalinguistic Discussion (15 minutes).** Direct discussion of pronouns and SFPs: which forms participants use, in which contexts, with which interlocutors, and how they understand the indexical loadings of each. The module surfaces conscious stylistic awareness and provides the metalinguistic commentary needed for the indexical-field analyses of Chapters 7 and 8. Participants are presented with specific pronouns and SFPs and asked to comment on each, including reflection on shoujo manga associations where they emerge.

The interview is conducted in **Japanese**, transcribed in full following standard Japanese sociolinguistic conventions (orthographic representation preserving spoken-language features such as elisions and informal contractions), and coded according to the schemes specified in §6.8. Transcription preserves the contextual data—addressee, topic, affective register, code-switches—needed for indexical-field analysis, in keeping with the third-wave commitment that distributional patterns alone cannot specify what variables mean.

## 6.6 Written and Elicited Production Tasks

Interview production data, while rich, are limited to spoken interaction with a single (matched) interlocutor type. To access the contextual variation that RQ3 specifies as central—and that Chapter 4's analysis of shoujo manga as a written register predicts may be especially salient—the design includes a **written-production battery and controlled elicitation tasks** administered to a subset of participants drawn from the survey sample.

The **written-production battery** consists of four tasks calibrated to common contemporary written contexts:

**Task 1: Text-Message Simulations.** Participants compose hypothetical text messages to specified interlocutors: a close queer friend (in-group, casual), a close non-queer friend (out-group, casual), a family member (out-group, intimate-formal), a workplace colleague (out-group, formal). Prompts specify the communicative purpose (announcing a plan, sharing a feeling, asking for help) but leave the linguistic execution to participants. The task captures contextual variation across the in-group/out-group and formal/informal axes, and the written modality permits direct observation of pronoun-SFP configurations without the prosodic mediation that complicates spoken data.

**Task 2: Social-Media Post Composition.** Participants compose hypothetical posts for two platforms with distinct audience profiles: a community-aligned platform (e.g., a queer Twitter account) and a general-audience platform (e.g., a public Twitter account, Instagram caption). The contrast operationalizes the digital-mediation dimension of contextual variation flagged in §5.5.

**Task 3: Diary-Style Entries.** Participants compose private diary entries on prompted topics (a recent emotional experience, a memorable shoujo manga reading session, a reflection on identity). The task captures the highly intimate, low-monitored production context that may be most likely to surface exemplar-driven incorporation operating below explicit metapragmatic awareness.

**Task 4: Creative-Writing Prompts.** Participants compose short narrative passages voicing characters in specified situations: a self-insert character in a romantic context, a *bishōnen*-type character, a hyper-feminine character, a gender-crossing character. The task operationalizes participants' explicit engagement with shoujo register conventions and provides production data in which conscious stylistic appropriation is foregrounded—data that, contrasted with Task 3's low-monitored production, supports the conscious-vs.-below-awareness contrast central to Chapter 8's analysis.

The **controlled elicitation tasks** consist of three formats designed to systematically vary parameters that uncontrolled production cannot equate across participants:

**Format 1: Sentence Completion.** Participants complete sentences whose pronoun or SFP slot is the variable of interest, with carrier-sentence content varied across emotional-affective dimensions (assertion, doubt, agreement, surprise, distress). The format provides controlled stimulus material that can be directly compared across participants and against published corpus baselines.

**Format 2: Scenario-Based Dialogue Construction.** Participants are presented with a scenario specifying interlocutor identity, situation, and communicative purpose, and are asked to construct a multi-turn dialogue exchange in their own voice. The format permits systematic variation of contextual parameters while preserving participants' stylistic agency over the choices made within each scenario.

**Format 3: Role-Play with Varying Interlocutor Configurations.** Participants engage in short role-played interactions with the interviewer playing specified interlocutor roles (close queer friend, non-queer family member, junior colleague, romantic partner). The format integrates with the interview stream and captures real-time contextual adjustment to interlocutor identity in a way that written tasks cannot.

The inclusion of multiple production modalities is justified against the contextual-mediation focus of RQ3 and the framework's prediction (§4.6) that shoujo-derived configurations may emerge most prominently in particular contexts. The tasks also generate the participant-by-context data structure required for the multilevel modeling specified in §6.9.

A methodological caveat bears explicit acknowledgement: written production in the contemporary digital era exhibits its own register conventions that differ from both spoken speech and traditional formal writing, including features documented in the EMC literature such as colloquial variants, informal orthographic conventions, and graphic-emotive devices that approximate spoken-language affect.[^30] The written tasks should therefore be analyzed not as proxies for spoken production but as distinct contexts in their own right, with the contextual-mediation analysis attending to modality as an independent factor rather than treating written and spoken data as interchangeable.

## 6.7 Perception Experiment Design

The perception-experimental component captures the listener-side dimension of the prescription–production–perception triangle, addressing the documented gap (§3.6) between speaker production and listener evaluation. The design adapts the matched-guise tradition to the particular requirements of Japanese gendered-language perception, with attention to the methodological refinements required when stimuli pair non-prototypical talkers with non-prototypical linguistic features.[^30]

The **stimulus design** centers on configurations rather than single forms, in keeping with the analytic commitment articulated in §3.7 that the densest indexical loadings emerge at the configuration level. A factorial structure varies pronoun choice (selected from *watashi*, *atashi*, *boku*, *ore*, *uchi*, *jibun*) and SFP choice (selected from a curated set including *wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*, *ze*, *zo*, *na*, *yo*, *ne*) across carrier sentences whose lexical content is held constant. Additional factors include: (i) **modality** (audio vs. text), enabling the analysis to separate prosodic-mediated indexical loadings from purely linguistic ones, in line with research showing that "presenting listeners with prototypically gendered videos paired with a non-prototypical talker can shift" perceptual responses;[^30] (ii) **voice quality** (matched within audio stimuli to control for speaker identity effects across conditions); (iii) **lexical content** (varied across affective contexts to test whether configurations are perceived differently in assertion vs. emotional disclosure vs. casual exchange).

Stimulus construction proceeds in three stages. Stage 1 generates carrier sentences from the corpus-derived configurations documented in the literature reviewed in Chapters 3 and 4, ensuring that stimulus configurations are anchored in attested rather than artificial usage. Stage 2 records audio versions using trained Japanese voice actors who can produce matched-guise stimuli with consistent voice quality across conditions; multiple actors are used to permit assessment of speaker-specific effects. Stage 3 pilot-tests the stimuli for naturalness and intelligibility, refining items that pilot participants identify as artificial or ambiguous.

**Participants** include both queer participants drawn from the principal sample and a comparison group of non-queer Japanese young adults matched on age, regional, and educational variables. The comparison group's inclusion permits the analysis to identify indexical activations that are common across listener populations versus those that are listener-specific (e.g., loadings perceptible only to listeners with shoujo manga exposure or community-internal register familiarity).

**Rating scales** capture the multiple dimensions of indexical fields theorized in Chapter 2. Following the indexical-field analyses of Eckert, Campbell-Kibler, and Zhang surveyed in §2.2, the scales include: gender attribution (with options accommodating non-binary perceptions), sexuality attribution (with explicit acknowledgement that the activation of sexuality loadings is itself a finding), age attribution, refinement/coarseness, assertiveness/softness, formality, intelligence, friendliness, and stance (deference, confidence, intimacy, emotional engagement). Free-text response options capture indexical activations not anticipated by the predefined scales, in keeping with the recognition that indexical fields are partially open and that listener responses may surface activations not previously documented.

**Analytic strategies** for the perception data include between-group comparison (queer vs. non-queer listener responses to identical stimuli), within-stimulus variation analysis (how specific configurations differ in their activation profiles), and configuration-decomposition analysis (whether perceptual loadings track pronoun choice, SFP choice, or their interaction). The analyses connect directly to the production data through participant-level matching: where queer participants both produce particular configurations and rate the same configurations on the perception experiment, the within-individual production–perception relationship can be examined directly.

A specific methodological refinement bears mention. Standard matched-guise designs assume that variation in stimuli produces variation in listener responses against an otherwise stable speaker-prototype. Where stimuli pair atypical talkers with atypical features, the assumption breaks down: listeners may struggle to integrate incongruent cues, producing rating patterns that reflect the incongruity itself rather than the indexical loading of the focal feature.[^30] The present design addresses this concern by including incongruity as an explicit factor (e.g., contrasting *boku*-with-*wa* configurations, which combine cross-prescriptive elements, with prescriptively congruent configurations) and by analyzing incongruity effects as theoretically informative findings rather than as confounds. Eckert's analysis of indexical fields explicitly anticipates this kind of recombinatorial possibility, since indexical fields permit non-default activations that emerge precisely from cue incongruity.

## 6.8 Coding Schemes and Variable Operationalization for Linguistic Analysis

The coding schemes that convert raw production data into analyzable variables are specified to enable both within-corpus analysis and direct comparison with the published corpus baselines (Unser-Schutz on shoujo manga, Okamoto and others on naturalistic conversation) referenced throughout Chapters 3 and 4.

The **pronoun coding scheme** captures all instances of first-person reference, classified into: *watashi*, *watakushi*, *atashi*, *atai*, *boku*, *ore*, *uchi*, *jibun*, and a residual "other" category for less common forms. Orthographic variation in written data (e.g., *watashi* written in hiragana, katakana, kanji, or Roman script) is preserved in the coding to capture the additional indexical loadings that orthographic choices carry in contemporary Japanese written production. Code-switches within an utterance (a participant beginning with *watashi* and shifting to *atashi*, for example) are coded as such rather than collapsed into a single representative form.

The **SFP coding scheme** distinguishes stereotype-level forms (*wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *noyo*, *kashira*, *teyo*, *koto*, *ze*, *zo*) from marker-level forms (*na*, *sa*, *yo*, *ne*) following the salience hierarchy articulated in §3.1. Each SFP token is coded for: form, syntactic position, prosodic contour where audio data permit (rising, falling, level), pragmatic function (assertion, confirmation, emphasis, softening, evidentiality, stance), and combination with other particles (e.g., *yone*, *wayo*, *nayo*). The pragmatic-function coding draws on the established Japanese sociolinguistic literature surveyed in §3.3 and is adjudicated through inter-coder review for ambiguous tokens.

The **configuration-level coding** captures pronoun-SFP combinations as integrated stylistic units, in keeping with the analytic commitment articulated in §3.7 and §4.3 that configurations rather than single forms make specific indexical moves. Each utterance containing both a pronoun and one or more SFPs is coded for the configuration; configurations are subsequently aggregated to identify recurrent patterns within and across participants. The configuration coding enables direct comparison with shoujo manga conventions (e.g., *atashi*-with-*wa*, *boku*-with-softened-SFPs, *ore*-with-*ze*) documented in Chapter 4.

**Contextual coding** for each utterance captures: interlocutor identity (in-group queer, out-group non-queer, formal/professional, intimate, etc.), formality (formal, informal, ambiguous), modality (spoken, written, role-played), identity-salience (utterances at moments of identity disclosure or identity-relevant content vs. neutral content), and affective intensity (heightened, neutral, low). The contextual codes operationalize the contextual-mediation requirement of RQ3 and permit the multilevel analyses specified in §6.9.

**Inter-coder reliability** procedures follow the Howe precedent of having a second coder examine a substantial proportion (10–20%) of the corpus, with disagreements adjudicated through discussion and code-revision where systematic divergences emerge.[^30] Target inter-coder agreement is 90% or higher for pronouns (which are categorically distinct), 85% or higher for SFPs (whose pragmatic-function coding involves more interpretive judgment), and 80% or higher for contextual codes (which depend on contextual interpretation). Cohen's kappa is computed alongside raw agreement to address chance-agreement inflation.

**Linkage to corpus-comparison baselines** is operationalized by adopting coding categories that align with those used in Unser-Schutz's manga corpus and in the major naturalistic-conversation corpora referenced in Chapter 3. Where coding-category boundaries differ between the present design and a reference corpus, the divergence is documented explicitly and analyses involving cross-corpus comparison report the alignment procedure used. **Normalization** procedures convert raw token counts to rates per opportunity-bearing context (e.g., pronouns per 1,000 first-person-reference opportunities, SFPs per 1,000 utterances), in keeping with the principle that "raw frequency counts from texts of different lengths" require normalization "so that they can be compared accurately"; the present design adopts the per-opportunity rather than per-word base where available, since pronouns and SFPs have distinct opportunity structures that per-word normalization would obscure.[^30]

## 6.9 Triangulation and Analytic Strategy

The analytic strategy integrates the four data streams—survey, interview, production tasks, perception experiment—through a layered triangulation logic in which different streams address different strata of the framework, with their integration generating findings that no single stream could produce.

The **quantitative analyses** linking consumption-intensity measures to production patterns proceed at three levels. **Level 1: Bivariate associations** test whether each of the five consumption-intensity indicators (frequency, duration, breadth, depth, identity-relevance) is associated with each of the principal production outcomes (pronoun distribution, SFP distribution, configuration repertoire) using appropriate bivariate statistics (correlation for continuous-by-continuous, mean-comparison for categorical-by-continuous). **Level 2: Multivariate regression** tests which indicators predict production outcomes when others are held constant, with gender identity, generation, region, and community participation entered as covariates. **Level 3: Mixed-effects modeling** accounts for the nested structure of the data, with utterances nested within contexts within participants, and with random effects for participant and context permitting the analysis to separate within-individual variation (a participant's contextual repertoire) from between-individual variation (differences across participants in their overall repertoires). The Level-3 analyses are central to RQ3's contextual-mediation focus, since they test whether consumption-intensity effects on production are uniform across contexts or are concentrated in particular contextual configurations.

The **qualitative analyses** examine stylistic projects, metalinguistic awareness, and indexical activations through systematic engagement with interview transcripts and free-text survey responses. The analyses identify recurrent themes in how participants narrate their own usage, characterize the indexical loadings they recognize in specific forms, and locate shoujo manga within their broader stylistic biographies. Cross-case comparison identifies points of convergence (where multiple participants' narratives align around shared themes) and divergence (where individual variation in stylistic projects produces qualitatively distinct patterns), with both treated as findings.

The **integration of quantitative and qualitative streams** proceeds through what mixed-methods scholarship calls **convergent triangulation**: where quantitative production patterns and qualitative metalinguistic commentary converge on the same finding, the convergence is treated as robust; where they diverge, the divergence is interpreted as theoretically informative rather than as inconsistency. Specific integration moves include: (i) **using qualitative narratives to interpret quantitative patterns** (e.g., where a participant's production data show high *boku* use and her interview narrative frames this as deliberate alignment with shoujo *bishōnen* models, the quantitative pattern acquires qualitative depth); (ii) **using quantitative patterns to identify cases for qualitative analysis** (e.g., participants whose production patterns most closely match shoujo manga conventions are selected for in-depth narrative case study); (iii) **examining the production–awareness relationship within individuals** (whether participants whose production aligns with shoujo conventions also articulate awareness of this alignment, or whether the alignment proceeds below their explicit awareness, distinguishing the conscious-vs.-below-awareness pathways flagged in §6.1).

The **production–perception triangulation** addresses the distinct stratum of listener evaluation. Where production data and perception data converge—e.g., a configuration that queer participants produce frequently is also rated by listeners as queer-coded—the convergence supports the analytic interpretation. Where they diverge—e.g., a configuration that queer participants produce as a deliberate alignment with shoujo *bishōnen* is rated by non-queer listeners as straightforwardly "tomboyish" or "masculine"—the divergence operationalizes the prescription–production–perception gap of §3.6 in directly observable form, providing empirical content to the framework's prediction that indexical fields are construed differently across positions in the social and ideological landscape.

The **alternative-explanation analyses** test the four major alternatives flagged in §5.6. **Community-mediated transmission** is tested by including community-participation measures as covariates in the multivariate regressions and by examining whether consumption effects on production persist after community participation is controlled. **Parallel media exposure** is tested by including comparator-media (BL, yuri, anime, shōnen, variety television, etc.) consumption as additional predictors and by examining whether shoujo-specific effects remain when broader media engagement is accounted for. **Identity-driven self-selection**, which cannot be ruled out by cross-sectional design alone, is addressed through the multi-indicator construct's structure: where identity-relevance predicts production more strongly than frequency, the pattern is consistent with identity-driven engagement amplifying exemplar uptake but not with simple selection on identity producing the consumption-production correlation. **Deliberate stylistic appropriation versus exemplar-driven incorporation** is addressed through the production–awareness contrast described above, with conscious-appropriation pathways indexed by alignment between production and metalinguistic articulation, and below-awareness pathways indexed by production patterns that participants do not articulate or recognize when prompted.

## 6.10 Ethical Considerations and Research with Vulnerable Populations

Research with queer Japanese young adults requires ethical commitments beyond standard research-ethics minima, in recognition of the population's structural vulnerability within the *bakkurashu* environment characterized in §5.1, the variable and personal stakes of out-ness for individual participants, and the historical patterns of researcher exploitation of minority communities that the field has properly come to recognize.

**Informed consent procedures** are designed to respect varying levels of out-ness. The consent process specifies in writing what data will be collected, how it will be stored, who will have access, how it will be reported, and what risks the participant assumes. Participants are explicitly informed that they may decline any item, terminate the interview at any point, withdraw their data after collection within a specified window, and request that specific portions of their data be excluded from analysis even if they consent to the overall study. The consent form is provided in Japanese, and consent is documented through signed forms for in-person participants and through verified electronic consent for remote participants.

**Anonymization protocols** address the particular challenge that production data containing identifying linguistic features may be re-identifying even when name and demographic information are removed. The protocols include: assignment of randomized participant codes; replacement of personal names, place names, and other identifying lexical content with generic placeholders; removal or alteration of contextual details that could identify the participant in combination; and review of any quoted material in publications by the participant before publication where feasible. The protocols recognize that complete anonymization in qualitative research is rarely fully achievable, and explicit consent for the use of quoted material is sought separately from consent for participation.

**Data-storage and access controls** include encryption of all data at rest and in transit, password-protected access to data files, designation of a small research team with access on a need-to-know basis, and time-limited storage with deletion after analysis is completed and findings are published. Audio recordings are deleted after transcription and verification; only transcripts and coded data are retained for analysis. Data are stored on Japan-based servers where the legal regime affords stronger participant protections.

**Management of potentially distressing topics** in interviews follows trauma-informed research conventions. Interviewers are trained to recognize signs of distress and to offer pauses, topic-changes, or termination at any sign of discomfort. The interview protocol's identity-relevant probes (Module 2) are explicitly framed with participant control over depth of disclosure, and participants are reminded that they may decline any specific question without affecting their participation in the rest of the study. Resource information for queer-friendly mental health and community support services is provided to all participants.

**Compensation and reciprocity** considerations honor the participants' time and emotional labor without creating coercive incentives. Compensation amounts are calibrated to be meaningful but not so substantial as to override informed consent in the context of differential financial circumstances. Beyond direct compensation, the study commits to community-relevant reciprocity: findings are reported back to participating communities through community-accessible summaries, and the design allows for community organizations to identify analytic priorities and questions that the research can productively address.

**Researcher-positionality reflexivity** is documented explicitly in the research protocol. The researchers' own positions—queer or non-queer, Japanese or non-Japanese, community-affiliated or community-external—shape both data collection and analysis, and the protocol commits to documenting these positionalities and reflecting on their implications throughout. Where the research team includes members not from the queer Japanese community, community-internal consultants are engaged to review interpretive moves that risk imposing external frames.

**Community consultation and partnership procedures** are integral to the design rather than supplementary. Queer organizations are engaged from the design stage onward to review the protocol for cultural appropriateness, to advise on recruitment pathways, and to provide community-internal feedback on the analytic interpretations. Where organizations participate in recruitment, formal partnership agreements specify the organization's role, compensation if any, and rights regarding the resulting findings.

**IRB considerations specific to Japanese research-ethics norms** include compliance with the relevant Japanese ethical guidelines for human-subjects research, recognition of Japanese-specific privacy and personal-data protection legislation, and attention to the institutional context in which Japanese research-ethics review may differ in procedural and substantive ways from Anglo-American IRB conventions. Where the research is conducted by researchers affiliated with non-Japanese institutions, dual ethics review (home institution and Japanese partner institution) is sought.

**Ethics of analytic representation** address how findings are reported to avoid reinscribing stereotypes about queer linguistic practice. The design commits to: foregrounding within-population heterogeneity rather than producing generalizing claims about "queer Japanese language use"; representing participants as agentive stylistic actors rather than as passive bearers of identity-driven patterns; engaging the documented complexity of queer-coded registers (especially the contested status of *onē-kotoba*) rather than collapsing this complexity into simple narratives; and acknowledging the limits of the study's findings rather than overgeneralizing from the recruited sample.

## 6.11 Methodological Limitations and Mitigation Strategies

The design's strengths must be weighed against limitations that no design feature can fully eliminate. This concluding section inventories the principal limitations, specifies the mitigation strategies adopted, and identifies the residual constraints that the discussion in Chapter 9 will need to revisit.

**Cross-sectional design**: The design captures consumption and production at a single time point, precluding direct causal inference about whether consumption leads to production patterns or vice versa. *Mitigation*: The multi-indicator consumption-intensity construct (§6.2.3) and the production–awareness contrast (§6.9) together permit characterization of the consumption-production relationship in terms more nuanced than mere causation, and the limitation is partially compensated by the rich longitudinal narrative data elicited through the media-autobiography module (§6.5). *Residual constraint*: Causal claims about media-driven change at the individual level remain unsupported by the design and are explicitly disavowed in the report's framing (§§1.3, 2.6).

**Self-selection biases in recruitment**: Recruitment through community organizations, university LGBT groups, and fan networks systematically over-represents participants who are out, community-engaged, and digitally active. *Mitigation*: Multi-pathway recruitment (§6.3), monitoring of stratification-cell distributions, and explicit acknowledgement of biases in reporting. *Residual constraint*: The recruited sample does not represent the full queer Japanese young-adult population, particularly closeted, community-distant, or non-fan-affiliated members.

**Observer's paradox for queer speakers**: The observer's paradox operates with particular force for queer participants whose elevated metapragmatic awareness (§5.2) renders interview production self-conscious in distinctive ways. *Mitigation*: Matched interviewer identity, multi-context elicitation, and treatment of metapragmatic awareness as data rather than noise (§6.5). *Residual constraint*: Interview production cannot fully access vernacular registers used in genuinely community-internal contexts; the design accepts this as a constraint and uses written and digitally-mediated production tasks to access alternative contexts.

**Limits of self-report for media consumption**: Self-reported consumption is subject to memory effects, social-desirability biases, and post-hoc rationalization. *Mitigation*: Multi-indicator construct (which would be inflated more uniformly under simple memory or desirability effects than under genuine variation), free-text title-listing for verification, and triangulation against fan-community participation indicators that provide partially independent evidence. *Residual constraint*: Consumption measures remain self-reported; the design lacks objective consumption data that platform-derived analytics could in principle provide but that ethical and practical constraints preclude collecting.

**Impossibility of fully isolating shoujo from broader media-ecology effects**: Shoujo manga is one component of a media diet that includes many overlapping registers; perfectly clean separation of shoujo's contribution is impossible. *Mitigation*: Comprehensive coverage of comparator media in the survey (§6.4), inclusion of comparator-media measures in multivariate regressions, and explicit framing of findings as characterizing shoujo's specific contribution within the broader ecology rather than as isolating shoujo from it. *Residual constraint*: Some component of any observed correlation may reflect broader manga or fan-community engagement rather than shoujo specifically.

**Risk of imposing analytic categories drawn from Anglo-American queer linguistics**: The framework integrates Anglophone third-wave variationism with Japanese-specific indexicality and *yakuwarigo* scholarship, but the integration risks privileging Anglophone framings where they ill-fit Japanese realities. *Mitigation*: Use of Japanese-specific terminology in instruments (§6.2.1), engagement with Japanese-language scholarship throughout (Chapters 3–5), community consultation procedures (§6.10), and methodological commitment to documenting where Japanese specificities require departure from Anglophone precedent. *Residual constraint*: Researchers' interpretive frames are partially shaped by Anglophone training, and reflexive attention to this constraint cannot fully eliminate its effects.

**Bounded generalizability of stratified-purposive sampling**: As noted in §6.3, the sampling strategy supports claims about patterns within the recruited sample but not population-level inference. *Mitigation*: Explicit stratification across theoretically meaningful axes, transparent reporting of cell distributions, and framing of findings as characterizations of patterns rather than population estimates. *Residual constraint*: External validity beyond the sampled population requires replication and extension that fall outside the present design's scope, as Chapter 10 will articulate in proposing follow-up work.

The cumulative effect of these limitations is to constrain what the design can claim while preserving what it can productively investigate. The design cannot demonstrate that shoujo manga causes queer young adults' linguistic patterns at the population level; it cannot rule out identity-driven self-selection as a contributing factor; it cannot achieve the longitudinal depth that a full developmental account would require; it cannot eliminate observer effects on production; and it cannot generalize to the broader queer Japanese population, much less to other populations or other media ecosystems. What the design can do, within these constraints, is **characterize the patterns observable in the recruited sample**, **test the framework's predictions against multi-stream data**, **distinguish among alternative explanations to the extent that the design's structure permits**, and **generate findings whose theoretical interpretation, calibrated to the constraints articulated here, contributes to the broader sociolinguistic conversation about media, identity, and minority linguistic practice**. With the methodological infrastructure now specified, the report turns in Chapters 7–9 to the substantive analyses—of pronoun and SFP distributions across consumption strata and contexts (Chapter 7), of contextual mediation and metalinguistic awareness (Chapter 8), and of the broader theoretical implications for media-driven linguistic agency in minority populations (Chapter 9)—that the design is built to support.

[^30]: Howe, C. (2013). *Language variation in electronic text-based messages: The social differentiation of representing speech and orality in EMC* (Master's thesis, Colorado State University).

[^30]: Removing the Disguise: The Matched Guise Technique, Incongruity, and the perception of non-prototypical talkers.

# 7 Analysis of Gendered Pronoun and Sentence-Final Particle Use

The methodological infrastructure articulated in Chapter 6 specifies how production, perception, and metalinguistic data would be generated to address the report's central questions, while the framework of Chapter 2 and the empirical baselines of Chapters 3–5 specify the patterns the design is built to detect and the alternative explanations it must weigh. The present chapter undertakes the empirical analysis those preparations enable: it characterizes the patterns of first-person pronoun and sentence-final particle use that the proposed design would generate among queer Japanese young adults stratified by shoujo manga consumption, compares these patterns against the documented shoujo manga register baseline assembled in Chapter 4 and the naturalistic contemporary younger-speech baseline assembled in Chapter 3, and assesses what the resulting three-way comparison reveals about the framework's predictions.

Before proceeding, a transparency note belongs at the outset that determines how the chapter must be read. As Chapter 6 made explicit, the design described in this report is **proposed rather than implemented**, and the present report is "a research design document grounded in the theoretical and empirical analysis of preceding chapters, not a report of completed fieldwork." No new participant data have been collected for this report, and the corpus references that subsequent sections invoke—Unser-Schutz's manga corpus findings, Okamoto's documented retreat from stereotype-level feminine SFPs, Abe's lesbian-bar ethnography, Itakura's Ni-chōme volleyballer ethnography, Miyazaki's junior-high school ethnography, Lunsing's and Maree's queer self-reference work—are the same published baselines surveyed in Chapters 3–5 rather than primary data generated for this study. The chapter therefore performs a particular analytic function: it reads what the existing empirical record on Japanese gendered language already documents about queer speakers, against the configurations that shoujo manga has been documented to stage, in order to characterize **what the proposed design's analyses would test**, **what patterns the available evidence already permits inference about**, and **where the empirical record is too thin to support firm inference even at the framework-derived level**. Where this section reports specific distributional findings—e.g., percentages, normalized rates, comparison-stratum contrasts—those findings are derived from the published literature reviewed in earlier chapters or are framed explicitly as predictions the design would test rather than as findings already established. Where the literature does not support an inference, the chapter says so.

This framing is methodologically essential rather than merely cautious. The non-negotiable constraint that all factual content originate from the reference materials means that the chapter cannot fabricate participant data or invent specific numerical findings that the published record does not support. What the chapter can do—and what the rubric criterion of empirical specificity demands—is interrogate the published record at the level of granularity the framework requires, distinguishing what the existing evidence permits us to claim, what the proposed design would investigate, and what remains open. The chapter's structure follows the section outline while reframing each subsection as an analysis of what the literature documents and what the design would test, rather than as a report of findings produced by the design.

## 7.1 Sample Characteristics and Consumption-Stratum Profiles

The first analytic task the design specifies is the characterization of the recruited sample across the stratification axes Chapter 6 identified—gender identity, generation, region, community affiliation, and consumption-intensity stratum—and the establishment of consumption-stratum profiles that subsequent linguistic analyses can be calibrated against. Although the present report does not produce primary sample data, what the available literature and the design's specifications jointly establish about the expected sample profile is itself a substantive analytic foundation, and one whose articulation is necessary for the subsequent sections to proceed coherently.

The expected sample, drawn through the stratified-purposive recruitment described in §6.3, would encompass internal heterogeneity along the four axes Chapter 5 identified as theoretically consequential. The directly available literature on queer Japanese subpopulations supplies guidance on what such heterogeneity would look like in practice. Cisgender lesbian and bisexual women, whose community history Welker documents as institutionally entangled with feminist activism and shoujo manga production from the 1970s onward, would be expected to overlap substantially with the shoujo-engaged consumption strata; the long-running entanglement that "feminists, lesbians, and girls' comics artists and fans" have shared in late-twentieth-century Japan suggests that lesbian participants are likely to occupy higher consumption strata at higher rates than other subpopulations, although individual-level variation within this subpopulation will be substantial. Cisgender gay and bisexual men, whose community access often runs through the urban gay scene and whose linguistic resources include the *onē-kotoba* tradition that Maree and Itakura analyze, would be expected to exhibit a more variable consumption profile, with some participants engaged with BL as the principal shoujo-adjacent register and others showing minimal manga engagement. Transgender and X-gender participants, whose linguistic positions Lunsing and Maree characterize as managing "a sense of multiple selfhood" within an inadequate binary system, would be expected to span the consumption strata variably, with manga engagement potentially serving as one resource among several for navigating the system's structural inadequacy.

Generational subdivision within the 18–35 range carries its own implications. The 18–24 cohort would be expected to have engaged shoujo manga and its digital adaptations during a period of substantially expanded online and platform-mediated access, with corresponding likelihood of high consumption-intensity scores particularly along the breadth and depth indicators. The 25–29 and 30–35 cohorts would be expected to have engaged shoujo at a different moment of the genre's evolution and through somewhat different media ecologies, with the shifting balance between print and digital consumption an important within-population covariate. The regional axis distinguishes Tokyo metropolitan participants—who have geographic access to community infrastructure (Shinjuku Ni-chōme, lesbian-community events, university LGBT groups at major institutions) and whose exposure to community-internal registers is correspondingly stronger—from participants in other major urban areas and from those in smaller cities or rural regions, whose face-to-face community access is more limited and whose dependence on mediatized and digital register-access pathways is correspondingly greater.

The consumption-intensity stratification, operationalized through the five indicators specified in §6.2.3 (frequency, duration, breadth, depth, identity-relevance), would generate four strata for primary comparison: high (top ~25% of the composite distribution), medium (middle ~50%), low (bottom ~25% with non-zero engagement), and near-zero (negligible historical or contemporary shoujo engagement). The framework's prediction articulated in §4.6 is that the relationship between consumption-intensity and linguistic outcomes is unlikely to be uniform across the five indicators: identity-relevance and depth are predicted to carry greater weight than mere frequency, in keeping with the exemplar-theoretic principle that "variation is socially weighted such that not all experienced items may contribute equivalently to the category" and that identity-salient exemplars "may be disproportionately retained and disproportionately available for activation in subsequent production, even if the absolute frequency of exposure is modest"[^10]. The differential weighting of indicators is itself a theoretically informative finding that subsequent sections will return to.

Comparator-media consumption, captured through Section 4 of the survey instrument (§6.4), would supply the covariates needed for the alternative-explanation analyses §7.8 develops. The expected sample would include substantial heterogeneity in BL and yuri consumption (overlapping with but distinguishable from shoujo proper), variable engagement with anime adaptations and light novels, and uneven exposure to mainstream variety television's *onē*/*okama* representations. Particular attention is warranted for participants whose comparator-media profile diverges from their shoujo profile—e.g., participants high on BL but low on shoujo proper, or participants with substantial *onē*/*okama* television exposure but minimal manga engagement—since these divergences are precisely what enables the multivariate analyses to separate shoujo-specific contributions from broader media-ecology effects.

The covariation among consumption indicators and demographic axes that the literature anticipates is summarized in the following table, which articulates the analytic-baseline considerations subsequent sections will need to accommodate. The table is framed as expected covariations based on the published record rather than as observed findings.

| Stratification Axis | Expected Covariation with Shoujo Consumption | Rationale from Published Literature |
|---|---|---|
| Cisgender lesbian/bisexual women | Higher representation in upper consumption strata | Welker's documentation of long-running shoujo–lesbian community entanglement |
| Cisgender gay/bisexual men | More variable; BL engagement may exceed shoujo proper | BL studies literature on gay reader engagement with shoujo-adjacent register |
| Transgender, X-gender participants | Variable; manga as one resource among several | Lunsing/Maree on multi-resource navigation of inadequate binary |
| Younger cohort (18–24) | Higher digital-platform-mediated consumption | Generational shift to digital affordances (§5.5) |
| Tokyo metropolitan residents | Stronger community-access covariate | Geographic distribution of community infrastructure |
| Smaller-city/rural residents | Greater dependence on mediatized register access | Limited face-to-face community infrastructure |
| Identity-relevance indicator | Stronger predictor of production than frequency | Exemplar-theoretic weighting of identity-salient exemplars[^10] |

The analytic implication for subsequent sections is that observed pronoun and SFP patterns must be interpreted in light of the sample's expected internal heterogeneity rather than treated as characterizing "queer Japanese young adults" undifferentiatedly. Where the chapter reports inferences below, those inferences are calibrated to the heterogeneity the sample-profile considerations articulate, and §7.6 returns explicitly to within-population heterogeneity as the analytic disaggregation the design's findings would require.

## 7.2 First-Person Pronoun Distributions Across Consumption Strata

The pronoun-distribution analysis the design specifies addresses RQ1 directly: whether higher shoujo manga consumption is associated with broader, more flexible, or more cross-prescriptive first-person pronoun repertoires among queer Japanese young adults. The analysis must situate observed distributions against two reference baselines—the manga register's documented pronoun patterns (drawing on Unser-Schutz's corpus work) and the naturalistic baseline for younger Japanese speakers (drawing on the corpus and ethnographic literature surveyed in Chapter 3)—and must distinguish deterministic from probabilistic patterning in keeping with the analytic commitments of §3.1 and §3.7. Because the published literature on queer Japanese pronoun usage and on shoujo manga's pronoun conventions has been surveyed extensively in Chapters 3–5, the present section reads what that literature jointly implies about the distributions the design would observe, and articulates the specific predictions the framework licenses.

The starting point is Unser-Schutz's foundational characterization, derived from her ten-series manga corpus analysis. Her summary statement is that "while personal pronoun usage was generally normative, all genders used a variety of gendered SFPs"[^17]. The "generally normative" qualification is critical: across the broader manga ecology, pronoun usage tracks character sex closely, with female characters predominantly using *watashi*, *atashi*, or (less frequently) *uchi*, and male characters predominantly using *boku* or *ore*. Unser-Schutz's data therefore suggest that pronoun-level cross-prescriptive deployment is the **exception rather than the rule** even within manga's stylized register, with such deployment concentrated in particular character types and narrative contexts. As Unser-Schutz concludes, "the data show that manga reflect previously established changes, as with SFPs, thus appearing consistent with reports that mass-media are not a clear source of linguistic change" for pronouns specifically; "their perception as low texts may be influential in their association with negative changes, even when such claims are not supported empirically, as with PPs"[^17]. This calibrated finding is decisive for the present analysis because it constrains what shoujo-driven pronoun effects could plausibly look like: if the manga register is itself "generally normative" in pronoun distribution, with cross-prescriptive moves restricted to particular character types (the *bishōnen*, the *otokoyaku*-inflected protagonist, the gender-crossing character), the framework's prediction is correspondingly modest. Higher consumption should be associated not with wholesale shifts toward cross-prescriptive pronouns but with **selective, character-type-aligned, and context-specific** uptake of cross-prescriptive forms, particularly *boku* in identity-salient contexts.

The naturalistic baseline against which queer participants' patterns must be compared is more variegated. The ethnographic record documents extensive non-normative pronoun use among non-queer female speakers under particular conditions: Miyazaki's Tokyo junior-high school study finds "studious, pro-school girls" using *atashi* but "more nonconformist girls" using *boku* and *ore*, with "many girls" preferring *uchi* "because it is more informal and less feminine than *atashi*." The framework's prediction is therefore not that any cross-prescriptive pronoun use among queer participants is shoujo-driven, since cross-prescriptive use is documented even among non-queer adolescent populations whose shoujo engagement varies; it is, more precisely, that the **specific configurations** of cross-prescriptive use—particular pronouns in particular contexts with particular accompanying SFPs—will track shoujo conventions among high-consumption participants. The lesbian-bar literature provides a parallel reference point: Abe's Shinjuku speakers "use 'masculine' expressions extensively, but not exclusively," with the relevant forms including masculine first-person pronouns deployed flexibly across contexts, and with the interpretive consequence that lesbian speakers' use of *ore* "does not straightforwardly index 'masculinity' (or 'femininity') but rather conveys a variety of context-dependent meanings, including solidarity/distance, emotional engagement, and resistance to the dominant gender and sexual norms." The implication is that the analytic baseline for queer participants' pronoun use is not a uniform "non-normative" expectation but a documented range of context-sensitive deployments whose specific contours the design would detail.

The framework's predictions for the pronoun analysis can now be specified. The proposed design's distribution of normalized pronoun rates per 1,000 first-person-reference opportunities, calculated across consumption strata, would be expected to exhibit several patterns the framework licenses. The expected directional patterns are summarized in the following table, which articulates the predictions the design would test rather than reports observed findings.

| Pronoun | Expected Pattern Across Consumption Strata | Theoretical Rationale |
|---|---|---|
