| *watashi* | Modest decline in raw frequency from low to high consumption strata in casual contexts; preserved in formal contexts | Cross-prescriptive bricolage selectively replaces *watashi* in identity-salient casual contexts only |
| *atashi* | Variable; potentially elevated in high-consumption strata for specific subgroups (lesbian participants engaging hyper-feminine register; gay participants engaging *onē*-coded register) | Stereotype-level form preserved by manga while majority practice retreats; selective uptake by participants engaging the relevant character types[^17] |
| *boku* | Increased use in high-consumption strata, especially among female-bodied participants, concentrated in identity-salient and creative-writing contexts | Cross-prescriptive uptake aligned with shoujo *bishōnen* and *otokoyaku* conventions documented in Chapter 4 |
| *ore* | Modest increase in high-consumption strata; selectively associated with stance-marking rather than general self-reference | Aligned with Abe's lesbian-bar findings and with Endō's earlier hypothesis about manga influence on female *ore* use[^17] |
| *uchi* | Moderately elevated across strata independent of consumption; possibly higher in younger cohorts | Reflects general younger-cohort retreat from *atashi*; not specifically manga-driven |
| *jibun* | Variable; potentially elevated in X-gender participants seeking ambiguity-as-strategy | Indexical-ambiguity-management hypothesis articulated in §5.3 |

Two specific predictions warrant particular emphasis. **First**, the framework predicts that the **identity-relevance indicator** of consumption intensity will predict cross-prescriptive pronoun use more strongly than the frequency indicator, in keeping with the exemplar-theoretic principle that identity-salient exemplars are disproportionately weighted in storage and retrieval[^10]. A participant who reports 200 titles read but rates shoujo as low-relevance for personal identity would be predicted to exhibit less cross-prescriptive pronoun use than a participant who reports 50 titles read but rates shoujo as central to identity construction. This differential pattern is theoretically informative because it dissociates exposure-driven accumulation from identity-mediated uptake, with the dissociation supporting the framework's prediction that exemplar weighting depends on identity-salience rather than on raw exposure frequency.

**Second**, the framework predicts that cross-prescriptive pronoun use will be **context-concentrated** rather than uniform across contexts. The Abe finding that lesbian speakers' *ore* use is "context-dependent" rather than constant background, combined with Itakura's documentation of *onē-kotoba* as "tactical performance," suggests that queer speakers generally—and high-consumption queer speakers specifically—are unlikely to use cross-prescriptive pronouns indiscriminately. The expected pattern is concentration of *boku* and *ore* in identity-salient contexts (peer in-group interaction, creative writing, identity-disclosure moments) and retention of normative pronouns in formal, family, and out-group contexts. This contextual concentration prefigures the analysis Chapter 8 develops in detail and connects the consumption-production correlation to the contextual-mediation findings RQ3 anticipates.

The deterministic-versus-probabilistic distinction articulated in §3.1 must also be returned to. The pronoun analysis is unlikely to identify any participant whose pronoun use is **deterministically** shoujo-aligned (e.g., a female-bodied participant who uses only *boku* across all contexts), in keeping with both the documented context-sensitivity of queer speakers' practice and the modular-bricolage principle articulated in §2.5. What the analysis is instead likely to identify is **probabilistic** patterning in which the relative rates of cross-prescriptive forms vary systematically with consumption-intensity and context, with the variation tracking the framework's predictions in degree rather than in absolute value. This probabilistic patterning is precisely what the indexical-field framework anticipates: pronouns are resources within fluid fields, not fixed indexes of identity, and their deployment is correspondingly graded rather than categorical.

A methodological caveat bears restating. The pronoun analysis depends critically on the assumption that the proposed design's elicited and naturalistic production data adequately represent participants' broader linguistic practice. The observer's-paradox considerations articulated in §6.5 mean that interview production may exhibit elevated formality relative to genuinely community-internal contexts, with the consequence that cross-prescriptive pronoun rates observed in interview data are likely to **underestimate** rates that would obtain in fully community-internal settings. The contrast between Module 4's peer-interaction reconstruction data (where participants reconstruct community-internal usage) and Module 1's rapport-building data (where production occurs in the formality of the interview itself) provides one within-design check on this caveat, but the residual constraint that no single design can fully eliminate observer effects remains a limit that any inference about pronoun patterns must respect.

## 7.3 Sentence-Final Particle Distributions Across Consumption Strata

The SFP analysis the design specifies addresses RQ2 directly: whether shoujo manga consumption correlates with the use of gendered sentence-final particles, and if so, in which particular forms and contexts. The analytic stakes are higher for SFPs than for pronouns because the published literature already establishes that **SFPs exhibit greater divergence between shoujo manga conventions and naturalistic majority practice** than pronouns do. As Unser-Schutz emphasizes, while pronoun usage in her manga corpus was "generally normative," the corresponding SFP finding is that "all genders used a variety of gendered SFPs"[^17]—a pattern she connects to broader claims about gendered SFPs in shoujo specifically when she draws attention to "sentence-final particles in manga for young girls and older women that manga are" deploying in distinctive ways[^17]. The implication is that the manga register's **distinctiveness** is concentrated in SFPs more than in pronouns, and the framework's prediction that media-derived exemplars supply stylistic resources to queer speakers is correspondingly more directly testable through the SFP analysis.

The naturalistic baseline against which queer participants' SFP patterns must be compared is well documented. As Chapter 3 established, contemporary younger women's speech is characterized by approximately 68.8% neutral SFP forms in Okamoto's data, with substantial retreat from the stereotype-level feminine inventory (*wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*, *teyo*, *koto*) that prescriptive ideology treats as the default. Women's actual speech is, in the synthesis Vranic offers drawing on Okamoto, "not feminine as is prescribed by language ideology, but neutral to moderately masculine." Sturtz Sreetharan's analyses of Kansai and Kantō men's conversations document the parallel pattern on the masculine side: "all speakers used 'masculine' forms relatively infrequently," with masculine SFPs functioning as pragmatic signals of "camaraderie, authority, anger, and so on" rather than as constant background features. The naturalistic baseline therefore exhibits **bilateral retreat from stereotype-level forms**, with both feminine and masculine forms operating at lower densities than the prescriptive ideology implies and at much lower densities than the manga register sustains.

The framework's prediction for queer participants' SFP distributions is that high-consumption participants will exhibit **selective preservation of stereotype-level SFPs** that real-world peers have substantially abandoned, with the preservation concentrated in identity-salient and affectively heightened contexts. The expected directional patterns are summarized in the following table, which again articulates predictions the design would test rather than reporting observed findings.

| SFP | Expected Pattern Across Consumption Strata | Reference Comparison |
|---|---|---|
| *wa* (rising) | Elevated in high-consumption strata, particularly in affectively intensified or stylized contexts | Sustained in manga; substantially abandoned in naturalistic younger speech[^17] |
| *wayo* | Elevated in high-consumption strata; concentrated in performative or creative-writing contexts | Stereotype-level form whose recognition signals shoujo-register competence |
| *no* | Variable; possibly elevated overall but less stratum-differentiated | More resilient than other feminine SFPs in contemporary use |
| *kashira* | Selectively elevated in high-consumption strata; rare in casual production | Stereotype-level form; one of the most strongly enregistered iconic feminine SFPs |
| *teyo* | Largely confined to creative-writing or stylized-performance contexts even in high-consumption strata | Mostly archaic/caricatural in contemporary perception |
| *ze* | Moderately elevated in high-consumption strata, particularly among participants engaging *bishōnen*-rival or *otokoyaku*-inflected registers | Stereotype-level masculine form; sustained in manga |
| *zo* | Pattern parallel to *ze*; possibly more frequent in self-directed assertion | Co-distributes with *ze* in masculine *yakuwarigo* |
| *na* | Likely elevated across strata regardless of consumption | Marker-level form already common in younger speech across genders |
| *sa* | Likely elevated across strata regardless of consumption | Marker-level form heavily used in contemporary youth speech[^17] |

Two analytic distinctions articulated in §3.1 carry substantial weight here. **First**, the salience hierarchy of indicator–marker–stereotype, in Silverstein's reframing, predicts that the **conscious, identity-performative deployment** of stereotype-level SFPs (*wa*, *wayo*, *kashira*, *teyo*, *ze*, *zo*) will track consumption-intensity more closely than the **below-awareness deployment** of marker-level SFPs (*na*, *sa*, *yo*, *ne*). Stereotype-level forms are objects of overt metapragmatic attention, are densely staged in shoujo manga's *yakuwarigo*, and are correspondingly the forms whose appropriation is most likely to involve conscious stylistic choice. Marker-level forms are deployed below explicit attention and are less likely to vary systematically with consumption-intensity even where the underlying exemplar storage may be media-mediated. The framework's prediction is therefore not that all SFP use will track consumption uniformly but that **stereotype-level deployment will track consumption while marker-level deployment will be relatively consumption-independent**.

**Second**, the multi-meaning indexicality of SFPs documented in §3.3—the principle that "speakers observed in these studies all use stereotypically gendered expressions to convey not only femininity or masculinity but also a variety of other pragmatic meanings (e.g., solidarity, relative power)"—means that distributional analysis alone cannot fully characterize the pragmatic work that SFPs perform. A queer participant who deploys *wa* in a particular utterance may be activating any of multiple regions of the indexical field anchored by *wa*: feminine-affective stylization, ironic or playful stance-marking, alignment with a specific shoujo character type, in-group community signaling, or some bricolage among them. The distributional analysis of §7.3 therefore needs to be read together with the configuration analysis (§7.4) and the strategic-deployment analysis (§7.7) to recover the indexical-field activations the distributional patterns alone cannot specify.

Particle combinations warrant specific attention. The combinations *yone*, *wayo*, *nayo*, and *kashira-ne*—where multiple particles co-occur in clause-final position—carry distinct pragmatic loadings that single-particle analysis cannot capture. The framework's prediction is that high-consumption participants will exhibit not only elevated rates of single stereotype-level forms but also **denser deployment of pragmatically loaded combinations** that the manga register stages routinely but that majority naturalistic speech rarely produces. The combination *wayo*, in particular, occupies a distinctive position: it is highly characteristic of stylized feminine *yakuwarigo* (especially *ojō-sama* register), is rare in genuinely casual contemporary speech, and is recognized iconically as "manga-feminine" in metapragmatic discourse. The deployment of *wayo* in production data would therefore function as a particularly clear marker of stylistic alignment with the manga register, with its presence in queer participants' production data among high-consumption strata supporting the framework's prediction more directly than any single stereotype-level form alone.

A methodological refinement bears mention. The SFP analysis depends on coding pragmatic function alongside form, in keeping with §6.8's coding scheme. The same form *wa* may function as soft assertion in one utterance, emphatic confirmation in another, and ironic-feminine performance in a third; the distributional analysis must distinguish among these functions where the data permit. The proposed design's coding scheme allocates pragmatic-function codes through inter-coder review (§6.8), and the resulting function-disaggregated distributions are likely to reveal that consumption-intensity tracks **specific pragmatic functions** of stereotype-level SFPs more closely than overall frequency. The expected pattern is that high-consumption participants deploy stereotype-level feminine SFPs disproportionately in performative, creative, and affectively heightened functions—precisely the functions in which shoujo manga's deployment of the same forms is concentrated—while the same participants' use of the same forms in routine declarative or confirmation functions remains comparable to that of low-consumption participants.

## 7.4 Configuration-Level Analysis: Pronoun-SFP Combinations

The configuration-level analysis is where the framework's predictions become most testably specific. As §3.7 and §4.3 emphasized, the densest indexical loadings in Japanese gendered grammar emerge at the configuration level rather than at the level of individual forms: *atashi*-with-*wa* makes a more specific indexical move than *atashi* alone or *wa* alone, and *boku*-with-softened-SFPs makes a more specific move than *boku* alone. Shoujo manga's *yakuwarigo*, as Chapter 4 documented, operates substantially through enregistered configurations whose iconic loadings are decomposable but pragmatically bundled, and the framework's prediction is that high-consumption queer participants will exhibit **configuration-level alignment** with manga conventions in ways that mere form-by-form analysis would miss.

The principal manga-aligned configurations the analysis would test for are those characterized in Chapter 4: the hyper-feminine heroine configuration (*atashi* or *watashi* with *wa*, *wayo*, *no*, *kashira*); the *bishōnen* configuration (*boku* with neutral or softened SFPs, *yo*, *ne*, occasionally *no*); the *otokoyaku*-inflected princely configuration (*boku* or *ore* with assertive SFPs deployed by female-bodied or gender-crossing characters); the masculine rival configuration (*ore* with *ze*, *zo*); and various queer-coded configurations from yuri (whose continuity with broader shoujo register makes them less distinctively differentiable) and BL (where the seme/uke dyad's linguistic correlates supply distinct configurational packages). The expected pattern is that high-consumption participants exhibit denser deployment of one or more of these configurations than low-consumption participants, with the specific configurations preferentially deployed varying by participant subgroup and by context.

The bricolage logic articulated in §2.1 and §4.6 supplies the framework for interpreting the expected configurational findings. Participants are unlikely to adopt manga configurations wholesale—e.g., reproducing the full *ojō-sama* register including elaborated honorifics, *kashira*, *teyo*, and *koto*—because the modular structure of *yakuwarigo* allows decomposition into separable elements that can be selectively appropriated and recombined. The framework's prediction is therefore that high-consumption participants will exhibit **partial alignment** with manga configurations: deploying *atashi* with *no* but not with *kashira*, *boku* with neutral SFPs but not with the full *bishōnen* prosodic profile, *ore* with *ze* in performative but not in routine assertion. The partial-alignment pattern is what the modular-bricolage logic predicts, and it distinguishes the framework's prediction from the alternative prediction that media exposure produces wholesale register replication.

A particularly informative configurational test concerns **innovative recombinations** that exceed manga conventions. The framework articulated in §2.1 anticipates that bricolage may produce configurations that none of the source registers themselves stage—e.g., *boku* combined with *no* for soft-assertive androgynous self-reference, *uchi* combined with *ze* for casual-emphatic performance, *jibun* combined with *yo* for ambiguous identity-marking. Such configurations would demonstrate that the participants are operating not as passive recipients of manga conventions but as stylistic agents whose bricolage extends the manga catalogue rather than merely replicating it. The presence of innovative recombinations in high-consumption participants' production—if the design's analyses were to detect them—would be theoretically significant precisely because it would distinguish exemplar-driven incorporation (which would produce manga-aligned configurations) from agentive bricolage (which would produce configurations exceeding manga conventions). The expected empirical finding, on the framework's account, is that **both** patterns coexist: high-consumption participants would exhibit denser deployment of manga-aligned configurations than low-consumption participants do, **and** they would exhibit innovative recombinations that the manga register itself does not stage.

The expected configurational profile of high-consumption queer participants can be characterized along three dimensions. **First**, configurational repertoire would be **broader** than that of low-consumption participants, encompassing more distinct configurations across more contexts. **Second**, the repertoire would include **manga-aligned configurations** at higher rates than low-consumption participants' repertoires do, with particular concentration of the *bishōnen*, *otokoyaku*, and (for participants engaging hyper-feminine performance) hyper-feminine configurations. **Third**, the repertoire would exhibit **innovative recombinations** at non-trivial rates, particularly among participants whose stylistic projects are most clearly articulated in interview commentary. These three dimensions would jointly characterize a configurational repertoire that the framework predicts emerges from selective bricolage with manga as a curated catalogue, rather than from either passive replication or pure individual innovation independent of the manga input.

A specific configurational hypothesis warrants particular attention. The ***boku*-with-softened-SFPs configuration**, characteristic of shoujo manga's *bishōnen* and (in modified form) *otokoyaku* protagonists, is the configuration whose appropriation by queer female-bodied or X-gender participants would most clearly support the framework's central prediction. The reason is that this configuration combines a cross-prescriptive pronoun (*boku* used by non-male-identified speakers) with SFPs that themselves diverge from the assertive-masculine deployment that *boku* would predict if the participant were appropriating a generically masculine register. The configuration is therefore characteristic of shoujo specifically rather than of masculine register generally, and its deployment by queer participants—particularly in identity-salient contexts—would support the framework's prediction that shoujo's *bishōnen* configuration supplies a distinctive stylistic resource that the prescriptive masculine register does not. The analytic interpretation of this finding, if observed, would connect the indexical fields of *boku* (sensitivity, modesty, intellectual seriousness, androgyny) with the activations these queer participants are theorized to be making, in keeping with the indexical-field framework articulated in §2.2.

## 7.5 Three-Way Comparison: Manga Register, Naturalistic Majority Norms, and Queer Participant Patterns

The three-way comparison this section synthesizes is the central analytic move on which the report's empirical contribution depends. The comparison juxtaposes the documented shoujo manga register baseline (drawing on Unser-Schutz's corpus work[^17]), the naturalistic contemporary younger-speech baseline (drawing on Okamoto's, Sturtz Sreetharan's, and Matsumoto's documented patterns), and the patterns the proposed design's analyses would observe in queer participants' production. The comparison enables identification of the specific points at which queer participants' patterns mirror manga conventions, the points at which they exceed those conventions through innovation or recombination, and the points at which they remain anchored to the majority pattern.

The structural framing of the comparison was anticipated in §4.2's table, which contrasted prescriptive *joseigo*/*danseigo* ideology, naturalistic contemporary younger speech, and the shoujo manga register. The present synthesis extends that table by adding the fourth column—queer participant patterns—and characterizing the relationships among the four reference points. The principal expected findings of the three-way comparison can be organized around four distinct patterns of alignment.

**Pattern 1: Mirroring of manga conventions in identity-salient configurations.** The framework predicts that high-consumption queer participants would exhibit, in identity-salient contexts (peer in-group interaction, creative writing, identity-disclosure moments), configurations that align closely with shoujo manga's documented conventions. *Boku*-with-softened-SFPs deployment by lesbian and X-gender participants in identity-disclosure contexts would mirror the *bishōnen* configuration; *atashi*-with-*wa* deployment by gay male participants in *onē*-coded peer settings would mirror a hyper-feminine configuration that has been adopted into the *onē-kotoba* tradition; selective deployment of stereotype-level feminine SFPs by lesbian participants in performative or affective contexts would mirror the manga register's preservation of forms that majority practice has retreated from. These mirroring patterns would support the framework's prediction that shoujo manga supplies indexical resources for identity-relevant stylistic moves, with the specific configurations adopted reflecting the participants' identification with particular character types and the indexical fields those types have come to anchor.

**Pattern 2: Exceeding manga conventions through innovative bricolage.** The framework also predicts that high-consumption queer participants would exhibit configurations that the manga register itself does not stage but that emerge from selective recombination of manga-derived elements with other indexical resources. Innovative recombinations such as *boku*-with-*no* (combining cross-prescriptive masculine pronoun with feminine-leaning SFP), *jibun*-with-stereotype-feminine-SFPs (combining ambiguous pronoun with strongly gendered particle), or *atashi*-with-*ze* (combining feminine pronoun with masculine emphatic) would constitute extensions of the manga catalogue rather than replications of it. These innovative configurations would support the framework's prediction that bricolage is genuinely agentive rather than imitative, with queer participants segmenting the manga register's modules and recombining them with elements from community-internal registers, marker-level neutral forms, and individual stylistic projects in ways that produce configurations specific to their own self-construction.

**Pattern 3: Anchoring to majority patterns in normatively oriented contexts.** The framework predicts that queer participants—including high-consumption participants—would exhibit configurations comparable to majority naturalistic patterns in contexts where normative orientation is more salient than stylistic differentiation: workplace interaction, family communication, formal address. In these contexts, *watashi* with neutral SFPs, *desu/masu* polite forms, and minimal stereotype-level marking would be expected across consumption strata, with consumption-intensity effects on production becoming attenuated or absent. This anchoring pattern is critical for the framework's account because it specifies the **boundedness** of media-derived stylistic uptake: shoujo manga supplies resources for identity-relevant stylistic moves, but it does not displace the normative repertoire that participants deploy in normatively oriented contexts. The design's contextual-mediation analysis (Chapter 8) is built precisely to test this prediction.

**Pattern 4: Cross-cutting differentiation by community-internal register access.** The three-way comparison is incomplete without attention to how community-internal registers interact with manga conventions to produce participant patterns that neither baseline alone would predict. Gay male participants with substantial Ni-chōme exposure may exhibit *onē-kotoba*-aligned configurations (*atashi* with feminine SFPs in male-bodied speech) that derive from the community-internal register tradition Maree and Itakura document rather than from shoujo manga's *bishōnen* configurations; lesbian participants whose community history is institutionally entangled with shoujo (per Welker's documentation) may exhibit configurations whose origins cannot be cleanly attributed to either community or manga since the two are historically continuous; X-gender participants without strong community access may rely more heavily on manga-derived resources because alternative resources are less available to them. The three-way comparison must therefore be supplemented by attention to the multi-source character of queer participants' configurational repertoires, with the specific source-mix varying systematically across the within-population subgroups Chapter 5 identified.

The disaggregation of these four patterns by consumption stratum yields the framework's most specific prediction: **the participants whose patterns most closely align with manga conventions are those at the high end of identity-relevance and depth indicators rather than mere frequency indicators**. The differential prediction, articulated in §7.1, is that identity-relevance and depth predict pattern-mirroring more strongly than frequency does, in keeping with the exemplar-theoretic principle that identity-salient and deeply engaged exemplars are disproportionately weighted in storage and retrieval[^10]. A participant who reports high frequency but low identity-relevance would be predicted to exhibit weaker manga-alignment than a participant who reports lower frequency but high identity-relevance. The differential pattern dissociates exposure-driven exemplar accumulation from identity-mediated uptake and supports the framework's prediction that the latter is the principal mechanism through which shoujo manga's configurations enter queer participants' repertoires.

The cumulative empirical picture the three-way comparison would generate is one in which queer participants' configurational repertoires are characterized by **selective and contextually concentrated alignment** with manga conventions, **innovative bricolage** that extends the manga catalogue, **anchoring to majority patterns** in normatively oriented contexts, and **cross-cutting differentiation** by community-internal register access. This picture is consistent with the framework's prediction that shoujo manga functions as one curated catalogue of indexical possibilities among several, with the specific contributions visible in particular patterns rather than in wholesale stylistic substitution. It is inconsistent with the strong "media drives change" claim that earlier sociolinguistic debate sometimes entertained—a claim the framework explicitly disavows—and consistent with the more modest claim that mediatized registers function as stylistic resources whose uptake is selective, agentive, and dependent on the speakers' identity-relevant projects.

## 7.6 Within-Population Heterogeneity: Patterns Across Gender Identity, Generation, and Community Affiliation

The design's commitment to within-population disaggregation is methodologically essential because the queer Japanese young-adult population is heterogeneous along axes that the literature documents as theoretically consequential, and findings aggregated across the population would obscure precisely the differentiation that the framework's predictions require. The present section disaggregates the principal pronoun and SFP findings across gender identity, generation, and community affiliation, drawing on the differentiated empirical baselines Chapter 5 surveyed.

**Cisgender lesbian and bisexual women** constitute the subpopulation for whom the framework's predictions are most directly testable, in part because the published empirical record on lesbian linguistic practice is the most fully developed (principally through Abe's work) and in part because the historical entanglement of lesbian community formation with shoujo manga production (per Welker) suggests that this subpopulation's media engagement and linguistic practice are institutionally continuous in ways other subpopulations may not be. The framework predicts that high-consumption lesbian participants would exhibit configurations that draw on both the broader shoujo register (including hyper-feminine and *otokoyaku*-inflected configurations) and the community-internal register documented by Abe. The specific expected pattern is **dual-register competence**: high-consumption lesbian participants would deploy *atashi* with feminine SFPs in some contexts (drawing on shoujo hyper-feminine register and on broader femme-coded community practice), *boku* or *ore* with mixed SFPs in others (drawing on Abe's documented Shinjuku-bar register and on shoujo *otokoyaku* configurations), and *uchi* with neutral SFPs in still others (drawing on younger-cohort general practice). The dual-register competence would distinguish lesbian participants' patterns from those of any single-source prediction, supporting the framework's account of bricolage from multiple sources.

**Cisgender gay and bisexual men** present a more analytically complex case because the principal community-internal queer register available to them (*onē-kotoba*) does not coincide with shoujo manga's *bishōnen* configuration, as §4.5 and §5.3 emphasized. Gay male participants therefore have access to two distinct queer-coded registers from different sources, and the framework's prediction is that their configurational repertoires will reflect the relative weighting they assign to each. Participants with substantial Ni-chōme exposure and *onē-kotoba* familiarity, following Itakura's documentation, would be expected to deploy *atashi* with feminine SFPs in tactical performances calibrated to particular community-internal contexts, with the deployment functioning as the kind of "tactical performance" Itakura characterizes rather than as constant background. Participants without strong community access, but with substantial shoujo or BL engagement, would be expected to deploy *bishōnen*-aligned configurations (*boku* with softened SFPs) more prominently, with the mediatized register supplying resources where community-internal access is limited. The framework's prediction is that consumption-intensity effects on production would be **stronger for gay male participants without community-internal access** than for those with such access, since the latter have access to alternative resources whereas the former depend more heavily on mediatized configurations.

**Transgender participants** face distinctive structural problems within the binary architecture, as §5.3 articulated. The framework's predictions for this subpopulation are correspondingly more nuanced. A transgender woman whose stylistic project is oriented toward feminine self-presentation may engage shoujo manga's hyper-feminine configurations as a resource whose pragmatic intensification supports the affective work of transition; a transgender man whose stylistic project is oriented toward masculine self-presentation may engage shoujo's *bishōnen* or masculine-rival configurations differently from how a cisgender male participant would. The empirical record on transgender Japanese linguistic practice is, as §5.3 acknowledged, comparatively underdeveloped in the directly available literature, and the design's findings for this subpopulation would extend the empirical record rather than test against an established baseline. The within-population analysis must accordingly proceed with explicit acknowledgment that any findings for this subgroup are exploratory rather than confirmatory of well-established patterns.

**X-gender participants** face the absence of any pronoun whose default loading aligns with non-binary self-presentation, with *jibun* offering the most ambiguity-as-strategy potential among available forms. The framework's prediction is that X-gender participants' configurational repertoires would exhibit higher rates of *jibun* than other subpopulations and would more frequently include innovative recombinations whose specific configurations none of the source registers stages. The proposed design's analyses for this subgroup would test whether X-gender participants' bricolage extends the manga catalogue more substantially than other subpopulations' bricolage does, in keeping with the framework's prediction that the structural inadequacy of available resources is most acute for X-gender speakers and that their stylistic agency is correspondingly most pronounced.

**Generational subdivision** within the 18–35 range is theoretically consequential because the media ecology and community-access pathways have shifted substantially across the cohorts the range encompasses. The 18–24 cohort would be expected to exhibit consumption profiles weighted more toward digital and platform-mediated engagement, with the corresponding implications that exemplar storage may include digitally circulating shoujo-derived content (fan-produced extensions, online dramatizations, social-media-circulated character speech) alongside the canonical manga corpus. The 30–35 cohort would be expected to exhibit consumption profiles weighted more toward print-era engagement, with potentially deeper engagement with specific canonical works but with less digital community participation. The framework's prediction is that the consumption-production relationship may be **modulated by generation** in ways the design's analyses would characterize: identity-relevance indicators may track production patterns more closely in younger cohorts (whose digital engagement supports denser identity-salient exemplar storage), while duration indicators may track production patterns more closely in older cohorts (whose long-term engagement supports cumulative exemplar accumulation).

**Community affiliation** introduces additional differentiation that crosscuts gender identity and generation. Participants centrally networked into urban gay or lesbian community infrastructure would be expected to exhibit configurations in which community-internal registers carry more weight than mediatized registers do; participants whose community access is principally digital would be expected to exhibit configurations in which platform-mediated registers and shoujo-derived resources carry more weight; participants without strong community affiliation would be expected to depend more heavily on mediatized registers as their primary source of identity-relevant stylistic resources. The differential predictions across community-affiliation subgroups support the framework's account of multi-source bricolage and provide a within-design test of the alternative-explanation analyses §7.8 develops.

The within-population disaggregation makes explicit a methodological caveat that bears restating. The stratified-purposive sampling specified in §6.3 targets adequate representation across the principal subpopulations, but cell sparsity is likely in some intersectional subgroups (e.g., transgender participants in smaller cities, X-gender participants in older generational subdivisions, gay male participants without any community access). The proposed design's analyses for sparse cells must be reported with explicit acknowledgment of the limited inference the data support, and the substantive interpretation of any patterns in such cells must be calibrated to the analytic uncertainty that small samples impose. The principle the design adopts, articulated in §6.3, is to "report findings disaggregated by cell with explicit acknowledgement of cells whose representation is too sparse to support robust inference," and the present section's prospective findings would be reported in keeping with that principle. The within-population heterogeneity that the design respects is therefore itself a finding: the queer Japanese young-adult population exhibits patterns whose differentiation across subgroups is theoretically consequential, and aggregate claims that obscure this differentiation would systematically misrepresent the empirical reality the design is built to characterize.

## 7.7 Strategic Deployment for Persona Construction, Stance, and Indexical Resistance

The quantitative distributional findings of §§7.2–7.6 acquire their full theoretical content only when they are integrated with the qualitative interview data and metalinguistic commentary that the design's interview module (§6.5) generates. The integration is what allows the analysis to move from the description of patterns to the characterization of the **strategic logic** through which participants deploy pronouns and SFPs to construct queer personae, modulate stance, signal in-group affiliation, and resist binary indexation. The framework articulated in Chapter 2 is decisive here: linguistic variables function as resources within indexical fields, with speakers strategically deploying them to make ideological moves, and the strategic logic of any individual deployment is recoverable only through attention to how participants narrate their own stylistic projects.

The expected qualitative findings can be characterized along four axes that the framework predicts will organize participants' strategic deployment.

**Persona construction** is the first axis. The framework's prediction is that high-consumption participants would articulate, in interview commentary, stylistic projects that connect their pronoun and SFP choices to specific personae they are constructing—not wholesale identity-categories but more granular character-types and stance-positions whose indexical loadings track shoujo manga's diversified *yakuwarigo*. A lesbian participant might articulate a stylistic project of "soft *boku*-using" that draws explicitly on *bishōnen* models without committing to the full register; a gay male participant might articulate alternation between "professional *watashi*" and "Ni-chōme *atashi*" that maps onto Itakura's tactical-performance characterization; a transgender participant might articulate ongoing experimentation with *boku*-with-various-SFPs as part of transition; an X-gender participant might articulate strategic *jibun* deployment as ambiguity-as-strategy. The framework predicts that these articulations would draw, where shoujo engagement is high, on character-types and configurations from the manga register, with participants citing specific characters or character-types as exemplars or counter-exemplars in their stylistic narratives.

**Stance modulation** is the second axis. The framework predicts that participants would describe their pronoun and SFP choices as varying with the stance they wish to project: assertion, doubt, intimacy, distance, emotional engagement, ironic detachment. The Abe finding that lesbian speakers' *ore* use conveys "solidarity/distance, emotional engagement, and resistance to the dominant gender and sexual norms" is paradigmatic of the multi-stance work that single forms can perform, and the framework predicts that participants would articulate analogous multi-stance work in their own deployments. The interview data the design generates would, on the framework's prediction, surface participants' awareness that the same form deployed in different moments serves different stance functions, and that the choice among forms within a given moment depends as much on stance as on identity in any abstract sense.

**In-group affiliation signaling** is the third axis. The framework predicts that participants would describe their stylistic choices as calibrated to interlocutor identity, with particular configurations functioning as in-group signals to other queer interlocutors and as discretion-preserving moves with normatively oriented interlocutors. The Itakura finding that Ni-chōme volleyballers' *onē-kotoba* deployment "lubricates an aesthetically pro-silence erotic play in tension with Japan's – retrospectively and arguably – family-oriented, if not homophobic, sociocultural orientation" exemplifies the in-group/out-group calibration the framework anticipates. Where shoujo-derived configurations are deployed for in-group signaling, the framework predicts that the deployment would be linked, in interview commentary, to either explicit shared engagement with the manga register (shared fan identity functioning as the affiliation marker) or to recognition of the configuration as queer-coded within the broader community discourse.

**Indexical resistance** is the fourth axis. The framework predicts that some participants would articulate their stylistic choices as deliberate resistance to the binary indexation the prescriptive system imposes, with particular forms or configurations chosen specifically because they refuse the indexical claims the prescriptive system would otherwise make. A participant who describes her use of *jibun* as a refusal to choose between *watashi* and *atashi*, or her use of *boku*-with-*no* as a refusal to be read as either feminine or masculine in the prescriptive sense, exemplifies the indexical-resistance logic. The framework's prediction is that indexical-resistance articulations would be more prominent among participants whose stylistic projects are most explicitly oriented toward non-binary or anti-prescriptive self-presentation, with X-gender participants and politically articulate participants from other subpopulations particularly likely to deploy this logic.

The relationship between the quantitative distributional findings of §§7.2–7.4 and the qualitative strategic-deployment findings of the present section is itself analytically consequential. **Where the two streams converge**—where a participant's production pattern aligns with the strategic logic she articulates—the convergence supports the analytic interpretation that the deployment is intentional and identity-performative. **Where the two streams diverge**—where a participant's production pattern exhibits configurations she does not articulate or recognize—the divergence supports the alternative interpretation that the deployment proceeds below explicit metapragmatic awareness, in keeping with the exemplar-theoretic prediction that some forms are incorporated through automatic activation of stored exemplars rather than through conscious stylistic choice. The conscious-versus-below-awareness distinction articulated in §6.1 is operationalized precisely through this convergence/divergence analysis, and the framework's prediction is that **stereotype-level forms** would more frequently align with explicit articulation (as objects of metapragmatic attention) while **marker-level forms** would more frequently diverge (as features deployed below awareness).

The cumulative qualitative finding the framework predicts is that participants' strategic deployment exhibits a graded structure, with some configurations articulated as deliberate stylistic moves (typically involving stereotype-level forms in identity-salient contexts), some configurations articulated as routine community register (typically involving forms shared with peer networks regardless of explicit reflection), and some configurations operating below explicit articulation (typically involving marker-level forms whose deployment varies systematically with consumption-intensity but which participants do not recognize as media-derived). The graded structure supports the framework's account of multiple, partially independent pathways through which mediatized exemplars enter individual repertoires, and it prepares the contextual-mediation analysis Chapter 8 develops by establishing the strategic logic that contextual variation will be shown to track.

## 7.8 Weighing Alternative Explanations for Observed Patterns

The framework's predictions surveyed in §§7.2–7.7 do not exhaust the analytic space. The alternative explanations flagged in Chapters 5 and 6—community-mediated transmission, parallel media exposure, identity-driven self-selection, and deliberate stylistic appropriation versus exemplar-driven incorporation—must be weighed against the empirical findings the design would generate, and the specific weighting determines what the consumption-production relationship can ultimately be claimed to support theoretically. The present section articulates how each alternative would be tested and what the framework's calibrated conclusion would entail.

**Community-mediated transmission** is the first alternative. The hypothesis is that observed correlations between shoujo consumption and pronoun/SFP patterns reflect not media-derived exemplar accumulation but community-internal transmission, with participants acquiring stylistic resources from peer networks, community organizations, and digital communities rather than from manga directly. The design's test is the multivariate regression specified in §6.9, which includes community-participation indicators as covariates and asks whether shoujo consumption effects on production persist after community participation is controlled. The framework's prediction is that consumption effects **would persist** after community-participation controls, but with **attenuated magnitude**, because community participation and manga consumption are partially overlapping rather than independent: participants centrally networked into queer communities are likely to engage shoujo manga at higher rates than less-networked participants, and the partial overlap means that some portion of any zero-order consumption-production correlation reflects community-mediated transmission rather than media exposure per se. The critical test is whether **identity-relevance and depth indicators**—which the framework predicts capture media-specific effects more cleanly than frequency does—retain their associations with production patterns after community-participation controls. If they do, the framework's account of media-derived exemplar accumulation receives support; if community-participation controls eliminate the identity-relevance and depth associations, the community-mediated alternative is more strongly supported.

**Parallel media exposure** is the second alternative. The hypothesis is that observed correlations reflect not shoujo-specific contributions but broader media-ecology effects, with shoujo's apparent contribution actually attributable to its overlap with BL, yuri, anime adaptations, light novels, and broader fan-community discourse. The design's test is the inclusion of comparator-media measures (Section 4 of the survey) as additional predictors in the multivariate regressions. The framework's prediction is that **shoujo-specific effects would persist** after comparator-media controls, particularly for the configurations characteristic of shoujo's *bishōnen* and *otokoyaku* registers (which are not equivalently staged in shōnen, seinen, or variety television), but that some apparent shoujo effects—particularly those concerning configurations also present in BL or yuri—would be **partially attributable to the broader shoujo-adjacent ecology** rather than to shoujo proper. The framework's calibrated conclusion would distinguish among (a) effects clearly specific to shoujo manga proper, (b) effects attributable to the broader shoujo–yuri–BL register continuum, and (c) effects attributable to manga consumption generally regardless of genre. The empirical analyses would specify which of these levels the observed patterns operate at, with implications for how the framework's prediction is read.

**Identity-driven self-selection** is the third alternative and the most theoretically consequential. The hypothesis is that the consumption-production correlation reflects not media-driven exemplar accumulation but pre-existing identity-driven preferences that select queer young adults for both shoujo consumption and non-default linguistic practices, with shoujo consumption operating as a downstream marker of identity orientation rather than as an upstream cause of linguistic practice. The cross-sectional design cannot definitively rule out this alternative, as §6.11 acknowledged. What the design can do is examine the **internal structure** of the consumption-production relationship for patterns more consistent with one account than the other. The framework's prediction, articulated in §6.9, is that if identity-relevance predicts production more strongly than frequency does, the pattern is consistent with identity-driven engagement amplifying exemplar uptake but inconsistent with simple selection on identity producing the consumption-production correlation. The reasoning is as follows: under pure selection, frequency and identity-relevance should both correlate with production through the common pathway of underlying identity orientation, with no reason for one to predict more strongly than the other. Under exemplar-mediated uptake, identity-relevance should predict more strongly because identity-salient exemplars are disproportionately weighted in storage and retrieval[^10]. The differential prediction therefore distinguishes the two accounts.

A complementary test concerns the **specific configurations** that consumption predicts. Under pure selection on identity, the consumption-production correlation would extend uniformly across forms that index identity, regardless of whether the forms are characteristic of shoujo manga's specific staging or of identity-orientation generally. Under exemplar-mediated uptake, the correlation would be concentrated specifically in configurations characteristic of shoujo's *yakuwarigo*—the *bishōnen*-aligned *boku*-with-softened-SFPs combination, the hyper-feminine *atashi*-with-*wa* combination, the *otokoyaku*-inflected configurations—rather than uniformly across all non-default forms. The framework's prediction is that the latter pattern would be observed: consumption would predict shoujo-specific configurations more strongly than it predicts non-default forms generally, supporting exemplar-mediated uptake over pure selection.

**Deliberate stylistic appropriation versus exemplar-driven incorporation** is the fourth alternative, and the design addresses it through the production–awareness contrast articulated in §6.9. The hypothesis at issue is whether observed patterns reflect conscious appropriation (participants actively choosing shoujo-derived configurations as deliberate stylistic moves) or below-awareness incorporation (participants deploying configurations whose origins they do not recognize). The framework's prediction is that **both** pathways are operative, with stereotype-level forms more typically involving conscious appropriation and marker-level forms more typically involving below-awareness incorporation. The empirical test is whether participants articulate awareness of the configurations their production exhibits: where production and awareness converge, the conscious-appropriation pathway is supported; where they diverge, the below-awareness pathway is supported. The framework predicts that both convergent and divergent patterns would be observed, with the specific pattern varying by form-type and by participant in keeping with the salience-hierarchy distinction articulated in §3.1.

The cumulative result of the alternative-explanation analyses is a calibrated characterization of the consumption-production relationship that respects the design's causal limits while specifying what the patterns do and do not support theoretically. The framework's calibrated conclusion is that the available evidence supports a **multi-pathway, partially media-mediated** account in which:

- Shoujo manga consumption is associated with specific configurational patterns characteristic of the genre's staged *yakuwarigo*, with the association persisting after community-participation and comparator-media controls.
- The association is concentrated among participants whose engagement with shoujo is identity-salient and deep rather than merely frequent, consistent with exemplar-mediated uptake under identity-weighted storage[^10].
- The association coexists with community-mediated transmission, parallel media exposure, and individual stylistic agency as co-determining pathways, with the relative weights varying across subpopulations and individual participants.
- Identity-driven self-selection cannot be ruled out as a contributing factor by the cross-sectional design alone, but the differential predictions among indicators support an interpretation in which selection and exemplar-mediated uptake operate jointly rather than the latter being entirely derivative of the former.
- Consciousness of shoujo-derived appropriation varies across forms and participants, with stereotype-level forms more frequently appropriated above explicit awareness and marker-level forms more frequently incorporated below it.

This calibrated characterization is what the design's analyses would generate, and it is what the framework's predictions support given the available empirical record. The characterization does not claim that shoujo manga causes queer young adults' linguistic patterns at the population level—a claim the framework explicitly disavows—nor that media exposure is the sole or primary driver of any patterns observed. It claims, more modestly and more theoretically defensibly, that mediatized exemplars, indexical fields, and stylistic agency interact in the repertoires of speakers whose identities are not adequately served by prescriptive language ideologies, with shoujo manga functioning as one curated catalogue of indexical possibilities among several. The empirical analyses prepared in this chapter establish the distributional foundation for the contextual-mediation analysis Chapter 8 develops, which examines how situational context, interlocutor identity, and metalinguistic awareness mediate the relationships the present chapter has characterized. With the production patterns now specified at the form, configuration, three-way comparison, within-population, and strategic-deployment levels, and with the alternative explanations weighed against those patterns, the report turns next to the contextual stratum that the framework predicts will reveal the strategic logic of media-derived stylistic uptake in its most articulated form.

# 8 The Role of Context, Awareness, and Indexical Salience

The distributional and configurational analyses developed in Chapter 7 characterized the patterns of pronoun and SFP use that the proposed design's analyses would observe across consumption strata, three-way comparisons among manga register, naturalistic majority norms, and queer participant patterns, and within-population subgroups stratified by gender identity, generation, and community affiliation. Those analyses culminated in a calibrated multi-pathway characterization in which shoujo manga consumption is associated with specific configurational patterns, the association is concentrated among participants whose engagement is identity-salient and deep, and the association coexists with community-mediated transmission, parallel media exposure, and individual stylistic agency as co-determining pathways. What that characterization establishes is the **distributional foundation** of the relationship between manga consumption and queer linguistic practice. What it does not yet establish is the **mechanistic structure** of that relationship: the contextual logic, cognitive pathways, and interpretive asymmetries through which the manga register's curated indexical possibilities are activated in particular moments by particular speakers and heard by particular listeners. The present chapter undertakes that mechanistic specification. It moves the empirical analysis from the description of patterns to the characterization of the conditions under which mediatized exemplars become salient enough to enter individual repertoires, and the conditions under which their salience is muted, contested, or selectively activated. Its analytic contribution is to specify, in the framework's own terms, the **mechanisms of strategic, salience-stratified, ideologically contested stylistic agency** that the consumption-production correlations of Chapter 7 manifest in distributional form but do not, on their own, explain.

The chapter proceeds in six steps that map the four interlocking dimensions identified in RQ3. §8.1 examines situational context—formality, modality, topic domain—as a first-order mediator that concentrates manga-aligned production in particular settings rather than distributing it uniformly. §8.2 examines interlocutor identity and audience design, drawing on the documented tactical-performance logic of contemporary Japanese queer linguistic practice to characterize how participants calibrate stylistic moves to audience configuration. §8.3 maps metalinguistic awareness onto the indicator–marker–stereotype salience hierarchy, distinguishing forms whose deployment is articulated as conscious stylistic project from forms that operate below explicit attention. §8.4 deepens this distinction by characterizing the dual cognitive pathways—deliberate appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation—through which manga configurations enter production, and specifies the empirical signatures that distinguish them. §8.5 integrates the perception-experimental stream with production data to operationalize the production–perception asymmetry as directly observable evidence of divergent indexical activations across speaker intention and listener reception. §8.6 synthesizes these strands into a characterization of indexical salience as the property that emerges from the intersection of context, audience, form-level salience, cognitive pathway, and perceptual ecology, preparing the broader synthesis Chapter 9 develops.

Two methodological framings carry over from Chapter 7. First, the analyses below are framed as predictions the proposed design would test and as inferences the available published record permits about the mechanisms at issue, rather than as findings produced by primary fieldwork. Where specific empirical patterns are reported, they derive from the literature surveyed in Chapters 3–5 (Abe's lesbian-bar ethnography, Itakura's Ni-chōme volleyballer ethnography, Lunsing's and Maree's queer self-reference work, Miyazaki's school-context analysis, Okamoto's and Sturtz Sreetharan's documented patterns, Unser-Schutz's manga corpus); where the literature does not support an inference, the chapter says so. Second, the chapter operationalizes the framework articulated in Chapter 2 rather than restating it, treating the indexical-field, exemplar-storage, and mediatization strata as the analytic vocabulary through which contextual mediation, cognitive pathway, and perceptual asymmetry are characterized.

## 8.1 Situational Context and Formality as Mediators of Manga-Aligned Production

The framework's most basic prediction about the contextual mediation of mediatized exemplar uptake is that manga-derived configurations will not distribute uniformly across the contexts in which queer participants speak and write, but will instead **concentrate in particular contextual configurations** whose properties align with the conditions under which mediatized exemplars are most likely to be activated in production. This prediction follows directly from the layered framework: exemplar-theoretic activation depends on contextual cues that match those associated with stored exemplars; the indexical-field framework treats variable deployment as situationally calibrated rather than constant; and the third-wave conception of style as bricolage treats stylistic moves as positioning acts whose deployment is responsive to situational stakes. Together these strata predict that manga-aligned production should cluster in contexts that share key properties with the contexts in which manga's exemplars were originally encountered—affectively heightened, identity-relevant, narratively saturated, low in normative-orientation pressure—and should attenuate in contexts whose properties diverge from these.

Three principal contextual axes structure the analysis: situational formality, communicative modality, and topic domain.

**Situational formality** is the axis whose mediating role is most strongly anticipated by the existing literature. The naturalistic baseline established in Chapter 3 already documents that female speakers' use of stereotype-level feminine SFPs varies substantially with formality: the approximately 68.8% neutral-form rate Okamoto's data report for university-aged women's speech aggregates across contexts in which the relative weight of neutral versus stereotype-level forms shifts considerably. Sturtz Sreetharan's documentation that masculine SFPs function as pragmatic signals of "camaraderie, authority, anger, and so on" rather than as constant background features similarly implies formality-sensitive deployment, with masculine forms concentrated in particular pragmatic-interactional contexts. The framework's prediction is that the same formality-sensitivity operates with **amplified force** in queer participants' production, because the formality dimension intersects directly with the heteronormative-pressure dimension that §3.5 and §5.2 documented as structurally over-determining of queer linguistic options. Formal contexts—workplace interaction, family communication addressed to non-out parents and senior relatives, official institutional settings—are precisely the contexts in which the prescriptive system's heteronormative entailments operate with the greatest force, and in which queer participants are most likely to anchor production to the unmarked *watashi*-with-neutral-SFPs configuration regardless of consumption stratum. Casual contexts, by contrast, relax the prescriptive pressure and open space for the cross-prescriptive and stereotype-level configurations that high consumption is theorized to make available.

The expected directional pattern, framed as predictions the proposed design would test, can be summarized in the following structure. In **formal contexts** (workplace, family-formal, institutional, scripted public communication), the framework predicts that consumption-intensity effects on production would be **substantially attenuated**, with high-consumption and low-consumption participants converging on configurations comparable to the naturalistic majority baseline of *watashi* with predominantly neutral SFPs. In **semi-formal contexts** (peer interaction with mixed audience composition, online interaction in semi-public settings, classroom or study-group communication), the framework predicts moderate consumption effects, with high-consumption participants exhibiting somewhat denser deployment of marker-level SFPs (*na*, *sa*) and occasional stereotype-level forms in affectively heightened utterances but with the configurational core remaining proximate to the naturalistic baseline. In **casual contexts** (peer interaction with in-group queer or shared-fan-identity audience, intimate communication with close friends, low-monitored personal communication), the framework predicts the strongest consumption effects, with cross-prescriptive pronouns and stereotype-level SFPs deployed at rates exceeding the naturalistic baseline and configurations aligning more closely with the manga register documented in Chapter 4. In **identity-salient affectively heightened contexts** (identity disclosure, romantic communication, emotional-disclosure narrative, creative-writing contexts), the framework predicts the most pronounced consumption effects, with the bricolage logic of §7.4 producing manga-aligned configurations and innovative recombinations at the highest rates in the design's data.

This formality-stratified prediction connects the boundedness articulated in Chapter 7's three-way comparison—Pattern 3 of §7.5, the anchoring to majority patterns in normatively oriented contexts—to a specific contextual logic. The boundedness is not arbitrary: it tracks the formality-heteronormativity axis along which the prescriptive system's operative pressure varies. Manga's contribution operates as a **contextually activated resource rather than as a wholesale repertoire shift** because the conditions under which manga-derived exemplars are activated in production—conditions of identity-relevance, affective heightening, low normative pressure, and audience-aligned in-group orientation—are present in some contexts and absent in others. The same participant whose casual identity-salient production aligns most closely with manga conventions would, on the framework's prediction, produce *watashi*-with-neutral-SFPs in workplace email, family conversation with non-out parents, and other normatively oriented contexts, with the contextual switch operating at the level of automatic register-selection rather than as conscious code-switch in many instances.

**Communicative modality** is the second axis. The framework's prediction is that **written and digitally-mediated production** would exhibit denser manga-aligned configurations than spoken interactional production, for three converging reasons. First, shoujo manga is itself a written register, and the exemplars participants accumulate from it are encoded in the modality of written character speech rendered in printed orthography; modality-matching effects in exemplar activation predict that production in written modalities would activate manga-derived exemplars more readily than production in spoken modalities. Second, written and digital production permits the orthographic-stylistic resources—pronoun rendering in hiragana, katakana, kanji, or Roman script; orthographic emphasis devices; non-verbal-affect markers—that manga itself uses, and these resources both invite and rely on the kind of stylistic calibration that manga exemplars supply. Third, digital production in particular allows for the audience-curation and platform-aligned register-selection that §5.5 characterized as a feature of contemporary queer young adults' digital practice, with platform-specific environments aggregating community-aligned interlocutors in ways that face-to-face communication cannot.

The expected modality-stratified pattern is therefore that the design's written-production tasks (Tasks 1–4 of §6.6: text-message simulations, social-media post composition, diary-style entries, creative-writing prompts) would generate higher rates of manga-aligned configurations than the corresponding interview production data, with the differential most pronounced for stereotype-level SFPs and configuration-level patterns and somewhat less pronounced for pronoun choice. Within the written tasks, **creative-writing prompts** (Task 4, voicing characters in specified situations) would generate the highest rates of explicit manga-aligned configurational deployment because the task itself foregrounds the kind of stylized character-voicing that manga registers stage. **Diary-style entries** (Task 3, low-monitored personal reflection) would generate distinctive patterns of below-awareness exemplar incorporation, accessing the cognitive pathway §8.4 develops separately. **Text-message simulations** (Task 1, varied across interlocutor types) and **social-media post composition** (Task 2, varied across platform audiences) would generate the contextual-variation data that operationalizes the audience-design analysis §8.2 develops.

A specific modality-related prediction warrants particular emphasis. The contrast between spoken interview production (the rapport-building module of §6.5, where formality is moderate and the interviewer is an unfamiliar but matched-identity participant) and written low-monitored production (Task 3 diary entries) provides a within-design test of the framework's modality-effect prediction. The framework predicts that the same participant would exhibit more manga-aligned configurations in low-monitored written production than in moderately formal spoken interview production, with the differential reflecting both modality-matching exemplar activation and the relaxation of monitoring pressure that low-stakes written contexts permit. This within-individual modality contrast is theoretically consequential because it dissociates participant-level identity orientation (which is constant across modalities) from situational activation of mediatized exemplars (which varies systematically with modality), supporting the framework's account of contextually activated rather than uniformly distributed mediatized uptake.

**Topic domain** is the third axis. The framework predicts that topics whose content aligns with the narrative material of shoujo manga—romance, intimacy, identity disclosure, emotional-disclosure narrative, aesthetic and creative discussion, gendered-character commentary—would activate manga-derived exemplars more readily than topics whose content diverges (workplace tasks, financial planning, technical or institutional matters). This prediction follows from the principle that exemplar activation is content-cued: stored exemplars associated with romantic confession in shoujo manga are more readily activated when the speaker is narrating her own romantic experience than when she is describing her commute. The proposed design's biographical-narrative module (Module 2 of §6.5), media-autobiography module (Module 3), and creative-writing prompts (Task 4) would generate topic-domain-varied production data permitting this prediction's empirical assessment, with the expected pattern being topic-aligned concentration of manga-derived configurations in moments of narrative content that parallels manga material.

The cumulative effect of the three contextual axes is the **multidimensional contextual concentration** of manga-aligned production that the framework predicts and that Chapter 7's distributional findings already partially anticipated. Manga's contribution to queer participants' repertoires operates through contextually activated resources that emerge most prominently at the intersection of casual formality, written or digital modality, and identity-aligned topic. This intersection defines the contextual envelope within which the consumption-production correlations of Chapter 7 are most pronounced, and outside which they substantially attenuate. The prediction that the design would observe this multidimensional concentration is, in the framework's own terms, the most direct test of the claim that mediatized registers function as situationally activated stylistic resources rather than as background features of speaker repertoire. The empirical confirmation or disconfirmation of this contextual structure would therefore carry substantial weight for the framework's broader account.

## 8.2 Interlocutor Identity, Audience Design, and Tactical Performance

Situational context, however precisely characterized along formality, modality, and topic axes, does not exhaust the contextual mediation the framework predicts. Layered on top of these axes is the more granular dimension of **interlocutor identity and audience design**, through which speakers calibrate their stylistic moves not only to the abstract properties of a situation but to the specific configuration of present and imagined audience members. The framework's prediction here is that interlocutor identity functions as a **fine-grained mediator** of manga-aligned production, with the specific configurations deployed varying systematically with the queerness or non-queerness of present interlocutors, the shared-fan-identity status of those interlocutors, the in-group versus out-group orientation of the audience, and the tactical visibility politics that characterize contemporary Japanese queer linguistic practice.

The decisive empirical anchor for this analysis is **Itakura's Ni-chōme volleyballer ethnography**, whose framing of *onē-kotoba* deployment as "tactical performance" between "discretion and disclosure" provides the most precise contemporary characterization of how queer Japanese speakers calibrate stylistic moves to audience configuration. Itakura's analysis, surveyed in §5.1 and §5.3, documents that Ni-chōme volleyballers' "tactical performance of *onē-kotoba* lubricates an aesthetically pro-silence erotic play in tension with Japan's – retrospectively and arguably – family-oriented, if not homophobic, sociocultural orientation resistant to 'out-and-proud' activism." The tactical character of the performance is not incidental to its meaning; it is constitutive. The same speakers who deploy *atashi*-with-feminine-SFPs in particular Ni-chōme moments do not deploy these forms uniformly across contexts and audiences, and the deployment's selectivity is precisely what makes it function as community-internal recognition rather than as stable identity register. The framework predicts that an analogous tactical logic operates for shoujo-derived configurations among queer young adults more broadly: the deployment of *bishōnen*-aligned *boku*-with-softened-SFPs, hyper-feminine *atashi*-with-*wa*, *otokoyaku*-inflected princely configurations, and other manga-aligned moves would be calibrated to audience configuration in ways that produce systematic variation across interlocutor types within individual participants.

The complementary empirical anchor is **Abe's documentation of context-dependent indexical activation in lesbian bar speech**, also surveyed in Chapter 5. Abe's finding that lesbian speakers' use of *ore* "does not straightforwardly index 'masculinity' (or 'femininity') but rather conveys a variety of context-dependent meanings, including solidarity/distance, emotional engagement, and resistance to the dominant gender and sexual norms" specifies that audience-design effects operate not only at the level of which forms are deployed but at the level of which **regions of the indexical field** are activated by a given form. The same form (*ore*) carries different activations depending on whom the speaker is addressing: solidarity activations are foregrounded with co-present community members, distance activations with out-group interlocutors who would otherwise read the form as gender-transgressive, resistance activations in moments of explicit positioning against heteronormative pressure. The framework's prediction is that manga-aligned configurations exhibit analogous audience-design-mediated activation: *boku*-with-softened-SFPs deployed by a lesbian participant with a fellow shoujo reader activates the *bishōnen*-aligned identity-affiliation region of *boku*'s indexical field, while the same configuration deployed with a non-queer non-fan interlocutor may activate distance, performance, or innovative-bricolage regions instead.

The expected interlocutor-stratified pattern can be characterized along five principal audience configurations the design's instruments would capture.

**Queer in-group with shared shoujo-fan identity** is the audience configuration where the framework predicts manga-aligned configurations to function as in-group recognition markers in their most concentrated form. Where both speaker and interlocutor share fan identity and therefore share access to the manga register's enregistered configurations, the deployment of a manga-derived configuration carries specific recognition value that the same deployment with an unfamiliar interlocutor cannot. The expected empirical pattern is the highest deployment rates for stereotype-level configurations and innovative recombinations in this audience configuration, with the deployment functioning—in the framework's terms—as activation of the indexical field's identity-affiliation region in its most fully articulated form. The metalinguistic interview commentary §8.3 examines would, on the framework's prediction, surface participants' explicit recognition of this functioning: "when I'm with [name] who I read [series] with, I find myself using *boku* like the characters do, and we both notice it."

**Queer in-group without shared fan identity** is the audience configuration where the framework predicts manga-aligned configurations to function more ambiguously. The interlocutor's queer in-group status supports identity-affiliation activation, but the absence of shared fan identity means that manga-specific recognition value is unavailable. The expected pattern is moderate deployment of cross-prescriptive pronouns and marker-level SFPs that align with broader queer-coded community register (drawing on Abe's lesbian bar register or related practices) but lower rates of manga-distinctive stereotype-level configurations whose recognition depends on shared shoujo competence. This audience configuration is theoretically informative because it dissociates identity-affiliation activation from manga-specific recognition: configurations sustained in this audience configuration but absent in the previous one would be those whose loadings extend beyond manga-specific reference, while configurations confined to the previous audience configuration would be those whose loadings depend on shared manga competence specifically.

**Mixed queer–non-queer audience** is the audience configuration where the framework predicts the strongest tactical-performance effects. With both in-group and out-group interlocutors present, the speaker faces audience-design pressure to manage simultaneous communications: visibility to in-group members, discretion or normative orientation toward out-group members. Itakura's "discretion-and-disclosure" framing applies directly.
