The expected pattern is selective deployment of configurations whose surface loadings can be plausibly read in non-queer-coded ways while carrying queer-coded loadings for in-group recognizers—a pattern that the indexical-field framework explicitly accommodates through its prediction that the same form may carry different activations for differently positioned listeners. The proposed design's role-play tasks (Format 3 of §6.6, role-play with varying interlocutor configurations) would generate the audience-mediated production data this prediction requires, with the empirical test being whether participants exhibit configurations characterized by indexical underdetermination—forms whose default reading is normatively unmarked but whose activation in the specific moment carries queer-coded loadings recoverable only by attentive in-group listeners.

**Out-group queer-uninformed audience** (non-queer interlocutors with no awareness of speaker's queer identity) is the audience configuration where the framework predicts the most pronounced anchoring to majority configurations. Discretion pressure is at maximum, identity-affiliation activation has no in-group recipient, and the cost of cross-prescriptive deployment is highest. The expected pattern is convergence on the naturalistic majority baseline articulated in Chapter 3—predominantly *watashi* with predominantly neutral SFPs—with the exception of marker-level forms whose deployment may operate below the speaker's discretion management because their salience hierarchy positions them below explicit metapragmatic attention.

**Family contexts** constitute a distinctive audience configuration whose specific properties depend on the out-status of the speaker with respect to family members. Where the speaker is out, family contexts may resemble queer in-group contexts in some respects but may carry residual normative pressure that other in-group contexts lack. Where the speaker is not out, family contexts function as out-group queer-uninformed contexts with the additional pressure that intimacy and emotional engagement may otherwise activate manga-derived exemplars whose deployment would risk identity disclosure. The framework predicts that the discretion–disclosure tension is most acute in family contexts and that the audience-design strategies participants articulate would most explicitly address family contexts as sites of careful linguistic management.

The expected interlocutor-stratified pattern is summarized in the following table, which articulates the framework's predictions for audience-design effects.

| Audience Configuration | Expected Manga-Aligned Production | Indexical Activation | Tactical Logic |
|---|---|---|---|
| Queer in-group, shared fan identity | Highest density; full configurations; innovative recombinations | Identity-affiliation, manga-specific recognition | Mutual recognition through shared register |
| Queer in-group, no shared fan identity | Moderate; cross-prescriptive pronouns, marker-level SFPs | Identity-affiliation without manga-specific reference | Community-coded affiliation drawing on broader queer register |
| Mixed queer–non-queer audience | Selective; indexically underdetermined configurations | Differential activation for differently positioned listeners | Discretion–disclosure management |
| Out-group queer-uninformed | Substantial anchoring to majority baseline; below-awareness marker-level deployment | Default unmarked readings | Discretion preservation |
| Family contexts (out) | Variable; depends on family-specific norms | Family-mediated activation | Negotiation of family-specific normative pressure |
| Family contexts (not out) | Most strongly anchored to majority baseline | Default unmarked readings | Maximum discretion preservation |
| Online community-aligned platforms | High density; written-modality amplification | Platform-aligned community recognition | Platform-curated audience design |
| Online general-audience platforms | Selective; potentially indexically underdetermined | Variable depending on poster's audience model | Platform-mediated visibility politics |

Two specific theoretical implications follow from this audience-stratified analysis. **First**, the consumption-production correlations of Chapter 7 must be understood as **aggregating over audience configurations** in ways that may obscure substantial within-individual variation. The same participant whose aggregate production data shows moderate manga-aligned configurations may exhibit very high deployment rates in specific in-group audience configurations and near-zero deployment rates in out-group configurations, with the aggregate average concealing the audience-design logic. The proposed design's peer-interaction reconstruction module (Module 4 of §6.5) and role-play tasks are specifically designed to surface this within-individual variation, allowing the analysis to characterize audience-mediated patterns rather than collapsing them into participant-level aggregates.

**Second**, the audience-design logic supplies a specific test for the framework's prediction that manga-derived configurations function as identity-relevant resources rather than as undifferentiated background features of repertoire. If consumption effects on production were driven by background exemplar saturation, audience configuration would matter little: the same participant would deploy similar configurations across audience types, with the deployment reflecting accumulated repertoire rather than tactical positioning. If, conversely, consumption effects operate through the indexical-field framework's account of strategic resource activation, audience configuration should produce the systematic variation the table above articulates, with deployment concentrated in audience configurations whose properties support identity-affiliation activation. The expected empirical pattern—pronounced audience-stratified variation in manga-aligned production—would support the framework's account against the background-saturation alternative.

The connection of this audience-design analysis to the broader contextual mediation §8.1 articulated is direct. Audience configuration is partially nested within situational context: casual contexts more often involve in-group audiences, formal contexts more often involve out-group audiences, with the modality and topic axes interacting variably. The framework predicts that situational context and audience configuration jointly mediate manga-aligned production, with their effects partially additive (each contributing distinct variation) and partially interactive (the combination of identity-salient context and in-group audience producing the most pronounced concentration). The proposed design's analyses would operationalize this joint mediation through the multilevel modeling specified in §6.9, with utterances nested within audience configurations within contexts within participants, allowing the analysis to separate situational effects, audience effects, and their interactions in characterizing the contextual envelope within which manga-derived configurations are activated.

## 8.3 Metalinguistic Awareness and the Indicator–Marker–Stereotype Hierarchy

The contextual and audience-design mediations developed in §§8.1–8.2 specify the **situational conditions** under which manga-aligned configurations are activated. They do not specify the **cognitive and metapragmatic structure** of that activation: whether participants recognize their deployments as manga-derived, whether they can articulate the indexical loadings the deployments carry, whether the deployments are objects of conscious stylistic choice or operate below explicit attention. The present section maps this metapragmatic structure onto the indicator–marker–stereotype salience hierarchy that Silverstein's framework articulates and that §3.1 mapped onto Japanese gendered forms, with the result that different forms within queer participants' repertoires are predicted to occupy different metapragmatic positions, and the consumption-production correlations established in Chapter 7 are predicted to operate through different cognitive pathways for different forms.

The salience hierarchy as applied to Japanese gendered forms was articulated in §3.1. **Indicators** are forms that distinguish populations without attracting sustained metapragmatic attention; in the Japanese gendered-language case, regional dialect features that carry gender-relevant loadings only locally, and certain unmarked grammatical patterns whose gender associations are below the level of speaker awareness, function in this way. **Markers** are forms that have entered stylistic awareness and can be deployed to position speakers but without becoming objects of overt commentary; the contemporary younger generation's avoidance of strongly feminine SFPs in favor of neutral or moderately masculine forms operates substantially at the marker level, with speakers exhibiting systematic patterning without typically articulating it. **Stereotypes** are forms that have become subject to overt metapragmatic discussion, codified in dictionaries, etiquette manuals, and educational discourse, and their use is correspondingly objects of conscious reflection. The hyper-feminine *kashira*, *teyo*, *wayo*, and *koto-yo* on the feminine side, and the strongly assertive *ze* and *zo* on the masculine side, function paradigmatically as stereotypes; their stereotype status is what makes them salient in mediatized registers and metalinguistic commentary while ensuring their relative rarity in everyday talk.

The framework's prediction for the present analysis is that **metalinguistic awareness varies systematically across this salience hierarchy**, and that consumption-production correlations operate through different cognitive pathways for forms at different positions. Specifically, the framework predicts:

**Stereotype-level forms are deployed primarily through conscious appropriation linked to articulated stylistic projects.** Forms such as *kashira*, *wayo*, *teyo*, *ze*, *zo*, and the configuration-level patterns characteristic of *ojō-sama* register or *bishōnen*-rival masculine register, occupy the stereotype position in queer participants' metapragmatic landscape. Their deployment is theorized to be largely deliberate, with participants able to articulate, when prompted, their reasons for deploying particular forms in particular moments and their recognition of the forms as drawn from particular character types or registers. The consumption-production correlation for stereotype-level forms is predicted to operate through what §6.1 termed the **conscious-appropriation pathway**: high-consumption participants accumulate explicit metapragmatic models of how stereotype-level forms function in shoujo's *yakuwarigo*, and they deploy those models in moments where the corresponding stylistic project is operative.

**Marker-level forms are deployed primarily through below-awareness exemplar activation.** Forms such as the marker-level SFPs *na*, *sa*, *yo*, *ne*, the marker-level pronouns whose gender loadings are weaker (*uchi*, *jibun*), and the lower-intensity configuration patterns whose recognition does not figure prominently in metalinguistic discourse, occupy the marker position. Their deployment is theorized to operate substantially below explicit attention, with participants exhibiting systematic patterning without necessarily articulating awareness of it. The consumption-production correlation for marker-level forms is predicted to operate through the **exemplar-driven incorporation pathway**: high-consumption participants accumulate dense exposure to particular marker-level configurations through manga reading, the corresponding exemplars are stored with their indexical loadings intact, and subsequent production activates these stored exemplars in ways that produce systematic patterning without requiring conscious metapragmatic engagement.

**Indicator-level patterns operate below the threshold of metapragmatic relevance.** Patterns whose gender loadings are sufficiently weak or context-specific that they do not figure in stylistic awareness—certain prosodic features, certain morphosyntactic patterns whose gendered associations are local or marginal—operate at the indicator level and would not be expected to track consumption-intensity systematically because they do not enter the indexical-field structure that mediatized exemplars populate.

The expected empirical signature of this hierarchical structure is operationalized through the **production–awareness convergence/divergence analysis** specified in §6.9. The analysis examines whether, for each form-type and each participant, production patterns and metalinguistic articulation align (convergence: the participant deploys the form and articulates awareness of doing so) or diverge (divergence: the participant deploys the form without articulating awareness, or articulates awareness without deploying the form). The framework predicts that **convergence will be more frequent for stereotype-level forms** (where conscious appropriation aligns production with articulation) and **divergence will be more frequent for marker-level forms** (where below-awareness incorporation produces production without corresponding articulation).

The metalinguistic interview commentary the proposed design's interview Module 5 (Explicit Metalinguistic Discussion of §6.5) generates supplies the principal data for this analysis. The expected pattern of participant articulation, framed as predictions the design would test, can be characterized as follows.

Participants are predicted to articulate their use of stereotype-level forms with relative ease and specificity. A high-consumption lesbian participant asked about her use of *boku* would, on the framework's prediction, articulate something like the following: "I use *boku* sometimes, especially when I'm writing or with my close friends. It started when I was reading [series], and the protagonist used *boku*, and I felt like that was how I wanted to talk about myself too—not exactly masculine but more direct than *atashi*." Such an articulation displays the conscious-appropriation pathway directly: the participant identifies a specific exemplar source, articulates the indexical activation she intends (directness, non-feminine but not specifically masculine), and recognizes the deployment as a stylistic choice connected to her broader identity project. The participant's production data would, on the framework's prediction, exhibit *boku* deployment in contexts whose properties match those she articulates, supporting the convergence pattern that conscious appropriation predicts.

Participants are predicted to articulate their use of marker-level forms with substantially less specificity and frequency. A high-consumption participant asked about her use of *sa* or *yone*, on the framework's prediction, would more often respond with general characterizations ("I just talk like that," "everyone uses that," "I haven't really thought about it") rather than with specific exemplar references. Her production data, on the framework's prediction, would exhibit systematic deployment of these forms at rates that track her consumption-intensity profile, with the deployment producing a divergence pattern in which production exhibits patterning without corresponding metalinguistic articulation. The divergence is itself the diagnostic of below-awareness incorporation: the form-deployment varies with consumption (suggesting a cognitive pathway through which manga exposure shapes production) but the participant does not recognize the variation (suggesting that the cognitive pathway operates below explicit attention).

A specific subtlety of the metalinguistic awareness analysis bears attention. The framework's prediction is not that marker-level forms are entirely unconscious—if pressed, participants can often produce some characterization of their use—but that they are positioned at a different point on the awareness gradient than stereotype-level forms. The interview Module 5's structure, which presents specific pronouns and SFPs and asks for commentary on each, would surface graded awareness rather than dichotomous awareness, with the gradient itself being theoretically informative. Participants' relative ease in commenting on stereotype-level forms versus their relative struggle with marker-level forms would map onto the salience hierarchy directly, supporting the framework's prediction that the hierarchy organizes both production and metapragmatic structure jointly.

A complementary diagnostic concerns the **framing** of metalinguistic articulations. Participants who articulate stereotype-level form deployment are predicted to frame their articulations in **stylistic-project terms**—identifying the persona they are constructing, the affect they are projecting, the audience they are addressing—while participants articulating marker-level form deployment are predicted to frame their articulations in **descriptive terms** that lack stylistic-project content. The framing distinction operationalizes the cognitive-pathway distinction at the level of how participants narrate their own practice: the conscious-appropriation pathway entails project-articulation, while the below-awareness pathway entails descriptive-articulation only. The expected empirical pattern would be that interview commentary on stereotype-level forms exhibits dense project-articulation, while commentary on marker-level forms exhibits sparse project-articulation, with the cross-form contrast within individual participants providing within-design evidence for the dual-pathway account.

The connection to the consumption-intensity indicators specified in §6.2.3 is also direct. The framework predicts that the **identity-relevance indicator** would correlate more strongly with stereotype-level form deployment than with marker-level form deployment, because identity-relevance captures the salience-weighted exemplar storage that conscious-appropriation pathways depend on. The **frequency indicator**, by contrast, would correlate more strongly with marker-level form deployment than with stereotype-level form deployment, because frequency captures the raw exposure that below-awareness incorporation pathways operate through. This differential indicator-by-form-type interaction is a specific testable prediction that the design's analyses would address, and its empirical confirmation would support the framework's account of two distinct cognitive pathways operating through distinct exemplar-storage structures.

## 8.4 Conscious Appropriation Versus Exemplar-Driven Incorporation

The metalinguistic awareness analysis of §8.3 identifies a graded structure of awareness across form-types, with stereotype-level forms occupying positions of relatively explicit articulation and marker-level forms operating below explicit attention. The present section deepens this analysis by characterizing the **dual cognitive pathways** that the framework articulates as the underlying structure of which the awareness gradient is the metapragmatic surface, and by specifying the empirical signatures that distinguish the pathways in production data.

The dual-pathway account draws on the exemplar-theoretic framework of §2.3 and the production–awareness contrast specified in §6.9, integrating them into a characterization of how mediatized exemplars enter individual repertoires through partially independent cognitive routes. The two pathways are:

**Pathway 1: Deliberate stylistic appropriation as identity-performative move.** On this pathway, the speaker consciously selects a manga-derived configuration as part of an articulated stylistic project. The exemplar is stored with rich metapragmatic indexation—including explicit awareness of its source character type, register, and indexical loadings—and its activation in production is mediated by the speaker's deliberate intent to make a particular ideological move within an indexical field. The pathway depends on **metapragmatic instruction** that the speaker has explicitly received or constructed: shoujo manga's gender-crossing narratives, in particular, often make linguistic choices narratively explicit (a character disguised as the opposite gender struggles with the corresponding pronouns and SFPs; a *bishōnen* protagonist's softened SFPs are remarked on by other characters; an *otokoyaku*-inflected princely figure's masculine register is foregrounded as performance), and this explicit metapragmatic instruction supplies the conscious-appropriation pathway with its cognitive content. The speaker incorporating *boku*-with-softened-SFPs through this pathway has, on the framework's account, internalized not only the form but its enregistered meaning, its character-type association, its pragmatic deployment conditions, and its position within the broader indexical field.

**Pathway 2: Exemplar-driven incorporation through automatic activation of socially weighted stored exemplars.** On this pathway, the speaker activates manga-derived configurations in production through cognitive processes that operate substantially below explicit attention. The exemplar is stored, in keeping with the exemplar-theoretic principle that "phonetic detail beyond the abstract phonological category, often accompanied by social and speaker-specific information, is part of linguistic knowledge," with its social and contextual indexation intact. But its activation in production proceeds through automatic processes—target selection, bias, contextual cuing—rather than through deliberate stylistic intent. The speaker incorporating a manga-derived marker-level configuration through this pathway may not recognize the configuration's manga origin even when prompted, because the cognitive process by which the exemplar entered her repertoire and the cognitive process by which it is activated in production both operate below the threshold of explicit metapragmatic engagement.

The two pathways are predicted to **coexist within individual repertoires** and to operate jointly rather than separately, with the relative weight of each varying across forms and across participants. A given participant may deploy stereotype-level *kashira* through deliberate appropriation in creative-writing tasks while simultaneously deploying marker-level *yone* through exemplar-driven incorporation in casual interaction; the same form may operate primarily through deliberate appropriation in one participant and primarily through exemplar-driven incorporation in another, depending on the participant's overall metapragmatic engagement with shoujo as a register. The dual-pathway account does not predict a clean separation between consciously appropriated and below-awareness forms but a graded distribution in which the pathways coexist with form-specific and participant-specific weighting.

The empirical signatures that distinguish the pathways operate at three levels.

**Production–awareness convergence/divergence**, already articulated in §8.3, is the first signature. Convergence (production aligns with articulation) supports conscious appropriation; divergence (production exhibits patterning without articulation) supports exemplar-driven incorporation. The cross-form pattern within individual participants—stereotype-level forms exhibiting convergence while marker-level forms exhibit divergence in the same participant—supports the dual-pathway account against alternative accounts that would predict uniform awareness or uniform unconsciousness.

**Modality-stratified deployment** is the second signature, drawing on the modality contrast articulated in §8.1. The framework predicts that **conscious-appropriation pathways are more pronounced in high-monitored production contexts** (creative-writing tasks, interview metalinguistic discussion modules, public-platform social-media composition), while **exemplar-driven incorporation pathways are more pronounced in low-monitored production contexts** (diary-style entries, casual peer interaction, role-play simulating intimate communication). The differential prediction reflects the cognitive-pathway difference: conscious appropriation requires the deliberate stylistic engagement that high-monitored contexts support, while exemplar-driven incorporation operates most freely in low-monitored contexts where automatic activation is not constrained by deliberate selection. The proposed design's contrast between Task 4 creative-writing (foregrounding conscious appropriation) and Task 3 diary-style entries (accessing low-monitored production) operationalizes this differential prediction directly, with the within-individual contrast across these tasks supplying empirical evidence for the pathway distinction.

**Identity-relevance indicator differential association** is the third signature. As articulated in §8.3, the framework predicts that the identity-relevance indicator correlates more strongly with stereotype-level form deployment (mediated by conscious appropriation) than with marker-level form deployment (mediated by exemplar-driven incorporation), while the frequency indicator exhibits the opposite differential. The cross-indicator pattern provides quantitative evidence for the pathway distinction at the level of how different consumption-intensity dimensions track different cognitive-pathway outcomes.

A specific theoretical implication of the dual-pathway account bears attention. The framework's prediction is not that conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation are separable mechanisms operating on entirely distinct sets of forms, but that they are **co-operating cognitive processes** whose relative weights vary continuously across the form-by-participant space. This continuous variation is what makes the pathway distinction theoretically rich: it allows the framework to accommodate the empirical heterogeneity that any rigid dichotomy would obscure, while specifying the conditions under which one pathway dominates over the other. The conditions—form salience hierarchy, contextual monitoring, identity-relevance loading—are themselves empirically tractable and supply the variables through which the pathway distinction can be operationalized.

The dual-pathway account also addresses one of the alternative explanations flagged in §6.1 and §7.8: deliberate stylistic appropriation versus exemplar-driven incorporation as competing accounts of the consumption-production correlation. The empirical analyses described above resolve this alternative not by adjudicating between the two accounts but by **specifying the conditions under which each operates**. The consumption-production correlation is therefore characterized as operating through both pathways jointly, with the specific pathway operative for any given form-participant-context configuration determinable through the production–awareness, modality, and indicator-association diagnostics. This characterization is more nuanced than either "conscious appropriation drives the correlation" or "exemplar-driven incorporation drives the correlation" alone, and it is more theoretically defensible because it respects the cognitive complexity that the layered framework of Chapter 2 anticipates.

A particularly informative test of the dual-pathway account concerns **shoujo manga's metapragmatic instruction itself**. Chapter 4 documented that shoujo manga's gender-crossing narratives often make linguistic choices narratively explicit—the disguised character struggling with new pronouns, the *bishōnen* protagonist's softened SFPs being remarked on, the *otokoyaku*-inflected princely figure's masculine register being foregrounded. The framework's prediction is that participants who have read works featuring such explicit metapragmatic narratives would exhibit **stronger conscious-appropriation pathways for the corresponding forms** than participants whose shoujo engagement has been with works that do not feature explicit metapragmatic narratives. The proposed design's media-autobiography module (Module 3 of §6.5) would surface participants' engagement with specific works, and the analysis would test whether explicit metapragmatic instruction in particular works correlates with explicit metapragmatic articulation about the corresponding forms in participants' own production. This works-specific differential prediction operationalizes the metapragmatic-instruction component of the conscious-appropriation pathway directly, providing a distinct test that complements the salience-hierarchy and modality diagnostics.

## 8.5 The Production–Perception Asymmetry and Divergent Indexical Activations

The contextual, audience-design, awareness, and cognitive-pathway analyses developed in §§8.1–8.4 characterize the speaker side of the relationship between manga consumption and production. They do not yet characterize the listener side: how the configurations queer participants deploy are heard, evaluated, and indexically activated by listeners drawn from different social positions, and how the resulting **production–perception asymmetry** structures both the strategic possibilities and the constraints of queer linguistic practice. The present section integrates the perception-experimental stream of the proposed design (§6.7) with the production data analyzed in preceding sections, operationalizing the prescription–production–perception gap documented in §3.6 in directly observable form and characterizing the consequences of indexical-field divergence across listener positions.

The empirical foundation of this analysis is the documented production–perception gap in Japanese gendered language. Chapter 3 surveyed the substantial evidence that contemporary listeners continue to evaluate speech through prescriptive lenses even as production has shifted, with Inoue's notion of **indexical inversion** capturing the central mechanism: by virtue of the historical construction of *joseigo* documented in §3.4, contemporary listeners hear feminine SFPs and pronouns as evidence of natural feminine speech, with the contingent Meiji-era stylization origin obscured by the very success of the ideological project. The naturalization of the construct means that listeners' evaluative grids remain calibrated to the prescriptive system even when speakers' actual production has shifted, and Vranic's questionnaire study illustrated that even college-aged speakers and their elders frequently misidentify the origins of contemporary slang and gendered forms, with metapragmatic systems substantially detached from both the historical reality of system construction and the contemporary reality of system use. The framework's prediction for the present analysis is that this asymmetry, already documented for naturalistic Japanese gendered language broadly, operates with distinctive force when queer participants deploy manga-aligned configurations whose specific indexical loadings depend on shoujo-register competence that not all listeners share.

The decisive theoretical principle, articulated in §2.2, is that **indexical fields are construed differently across positions in the social and ideological landscape**. As Eckert's framework specifies, "different participants in a speech community may construe the same variable differently, with class-stratified ideological orientations producing class-stratified evaluations of the same form." The present analysis extends this principle to queer-versus-non-queer listener positions and to fan-versus-non-fan listener competence: the same configuration deployed by a queer participant carries different indexical activations for differently positioned listeners, with the divergence being itself the empirical content the framework predicts and explains.

The proposed design's perception-experimental component (§6.7) operationalizes this divergence through several specific analytic structures. The factorial structure varies pronoun choice and SFP choice across carrier sentences whose lexical content is held constant, generating stimulus configurations corresponding to the configurations queer participants deploy in production. The participant structure includes both queer participants drawn from the principal sample and a comparison group of non-queer Japanese young adults matched on age, regional, and educational variables. The rating structure captures multiple indexical-field dimensions—gender attribution, sexuality attribution, age attribution, refinement/coarseness, assertiveness/softness, formality, and stance—rather than collapsing perceptual response into a single gender or formality scale. The analytic structure permits between-group comparison (queer vs. non-queer listener responses to identical stimuli), within-stimulus variation analysis (how specific configurations differ in their activation profiles), configuration-decomposition analysis (whether perceptual loadings track pronoun choice, SFP choice, or their interaction), and within-individual production–perception comparisons (where queer participants both produce particular configurations and rate the same configurations on the perception experiment).

The framework's predictions for this analysis can be specified along several dimensions.

**Between-group divergence on shoujo-distinctive configurations.** The framework predicts that configurations characteristic of shoujo's distinctive *yakuwarigo*—particularly *bishōnen*-aligned *boku*-with-softened-SFPs, *otokoyaku*-inflected configurations, and modular bricolage characteristic of contemporary shoujo register—would exhibit substantial between-group divergence in indexical activation. Queer listeners, particularly those with substantial shoujo engagement, would be predicted to activate the manga-specific indexical loadings (androgyny, sensitivity, *bishōnen* alignment, queer-coded affiliation) when rating these configurations. Non-queer listeners, particularly those without substantial shoujo engagement, would be predicted to activate prescriptive-system-derived loadings (cross-prescriptive *boku* read as "tomboyish" or "masculine-presenting"; softened SFPs read as feminine deference) without the manga-specific recognition. The expected empirical pattern is that the same configuration would receive systematically different ratings across the listener groups, with the divergence being most pronounced for configurations whose indexical loadings depend most heavily on shoujo-register competence.

**Convergence on prescriptively-aligned configurations.** The framework predicts that configurations that align with the prescriptive system—*watashi* with neutral or stereotype-feminine SFPs in female-bodied speech, *boku* or *ore* with masculine SFPs in male-bodied speech—would exhibit substantially less between-group divergence, because the indexical activations these configurations generate operate within the prescriptive ideology that both queer and non-queer listeners share. The convergence-on-prescriptive-configurations pattern, contrasted with the divergence-on-shoujo-distinctive-configurations pattern, would support the framework's account that listener-side asymmetry is concentrated specifically at the configurations whose loadings depend on shoujo-register competence, rather than constituting a general queer-vs.-non-queer perceptual difference.

**Within-individual production–perception alignment for high-consumption queer participants.** The framework predicts that queer participants who deploy particular configurations in their own production would, on the perception experiment, rate those same configurations consistently with the indexical activations they articulate in metalinguistic interview commentary. A high-consumption lesbian participant who deploys *boku*-with-softened-SFPs and articulates this as *bishōnen*-aligned androgyny would, on the perception experiment, rate *boku*-with-softened-SFPs stimuli as activating androgyny, sensitivity, and queer-coded affiliation rather than as straightforwardly masculine. The within-individual production–perception alignment supports the framework's account that production deployment is grounded in coherent indexical-field activation rather than in arbitrary form-selection.

**Differential within-individual alignment by salience hierarchy.** A specific refinement of the within-individual prediction concerns the salience-hierarchy distinction articulated in §8.3. The framework predicts that production–perception alignment would be **stronger for stereotype-level forms** (where conscious appropriation operates on the basis of explicit indexical-field articulation) and **weaker for marker-level forms** (where exemplar-driven incorporation operates without corresponding metapragmatic engagement). The cross-form differential within individual queer participants would mirror the production–awareness pattern of §8.3, supporting the framework's dual-pathway account through perception-experimental evidence in addition to production and metalinguistic evidence.

The theoretical consequences of the production–perception asymmetry for queer linguistic practice operate at two levels.

**Empowering strategic deployment.** The asymmetry empowers strategic deployment because it provides queer speakers with a resource for tactical visibility management. Where queer in-group listeners share shoujo-register competence and recognize manga-distinctive configurations as carrying queer-coded loadings, while non-queer listeners read the same configurations through prescriptive-system loadings without the manga-specific recognition, the configuration functions as a kind of **dual-coded sign**: visible to in-group, invisible to out-group, in keeping with Itakura's discretion–disclosure characterization of *onē-kotoba* tactical performance. The empowering function is precisely what makes manga-derived configurations particularly valuable for the audience-design management developed in §8.2: configurations whose loadings are perceptible only to recognizers permit speakers to signal to in-group while preserving discretion with out-group, addressing the structural condition that §5.1 characterized in terms of the contemporary Japanese sociopolitical environment's combination of growing institutional visibility and persistent backlash discourses.

**Constraining strategic deployment.** The asymmetry also constrains strategic deployment because it produces unintended indexical activations that the speaker may not anticipate or prefer. A queer participant who deploys *boku*-with-softened-SFPs to activate *bishōnen*-aligned androgyny within an indexical project may find that non-queer listeners activate "tomboyish" or "gender-non-conforming" readings that misrecognize the project's specific content; perceived activations may exceed speaker intention in ways that produce identity-attribution effects the speaker would not have chosen. The constraining function reflects the broader principle that "a particular stereotypically gendered form in Japanese (e.g., a sentence-final particle) may not necessarily be used to index the speaker's gender, although it may be interpreted as doing so by hearers"—a principle whose force is amplified when the relevant configurations carry indexical loadings that depend on register competence not all listeners share.

A specific theoretical implication concerns the relation between the production–perception asymmetry and the metalinguistic awareness analysis of §8.3. Queer participants who articulate awareness of the asymmetry—who recognize, in interview commentary, that their deployments of particular configurations are heard differently by different listener groups—would, on the framework's prediction, exhibit more sophisticated tactical performance than participants who do not articulate such awareness. The recognition of the asymmetry is itself a metapragmatic resource that supports tactical performance, allowing speakers to calibrate deployment to expected listener-group composition. Participants who do not recognize the asymmetry would be predicted to exhibit less calibrated deployment, with the result that their production may produce indexical activations they did not intend in mixed-audience contexts.

The cumulative empirical picture the production–perception asymmetry analysis would generate is one in which the same configurations carry systematically different indexical activations across listener positions, with the divergence concentrated on shoujo-distinctive configurations and substantially reduced on prescriptively-aligned configurations. The asymmetry is itself the operative content of the prescription–production–perception gap that §3.6 characterized in general terms; its operationalization through the production–perception comparison provides directly observable evidence of the gap's specific shape in the queer-young-adult-and-shoujo-manga case. The asymmetry both empowers and constrains strategic deployment, with the dual function reflecting the broader theoretical principle that indexical fields are construed differently across positions in the social and ideological landscape and that media-derived stylistic resources operate within—rather than escaping—the ideologically contested perceptual ecology that Japanese gendered-language sociolinguistics has long documented.

## 8.6 Indexical Salience and the Conditions for Mediatized Uptake

The contextual, audience-design, awareness, cognitive-pathway, and perceptual analyses developed in §§8.1–8.5 characterize separate dimensions of the mediating structure through which manga-derived exemplars enter queer participants' repertoires and circulate among listener positions. The present concluding section synthesizes these dimensions into a unified characterization of **indexical salience** as the property that emerges from their intersection, and articulates the conditions under which manga-derived exemplars become salient enough to enter individual repertoires versus the conditions under which their salience is muted or contested. The synthesis is the analytical bridge from the specific findings of the present chapter to the broader theoretical evaluation Chapter 9 develops, characterizing what the contextual-mediation analyses reveal about the mechanisms of media-influenced linguistic agency in minority populations.

Indexical salience, on the framework's account, is not a property intrinsic to a form but a relational property that emerges from the intersection of five dimensions:

**Contextual concentration** (§8.1) supplies the situational envelope within which manga-derived exemplars are activated. Casual, written or digital, identity-aligned, affectively heightened contexts together produce the situational conditions under which mediatized exemplars achieve sufficient activation to enter production; formal, spoken, normatively oriented contexts produce conditions under which the same exemplars remain dormant or are overridden by competing register pressures.

**Audience configuration** (§8.2) supplies the interlocutor-mediated calibration through which the situational activation is fine-tuned. Queer in-group with shared fan identity supplies the audience configuration that maximally supports indexical-field activation in its identity-affiliation region; out-group queer-uninformed audiences suppress activation; mixed audiences produce the tactical-performance logic that calibrates deployment to discretion–disclosure management. The audience-mediated calibration determines, within any given situational context, which specific indexical-field regions are activated and which are suppressed.

**Salience hierarchy of forms** (§8.3) supplies the metapragmatic structure through which different forms occupy different positions in the awareness gradient. Stereotype-level forms attract overt metapragmatic attention and are deployed primarily through conscious-appropriation pathways; marker-level forms operate below explicit attention and are deployed primarily through exemplar-driven incorporation; indicator-level patterns operate below the threshold of metapragmatic relevance entirely. The form-level salience determines the **kind of cognitive process** through which the form-context-audience configuration is realized in production.

**Cognitive pathway of incorporation** (§8.4) supplies the mechanistic structure through which the salience-hierarchy positioning is operationalized in individual repertoires. Conscious appropriation operates on stereotype-level forms in high-monitored contexts, depending on metapragmatic instruction received from shoujo's gender-crossing narratives and from broader register-discourse; exemplar-driven incorporation operates on marker-level forms in low-monitored contexts, depending on socially weighted automatic exemplar activation. The two pathways coexist within individual repertoires, with their relative weights determining how the same participant deploys different forms across different contexts.

**Perceptual ecology** (§8.5) supplies the listener-side completion of the meaning-making process. The production–perception asymmetry means that the indexical activations a deployment produces depend on the listener's position and competence, with the same configuration carrying different activations for different listener groups. The asymmetry both enables tactical-performance management of audience-mediated interpretation and constrains it through unintended activations exceeding speaker intention.

These five dimensions intersect to determine, for any specific form-context-audience-pathway-listener configuration, the indexical salience the deployment carries. The framework's prediction is that **manga-derived exemplars achieve maximum salience** when the dimensions align favorably: a stereotype-level configuration deployed through conscious appropriation in casual identity-salient contexts with queer in-group shared-fan-identity audiences, and heard by listeners with the register competence to recognize the manga-specific indexical loadings. They achieve **minimum salience** when the dimensions misalign: a configuration deployed through exemplar-driven incorporation in formal contexts with out-group audiences, heard by listeners without the register competence, would either fail to activate the manga-specific loadings entirely or activate them in ways the speaker did not intend.

The conditions under which mediatized uptake is observed in production are therefore not arbitrary or undifferentiated but **stratified along the five dimensions**, with the cumulative effect being that any aggregate consumption-production correlation must be understood as the integration over a high-dimensional space within which manga's contribution is concentrated in specific regions and absent or attenuated in others. This stratified character is what distinguishes media-derived stylistic uptake from background exemplar saturation: if uptake were undifferentiated, observed patterns would distribute uniformly across the five dimensions; the prediction—and, on the framework's account, the empirical reality—is that uptake distributes systematically with the conditions specified above, supporting the framework's account of contextually activated rather than uniformly distributed mediatized resources.

The synthesis articulates a specific characterization of media-influenced linguistic agency that subsequent theoretical evaluation will take up. Mediatized uptake is, on this characterization:

- **Selective**: mediatized exemplars enter production in particular form-context-audience configurations rather than across all configurations, with the selectivity reflecting the conditions under which indexical salience is achieved.
- **Agentive**: even where exemplar-driven incorporation operates below explicit attention, the cognitive pathway through which the exemplars enter production is structured by the speaker's identity-salient engagement with the source register, with the engagement itself being agentive rather than passive.
- **Stratified along the salience hierarchy**: stereotype-level forms operate through different pathways than marker-level forms, with the salience-hierarchy structure being both a consequence of mediatized uptake and a condition for it.
- **Contextually concentrated**: situational and audience-configurational conditions determine when the uptake manifests in production, with the contextual concentration being itself a key empirical signature of mediatized uptake versus alternative pathways.
- **Ideologically contested**: the asymmetry between speaker indexical activation and listener perceptual activation means that mediatized uptake operates within ideologically contested perceptual ecologies, with the contestation being both empowering (through tactical-performance possibilities) and constraining (through unintended activation effects).

This five-fold characterization specifies what the contextual-mediation analyses of the present chapter reveal about the broader question of media-influenced linguistic agency in minority populations. The characterization is sufficient neither to claim that manga "drives" queer linguistic practice nor to dismiss its contribution as marginal; it specifies the **mechanisms** through which manga's contribution operates, with the specification serving as the analytical bridge to Chapter 9's broader synthesis. The mechanisms operate through indexical fields populated by mediatized stagings, through cognitive pathways of conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation, through audience-design tactical performances calibrated to discretion–disclosure management, through situational concentration in casual identity-salient contexts, and through perceptual ecologies in which the same forms carry different activations across listener positions.

Two final implications carry forward to Chapter 9. **First**, the framework's account of media influence as selective, agentive, salience-stratified, contextually concentrated, and ideologically contested distinguishes it sharply from older accounts of "media-driven change" at the population level. The present chapter's contextual-mediation analysis specifies the granular structure of mediatized influence in ways that population-level accounts cannot, with the granular structure being itself the analytical contribution that the queer-Japanese-shoujo-manga case offers to broader sociolinguistic theory. **Second**, the contextual-mediation analysis specifies the conditions under which **minority linguistic agency** operates within—and against—prescriptive language ideologies. The agency is bounded by the ideological apparatus the prescriptive system reproduces, by the perceptual ecologies that activate prescriptive readings even of cross-prescriptive deployments, and by the structural conditions of the contemporary Japanese sociopolitical environment. But the agency is real: queer participants, on the framework's account, engage manga's curated catalogue of indexical possibilities through conscious and below-awareness pathways, calibrate deployment to situational and audience-mediated conditions, and produce configurations whose indexical activations contribute to identity construction in ways the prescriptive system does not authorize.

The empirical foundation for evaluating these broader claims is now in place. Chapter 7 characterized the distributional patterns; the present chapter has characterized the contextual mechanisms; Chapter 9 takes up the theoretical evaluation of what these jointly reveal about the relationship between mass media, minority identity, and linguistic agency in the queer Japanese case—weighing the framework's account against competing explanations, situating the findings within broader sociolinguistic debates about media influence, and articulating what the case contributes to understanding the limits and mechanisms of media-driven linguistic change.

# 9 Discussion: Media, Minority Identity, and Linguistic Agency

The empirical and analytical chapters of this report have built, layer by layer, toward a question that has animated sociolinguistic theory since the discipline's earliest engagements with broadcast media: under what conditions, and through what mechanisms, does mass media participate in shaping the linguistic practices of those who consume it? The framework articulated in Chapter 2 offered a layered theoretical apparatus for addressing this question; the structural analysis of Chapter 3 specified the architecture of Japanese gendered grammar against which any media-influence claim must be calibrated; Chapter 4 characterized shoujo manga as a curated, modular catalogue of indexical possibilities that the prescriptive binary system does not authorize; Chapter 5 specified queer Japanese young adults as an unusually informative speaker-type whose practice is structurally engaged with the indexical fields the prescriptive system saturates; Chapter 6 specified the methodological infrastructure that would operationalize the framework's predictions; and Chapters 7 and 8 characterized, respectively, the distributional and the contextual-mechanistic patterns that the proposed design would investigate. The present chapter undertakes the synthetic theoretical evaluation that integrates these strands into a sustained discussion of what the queer Japanese shoujo manga case reveals about the relationship between mass media, minority identity, and linguistic agency.

The discussion proceeds in seven steps. §9.1 consolidates the empirical findings into a calibrated multi-pathway account of media influence as it operates in this case. §9.2 weighs exposure-based exemplar accumulation against the three competing pathways the report has tracked throughout. §9.3 specifies the limits of what the case can support theoretically about media-driven change, situating the framework's modest claims within the long-running Trudgill–Stuart-Smith debate. §9.4 articulates what the case substantively contributes to understanding the mechanisms of mediatized influence. §9.5 addresses minority linguistic agency as bounded yet substantive, drawing the case's findings together with broader scholarship on minority linguistic practice. §9.6 specifies the theoretical implications for the four bodies of scholarship the framework integrates. §9.7 addresses generalizability and comparative perspectives, characterizing the case's distinctiveness as the source of its theoretical purchase rather than as a limit on its broader relevance.

Two framings carry forward from preceding chapters and structure the discussion below. First, as Chapters 6 and 7 made explicit, the design described in this report is proposed rather than implemented, and the empirical findings invoked below derive from the published literature reviewed in earlier chapters and from the framework-derived predictions the design would test, rather than from primary fieldwork. The discussion is therefore an evaluation of what the framework, when calibrated against the available empirical record on Japanese gendered language and shoujo manga, supports theoretically, rather than a report of completed research. Second, the discussion explicitly disavows population-level causal claims and adopts what §6.1 termed a "calibrated humility" with respect to what cross-sectional design and stratified-purposive sampling can establish. The theoretical contributions are correspondingly framed as refinements to the conceptual apparatus of media-language sociolinguistics rather than as universal generalizations about media-driven change.

## 9.1 Synthesis of Findings: A Calibrated Multi-Pathway Account of Media Influence

The integrated account of how shoujo manga functions as a stylistic resource for queer Japanese young adults that emerges from Chapters 7 and 8 is best characterized as a **calibrated multi-pathway account**, one whose calibration consists in specifying both what the framework predicts the case demonstrates and the boundary conditions within which those demonstrations operate. The account neither aligns with the strong "media drives change" claims that earlier sociolinguistic debate sometimes entertained nor with the dismissive accounts that minimize media's role to mere reflection of pre-existing patterns. It occupies, instead, the theoretically defensible middle position that the framework articulated in Chapter 2 anticipates and that the empirical analyses of Chapters 7 and 8 substantiate.

The account's principal substantive content can be stated as five interlocking propositions, each grounded in the empirical and analytical findings of preceding chapters.

**Proposition 1: Shoujo manga consumption is associated with specific configurational patterns characteristic of the genre's staged *yakuwarigo*, with the association persisting after community-participation and comparator-media controls.** This proposition consolidates the configuration-level findings developed in §7.4 and the alternative-explanation analyses of §7.8. The shoujo register, as Chapter 4 documented through Unser-Schutz's corpus work, sustains stereotype-level *yakuwarigo* configurations—hyper-feminine *atashi*-with-*wa*, *bishōnen*-aligned *boku*-with-softened-SFPs, *otokoyaku*-inflected princely configurations, masculine-rival *ore*-with-*ze*—whose density and pragmatic intensification exceed what naturalistic majority production supplies. High-consumption queer participants, on the framework's prediction, exhibit configurational repertoires that include these manga-aligned configurations at higher rates than low-consumption participants do, with the differential most pronounced for the configurations whose indexical loadings depend most heavily on shoujo-register competence. The persistence of the association after community-participation controls (which test the community-mediated transmission alternative) and comparator-media controls (which test the parallel-media-exposure alternative) supports the inference that shoujo's contribution operates through pathways partially independent of these alternatives.

**Proposition 2: The association is concentrated among participants whose engagement with shoujo is identity-salient and deep rather than merely frequent, consistent with exemplar-mediated uptake under identity-weighted storage.** This proposition draws on the multi-indicator construct of consumption-intensity articulated in §6.2.3 and the differential indicator-association findings characterized in §§7.1, 7.2, and 7.3. The framework predicts that the identity-relevance and depth indicators—which capture the salience-weighted exemplar storage that exemplar-theoretic frameworks anticipate—correlate more strongly with cross-prescriptive pronoun use and stereotype-level SFP deployment than the frequency indicator does. The differential pattern dissociates exposure-driven exemplar accumulation from identity-mediated uptake and supports the framework's prediction that exemplar weighting depends on identity-salience rather than on raw exposure frequency. As Babel and colleagues' synthesis of exemplar-based theory emphasizes, "phonetic detail beyond the abstract phonological category, often accompanied by social and speaker-specific information, is part of linguistic knowledge," and "variation is socially weighted such that not all experienced items may contribute equivalently to the category"[^10]. The proposition extends this principle from phonetic to morphosyntactic exemplar storage and operationalizes it through the differential association of consumption indicators with production patterns.

**Proposition 3: Media-derived configurations enter production through dual cognitive pathways—conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation—whose relative weights vary across the indicator–marker–stereotype salience hierarchy.** This proposition consolidates the metalinguistic awareness analysis of §8.3 and the dual-pathway account of §8.4. Stereotype-level forms—*kashira*, *wayo*, *teyo*, *ze*, *zo*, and the configuration-level patterns characteristic of *ojō-sama* register or *bishōnen*-rival masculine register—occupy the position of explicit metapragmatic engagement and are deployed primarily through conscious appropriation linked to articulated stylistic projects. Marker-level forms—*na*, *sa*, *yo*, *ne*—operate below explicit attention and are deployed primarily through exemplar-driven incorporation. The proposition is supported by the predicted production–awareness convergence/divergence pattern, with stereotype-level forms exhibiting convergence (production aligns with articulation) and marker-level forms exhibiting divergence (production exhibits patterning without articulation), and by the differential indicator-association pattern whereby identity-relevance correlates more strongly with stereotype-level deployment and frequency correlates more strongly with marker-level deployment.

**Proposition 4: Media-derived configurations are activated in production through situationally and audience-mediated tactical performance, concentrated in casual, written-or-digital, identity-salient contexts and in queer in-group or shared-fan-identity audience configurations.** This proposition consolidates the contextual mediation of §8.1, the audience-design analysis of §8.2, and the indexical-salience synthesis of §8.6. Manga-derived configurations do not distribute uniformly across contexts and audiences but concentrate in particular configurations whose properties align with the conditions under which mediatized exemplars are most likely to be activated in production. The empirical anchor for this proposition is the documented tactical-performance logic of contemporary Japanese queer linguistic practice, characterized most precisely in Itakura's Ni-chōme volleyballer ethnography as deployment "between discretion and disclosure" calibrated to the specific dynamic of community-internal contexts within Japan's "family-oriented, if not homophobic, sociocultural orientation resistant to 'out-and-proud' activism"[^31]. The framework predicts that an analogous tactical logic operates for shoujo-derived configurations among queer young adults more broadly, with the concentration of manga-aligned production in casual identity-salient in-group contexts being the principal empirical signature.

**Proposition 5: The same configurations carry systematically different indexical activations across listener positions, producing a production–perception asymmetry that both empowers and constrains queer speakers' strategic positioning.** This proposition consolidates the production–perception analysis of §8.5 and the broader perceptual-ecology synthesis of §8.6. The principle that "different participants in a speech community may construe the same variable differently" is fundamental to indexical-field theory, and the queer Japanese case extends this principle to the specific case in which the same configuration deployed by a queer participant carries different activations for queer in-group listeners with shoujo-register competence and for non-queer out-group listeners without such competence. The asymmetry empowers tactical performance—configurations whose loadings are perceptible only to recognizers permit speakers to signal in-group while preserving discretion with out-group, addressing the contemporary Japanese sociopolitical condition that combines growing institutional visibility with persistent backlash discourses—and constrains it through unintended activations that exceed speaker intention.

Taken together, these five propositions characterize media influence in the queer Japanese case as **selective, agentive, salience-stratified, contextually concentrated, and ideologically contested**. The selectivity reflects the concentration of manga-aligned production in particular configurations rather than across all production. The agency reflects the role of identity-relevant engagement with the source register in determining whether and how exemplars enter individual repertoires. The salience stratification reflects the operation of distinct cognitive pathways for forms at different positions in the indicator–marker–stereotype hierarchy. The contextual concentration reflects the situational and audience-mediated conditions under which mediatized exemplars achieve sufficient indexical salience for activation. The ideological contestation reflects the production–perception asymmetries that operate within rather than escaping the perceptual ecologies the prescriptive system reproduces.

This calibrated multi-pathway account is the analytical baseline against which the competing explanatory pathways evaluated in §9.2 must be weighed. It is also the content of the report's specific contribution to the broader sociolinguistic conversation about media-language relationships: not the demonstration that shoujo manga "causes" queer linguistic patterns at the population level (a claim the framework explicitly disavows), but the specification of the **mechanisms** through which a stylized media register becomes available as a stylistic resource for speakers whose identities are inadequately served by prescriptive language ideologies, and the **conditions** under which those mechanisms produce observable effects in production, perception, and metapragmatic articulation. The mechanisms operate through indexical fields populated by mediatized stagings, through cognitive pathways of conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation, through audience-design tactical performances, through situational concentration in casual identity-salient contexts, and through perceptual ecologies in which the same forms carry different activations across listener positions. The specification of these mechanisms is what the case offers to broader theoretical understanding of media's role in linguistic practice.

## 9.2 Weighing Exposure-Based Exemplar Accumulation Against Alternative Pathways

The framework's principal causal mechanism—exposure-based exemplar accumulation through socially weighted storage and retrieval—must be evaluated against the three competing pathways flagged throughout the report: deliberate stylistic appropriation, community-mediated transmission, and counter-influences from parallel media and peer networks. The evaluation cannot proceed by treating these as mutually exclusive alternatives, for reasons the framework articulates and the empirical record supports. It proceeds, instead, by examining the differential empirical signatures that distinguish among the pathways and by characterizing the conditions under which each operates with relatively greater or lesser weight in the joint mechanism the case manifests.

**Exposure-based exemplar accumulation** operates through the cognitive mechanism articulated in §2.3 and elaborated in §8.4: speakers store experienced tokens of mediatized speech with their indexical and contextual loadings intact; the stored exemplars are weighted differentially by social and identity-relevance criteria, with identity-salient exemplars receiving disproportionate retention; and subsequent production activates these stored exemplars through processes including target selection, bias, and contextual cuing that operate substantially below explicit attention. The pathway predicts that consumption-intensity, particularly when measured through the depth and identity-relevance indicators specified in §6.2.3, will correlate with production patterns in ways that track the specific configurations the source register stages, with the correlation persisting after controls for community participation and parallel media exposure. The pathway also predicts that the correlation will be more pronounced for marker-level forms whose deployment operates below explicit attention than for stereotype-level forms whose deployment is mediated by conscious metapragmatic engagement.

**Deliberate stylistic appropriation** operates through the cognitive mechanism articulated in §8.4 and grounded in third-wave variationism's account of stylistic agency: speakers consciously select linguistic configurations as part of articulated stylistic projects, drawing on metapragmatic instruction received from explicit narratives (including shoujo manga's gender-crossing storylines, where linguistic choices are made narratively explicit) and from broader register discourse. The pathway predicts that deployment of consciously appropriated forms will exhibit production–awareness convergence (the speaker articulates her own usage in stylistic-project terms, identifies specific exemplar sources, and recognizes the deployment as a deliberate ideological move), and that the deployment will be concentrated in high-monitored contexts (creative-writing tasks, performative settings, identity-disclosure moments) where deliberate selection operates with greatest force. The pathway is more pronounced for stereotype-level forms whose objects-of-explicit-metapragmatic-attention status supports conscious appropriation, and less pronounced for marker-level forms whose below-awareness status renders deliberate selection less applicable.

**Community-mediated transmission** operates through the cognitive mechanism by which speakers acquire stylistic resources from peer networks, community organizations, and digital communities rather than from media directly. The empirical anchor for this pathway is the documented existence of community-internal queer registers in Japan—Abe's lesbian bar register, Maree's and Itakura's *onē-kotoba* tradition—whose forms are transmitted through community participation rather than through manga consumption[^31]. The pathway predicts that participants centrally networked into community infrastructure will exhibit configurations drawn from community-internal registers regardless of consumption-intensity, and that consumption-production correlations will substantially attenuate after community-participation controls if community transmission is the dominant pathway. The pathway is the principal alternative to media-derived exemplar accumulation and is the alternative whose operation is most clearly documented in the existing empirical record on queer Japanese linguistic practice.

**Counter-influences from parallel media and peer networks** operate through the broader media ecology and peer-network dynamics that surround any individual's media engagement. Shoujo manga is one component of a media diet that includes BL, yuri, anime adaptations, light novels, drama CDs, fan fiction, social media discourse, mainstream variety television, and (for some) shōnen and seinen registers. Peer networks through which media discourse circulates—fan communities, online forums, social media platforms—mediate exposure and shape interpretation. The pathway predicts that observed correlations between shoujo consumption and production patterns may partially reflect broader media-engagement effects rather than shoujo-specific contributions, with the test being whether shoujo-specific effects persist after comparator-media controls. The pathway also predicts that platform-mediated peer-network discourse may shape register access in ways that aggregate across multiple media sources, potentially confounding any clean attribution to shoujo specifically.

The differential empirical signatures that distinguish among these pathways are summarized in the following table, which characterizes the framework's predictions for what the proposed design's analyses would reveal about the relative weights of each pathway.

| Pathway | Primary Empirical Signature | Secondary Signatures | Form-Type Concentration |
|---|---|---|---|
| Exposure-based exemplar accumulation | Consumption-production correlation persists after community and comparator-media controls; identity-relevance and depth indicators predict more strongly than frequency | Production–awareness divergence (production without articulation); contextual concentration in low-monitored modalities | Marker-level forms; configurations operating below explicit attention |
| Deliberate stylistic appropriation | Production–awareness convergence (production aligned with articulation); explicit exemplar-source attribution in interview commentary | Concentration in high-monitored contexts (creative writing, performative settings); explicit project articulation | Stereotype-level forms; configurations subject to overt metapragmatic discussion |
| Community-mediated transmission | Substantial attenuation of consumption effects after community-participation controls; community-internal register configurations independent of consumption stratum | Configurations drawn from documented community registers (Abe's lesbian bar register; Itakura's *onē-kotoba*); face-to-face community access as principal predictor | Community-specific registers; tactical-performance configurations |
| Parallel media and peer counter-influences | Substantial attenuation of shoujo-specific effects after comparator-media controls; configurations not specifically staged in shoujo but shared with adjacent registers | Platform-mediated production patterns; fan-community-discourse-aligned configurations | Configurations shared across the broader shoujo–yuri–BL register continuum |

The framework's prediction, articulated in §7.8 and reaffirmed here, is that **the four pathways operate jointly rather than competitively**, with their relative weights varying across forms, contexts, and participant subgroups. The case-specific characterization can be elaborated as follows.

For **stereotype-level forms in identity-salient high-monitored contexts**, deliberate stylistic appropriation is predicted to be the dominant pathway. The conscious-appropriation account, supported by production–awareness convergence and explicit exemplar-source attribution, is most clearly supported when participants articulate their use of *kashira*, *wayo*, *teyo*, *ze*, *zo*, or configuration-level patterns characteristic of *ojō-sama* register or *bishōnen*-rival masculine register in stylistic-project terms that connect deployment to specific manga sources. The framework predicts that this pathway operates with greatest force in creative-writing tasks (Task 4 of §6.6), in identity-disclosure moments within biographical narratives, and in performatively elaborated peer interaction with shared-fan-identity interlocutors.

For **marker-level forms in casual low-monitored contexts**, exposure-based exemplar accumulation is predicted to be the dominant pathway. The exemplar-driven incorporation account, supported by production–awareness divergence and by the differential association of frequency indicators with marker-level deployment, is most clearly supported when participants exhibit systematic patterning of *na*, *sa*, *yo*, *ne* deployment that varies with consumption-intensity but that participants do not recognize or articulate when prompted. The framework predicts that this pathway operates with greatest force in diary-style entries (Task 3 of §6.6), in low-monitored peer interaction, and in role-played intimate communication.

For **community-internal register configurations**, community-mediated transmission is predicted to be the dominant pathway, with media-derived exemplar accumulation operating as a secondary supplement. The configurations characteristic of Abe's lesbian bar register or Itakura's *onē-kotoba*[^31], drawing on community-specific tactical-performance traditions, are predicted to be acquired primarily through community participation rather than through manga consumption, with the consumption-production correlation for these specific configurations attenuating substantially after community-participation controls. The framework's prediction is consistent with the documented heterogeneity within the queer Japanese population: gay male participants with substantial Ni-chōme exposure are predicted to deploy *onē-kotoba* configurations independently of their shoujo engagement, while participants without strong community access are predicted to depend more heavily on mediatized configurations as their principal source of identity-relevant stylistic resources.

For **configurations shared across the broader shoujo-adjacent register continuum** (BL, yuri, josei manga, fan-community discourse), parallel media and peer counter-influences operate as substantial co-determinants alongside shoujo-specific effects. The framework predicts that some apparent shoujo effects—particularly those concerning configurations also present in BL or yuri—are partially attributable to the broader shoujo-adjacent ecology rather than to shoujo proper, with the empirical analyses needing to specify which level of effect is operative.

The differential predictions across these pathway-form-context configurations are what the proposed design is structured to test, and they are what the empirical analyses Chapters 7 and 8 anticipated would generate. The cumulative result is a characterization of media influence in which **no single pathway operates as the sole or primary mechanism**, and in which the joint operation of the pathways produces the patterns that aggregate analyses would observe. The multi-pathway account is theoretically more defensible than any single-mechanism explanation because it respects the cognitive complexity that the layered framework of Chapter 2 anticipates, accommodates the empirical heterogeneity that any rigid single-mechanism account would obscure, and aligns with the evidence-base that the published literature on queer Japanese linguistic practice already documents.

A specific theoretical implication bears emphasis. The multi-pathway account does not relativize the framework's claims about media influence; it specifies them. The claim that shoujo manga functions as a stylistic resource for queer Japanese young adults is not weakened by the recognition that community-mediated transmission, deliberate appropriation, and parallel media exposure also operate; it is sharpened by the recognition that media's specific contribution emerges in particular form-context-pathway configurations whose identification is itself the framework's analytical contribution. The case demonstrates not that media operates in isolation but that media's contribution, when properly specified, is empirically tractable and theoretically distinguishable from the alternative pathways with which it co-operates—and the specification, rather than any monocausal claim, is what the framework supports and what the case substantiates.

## 9.3 The Limits of Media-Driven Linguistic Change: Boundedness, Selection, and Causal Humility

The calibrated multi-pathway account developed in §§9.1–9.2 specifies what the case can support theoretically about the relationship between shoujo manga and queer Japanese linguistic practice. The present section specifies the **limits** of what the case can support—the boundary conditions within which the framework's claims operate, the alternative interpretations that cross-sectional design cannot rule out, and the broader theoretical positioning of the case within the long-running sociolinguistic debate about media-driven linguistic change.

The limits operate along three principal axes.

**Boundedness through contextual concentration.** As §8.1 and §8.6 articulated, manga-derived configurations enter queer participants' production not uniformly but in particular contextual configurations whose properties align with the conditions under which mediatized exemplars achieve indexical salience.
