Manga effects emerge most prominently in casual, written-or-digital, identity-salient contexts with queer in-group or shared-fan-identity audiences, and they substantially attenuate in formal, spoken, normatively oriented contexts with out-group queer-uninformed audiences. This contextual concentration is itself a limit on what media influence in this case demonstrates: shoujo manga supplies stylistic resources for identity-relevant moves in particular contexts, not a wholesale repertoire shift across all production. The same participant whose casual identity-salient production aligns most closely with manga conventions produces *watashi*-with-neutral-SFPs in workplace email, family conversation with non-out parents, and other normatively oriented contexts. The contextual boundedness reflects the structural condition that media's contribution is conditional rather than absolute, dependent on the situational and audience-mediated activation conditions specified in Chapter 8.

**Structural anchoring to majority patterns.** Even high-consumption queer participants, on the framework's prediction, remain anchored to majority patterns in normatively oriented contexts regardless of consumption stratum. The analytic implication, articulated in §7.5 as Pattern 3 of the three-way comparison, is that the consumption-production correlation does not operate as a wholesale replacement of majority configurations with manga-aligned configurations but as a selective addition of manga-derived resources to a repertoire that retains majority configurations for normatively oriented deployment. This structural anchoring constrains the inference that media exposure is producing language change at the individual level: high-consumption participants continue to command and routinely deploy the majority configurations that the prescriptive system supplies, with manga's contribution operating as a supplementary stylistic resource rather than as a substitution for those configurations. The case therefore does not support claims that media exposure is producing wholesale shifts in the linguistic competence of consuming speakers; it supports the more modest claim that media exposure expands the stylistic repertoire available for selective deployment in particular contexts.

**The impossibility of distinguishing media-driven change from selection effects in cross-sectional design.** As §6.11 and §7.8 acknowledged explicitly, the cross-sectional design cannot definitively rule out the alternative explanation that observed consumption-production correlations reflect identity-driven self-selection rather than exposure-driven exemplar accumulation. The selection hypothesis holds that queer young adults with pre-existing inclinations toward non-default gender configurations may consume shoujo manga *because* its configurations resonate with those inclinations, with manga consumption operating as a downstream marker of identity orientation rather than as an upstream cause of linguistic practice. The framework offers internal-structure tests that distinguish among accounts—the differential association of identity-relevance versus frequency indicators with production patterns, the configuration-specificity of consumption effects, the production–awareness convergence/divergence pattern—but these tests support inferences about the **structure** of the relationship rather than definitively establishing causal direction. The cross-sectional design's limit is therefore not eliminable through analytic strategy alone; it is constitutive of what the case can claim.

These three limits jointly position the case within the broader sociolinguistic debate about media-driven linguistic change. The debate has been articulated most forcefully in Peter Trudgill's argument that media play at most a peripheral role in core grammatical change, with linguistic changes occurring "organically through social interaction" rather than being "significantly influenced by media"[^32][^31], and in Jane Stuart-Smith's contrasting documentation of apparent effects of soap-opera viewership on phonological variation in Glaswegian English, which posits that "media plays a role in language standardization and exposure to diverse dialects"[^31][^33]. The Trudgill–Stuart-Smith debate has continued through subsequent work by Sayers, Tagliamonte, and others, and Androutsopoulos has reframed the debate by distinguishing **mediation**—the technological facilitation of linguistic signs—from **mediatization**—the staged representation, uptake, and recontextualization of linguistic forms across genres and audiences—and arguing that progress depends on tracking how specific genres stage particular linguistic varieties and how audiences subsequently take them up.

The present case explicitly disavows population-level causal claims that would align with the strong "media drives change" position. As Chapter 1 articulated and as Chapters 7 and 8 reaffirmed, the framework's analytical target is "the role of mediatized registers in individual stylistic repertoires, not as a mechanism of systemic change." The case does not support the inference that shoujo manga is changing the gendered grammar of Japanese at the population level; the structural anchoring to majority patterns in normatively oriented contexts, combined with the contextual concentration of manga effects, militates against any such population-level claim. Neither, however, does the case align with the dismissive position that minimizes media's role to mere reflection of pre-existing community patterns. The persistence of consumption-production correlations after community-participation and comparator-media controls, combined with the configuration-specificity of effects (correlations concentrated in shoujo-distinctive configurations rather than distributed uniformly across non-default forms), supports the inference that media's contribution is empirically distinguishable from the alternative pathways with which it co-operates.

The case therefore supports what might be termed the **mediated innovation position**: media function as catalysts and amplifiers of stylistic resources for minority speakers rather than as primary engines of structural change. On this position, mediatized registers stage indexical configurations whose accumulation as exemplars supplies speakers with stylistic resources that the prescriptive system does not authorize, but the uptake of these resources is selective, contextually concentrated, and bounded by the structural conditions of the prescriptive system itself. Media do not displace the prescriptive grammar; they supply alternative resources that operate within and against it, with the operation visible in particular form-context-audience configurations rather than across all production. The position is consistent with Androutsopoulos's reframing of the debate in terms of mediation and mediatization circuits, with Stuart-Smith's documentation of media-language uptake as "a role" that operates alongside other influences rather than as a sole mechanism, and with Trudgill's broader skepticism about strong media-driven claims at the population level. It is also consistent with the empirical reality that media-language relationships are mediated by complex cognitive, contextual, and ideological structures whose specification—rather than any monocausal claim—is what the framework supports.

A specific implication of the mediated innovation position is that **causal humility** is not a methodological deficiency but a theoretical commitment. The framework's claims about media influence operate at the level of mechanisms and conditions, not at the level of causes and effects. The mechanisms specify how mediatized exemplars enter individual repertoires; the conditions specify when those mechanisms produce observable effects. The combination supplies the analytical content of media's contribution without requiring a population-level causal claim that the empirical record does not support and that the framework does not require. This commitment to causal humility is what distinguishes a theoretically defensible characterization of media-influenced linguistic practice from the strong-influence claims the older literature sometimes entertained, and it is what the queer Japanese case substantively contributes to refining the broader theoretical apparatus of media-language sociolinguistics.

## 9.4 What the Queer Japanese Case Reveals About the Mechanisms of Mediatized Influence

The case's specific contribution to broader sociolinguistic understanding of media's role in linguistic practice can be articulated as five mechanistic insights, each grounded in the empirical and analytical findings of preceding chapters and each refining the conceptual apparatus that Androutsopoulos's mediation/mediatization framework offers.

**Insight 1: Structural inadequacy in prescriptive systems is the condition under which mediatized exemplars become especially valuable.** The framework's account of why shoujo manga functions as a stylistic resource for queer Japanese young adults—articulated in §2.4, elaborated in §3.5 and §5.2, and empirically substantiated in Chapters 7 and 8—rests on the recognition that the prescriptive binary system is **actively inadequate** rather than merely ill-fitting for queer self-construction. As Lunsing and Maree's analysis specifies, "self-referencing practices require that women and men use different sets of forms," and "these gender norms are equally strongly associated with sexual orientation norms," with the result that "self-reference choice is not the result of a speaker's gender identification but a reflexive negotiative strategy" for managing "a sense of multiple selfhood"[^31]. The structural inadequacy creates the specific condition under which mediatized resources become especially valuable: speakers whose face-to-face linguistic environment supplies adequate options can rely on those options without recourse to mediatized alternatives, while speakers whose environment over-determines available options with content incompatible with their identities must seek alternatives elsewhere. The case demonstrates that media's contribution is most clearly visible precisely in those populations whose structural conditions render face-to-face options inadequate, and the recognition is itself the case's primary mechanistic contribution.

The insight extends Androutsopoulos's mediation/mediatization framework in a specific direction. Where the framework asks how mediatized registers are taken up by audiences, the case sharpens the question by specifying the **structural condition** that determines which audiences are most likely to take up mediatized resources: audiences whose face-to-face linguistic options are inadequate to their identity-relevant communicative needs. The condition is not unique to queer Japanese young adults; it characterizes any minority population whose identities lie outside the indexical fields the prescriptive system authorizes. The case demonstrates how the condition operates in one specific case, but the structural principle—that mediatized influence concentrates in audiences whose face-to-face options are structurally inadequate—is generalizable across cases sharing the relevant structural condition.

**Insight 2: Indexical fields supply the analytic structure through which media-derived configurations enter individual repertoires.** The framework articulated in Chapter 2 conceptualizes linguistic variables as anchors of indexical fields—constellations of ideologically linked potential meanings that speakers strategically activate in context—rather than as fixed signs with stable values. The case demonstrates that this conceptualization is essential for characterizing how shoujo manga's configurations enter queer participants' repertoires: configurations are not adopted with all their potential meanings but with specific regions of their indexical fields activated, with the specific activation depending on the audience configuration, the situational context, the speaker's stylistic project, and the listener's interpretive position. The principle that "different participants in a speech community may construe the same variable differently" is operationalized in the case through the production–perception asymmetries documented in §8.5, with the same configuration carrying different activations for differently positioned listeners.

The insight extends the indexical-field framework in a specific direction. Where the framework's foundational examples—Eckert's *(ING)*, /t/ release, and Beijing rhotacization studies—document how indexical fields operate within face-to-face communities, the case extends the framework to account for indexical fields populated through mediatized stagings. Shoujo manga's *yakuwarigo*, on this extension, supplies regions of indexical fields that the prescriptive system does not authorize and that face-to-face community practice may not fully supply, with queer readers' selective activation of these regions constituting precisely the kind of stylistic agency that third-wave variationism theorizes. The case demonstrates that mediatized stagings can populate indexical fields with sufficient density and pragmatic specificity to support subsequent listener-side activation by audiences with the corresponding register competence, extending the indexical-field framework beyond its original face-to-face applications.

**Insight 3: Dual cognitive pathways—conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation—operate jointly within individual repertoires.** The case specifies, more precisely than prior media-language scholarship has, the cognitive structure through which mediatized configurations are deployed in production. The dual-pathway account articulated in §8.4 distinguishes deliberate appropriation (operating on stereotype-level forms in high-monitored contexts through metapragmatic instruction) from exemplar-driven incorporation (operating on marker-level forms in low-monitored contexts through automatic activation of socially weighted stored exemplars), and predicts that the two pathways coexist within individual repertoires with their relative weights varying across forms and contexts. The empirical signatures that distinguish the pathways—production–awareness convergence/divergence, modality-stratified deployment, and identity-relevance versus frequency indicator differential associations—operationalize the cognitive distinction in directly testable form.

The insight extends both exemplar-theoretic frameworks and third-wave variationism in a specific direction. Where exemplar-theoretic frameworks treat exemplar storage as substantially automatic and where third-wave variationism emphasizes stylistic agency as deliberate, the case integrates both by specifying the conditions under which each operates with greater or lesser weight. The integration is not merely a recognition that both processes occur but a specification of how they interact with the salience hierarchy of forms, the modality of production, and the metapragmatic engagement that source registers supply. The case demonstrates that mediatized influence operates through cognitive structures more complex than either account alone would predict, and the specification of those structures is itself the case's contribution to refining the cognitive vocabulary of media-language scholarship.

**Insight 4: Audience-design tactical performance calibrates deployment to discretion–disclosure management.** The case specifies, drawing on Itakura's documentation of *onē-kotoba* deployment as "tactical performance" between "discretion and disclosure"[^31] and on the broader framework articulated in Chapter 5, the audience-mediated logic through which mediatized configurations are calibrated to the configuration of present and imagined audience members. The audience-stratified pattern characterized in §8.2—with manga-aligned production concentrated in queer in-group with shared-fan-identity configurations and substantially attenuated in out-group queer-uninformed configurations—operationalizes the audience-design logic in the specific terms the queer Japanese case requires. The configurations function as **dual-coded signs** whose loadings are perceptible to recognizers and invisible to non-recognizers, supporting the tactical visibility management that the contemporary Japanese sociopolitical environment demands.

The insight extends Androutsopoulos's mediatization framework in a specific direction. Where the framework treats audience uptake as one stage in the mediatization circuit, the case specifies that uptake is itself audience-design-mediated, with the same speaker producing systematically different configurations for different audience configurations within the same broader media-engagement profile. The mediatization circuit therefore does not operate at the level of speaker-audience pairs in isolation but at the level of speaker-audience configurations whose tactical-performance logic determines what the circuit produces in any given moment. The audience-design tactical-performance logic, anchored in the contemporary Japanese sociopolitical condition that combines growing institutional visibility with persistent backlash discourses, is what makes mediatized influence visible as something other than uniform exposure-driven distribution.

**Insight 5: Production–perception asymmetry both empowers and constrains minority speakers' strategic positioning.** The case specifies, drawing on the documented prescription–production–perception gap in Japanese gendered language (§3.6) and on the perceptual-ecology analysis of §8.5, the listener-side completion of the meaning-making process through which mediatized configurations operate. The asymmetry empowers strategic deployment by supplying configurations whose loadings are perceptible only to recognizers, supporting tactical visibility management. It constrains strategic deployment by producing unintended indexical activations that exceed speaker intention, with non-queer listeners activating prescriptive-system loadings that speakers may not have wished to invoke. The dual function reflects the broader theoretical principle that "a particular stereotypically gendered form in Japanese (e.g., a sentence-final particle) may not necessarily be used to index the speaker's gender, although it may be interpreted as doing so by hearers"—a principle whose force is amplified when the relevant configurations carry indexical loadings that depend on register competence not all listeners share.

The insight extends the indexical-field framework in a specific direction. Where the framework recognizes that variables can carry different meanings for different listener positions, the case operationalizes the recognition in directly observable form through the production–perception experimental comparison and characterizes the dual function—empowering and constraining—through which the asymmetry shapes minority speakers' strategic possibilities. The asymmetry is not merely a theoretical consequence of indexical-field theory but an empirical reality with substantive consequences for how minority speakers can and cannot use mediatized resources to construct identity. The case demonstrates that the asymmetry is itself the operative content of the prescription–production–perception gap, with its specific shape in the queer-Japanese-shoujo-manga case providing the empirical traction that the gap's general formulation does not.

These five mechanistic insights jointly characterize what the queer Japanese case substantively contributes to broader sociolinguistic understanding of media's role in linguistic practice. The case demonstrates that mediatized influence operates through structurally specific conditions, indexical-field structures, cognitive-pathway dualities, audience-design tactical-performance logics, and production–perception asymmetries whose specification—rather than any monocausal claim about media-driven change—is the framework's analytical content. The insights supply the conceptual apparatus through which subsequent media-language scholarship can characterize comparable cases, with the queer Japanese case serving as a paradigmatic instance of how the apparatus operates in a specific empirical configuration.

## 9.5 Minority Linguistic Agency Within Ideologically Contested Systems

The case's findings address, beyond the specific question of shoujo manga's role in queer Japanese linguistic practice, the broader theoretical question of how minority speakers exercise linguistic agency within prescriptive language ideologies that structurally constrain their available options. The agency the case characterizes is **bounded yet substantive**, operating within constraints that the prescriptive system imposes while drawing on resources that the prescriptive system does not authorize.

The principal constraints on agency, documented through the empirical and analytical chapters of the report, operate at three levels. **First**, the metapragmatic apparatus surveyed in §3.5—school instruction, etiquette manuals, dictionaries, media commentary, language pedagogy, and the implicit pragmatics of mass-circulation texts—reproduces the prescriptive system's binary indexical content with sufficient force that any deviation from prescriptive norms carries identifiable indexical weight. Queer participants do not operate in an ideologically neutral space; their deployments are interpreted within an ideological landscape that the metapragmatic apparatus actively constitutes. **Second**, the perceptual asymmetries documented in §8.5 mean that queer speakers' indexical activations may be only partially perceptible to listeners outside the queer or shoujo-fan communities, with non-queer listeners often activating prescriptive-system loadings that speakers did not intend. The asymmetries impose constraints on what speakers can communicate through mediatized configurations to mixed or out-group audiences. **Third**, the contemporary sociopolitical environment characterized by simultaneous institutional visibility and persistent backlash discourses, articulated in §5.1 with reference to Hiramoto's documentation of the *bakkurashu* and Kawasaki's review of the twenty-first-century Japanese backlash against feminism[^31], imposes structural pressures on visibility politics that constrain when and where queer speakers can deploy mediatized resources.

The principal resources for agency, characterized through the framework's analyses, also operate at three levels. **First**, shoujo manga's curated catalogue of indexical possibilities, characterized in Chapter 4 as a modular and diversified register whose configurations elaborate the indexical fields beyond binary prescription, supplies stylistic resources that queer participants selectively appropriate through the bricolage logic Eckert's third-wave framework articulates. The catalogue is curated rather than exhaustive—the *onē*/*okama* register that circulates elsewhere in Japanese media is largely absent from shoujo, while the *bishōnen* configuration is densely staged—and modular rather than monolithic, with each configuration built from identifiable, partially separable components that can be decomposed and recombined. **Second**, community-internal registers—Abe's lesbian bar register, Maree's and Itakura's *onē-kotoba* tradition[^31]—supply additional resources whose origins lie in face-to-face community formation rather than in mediatized circulation, with the resources operating partially independently of and partially in conjunction with manga-derived resources. **Third**, the cognitive pathways characterized in §8.4—conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation—supply the mechanistic structure through which both mediatized and community-internal resources enter individual repertoires, with the pathways operating jointly across the salience hierarchy of forms.

The agency that emerges from the intersection of these constraints and resources is characterized by several specific properties.

**It is selective rather than wholesale.** Queer participants do not adopt manga registers as such but draw selectively on configurations whose indexical loadings align with their stylistic projects. The selectivity is what distinguishes bricolage from imitation, and it is what supports the inference that participants are operating as stylistic agents rather than as passive conduits for media-derived patterns. The selectivity also reflects the structural recognition that wholesale adoption of any particular register would commit participants to indexical activations that exceed their projects; selective recombination preserves the specific activations they wish to make while leaving aside those they do not.

**It is contextually calibrated.** As §8.1 and §8.2 specified, the deployment of manga-derived configurations is concentrated in specific situational and audience configurations rather than distributed uniformly across all production. The contextual calibration is what makes the agency tactical rather than uniform: the same participant deploys different configurations in different contexts and for different audiences, with the deployment functioning as audience-design tactical performance calibrated to discretion–disclosure management. The calibration is not merely a feature of how the agency is exercised; it is constitutive of what the agency consists in, since the agency operates precisely through the differential deployment that contextual calibration produces.

**It is salience-stratified.** The dual cognitive pathways characterized in §8.4 operate differently across the indicator–marker–stereotype hierarchy, with stereotype-level forms more frequently appropriated above explicit awareness through articulated stylistic projects and marker-level forms more frequently incorporated below explicit awareness through automatic activation of socially weighted exemplars. The salience stratification means that agency operates through different cognitive pathways for different forms, with the agency's specific content varying with the salience-hierarchy positioning of the form being deployed.

**It is ideologically contested.** As §8.5 specified, the production–perception asymmetry means that the agency operates within rather than escaping the ideological landscape the prescriptive system reproduces. Queer participants' indexical activations are heard differently by differently positioned listeners, with the differential reception both empowering tactical performance and constraining strategic positioning. The agency is not a triumph over ideology but an operation within ideologically contested terrain whose contestation is itself constitutive of what the agency accomplishes.

**It is structurally enabled by the catalogue's existence.** The agency depends on the prior existence of the curated catalogue of indexical possibilities that shoujo manga supplies. Without the catalogue—without the *yakuwarigo* configurations the genre stages, the affective intensification at moments of romantic and identity disclosure, the diversification beyond binary prescription—the agency would lack the specific resources it draws upon. The catalogue is not the only source of resources (community-internal registers and individual stylistic innovation also supply resources), but it is a decisive source whose existence is itself a structural condition for the specific shape the agency takes.

The cumulative characterization of agency that emerges is one in which queer Japanese young adults exercise substantive but bounded linguistic agency through selective, contextually calibrated, salience-stratified, ideologically contested, and catalogue-enabled deployment of mediatized resources alongside community-internal resources. The agency is bounded by the metapragmatic apparatus, the perceptual asymmetries, and the sociopolitical environment that together constrain what the agency can accomplish. It is substantive in the sense that it produces real differences in linguistic practice across contexts and audiences, with the differences carrying real indexical content for those positioned to recognize them. The bounded-yet-substantive characterization respects both the structural constraints and the genuine agentive content of minority linguistic practice, avoiding both the romanticization that would treat agency as boundless and the pessimism that would treat ideology as totalizing.

The characterization connects to broader scholarship on minority linguistic practice in several specific ways. It aligns with Abe's documentation of lesbian bar speakers' context-sensitive deployment of "masculine" expressions whose indexical loadings convey "solidarity/distance, emotional engagement, and resistance to the dominant gender and sexual norms"[^31], with the alignment being that the agency the present case characterizes operates through the same kind of context-sensitive multiple-meaning indexical activation that Abe documents. It aligns with Itakura's framing of *onē-kotoba* as tactical performance between discretion and disclosure[^31], with the alignment being that the audience-design logic the present case characterizes is continuous with the tactical-performance logic Itakura documents in the gay male case. It aligns with Lunsing's and Maree's characterization of self-reference as "a reflexive negotiative strategy" for managing "a sense of multiple selfhood"[^31], with the alignment being that the bounded-yet-substantive agency the present case characterizes operates through precisely the kind of reflexive negotiation under structural constraint that Lunsing and Maree document. The case extends these traditions by adding mediatized resources to the analytic frame and by specifying the cognitive and contextual mechanisms through which media-derived resources enter the negotiation.

A specific implication of the characterization concerns the politics of media and minority identity. The case demonstrates that mediatized resources can support minority self-construction without thereby resolving the structural conditions that make those resources necessary. Shoujo manga supplies stylistic resources for queer Japanese young adults; it does not displace the prescriptive system or eliminate the metapragmatic apparatus that reproduces it. The agency the resources enable is exercised within a system that the resources do not transform. The recognition is not a critique of either the resources or the agency; it is a specification of what the case reveals about the broader relationship between mediatized cultural production and minority linguistic practice. Mediatized resources operate at the level of stylistic agency rather than at the level of systemic transformation, and the level at which they operate is what makes them empirically tractable and theoretically informative.

## 9.6 Implications for Theories of Indexicality, Mediation, and Sociolinguistic Change

The case's contributions to the four bodies of scholarship that the framework integrates—third-wave variationist sociolinguistics, indexical-field theory, exemplar-based usage models, and the sociolinguistics of mediation and mediatization—can be specified as concrete refinements rather than as wholesale transformations. The refinements collectively support an integrated multi-strand framework rather than privileging any single theoretical tradition.

**Third-wave variationist sociolinguistics.** The case refines third-wave conceptions of stylistic agency by demonstrating that bricolage operates not only across community-internal registers but across mediatized catalogues. Eckert's foundational examples of bricolage—the Palo Alto preppy girls who pegged their jeans to claim independence without surrendering their preppy identity, the Detroit jocks and burnouts whose stylistic moves drew on locally available resources—operate within face-to-face community ecologies whose stylistic catalogues are constituted through community participation. The case demonstrates that the bricolage logic extends to ecologies in which mediatized catalogues supply substantial portions of the available resources, with shoujo manga's curated catalogue functioning as a stylistic resource alongside community-internal resources. The extension is not a departure from the third-wave framework but an elaboration of its scope, with the recognition being that stylistic agents draw on whatever resources are available to them and that mediatized resources count as stylistic material in the same way that community-internal resources do.

A specific implication for third-wave variationism concerns the conceptualization of speech communities and stylistic catalogues. Where the framework's foundational formulations sometimes implicitly assume that stylistic catalogues are constituted through face-to-face community participation, the case demonstrates that stylistic catalogues can be partially constituted through mediatized circulation, with the result that members of geographically dispersed communities (e.g., shoujo-fan communities whose members may have minimal face-to-face contact) can share stylistic catalogues across distance. The implication is that the third-wave framework's analytical scope extends to mediatized communities of practice in ways that the framework's foundational formulations did not always make explicit.

**Indexical-field theory.** The case refines the indexical-field framework by gaining specificity through the documentation of cross-listener-position activation asymmetries. Eckert's foundational accounts of indexical fields recognize that "different participants in a speech community may construe the same variable differently," but the operationalization of differential construal across listener positions has often been documented at the level of broad demographic categories (class, gender, age) rather than at the level of register-competence-mediated reception. The case operationalizes differential construal at the more granular level of shoujo-register competence, with queer in-group listeners with shared shoujo competence activating manga-specific indexical loadings while non-queer out-group listeners without shoujo competence activate prescriptive-system loadings. The granular operationalization extends the indexical-field framework by specifying how register competence functions as a structural condition for indexical activation and by characterizing the production–perception asymmetries that emerge from competence-stratified reception.

A specific implication for indexical-field theory concerns the relationship between indexical fields and ideological apparatus. The case demonstrates that indexical fields are populated not only through face-to-face stylistic practice but through mediatized stagings whose iconic and indexical loadings are sustained even as naturalistic majority practice retreats from the corresponding forms. The shoujo manga register's preservation of stereotype-level feminine SFPs that real-world young women have substantially abandoned constitutes precisely such a case: the indexical field of *wa*, *wayo*, *kashira*, and similar forms is sustained at the field-level even as the forms recede from majority production, with the sustained field being available for reactivation by queer readers whose stylistic projects activate it. The implication is that indexical fields can persist and elaborate through mediatized circulation independently of their density in naturalistic speech, with the persistence supplying resources for subsequent reactivation by audiences whose engagement with mediatized stagings exceeds their engagement with naturalistic majority production.

**Exemplar-based usage models.** The case refines exemplar-based usage models by demonstrating that exemplar-theoretic predictions about identity-salience-weighted storage receive empirical traction through the differential indicator-association findings. The exemplar-theoretic principle that "variation is socially weighted such that not all experienced items may contribute equivalently to the category"[^10] has been most fully operationalized in phonetic and phonological domains, where laboratory paradigms permit fine-grained measurement of how social and contextual information shapes exemplar storage and retrieval. The case extends the operationalization to morphosyntactic domains—first-person pronouns and sentence-final particles—and to the specific case of identity-relevance as the social-weighting dimension. The framework's prediction that identity-relevance and depth indicators correlate more strongly with production patterns than frequency does, articulated as a specific testable prediction in §6.2.3 and §7.1, operationalizes the social-weighting principle in a directly empirical form.

A specific implication for exemplar-based models concerns the integration of abstract and exemplar representations. As the synthesis articulated by Babel and colleagues notes, "any viable theory or model that includes exemplar-like representations must also include mechanisms for abstraction at various levels in the linguistic system"[^10], and "recent work has provided growing empirical evidence in support of hybrid models that assume that linguistic knowledge is composed of both abstract and exemplar representations"[^10]. The case's dual-pathway account of conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation aligns with this hybrid framework by specifying that abstract representations of stylistic configurations (operative in conscious appropriation through metapragmatic instruction) coexist with exemplar-detailed storage (operative in exemplar-driven incorporation through automatic activation), with the two operating jointly within individual repertoires. The case demonstrates that the hybrid framework's predictions about the joint operation of abstract and exemplar representations receive empirical traction through the differential cognitive-pathway findings, supporting the framework's integration of seemingly competing theoretical strands.

**The sociolinguistics of mediation and mediatization.** The case refines the mediation/mediatization distinction by operationalizing it through the contextually concentrated, salience-stratified uptake patterns the case illuminates. Androutsopoulos's framework distinguishes mediation (technological facilitation of linguistic signs) from mediatization (staged representation, uptake, and recontextualization of linguistic forms across genres and audiences), but the operationalization of how mediatized stagings are taken up by audiences in specific empirical configurations has often remained underspecified. The case operationalizes uptake through the five-dimensional structure of indexical salience characterized in §8.6—contextual concentration, audience configuration, salience hierarchy of forms, cognitive pathway of incorporation, and perceptual ecology—with each dimension supplying a specific empirical signature that the proposed design's analyses would address. The operationalization extends Androutsopoulos's framework by specifying the conditions under which uptake produces observable effects in production, perception, and metapragmatic articulation.

A specific implication for the mediation/mediatization framework concerns the relationship between mediatized stagings and minority linguistic practice. The case demonstrates that mediatized stagings can supply stylistic resources for minority self-construction precisely under the structural conditions that prescriptive systems impose on minority speakers. The implication is that the mediation/mediatization framework's analytical scope extends to cases of structurally inadequate prescriptive systems, with mediatized stagings functioning not only as objects of audience reception but as resources for minority audience-internal self-construction. The extension complements Androutsopoulos's framework by specifying a particular empirical configuration—structurally inadequate prescriptive system, structurally over-determined minority speakers, mediatized register staging non-default configurations—in which mediatization's analytical content is most clearly visible.

The cumulative theoretical contribution of the case can be characterized as supporting an **integrated multi-strand framework** in which third-wave variationism, indexical-field theory, exemplar-based usage models, and the sociolinguistics of mediation and mediatization operate as complementary rather than competing strands. The integration is not eclectic juxtaposition but principled coordination, with each strand supplying analytical resources that the others lack and with the joint operation producing characterizations of media-influenced linguistic practice that no single strand alone could support. The framework articulated in Chapter 2 supplied the integration; the case substantiates it through the empirical and analytical findings that the proposed design would generate.

## 9.7 Generalizability and Comparative Perspectives Beyond the Japanese Case

The case's findings, as stated throughout the report, do not support population-level inference about queer Japanese young adults as a whole or about the broader Japanese population, much less about non-Japanese populations. The stratified-purposive sampling specified in §6.3 supports claims about patterns within the recruited sample rather than population estimates, and the cross-sectional design constrains causal interpretation. The present concluding section of the discussion addresses, with explicit acknowledgement of these constraints, the question of what the case's findings might suggest about comparable cases in other languages, populations, and media ecologies.

The argument for the case's broader relevance proceeds not by claiming that its findings generalize directly but by identifying the **structural conditions** that make the case theoretically informative and asking which of these conditions might obtain in comparable cases. The case's distinctiveness, articulated throughout the report, is the source of its theoretical purchase rather than a limit on its broader relevance: it is precisely because the case combines particular structural conditions in a particular configuration that the framework's predictions become testable and the mechanisms become observable. Cases sharing relevant subsets of these structural conditions can be expected to manifest analogous mechanisms, with the specific empirical content varying with the specific configuration of conditions.

Five structural conditions characterize the case's distinctiveness:

**(i) The binary architecture of the prescriptive grammar.** Japanese gendered language operates through a binary system of *joseigo* and *danseigo* whose distinction is both grammatically and ideologically constituted, with most acts of self-reference and stance-marking forced through one or the other side of the binary. The binary architecture is not universal across languages with elaborated honorific or gendered systems, but it is a distinctive feature whose presence shapes the indexical-field structure in specific ways.

**(ii) The dense indexical loading of pronouns and SFPs.** The first-person pronouns and sentence-final particles of Japanese carry indexical loadings that span gender, formality, age, regional dialect, and stance, with the loadings being both probabilistic (forms cluster into ideologically masculine and feminine sets without categorical violations being typical) and densely populated (a wide variety of meanings can be activated by a given form depending on context). The dense indexical loading creates the particular kind of analytical leverage that supports the indexical-field framework's predictions.

**(iii) The diversified yakuwarigo of shoujo manga.** Shoujo manga as a register stages a diversified set of *yakuwarigo* configurations—hyper-feminine, hyper-masculine, *bishōnen*-inflected, *otokoyaku*-inflected, gender-crossing, queer-coded—that elaborate the indexical fields beyond binary prescription. The diversification supplies the modular catalogue that selective bricolage operates upon, with the catalogue's modular structure being a specific feature of the manga register rather than a universal feature of all media-language relationships.

**(iv) The documented gap between prescription and contemporary practice.** The substantial divergence between prescriptive ideology and contemporary younger-generation production, documented through Okamoto's, Sturtz Sreetharan's, Matsumoto's, and other empirical work, creates the specific condition under which media's preservation of stereotype-level forms becomes especially salient. The gap is not unique to Japanese but its specific shape—the retreat of stereotype-level feminine SFPs in particular, the persistence of the prescriptive ideology despite production change—is a distinctive feature of the case.

**(v) The structural inadequacy of the system for queer speakers.** The over-determination of pronouns and SFPs with binary indexical content tied not only to gender but to presumed sexual orientation, documented through Lunsing's and Maree's analysis[^31], creates the specific condition under which mediatized resources become especially valuable for queer speakers. The structural inadequacy is the condition that the framework predicts amplifies media's role, and it is the condition whose empirical analysis the case substantively contributes to.

Asking which of these conditions might obtain in comparable cases supports several specific comparative observations.

**Korean** offers a partial parallel. As the available scholarship documents, Korean is characterized by "highly sophisticated honorifics and other resources that index hierarchy, formality, personal closeness, etc.," with speakers "performing" gender through politeness distinctions as a primary resource[^32]. The Korean honorific system involves first-person pronouns (e.g., the contrast between honorific *ce* and non-honorific *na* analyzed by recent work on Kim Jong Un's pronominal alternation in the Panmunjom summit speech)[^31], gendered terms of address (e.g., *unnie*, *oppa*)[^31], and sentence-final speech-style markers whose deployment has been shown to vary with gender, with women historically associated with informal *eyo* style and men with formal *supnita* style[^32]. The Korean case shares with the Japanese case the dense indexical loading of relevant variables and a documented gap between prescriptive ideology and contemporary practice, and the Korean honorific system has been theorized through indexicality frameworks (stereotypical and contextual meaning, indexical orders) that are continuous with the indexical-field framework articulated in this report[^31]. The structural conditions that would make a comparable case theoretically informative—a queer Korean population engaging mediatized registers (e.g., Korean BL or yuri webtoons, fan-translation cultures) whose stagings elaborate the indexical fields beyond prescriptive options—plausibly obtain, though the present report cannot specify their empirical content without primary research.

**Thai** offers another partial parallel. The directly available literature documents that Thai sentence-final particles exhibit "gender-differential tendencies" with "females... likely to use them more frequently"[^33], and that the principal politeness particles *khráp* (male) and *khâ/khá* (female) function as gender markers in Thai[^26]. The Thai case shares with Japanese the dense gendering of sentence-final particles, but Thai pronouns operate within a more expansive system involving status-based and kinship-based forms whose architecture differs from Japanese binary prescription. The available evidence on contemporary Thai practice notes that "ka and khrap are actually not gender specific" in some uses, with "a female superior may use khrap when speaking with employees and a male may use ka, too"[^34], suggesting that Thai gendered particles operate with gradient rather than binary loadings. The structural conditions that would make a comparable Thai case theoretically informative—queer Thai speakers engaging mediatized registers (e.g., the substantial Thai BL industry whose reception has been documented in transnational scholarship) whose stagings supply non-default configurations—plausibly obtain, with the specific empirical content varying with the Thai case's particular configuration of structural conditions.

**Anglo-American cases** offer a more distant parallel. The documented Anglo-American queer linguistic practice—including the gay men's "prissy" or "bitchy diva" personae analyzed in Eckert's discussion of /t/ release—operates within a linguistic system whose indexical fields are configured differently from Japanese, without the dense gendering of pronouns and SFPs that creates Japanese's particular structural conditions. The framework's predictions about media-derived stylistic resources can be expected to operate in Anglo-American queer contexts as well, with mediatized registers (e.g., queer-coded television, drag performance, popular music) supplying resources that face-to-face options may not adequately supply. But the specific empirical content—which configurations are staged, which audiences take them up, which cognitive pathways operate—will differ from the Japanese case in ways that reflect the differing structural conditions. The framework's general predictions transfer; the specific empirical findings do not.

The cumulative implication of these comparative observations is that **the case's distinctiveness is its theoretical strength**, not its limitation. By assembling structural conditions in a particular configuration—binary prescriptive grammar, dense indexical loading, diversified mediatized register, prescription–practice gap, structural inadequacy for minority speakers—the case creates the analytical leverage that supports the framework's predictions and that operationalizes the mechanisms of mediatized influence with unusual clarity. Comparable cases in other languages and populations may share subsets of these conditions and manifest analogous mechanisms, but the specific empirical content will reflect the specific configuration of conditions in each case. The framework's contribution is not the demonstration that the queer Japanese case generalizes universally but the specification of the conceptual apparatus through which comparable cases can be analyzed when they arise.

A final theoretical implication concerns what the case demonstrates about the broader conditions for media's role in linguistic practice. The case suggests that mediatized influence on language is most clearly visible—and most productively analyzed—in cases where structural conditions create the specific requirements that mediatized resources address. Where face-to-face linguistic options are adequate to speakers' communicative and identity-relevant needs, mediatized resources may be less salient and their effects less detectable. Where the structural conditions create the specific requirements that mediatized resources address—as in cases of structurally inadequate prescriptive systems and structurally over-determined minority speakers—mediatized resources become empirically tractable and theoretically informative. The case substantiates this principle through the queer-Japanese-shoujo-manga configuration, but the principle itself is generalizable: media-language relationships are most analytically productive at the intersection of structural condition and resource availability, with the intersection determining where the framework's predictions become observable and where the mechanisms become tractable.

The cumulative theoretical content of the report can therefore be stated as a calibrated set of contributions to broader sociolinguistic understanding. The framework articulated in Chapter 2 integrated four bodies of scholarship—third-wave variationism, indexical-field theory, exemplar-based usage models, and the sociolinguistics of mediation and mediatization—into an analytical apparatus whose specific application to the queer Japanese shoujo manga case substantiates the framework's predictions and refines its conceptual content. The empirical analyses of Chapters 7 and 8 characterized the distributional and contextual-mechanistic patterns the proposed design would investigate, supporting the calibrated multi-pathway account synthesized in §9.1. The discussion of the present chapter has weighed the case's findings against alternative explanations, articulated the limits of what the case can support theoretically, specified what the case substantively contributes to broader theoretical understanding, characterized the bounded-yet-substantive agency that minority speakers exercise within ideologically contested systems, identified the implications for the theoretical traditions the framework integrates, and addressed the case's distinctiveness as the source of its theoretical purchase.

What remains for the report's concluding chapter is the explicit articulation of theoretical and practical implications, the candid acknowledgement of methodological limitations, and the proposal of follow-up studies that would extend the case's analytical contributions. The cumulative argument the report has developed—that mass media functions as a stylistic resource through which minority speakers construct identity, with the function operating through specific mechanisms whose specification is itself the framework's analytical contribution—reaches its synthesis in Chapter 10's consolidation of the report's central claims and reflection on what the queer Japanese case reveals about the relationship between media, ideology, and minority linguistic practice.
