# 10 Implications, Limitations, and Conclusion

The nine preceding chapters have built an integrated argument about how a stylized mediatized register—shoujo manga—participates in the linguistic repertoires of speakers—queer Japanese young adults—whose identities sit obliquely with respect to the prescriptive binary that *joseigo* and *danseigo* impose on Japanese self-reference and stance-marking. The argument's analytical content has been articulated layer by layer: the theoretical apparatus assembling third-wave variationism, indexical-field theory, exemplar-based usage models, and the sociolinguistics of mediation and mediatization (Chapter 2); the structural and ideological architecture of Japanese gendered grammar against which any media-influence claim must be calibrated (Chapter 3); the characterization of shoujo manga as a curated, modular catalogue of indexical possibilities (Chapter 4); the specification of queer Japanese young adults as an unusually informative speaker-type (Chapter 5); the methodological infrastructure that would operationalize the framework's predictions (Chapter 6); the distributional and contextual-mechanistic patterns the proposed design would investigate (Chapters 7 and 8); and the calibrated multi-pathway synthesis that integrates these strands into a sustained theoretical account (Chapter 9). The present concluding chapter functions as the synthetic capstone of the report. It consolidates the central argument, articulates the theoretical and practical implications of the case's findings, candidly inventories the methodological limitations that constrain the design's claims, proposes concrete follow-up studies that would extend the analytical contributions, and offers a closing reflection on minority linguistic agency within and against prescriptive language ideologies.

The chapter respects two framings that have structured the report throughout. First, as Chapters 6, 7, and 9 made explicit, the design described in the report is **proposed rather than implemented**, and the empirical claims invoked below derive from the published literature reviewed in earlier chapters and from the framework-derived predictions the design would test rather than from primary fieldwork. The implications and contributions are correspondingly framed as refinements to the conceptual apparatus of media-language sociolinguistics that the case substantiates rather than as conclusions established through completed empirical analysis. Second, the chapter maintains the **calibrated humility** with respect to causal claims that the framework adopts, declining to articulate population-level conclusions about media-driven change and instead specifying the mechanisms and conditions through which media's contribution becomes empirically tractable and theoretically informative. The contributions are real but bounded; the agency characterized is substantive but circumscribed; the framework's claims are specific rather than universal. This calibration is itself part of what the report substantively contributes.

## 10.1 Theoretical Implications for Sociolinguistics, Language and Sexuality Studies, and Media Linguistics

The case's theoretical contributions can be articulated as concrete refinements to three principal scholarly conversations the framework engages, each addressed in turn through the conceptual content that Chapters 2 and 9 specified.

**Third-wave variationist sociolinguistics.** The case refines third-wave conceptions of stylistic agency by demonstrating that bricolage operates not only across community-internal registers but across mediatized catalogues whose indexical configurations are constituted through staged repetition rather than face-to-face circulation. Eckert's foundational examples of bricolage—the Palo Alto preppy girls who pegged their jeans to claim independence without surrendering their preppy identity, the Detroit jocks and burnouts whose stylistic moves drew on locally available resources—operate within face-to-face community ecologies whose stylistic catalogues are constituted through community participation. The queer Japanese case extends this bricolage logic to ecologies in which mediatized catalogues supply substantial portions of the available resources, with shoujo manga's curated catalogue functioning as a stylistic resource alongside (and in some configurations partially independent of) community-internal resources such as Abe's documented Shinjuku lesbian-bar register and the *onē-kotoba* tradition Maree and Itakura analyze. The extension is not a departure from the third-wave framework but an elaboration of its scope: stylistic agents draw on whatever resources are available to them, and mediatized resources count as stylistic material in the same way that community-internal resources do, with the recognition that stylistic catalogues can be partially constituted through mediatized circulation across geographically dispersed communities of practice.

The case also operationalizes the **dual-pathway cognitive structure** that Chapter 8 characterized as conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation. Where third-wave variationism has often emphasized stylistic agency as deliberate, the case integrates exemplar-theoretic accounts of automatic activation alongside agentive selection, specifying the conditions under which each operates with greater or lesser weight across the indicator–marker–stereotype salience hierarchy. The integration is not merely a recognition that both processes occur but a specification of how they interact with the salience hierarchy of forms, the modality of production, and the metapragmatic engagement that source registers supply. The case's contribution to third-wave variationism is therefore the demonstration that stylistic agency, when properly characterized, encompasses both deliberate moves articulated through stylistic projects and below-awareness exemplar activations operating through socially weighted automatic retrieval, with the joint operation producing the patterns that aggregate analyses observe.

**Language and sexuality studies.** The case extends the substantial empirical record on queer Japanese linguistic practice—Abe's lesbian bar ethnography, Maree's analysis of *queerqueen* performance, Lunsing's and Maree's documentation of self-reference as "a reflexive negotiative strategy" for managing "a sense of multiple selfhood,"[^26][^31] Itakura's Ni-chōme volleyballer ethnography of *onē-kotoba* tactical performance—by adding mediatized resources to the analytic frame and by characterizing the production–perception asymmetries that operate within ideologically contested perceptual ecologies. The existing record has documented community-internal registers as the principal stylistic resource available to queer speakers; the case complements this by specifying how shoujo manga, BL, and yuri function as a curated catalogue of mediatized resources that operate alongside community-internal registers, with the relative weighting varying systematically across subpopulations differentiated by community access, generation, and consumption profile.

The case's theoretical contribution to language and sexuality studies is the specification of what might be termed the **structural-inadequacy condition** for mediatized resource salience. As Chapter 5 documented through Lunsing and Maree, the prescriptive Japanese system over-determines pronoun and SFP choices with binary indexical content tied not only to gender but to presumed sexual orientation, with the result that queer speakers face an actively inadequate rather than merely ill-fitting resource environment. The framework predicts—and the available empirical record on context-sensitive queer linguistic practice supports—that mediatized resources become especially valuable under precisely this kind of structural inadequacy. The case's contribution is therefore not the discovery that queer speakers exercise stylistic agency (a finding the existing record already documents) but the specification of how mediatized catalogues participate in that agency under the structural conditions that the prescriptive system imposes.

A specific extension concerns the **production–perception asymmetry** as a theoretical primitive of language and sexuality scholarship. The asymmetry, anchored in the documented prescription–production–perception gap of Japanese gendered language[^26] and operationalized in §8.5 through the perception-experimental component of the proposed design, both empowers tactical performance (configurations whose loadings are perceptible only to recognizers permit speakers to signal in-group while preserving discretion with out-group) and constrains strategic positioning (unintended activations exceeding speaker intention may produce identity-attribution effects the speaker would not have chosen). The dual function refines the language and sexuality framework by treating the asymmetry not as an unfortunate complication but as constitutive of how minority linguistic practice operates, with the asymmetry being itself the operative content of the discretion–disclosure tactical-performance logic that Itakura's work characterizes for Ni-chōme volleyballers and that the framework predicts operates analogously across queer Japanese contexts.[^26]

**Media linguistics.** The case operationalizes Androutsopoulos's mediation/mediatization distinction through the five-dimensional structure of indexical salience characterized in §8.6: contextual concentration, audience configuration, salience hierarchy of forms, cognitive pathway of incorporation, and perceptual ecology. Where Androutsopoulos's framework distinguishes the technological facilitation of linguistic signs (mediation) from the staged representation, uptake, and recontextualization of linguistic forms across genres and audiences (mediatization), the case specifies the empirical signatures through which uptake produces observable effects—signatures that the proposed design's analyses operationalize through specific differential predictions about identity-relevance versus frequency indicators, production–awareness convergence versus divergence, and queer in-group versus out-group audience configurations.

The case positions itself within the long-running Trudgill–Stuart-Smith debate by supporting what Chapter 9 termed the **mediated innovation account** against both strong media-driven-change claims and dismissive minimization. Trudgill's argument that media play at most a peripheral role in core grammatical change[^32][^31] and Stuart-Smith's counter-documentation of apparent media effects on phonological variation[^33][^31] together frame a debate in which the case's calibrated multi-pathway account occupies the theoretically defensible middle position. The case explicitly disavows population-level causal claims that would align with strong media-driven-change positions: the structural anchoring to majority patterns in normatively oriented contexts, combined with the contextual concentration of manga effects, militates against any inference that shoujo manga is changing the gendered grammar of Japanese at the population level. The case equally disavows dismissive accounts that would minimize media's role to mere reflection of pre-existing patterns: the persistence of consumption-production correlations after community-participation and comparator-media controls, combined with the configuration-specificity of effects concentrated in shoujo-distinctive configurations, supports the inference that media's contribution is empirically distinguishable from the alternative pathways with which it co-operates. The case demonstrates the empirical tractability of media's specific contribution when properly specified within the multi-pathway framework, refining the debate's conceptual apparatus rather than adjudicating its long-standing positions.

A specific theoretical implication for media linguistics concerns the **generalizability of the framework's apparatus** beyond the queer Japanese case. The structural conditions characterized in §9.7—binary architecture of the prescriptive grammar, dense indexical loading of pronouns and SFPs, diversified *yakuwarigo* of the mediatized register, documented prescription–practice gap, structural inadequacy for minority speakers—obtain in particular configurations across other linguistic ecologies. Korean, with its highly elaborated honorific system, sophisticated indexical structure, and gendered politeness distinctions documented in the available scholarship,[^32][^31] shares relevant subsets of these conditions; Thai, with its gender-differentiated sentence-final particles[^33] and gendered politeness markers,[^26][^34] shares others. The framework's apparatus transfers to such cases at the conceptual level, with the specific empirical findings reflecting the specific configuration of structural conditions in each case.

The cumulative theoretical contribution can be stated as supporting an **integrated multi-strand framework** in which third-wave variationism, indexical-field theory, exemplar-based usage models, and the sociolinguistics of mediation and mediatization operate as complementary rather than competing strands. The integration is not eclectic juxtaposition but principled coordination, with each strand supplying analytical resources that the others lack and with the joint operation producing characterizations of media-influenced linguistic practice that no single strand alone could support.

## 10.2 Practical Implications for Understanding Minority Language Practices in Japan

The case's findings carry practical implications that, while remaining within the report's commitment to descriptive and analytical rather than normative argumentation, illuminate the lived realities of queer Japanese young adults and the sociolinguistic structures within which they navigate identity-relevant communicative tasks. The implications operate at four levels.

**Tactical performance under contemporary sociopolitical conditions.** The bounded-yet-substantive agency the case characterizes informs understanding of how minority speakers navigate the prescriptive binary system through the discretion–disclosure calibration that Itakura's Ni-chōme ethnography documents in the gay male case[^26] and that the framework predicts operates analogously across queer Japanese contexts. The contemporary sociopolitical environment, characterized in §5.1 with reference to Hiramoto and colleagues' documentation of the Japanese *bakkurashu* against feminism and the broader anti-feminist online discourse,[^26] combines growing institutional visibility with persistent backlash discourses, and the simultaneous presence of these countervailing pressures means that queer speakers' tactical-performance logic is not optional but structural. The case demonstrates that mediatized resources—shoujo manga, BL, yuri—participate in this tactical performance by supplying configurations whose dual-coded character (perceptible to in-group, less perceptible or differently activated for out-group) addresses the specific communicative challenge that the contemporary environment imposes. The practical recognition is that minority linguistic practice in Japan operates under conditions where stylistic choices carry significant interpretive consequences across audience positions, and that the resources mediatized cultural production supplies become especially valuable precisely because of those conditions.

**Cultural-linguistic policy and educational engagement.** The documented role of shoujo manga as a curated catalogue of indexical possibilities informs broader questions about how mediatized cultural production participates in language socialization. The available scholarship on Japanese language education has begun to engage explicitly with "the intersections of gender and sexuality" and with "identity construction in an LGBT youth group" within the broader project of "sustainable practices of diversity and inclusion" in Japanese language education.[^26] The case's findings suggest that this educational engagement, if it is to be empirically grounded, must reckon with the actual stylistic resources that learners and speakers draw upon, including the mediatized catalogues that shoujo manga and its subgenres supply. The implication is not that Japanese language pedagogy should explicitly teach manga registers, nor that prescriptive instruction should be abandoned, but that pedagogical engagement with the actual sociolinguistic landscape of contemporary Japanese—including its mediatized catalogues, its community-internal registers, and the documented prescription–production–perception gap—is more empirically defensible than pedagogy calibrated solely to the prescriptive system. The case substantiates the more general principle that responsible language pedagogy must engage the linguistic ecologies within which its learners actually operate, including the mediatized resources those ecologies supply.

**Critical engagement with the metapragmatic apparatus.** The case's recognition of the metapragmatic apparatus's role in reproducing prescriptive ideologies—the school instruction, etiquette manuals, dictionaries, media commentary, and implicit pragmatics of mass-circulation texts that Chapter 3 characterized through Okamoto's analysis,[^26] Endō's dictionary research, Miller's *burikko* analysis, and Nakamura's magazine analysis[^26]—informs critical engagement with how the prescriptive system maintains its operative force even as naturalistic majority practice retreats from its ideologically central forms. The metapragmatic apparatus does not merely describe prescriptive norms; it actively reproduces them through the institutional channels that successive generations of speakers encounter. The case's contribution is the recognition that this reproduction shapes the indexical fields against which all speakers, including queer speakers, must navigate, and that the "lingering distaste" Lunsing's and Maree's queer consultants express for the available pronoun choices[^26] is not merely an individual matter but a symptom of the apparatus's structural operation. Critical engagement with the apparatus, on the case's recognition, is not a normative project external to sociolinguistic analysis but an analytical commitment that responsible characterization of the system requires.

**Engagement with mediatized cultural production.** The case's specification of shoujo manga as a curated catalogue informs broader questions about how mediatized cultural production participates in—rather than merely reflects—the sociolinguistic system. The genre's preservation of stereotype-level forms that real-world young women have substantially abandoned, its modular elaboration of indexical fields beyond binary prescription, and its narrative intensification of configurations at moments of romantic and identity disclosure, together constitute a distinctive contribution to the broader linguistic ecology that majority face-to-face speech does not supply. The practical implication is that mediatized cultural production—including but not limited to shoujo manga—operates not as a marginal influence on language but as a substantive participant in the constitution and elaboration of indexical fields, with the participation being most clearly visible in cases where structural conditions make the contribution especially salient. The case's findings about the queer Japanese configuration substantiate this more general principle, with implications for how mediatized cultural production should be analyzed in any sociolinguistic case where stylized registers stage configurations that face-to-face options do not adequately supply.

The four levels of practical implication share a common structure: the case's findings inform engagement with the contemporary Japanese sociolinguistic landscape by making explicit the structural conditions, mediatized resources, and tactical-performance logics that characterize minority linguistic practice within it. The practical implications do not commit the report to specific policy or pedagogical proposals beyond what the descriptive analysis substantiates, but they make explicit how the descriptive analysis bears on the broader questions that engaged practitioners—educators, advocates, cultural producers, community members—necessarily face.

## 10.3 Methodological Limitations and Constraints on Inference

The methodological limitations that constrain the design's claims have been identified throughout the report, particularly in Chapter 6 (where they were inventoried as design constraints) and Chapter 9 (where they were specified as theoretical limits on what the case can support). The present section consolidates these limitations into a single accounting, framing them as constraints on what the case can support theoretically rather than as failures to be corrected, in keeping with the calibrated humility the framework adopts.

**Sample representativeness and bounded generalizability.** The stratified-purposive sampling specified in §6.3 supports claims about patterns within the recruited sample but does not support population-level inference about queer Japanese young adults as a whole, much less about queer Japanese speakers across age ranges or about the broader Japanese population. The recruitment pathways—queer community organizations, university LGBT student groups, online communities, snowball referrals, and shoujo/BL/yuri fan networks—each introduce specific selection effects that systematically over-represent participants who are out, community-engaged, digitally engaged, and fan-affiliated. The recruited sample correspondingly under-represents closeted, community-distant, and non-fan-affiliated members of the population, and the case's findings cannot be extrapolated to those under-represented segments without explicit acknowledgement of the extrapolation's limits.

**Self-report bias and the limits of consumption measurement.** The multi-indicator consumption-intensity construct specified in §6.2.3 is operationalized through self-reported measures whose validity is constrained by memory effects, social-desirability biases, and post-hoc rationalization. The design's use of multiple indicators (frequency, duration, breadth, depth, identity-relevance) provides partial mitigation by treating uniform inflation across indicators as a diagnostic of bias-driven response rather than of genuine variation, and by including free-text title-listing for partial verification. Objective consumption data that platform-derived analytics could in principle provide are precluded by the ethical and practical constraints articulated in §6.10, and the residual constraint that consumption measures remain self-reported is one that the design accepts rather than eliminates.

**The difficulty of isolating manga effects from broader media ecosystems.** Shoujo manga is one component of a media diet that includes BL, yuri, anime adaptations, light novels, drama CDs, fan fiction, social media discourse, mainstream variety television, and (for some) shōnen and seinen registers. Perfectly clean separation of shoujo's contribution from these adjacent registers is impossible, and the design's mitigation through comprehensive coverage of comparator media in the survey instrument (§6.4) supports separation only at the level of multivariate regression rather than at the level of fully orthogonal experimental control. Some component of any observed correlation between shoujo consumption and production patterns may reflect broader manga or fan-community engagement rather than shoujo specifically, and the case's findings must be calibrated to characterize shoujo's specific contribution within the broader ecology rather than as isolating shoujo from it.

**The cross-sectional design's limits on causal inference.** As §6.11, §7.8, and §9.3 articulated explicitly, the cross-sectional design cannot definitively rule out identity-driven self-selection as a contributing factor in observed consumption-production correlations. The selection hypothesis—that queer young adults with pre-existing inclinations toward non-default gender configurations may consume shoujo manga *because* its configurations resonate with those inclinations, with consumption operating as a downstream marker of identity orientation rather than as an upstream cause of linguistic practice—cannot be directly tested through cross-sectional design alone. The framework's internal-structure tests (the differential association of identity-relevance versus frequency indicators with production patterns; the configuration-specificity of consumption effects; the production–awareness convergence/divergence pattern) support inferences about the **structure** of the relationship rather than definitively establishing causal direction. The cross-sectional design's limit is therefore constitutive of what the case can claim, and the calibrated multi-pathway account of Chapter 9 explicitly accommodates this limit by characterizing the consumption-production relationship in terms more nuanced than mere causation.

**The observer's paradox and queer participants' elevated metapragmatic awareness.** The observer's paradox operates with distinctive force for queer participants whose elevated metapragmatic awareness, documented in the empirical literature on queer Japanese linguistic practice through Lunsing's, Maree's, Abe's, and Itakura's work,[^26][^31] renders interview production self-conscious in ways that the design treats as data rather than as noise to be eliminated. The matched interviewer identity, multi-context elicitation, and treatment of metapragmatic awareness as analytic resource together provide partial mitigation, but interview production cannot fully access vernacular registers used in genuinely community-internal contexts. The written and digitally-mediated production tasks (§6.6) supply complementary access to alternative contexts, but the residual constraint that no single design can fully eliminate observer effects remains a limit that any inference about production patterns must respect. Cross-prescriptive pronoun rates and stereotype-level SFP rates observed in the design's data are likely to under-estimate rates that would obtain in fully community-internal settings.

**The risk of imposing analytic categories drawn from Anglo-American queer linguistics.** The framework integrates Anglophone third-wave variationism with Japanese-specific indexicality and *yakuwarigo* scholarship, but the integration risks privileging Anglophone framings where they ill-fit Japanese realities. The mitigation strategies—use of Japanese-specific terminology in instruments (§6.2.1), engagement with Japanese-language scholarship throughout (Chapters 3–5), community consultation procedures (§6.10), methodological commitment to documenting where Japanese specificities require departure from Anglophone precedent—provide partial protection, but researchers' interpretive frames are partially shaped by their training, and reflexive attention to this constraint cannot fully eliminate its effects. The case's specific commitments to Japanese-specific terminology (*seiteki shōsūsha*, *X-jendā*, *onee*), to engagement with Itakura's framing of *onē-kotoba* deployment as operating against "the Euro-American resistance-minded queer theory,"[^26] and to acknowledgement that Japanese queer identity does not map cleanly onto Anglo-American LGBTQ+ categories, exemplify the kind of reflexive engagement the limit demands without fully resolving it.

**The design's proposed rather than implemented status.** As Chapters 6 and 7 made explicit, the design described in the report is proposed rather than implemented, and the report's claims are framed as predictions the design would test rather than findings produced by primary fieldwork. This limit is foundational rather than incidental: the calibrated multi-pathway account of Chapter 9 specifies what the framework predicts the case demonstrates given the available empirical record, but the actual empirical content of those predictions awaits the design's implementation and validation through pilot work, IRB review, community consultation, and full-scale data collection. The report's contribution is therefore the integrated analytical apparatus and its operationalization through specific methodological commitments, with the actual empirical findings awaiting future research that the present report's design specifications would support.

The cumulative effect of these limitations is to constrain what the case can claim while preserving what it can productively investigate. The case cannot demonstrate that shoujo manga causes queer young adults' linguistic patterns at the population level; it cannot rule out identity-driven self-selection as a contributing factor; it cannot achieve the longitudinal depth that a full developmental account would require; it cannot eliminate observer effects on production; it cannot generalize to the broader queer Japanese population; and, in its present form as a research design rather than an implementation, it cannot supply the empirical data that the predictions specified throughout require for validation. What the case can do, within these constraints, is **specify the conceptual apparatus** through which the relationship between shoujo manga consumption and queer Japanese linguistic practice can be analyzed; **articulate the framework-derived predictions** the apparatus generates; **integrate the existing empirical record on Japanese gendered language and queer linguistic practice** with the framework's predictions to characterize what the predictions imply; and **specify the methodological design** through which the predictions could be tested. Each of these contributions is real, and each is calibrated to what the case can support.

## 10.4 Proposed Follow-Up Studies and Future Research Directions

The methodological limitations of the present design specify, by their structure, the principal directions in which future research can extend the case's analytical contributions. Six follow-up directions emerge as particularly productive, each addressed in turn.

**Longitudinal designs.** The cross-sectional limitation that constrains causal inference about consumption-production relationships could be partially addressed through longitudinal designs that track participants' consumption and production over time. A multi-wave design surveying participants at intervals of (for example) 12 to 24 months would allow the analysis to assess whether changes in consumption-intensity indicators are associated with subsequent changes in production patterns, providing temporal evidence that directly addresses the selection-versus-exposure ambiguity that single-time-point designs cannot resolve. The framework's prediction is that within-individual increases in identity-relevance and depth indicators would precede within-individual increases in shoujo-distinctive configurational deployment, while within-individual increases in frequency alone would not, with the temporal differential supporting the exemplar-mediated uptake account against the pure-selection alternative. Longitudinal designs face their own methodological challenges (attrition, panel-conditioning effects, the difficulty of distinguishing within-individual change from measurement variability), but they supply the temporal evidence that the present cross-sectional design's limit makes impossible. A particular variant—cohort-comparison designs that track distinct generational cohorts through equivalent observation windows—would also illuminate the generational dimension flagged in §5.5 by characterizing how consumption-production relationships differ across cohorts whose media-ecology pathways have differed.

**Comparative work across shoujo-adjacent subgenres.** The difficulty of isolating shoujo's specific contribution from the broader shoujo-adjacent ecology could be addressed through dedicated comparative studies of Boys' Love, yuri, and josei manga as distinct registers whose specific contributions can be separated from the broader shoujo register through targeted analysis. A study comparing high-BL-consumption participants with low-BL-consumption participants matched on shoujo-narrow consumption would characterize BL's specific contribution to configurational repertoire; a parallel study for yuri would characterize yuri's specific contribution; and a study engaging josei manga as an adult-themed extension of shoujo conventions would characterize whether the adult-themed register supplies additional or alternative resources beyond what shoujo proper provides. The comparative work would also illuminate the question, raised in §4.5, of how BL's *bishōnen*-inflected register relates differently from broader media's *onē*/*okama* register to gay male participants' linguistic practice, and how yuri's continuity with shoujo configurations relates to lesbian participants' engagement with both registers. The empirical literature on the BL genre, its production conditions, and its reception by both heterosexual and queer audiences across multiple cultural contexts (Mizoguchi, Welker, Wood, Baudinette, Zhao and Madill, Jirattikorn, Fujimoto, Ishida) supplies the foundation that targeted comparative work could build on.

**Comparator studies engaging shōnen and seinen registers.** The framework's prediction that shoujo's configurations are taken up specifically by queer audiences whose structural inadequacy makes the configurations especially valuable could be tested through comparator studies engaging shōnen and seinen registers among queer participants and broader populations. The principal empirical question is whether high consumption of shōnen or seinen manga produces analogous configurational uptake among queer participants, or whether the specific structural alignment between shoujo's diversified *yakuwarigo* and queer indexical needs is what makes shoujo particularly productive. The framework predicts the latter, with shōnen and seinen consumption producing configurational uptake more strongly among male-identified participants whose stylistic projects align with masculine *yakuwarigo* and less strongly among queer participants whose stylistic projects do not. The comparator study would also illuminate whether the case's findings generalize to other manga genres or remain specific to shoujo's particular configurations, providing empirical traction for the framework's claim that media's contribution depends on the alignment between mediatized configurations and minority indexical needs.

**Cross-cultural extensions to other gendered-language communities.** The structural-condition analysis of §9.7 identified Korean and Thai as cases sharing relevant subsets of the structural conditions that make the Japanese case theoretically informative. Cross-cultural extensions could productively engage these and other cases.

For **Korean**, the case would build on the available scholarship on Korean honorifics, gendered terms of address, and gender performance through politeness distinctions.[^32][^31] As the available scholarship documents, "Korean speakers 'perform' gender using politeness distinctions as a primary resource"[^32] and Korean is "well-known for its highly sophisticated honorifics and other resources that index hierarchy, formality, personal closeness, etc."[^32] The empirical work on Kim Jong Un's Panmunjom-summit pronominal choices between honorific *ce* and non-honorific *na* operationalizes the indexical framework in a Korean political context,[^31] and the broader work on identity construction through gendered terms of address such as *unnie* and *oppa*[^31] supplies the empirical foundation for parallel analyses of queer Korean linguistic practice. Korean BL and yuri webtoons, fan-translation cultures, and the broader Korean queer cultural-production ecology would supply the mediatized catalogues whose configurational uptake among queer Korean young adults could be analyzed through the framework's apparatus, with the specific empirical findings reflecting Korean's distinctive structural conditions—the centrality of speech-style distinctions (*supnita* vs. *eyo*),[^32] the elaborated honorific architecture, the documented shift "as Korea is rapidly moving away from a hierarchical society and toward a more egalitarian one, informal language... is becoming preferred over formal language to some extent across genders... especially evident among the younger generation."[^32]

For **Thai**, the case would build on the available scholarship documenting "gender-differential tendencies in the use of Thai final particles where females are likely to use them more frequently"[^33] and the gendered politeness particles *khráp* (male) and *khâ/khá* (female) that mark formality and respect.[^26] The Thai pronoun system, with its multi-tiered status- and kinship-based architecture distinguishing first-person *phǒm* (male formal) from *dichan* (female formal) and the unisex *chan*, supplies indexical structure substantially different from Japanese binary prescription[^26] but no less rich for indexical-field analysis. The available evidence that "ka and khrap are actually not gender specific" in some contemporary uses[^34] indicates that Thai gendered particles operate with gradient rather than categorical loadings, suggesting that contemporary practice may be in flux in ways the framework's apparatus could productively characterize. Thai BL, whose substantial industry and transnational reception have been documented in the comparative scholarship cited in §5.4 (Baudinette, Jirattikorn), supplies the mediatized catalogue whose configurational uptake among queer Thai young adults could be analyzed through the framework's apparatus.

The cross-cultural extensions would not seek to demonstrate that the queer Japanese case generalizes universally—the case's distinctiveness, articulated in §9.7, is its theoretical strength rather than its limitation—but to specify how the framework's apparatus operates across cases sharing relevant subsets of structural conditions. The cumulative contribution would refine the framework's general predictions through comparative cases whose specific empirical findings vary with the specific configuration of conditions in each case.

**Perception-experimental extensions.** The production–perception asymmetries that §8.5 characterized as both empowering and constraining queer speakers' strategic positioning could be more fully operationalized through perception-experimental extensions that test the asymmetries with broader listener populations and across additional configuration-stimulus combinations. The proposed design's perception-experimental component (§6.7) operates within scale constraints; a dedicated perception-experimental program could expand the stimulus set, vary additional factors (interlocutor age, speaker accent, prosodic features), and recruit listener populations stratified along additional dimensions (regional location, generational cohort, fan-community participation, queer-community access). The expanded program would supply finer-grained characterization of how indexical activations vary across listener positions and how the asymmetries operate at scale.

**Ethnographic deepening within underdeveloped subpopulations.** The unevenness of coverage in the existing empirical record on queer Japanese linguistic practice—with lesbian and gay male practice substantially documented through Abe's, Maree's, Lunsing's, and Itakura's work[^26] but transgender and X-gender practice less densely documented—indicates that ethnographic deepening within these underdeveloped subpopulations is a substantive priority. Dedicated ethnographic studies of transgender Japanese linguistic practice across transition stages and identity orientations, and of X-gender linguistic practice within the developing community infrastructure that supports non-binary self-presentation in contemporary Japan, would fill empirical gaps that any aggregate analysis of queer Japanese linguistic practice currently must work around. The ethnographic work would also illuminate whether the framework's predictions about media-derived stylistic resources operate analogously across these subpopulations or whether subpopulation-specific structural conditions produce differential patterns that aggregate analyses could not detect.

**Engagement with the digital-mediation dimension.** The contemporary digital landscape characterized in §5.5 reorganizes queer community formation and stylistic-resource access in ways that the proposed design's survey-based capture of digital community participation can address only partially. Platform-specific corpus analysis—of queer Japanese Twitter/X discourse, of fan-community discourse on Pixiv and dedicated platforms, of BL/yuri fan-translation and discussion communities—would complement participant-level production data with platform-aggregated patterns that operate at scales no individual-level study can match. The work would draw on the broader sociolinguistic literature on digital mediation to characterize how platform affordances shape register access, audience design, and tactical-performance possibilities for queer Japanese young adults, with specific attention to how mediatized stylistic resources circulate through digital platforms in ways that amplify or modify the configurations the source manga register stages.

The six follow-up directions share a common structure: each addresses a specific limitation of the present design while extending the analytical contributions to questions the present design's scope did not fully accommodate. Together they specify a research program that the case's contributions could productively support, with the program's individual components addressable through dedicated future projects whose empirical findings would refine and extend the framework's apparatus.

## 10.5 Consolidation of the Central Argument and Closing Reflection

The report's central argument, articulated across nine preceding chapters and synthesized in Chapter 9, can be consolidated in its final form as follows. The argument concerns the relationship between shoujo manga consumption and gendered linguistic variation among queer Japanese young adults, and it specifies the mechanisms through which a stylized media register becomes available as a stylistic resource for speakers whose identities are inadequately served by prescriptive language ideologies.

The research questions and hypotheses articulated in Chapter 1 supply the structure for this consolidation. **RQ1** asked to what extent the frequency and intensity of shoujo manga consumption correlate with queer Japanese young adults' choice and distribution of first-person pronouns across communicative contexts, with the working hypothesis that higher consumption is associated with more diverse and contextually flexible pronoun repertoires. The framework-derived characterization developed in Chapter 7 supports a calibrated form of this hypothesis: higher consumption, particularly along the identity-relevance and depth indicators, is predicted to correlate with selective uptake of cross-prescriptive pronouns (especially *boku* in identity-salient contexts) that align with shoujo's *bishōnen*-inflected and *otokoyaku*-inflected configurations, but the correlation is concentrated in particular contextual configurations rather than distributed uniformly. **RQ2** asked the parallel question about gendered sentence-final particles, with the hypothesis that consumption correlates with selective uptake of marked SFPs patterning with manga conventions in identity-salient or affectively heightened utterances. The framework-derived characterization supports this hypothesis with particular force: shoujo manga's preservation of stereotype-level feminine SFPs that real-world young women have substantially abandoned[^26] supplies precisely the kind of mediatized resource whose uptake by high-consumption queer participants the framework predicts, with the uptake concentrated in performative, creative, and affectively heightened functions. **RQ3** asked how contextual factors mediate the relationship between manga exposure and observed linguistic patterns, with the hypothesis that manga-derived forms appear most prominently in peer, in-group, written, and identity-performative contexts. The framework-derived characterization developed in Chapter 8 supports this hypothesis through the five-dimensional structure of indexical salience, with manga effects concentrated at the intersection of casual formality, written or digital modality, identity-aligned topic, queer in-group with shared-fan-identity audience, and stereotype-level form positioning.

The case's contribution to sociolinguistic theory can be restated as the **specification of the mechanisms** through which a stylized media register becomes available as a stylistic resource for speakers whose identities are inadequately served by prescriptive language ideologies. The mechanisms operate through:

- **Indexical fields populated by mediatized stagings**, with shoujo manga's curated and modular *yakuwarigo* supplying configurations that elaborate the indexical fields beyond binary prescription;
- **Dual cognitive pathways of conscious appropriation and exemplar-driven incorporation**, operating jointly across the indicator–marker–stereotype salience hierarchy with their relative weights determined by form-level salience, modality of production, and metapragmatic engagement;
- **Audience-design tactical performances** calibrated to the discretion–disclosure management that the contemporary Japanese sociopolitical environment imposes on queer speakers,[^26] with configurations functioning as dual-coded signs whose loadings are perceptible to in-group recognizers and less perceptible or differently activated for out-group listeners;
- **Situational concentration in casual identity-salient contexts**, with manga effects emerging most prominently at the intersection of formality, modality, and topic conditions that align with the conditions under which mediatized exemplars are most likely to be activated in production;
- **Perceptual ecologies in which the same forms carry different activations across listener positions**, with the production–perception asymmetry being itself the operative content of the prescription–production–perception gap that Japanese sociolinguistics has long documented.[^26]

The case's contribution to understandings of media's role in personal expression can be restated as the **demonstration that media's contribution operates at the level of stylistic agency rather than at the level of systemic transformation**. The framework's analytical target is not the establishment that shoujo manga changes the gendered grammar of Japanese at the population level—a claim the framework explicitly disavows and that the structural anchoring to majority patterns in normatively oriented contexts militates against—but the specification of how mediatized exemplars, indexical fields, and stylistic agency interact in the repertoires of speakers whose identities are not adequately served by prescriptive language ideologies. The level at which media's contribution operates is what makes it empirically tractable and theoretically informative: at the level of stylistic agency, the contribution is observable through the differential signatures the framework specifies and the mechanisms that the case's analyses operationalize; at the level of systemic transformation, the contribution would be either invisible against the structural anchoring or indistinguishable from the alternative pathways with which it co-operates. The case's specific contribution is therefore the demonstration that, when the level of analysis is properly calibrated, media's role in linguistic practice is neither marginal (as strong dismissive accounts would have it) nor dominant (as strong media-driven-change accounts would have it) but **selective, agentive, salience-stratified, contextually concentrated, and ideologically contested** in ways the framework's apparatus can characterize.

A closing reflection on minority linguistic practices. The agency that the case characterizes, articulated in §9.5, is **bounded yet substantive**, operating within constraints that the prescriptive system imposes while drawing on resources that the prescriptive system does not authorize. The boundedness reflects the structural conditions—the metapragmatic apparatus that reproduces prescriptive ideologies, the perceptual asymmetries that operate within ideologically contested ecologies, the contemporary sociopolitical environment that combines institutional visibility with persistent backlash[^26]—that constrain what queer linguistic practice can accomplish. The substantiveness reflects the genuine differences that the practice produces in linguistic deployment across contexts and audiences, the real indexical content that the deployments carry for those positioned to recognize them, and the structural enablement that mediatized circulation of curated catalogues supplies. Neither boundedness alone nor substantiveness alone adequately characterizes the agency; both are constitutive of what it consists in.

The case substantiates a more general principle: minority linguistic practices reveal the agency of speakers within and against prescriptive language ideologies, with the agency being characterized by selectivity rather than wholesale transformation, contextual calibration rather than uniform deployment, salience-stratified cognitive structure rather than uniform consciousness or unconsciousness, ideological contestation rather than ideological transcendence, and structural enablement by the catalogues that mediatized circulation supplies rather than autonomous creation independent of available resources. The principle is not a celebration of agency that ignores its constraints, nor a critique of constraint that dismisses agency's reality, but a specification of how agency and constraint jointly constitute minority linguistic practice within ideologically organized linguistic systems.

The broader question that animated the report from its opening chapter—under what conditions, and through what mechanisms, does mass media participate in shaping the linguistic practices of those who consume it—receives the case's calibrated answer as a contribution to ongoing sociolinguistic conversation rather than as a final settlement of long-running debates. The conditions that make media's participation most clearly visible are those of structural inadequacy in face-to-face linguistic options for the speakers' identity-relevant communicative needs, alignment between mediatized configurations and minority indexical needs, and engagement with the source register sufficient to support identity-salience-weighted exemplar storage. The mechanisms through which the participation operates are indexical fields populated by mediatized stagings, dual cognitive pathways of appropriation and incorporation, audience-design tactical performances, situational concentration in identity-salient contexts, and perceptual ecologies in which mediatized configurations carry differential activations across listener positions. The conditions and mechanisms specify, jointly, what the case substantively contributes to the broader sociolinguistic conversation about media-language relationships—a contribution that refines the conceptual apparatus through which comparable cases can be analyzed, supports the mediated innovation account against both strong-driven-change and dismissive-minimization positions in the long-running debate descending from Trudgill's, Stuart-Smith's, and Androutsopoulos's work,[^32][^33][^31] and demonstrates that the empirical tractability of media's specific contribution depends not on simplifying the relationship to monocausal claims but on specifying the multi-pathway structure through which the relationship operates.

The queer Japanese shoujo manga case is, in its specificity, one paradigmatic instance of how the framework's apparatus operates. Its findings do not generalize universally; its distinctiveness is its theoretical strength rather than a limitation; its contribution is the conceptual refinement that comparable cases can build upon. What the case substantiates is the general principle that mass media participates in linguistic practice in ways that are real, bounded, and analytically tractable when properly specified—and that minority speakers, navigating prescriptive systems whose architectures are structurally inadequate to their identity-relevant communicative needs, exercise the bounded-yet-substantive agency through which mediatized resources are taken up, recombined, and deployed in service of self-construction within and against the ideological landscapes that constrain them. The closing reflection the report offers is not a triumphalist account of media-enabled liberation, nor a pessimistic account of ideological totalization, but a calibrated characterization of how speakers, resources, contexts, and ideologies jointly constitute the linguistic practice in which minority identity is continuously enacted. That characterization, in its calibration, is what the report substantively contributes—and what subsequent research, building on the framework's apparatus and the methodological design the report specifies, can extend, refine, and ultimately substantiate through the empirical work that the design's implementation would generate.
